# Section I: Grammar & Sentence Structure

## Chapter 1: Error Detection & Core Grammar Rules

### Q001 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The principal, along with the senior teachers, ___ attending the science exhibition.

A. are
B. were
C. is
D. have been

**Answer:** C. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — is (Correct):** When two subjects are joined by 'along with', the verb agrees with the first subject ('The principal'), which is singular, so 'is' is correct.
- **A — are:** The plural auxiliary 'are' wrongly agrees with the plural noun 'teachers' inside the parenthetical phrase rather than the singular head subject.
- **B — were:** The plural past verb 'were' disagrees in number with the singular subject 'The principal'.
- **D — have been:** The plural present perfect form 'have been' cannot agree with a singular subject like 'The principal'.
- **Exam Extra:** Prepositional linkers such as 'along with', 'together with', 'as well as', 'besides', and 'in addition to' never make a singular subject plural.

### Q002 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The quarterly report, together with all financial annexures, ___ lying on the director's desk.

A. are
B. is
C. were
D. have been

**Answer:** B. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — is (Correct):** Because 'together with' connects a parenthetical phrase to the first subject ('The quarterly report'), the verb must agree with the singular noun 'report', making 'is' correct.
- **A — are:** The plural present verb 'are' wrongly follows the plural noun 'annexures' instead of the singular subject 'report'.
- **C — were:** The plural past form 'were' fails number agreement with the singular head noun 'report'.
- **D — have been:** The plural form 'have been' does not match the singular subject 'The quarterly report'.
- **Exam Extra:** Mentally bracket out any phrase starting with 'together with' or 'along with' to locate the true subject quickly in SSC error-spotting items.

### Q003 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Spot the error: The captain as well as / the eleven players were / felicitated by the / sports ministry yesterday.

A. The captain as well as
B. the eleven players were
C. felicitated by the
D. sports ministry yesterday.

**Answer:** B. the eleven players were
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — the eleven players were (Correct):** In 'The captain as well as the eleven players', the verb must agree with the first subject ('The captain'), so 'the eleven players was' should replace 'the eleven players were'.
- **A — The captain as well as:** The opening segment correctly introduces the singular subject 'The captain' followed by the additive linker 'as well as'.
- **C — felicitated by the:** The passive participle phrase 'felicitated by the' is properly formed and links smoothly to the agent.
- **D — sports ministry yesterday.:** The concluding noun phrase 'sports ministry yesterday.' correctly specifies the agent and past time marker.
- **Exam Extra:** With 'as well as', verb agreement always locks onto the preceding subject; unlike 'and', it does not compound subjects into a plural.

### Q004 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Each of the shortlisted applicants ___ submitted the attested certificates.

A. has
B. have
C. are
D. were

**Answer:** A. has
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — has (Correct):** 'Each of + plural noun' always takes a singular verb because 'each' refers to individuals one by one; therefore 'has' is correct.
- **B — have:** The plural auxiliary 'have' wrongly agrees with the object of the preposition ('applicants') instead of the singular subject 'Each'.
- **C — are:** The plural linking verb 'are' disagrees with 'Each' and cannot form an active perfect tense with 'submitted'.
- **D — were:** The plural past verb 'were' fails both number agreement with 'Each' and voice structure before 'submitted the attested certificates'.
- **Exam Extra:** Indefinite distributives ('each', 'every', 'either', 'neither') followed by 'of + plural noun' always govern a singular verb and singular pronoun.

### Q005 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Every boy and every girl in the auditorium ___ issued a laminated identity card.

A. were
B. are
C. have been
D. was

**Answer:** D. was
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — was (Correct):** When singular nouns are preceded by 'Every' and joined by 'and', the subject remains singular in meaning and requires the singular verb 'was'.
- **A — were:** The plural past verb 'were' violates the rule that 'Every X and every Y' takes a singular verb.
- **B — are:** The plural present form 'are' fails singular agreement with the distributive subject.
- **C — have been:** The plural present perfect passive auxiliary 'have been' disagrees in number with 'Every boy and every girl'.
- **Exam Extra:** Even when repeated ('Every boy and every girl'), 'every' always treats the group distributively and takes a singular verb.

### Q006 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Neither of the two budget proposals ___ acceptable to the audit committee.

A. are
B. were
C. is
D. have been

**Answer:** C. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — is (Correct):** 'Neither of + plural noun' means 'not one of the two' and takes a singular verb, making 'is' the right choice.
- **A — are:** The plural present verb 'are' wrongly tracks the plural noun 'proposals' instead of the singular pronoun 'Neither'.
- **B — were:** The plural past verb 'were' violates singular agreement with 'Neither of the two budget proposals'.
- **D — have been:** The plural auxiliary 'have been' does not agree with the singular subject 'Neither'.
- **Exam Extra:** Do not confuse 'neither of + plural noun' (singular verb) with 'neither A nor B' (verb agrees with the nearer subject B).

### Q007 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Neither the head clerk nor the junior assistants ___ available in the office.

A. were
B. was
C. is
D. has been

**Answer:** A. were
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — were (Correct):** With correlative conjunctions 'neither ... nor', the verb agrees with the nearer subject ('the junior assistants'), which is plural, so 'were' is correct.
- **B — was:** The singular past verb 'was' wrongly agrees with the farther singular subject 'head clerk' instead of 'junior assistants'.
- **C — is:** The singular present verb 'is' disagrees with the plural nearer subject 'the junior assistants'.
- **D — has been:** The singular form 'has been' cannot pair with the plural nearer subject 'the junior assistants'.
- **Exam Extra:** Under the proximity rule for 'neither...nor', 'either...or', and 'not only...but also', always match the verb to the subject closer to the verb.

### Q008 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Spot the error: Either the project manager / or his two associates / is responsible for / this scheduling delay.

A. Either the project manager
B. or his two associates
C. is responsible for
D. this scheduling delay.

**Answer:** C. is responsible for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — is responsible for (Correct):** Under the proximity rule for 'Either ... or', the verb must agree with the nearer subject ('his two associates'), so 'is responsible for' must be corrected to 'are responsible for'.
- **A — Either the project manager:** The opening segment accurately introduces the first singular alternative with 'Either'.
- **B — or his two associates:** The second segment correctly pairs 'or' with the plural nearer subject 'his two associates'.
- **D — this scheduling delay.:** The final noun phrase 'this scheduling delay.' is grammatically complete as the object of the preposition 'for'.
- **Exam Extra:** Place the plural noun second in 'either...or' / 'neither...nor' constructions when both singular and plural subjects appear, and match the plural verb to it.

### Q009 · Subject-Verb Agreement

One of my closest childhood friends ___ in Jaipur now.

A. live
B. lives
C. are living
D. have lived

**Answer:** B. lives
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — lives (Correct):** In 'One of + plural noun', the head word is singular ('One'), so it requires the singular present verb 'lives'.
- **A — live:** The base plural verb 'live' wrongly agrees with 'friends' rather than the singular head pronoun 'One'.
- **C — are living:** The plural progressive form 'are living' disagrees with the singular subject 'One'.
- **D — have lived:** The plural present perfect 'have lived' fails singular subject-verb agreement with 'One'.
- **Exam Extra:** Pattern: 'One of + plural noun/pronoun + singular verb' (e.g., 'One of the machines was faulty').

### Q010 · Subject-Verb Agreement

She is one of the few employees who ___ received the annual merit award.

A. has
B. was
C. is
D. have

**Answer:** D. have
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — have (Correct):** In 'one of the [plural noun] + who/that', the relative pronoun 'who' refers to its plural antecedent ('employees'), so the clause takes the plural verb 'have'.
- **A — has:** The singular auxiliary 'has' ignores the plural antecedent 'employees' of the relative pronoun 'who'.
- **B — was:** The singular past verb 'was' cannot form the active perfect construction with 'received an award' and disagrees with 'employees'.
- **C — is:** The singular present verb 'is' neither agrees with 'employees' nor pairs grammatically with active 'received an award'.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'One of the employees HAS left' with 'She is one of the employees who HAVE left' — only 'the only one of the...' reverts to a singular verb after 'who'.

### Q011 · Subject-Verb Agreement

A large number of students ___ applied for the scholarship examination.

A. has
B. is
C. have
D. was

**Answer:** C. have
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — have (Correct):** 'A number of + plural noun' means 'several/many' and takes a plural verb, so 'have' is required before 'applied'.
- **A — has:** The singular auxiliary 'has' confuses 'A large number of' with 'The number of'.
- **B — is:** The singular verb 'is' disagrees in number and cannot form an active present perfect with 'applied'.
- **D — was:** The singular past verb 'was' fails both plural agreement and active tense structure with 'applied'.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the contrast: 'A number of + plural noun + plural verb', whereas 'The number of + plural noun + singular verb'.

### Q012 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The number of pending applications ___ increased sharply since Monday.

A. have
B. are
C. were
D. has

**Answer:** D. has
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — has (Correct):** 'The number of + plural noun' refers to a single numerical figure/count and therefore takes a singular verb ('has').
- **A — have:** The plural auxiliary 'have' wrongly agrees with 'applications' instead of the singular head noun 'number'.
- **B — are:** The plural verb 'are' disagrees with 'The number' and does not work with 'increased' in the active sense here.
- **C — were:** The plural past verb 'were' fails singular subject-verb agreement and cannot form an active perfect tense.
- **Exam Extra:** In 'The number of applications has risen', 'number' is the true singular head noun, not 'applications'.

### Q013 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The quality of these Alphonso mangoes ___ praised by every customer.

A. are
B. is
C. were
D. have been

**Answer:** B. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — is (Correct):** In 'noun + of + noun' phrases, the verb agrees with the head noun before 'of' ('The quality'), which is singular, so 'is' is correct.
- **A — are:** The plural verb 'are' wrongly agrees with 'mangoes' inside the prepositional phrase rather than 'The quality'.
- **C — were:** The plural past verb 'were' disagrees with the singular uncountable head noun 'quality'.
- **D — have been:** The plural auxiliary 'have been' fails singular agreement with 'The quality'.
- **Exam Extra:** Examples like 'The list of selected candidates is ready' and 'The quality of these mangoes is good' follow the same head-noun agreement rule.

### Q014 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Many a diligent candidate ___ failed to clear the typing test due to nervousness.

A. have
B. are
C. were
D. has

**Answer:** D. has
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — has (Correct):** The idiomatic expression 'Many a + singular noun' always takes a singular verb despite its plural meaning, making 'has' correct.
- **A — have:** The plural auxiliary 'have' wrongly follows the plural sense of 'many' instead of the singular structure 'Many a candidate'.
- **B — are:** The plural linking verb 'are' disagrees in number and cannot form the active perfect tense with 'failed'.
- **C — were:** The plural past verb 'were' fails singular agreement and active voice structure before 'failed'.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'Many candidates have failed' (plural noun + plural verb) with 'Many a candidate has failed' (singular noun + singular verb).

### Q015 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The principal and the secretary ___ scheduled to inspect the laboratory at noon.

A. is
B. was
C. are
D. has been

**Answer:** C. are
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — are (Correct):** When two nouns joined by 'and' each carry their own article ('The principal and the secretary'), they refer to two distinct individuals and take a plural verb ('are').
- **A — is:** The singular present verb 'is' treats the two separately articled titles as a single person.
- **B — was:** The singular past verb 'was' fails plural agreement with two distinct officials.
- **D — has been:** The singular perfect form 'has been' does not match the plural compound subject.
- **Exam Extra:** Two articles = two people = plural verb; one article before the first noun ('The principal and secretary') = one person holding two roles = singular verb.

### Q016 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Spot the error: The renowned poet and / distinguished statesman have / arrived at the venue / to inaugurate the seminar.

A. The renowned poet and
B. distinguished statesman have
C. arrived at the venue
D. to inaugurate the seminar.

**Answer:** B. distinguished statesman have
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — distinguished statesman have (Correct):** Because only one article 'The' precedes 'renowned poet and distinguished statesman', both titles refer to a single person, so 'distinguished statesman has' should replace 'distinguished statesman have'.
- **A — The renowned poet and:** The opening segment properly pairs the single definite article 'The' with the first descriptor 'renowned poet and'.
- **C — arrived at the venue:** The verb phrase 'arrived at the venue' correctly completes the present perfect construction with 'at' for a specific location.
- **D — to inaugurate the seminar.:** The infinitive phrase of purpose 'to inaugurate the seminar.' is grammatically accurate.
- **Exam Extra:** When two nouns joined by 'and' share one determiner ('my friend and mentor', 'a black and white cow'), use a singular verb.

### Q017 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The selection committee ___ reached its unanimous decision after three hours.

A. have
B. were
C. are
D. has

**Answer:** D. has
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — has (Correct):** Here the collective noun 'committee' acts as a unified single body (shown by 'unanimous' and 'its'), so it takes the singular verb 'has'.
- **A — have:** The plural auxiliary 'have' conflicts with the singular possessive determiner 'its' and the word 'unanimous'.
- **B — were:** The plural past verb 'were' disagrees with a unified collective noun and fails active perfect syntax with 'reached'.
- **C — are:** The plural present verb 'are' neither matches the singular collective sense nor pairs with active 'reached its decision'.
- **Exam Extra:** Watch pronoun clues: 'its' signals singular collective agreement ('has'), while 'their' signals divided members ('have').

### Q018 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The committee members ___ divided in their opinions over the proposed amendment.

A. are
B. is
C. was
D. has been

**Answer:** A. are
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — are (Correct):** The explicit plural noun 'members' and the plural context ('divided in their opinions') require the plural verb 'are'.
- **B — is:** The singular present verb 'is' disagrees with the plural subject 'The committee members'.
- **C — was:** The singular past verb 'was' fails plural subject-verb agreement with 'members'.
- **D — has been:** The singular form 'has been' cannot agree with 'members' or match the adjective 'divided' cleanly in this clause.
- **Exam Extra:** Collective nouns like 'jury', 'committee', and 'audience' take plural verbs when acting individually, and 'committee members' is always plural.

### Q019 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Bread and butter ___ his only breakfast during his college days.

A. are
B. were
C. was
D. have been

**Answer:** C. was
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — was (Correct):** 'Bread and butter' represents a single combined meal/concept, and 'during his college days' places it in the past, so the singular past verb 'was' is required.
- **A — are:** The plural present verb 'are' breaks both the single-concept rule and the past-time context ('during his college days').
- **B — were:** The plural past verb 'were' wrongly treats the unified breakfast dish as two separate items.
- **D — have been:** The plural present perfect form 'have been' disagrees in number and tense with the singular past statement.
- **Exam Extra:** Paired nouns expressing one unified idea ('bread and butter', 'slow and steady', 'rice and curry', 'horse and carriage') take a singular verb.

### Q020 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Mathematics ___ considered a scoring subject by most engineering aspirants.

A. is
B. are
C. were
D. have been

**Answer:** A. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — is (Correct):** Names of academic disciplines ending in '-ics' (such as Mathematics, Physics, Economics) are singular in meaning and take a singular verb ('is').
- **B — are:** The plural verb 'are' mistakes the '-s' ending of 'Mathematics' for a plural noun.
- **C — were:** The plural past verb 'were' fails singular subject-verb agreement for a general academic truth.
- **D — have been:** The plural perfect form 'have been' disagrees in number with the singular subject 'Mathematics'.
- **Exam Extra:** When preceded by 'his/the/my' to mean calculation ability or statistical data (e.g., 'His mathematics are weak', 'The economics of the project are sound'), '-ics' nouns can take plural verbs.

### Q021 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The tailor's heavy scissors ___ lying near the cutting table.

A. is
B. was
C. has been
D. are

**Answer:** D. are
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — are (Correct):** Two-part instruments and garments such as 'scissors', 'trousers', 'spectacles', and 'tongs' are always plural and take a plural verb ('are').
- **A — is:** The singular verb 'is' treats the inherently plural noun 'scissors' as singular.
- **B — was:** The singular past verb 'was' disagrees with the plural-only noun 'scissors'.
- **C — has been:** The singular form 'has been' fails plural subject-verb agreement with 'scissors'.
- **Exam Extra:** To make 'scissors' or 'trousers' singular, precede them with 'a pair of' ('A pair of scissors is on the desk').

### Q022 · Subject-Verb Agreement

This pair of leather shoes ___ surprisingly comfortable for long walks.

A. are
B. were
C. is
D. have been

**Answer:** C. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — is (Correct):** When a two-part noun is preceded by 'This pair of', the singular head noun 'pair' governs the verb, requiring 'is'.
- **A — are:** The plural present verb 'are' wrongly agrees with 'shoes' instead of the singular head noun 'pair'.
- **B — were:** The plural past verb 'were' disagrees with the singular demonstrative phrase 'This pair'.
- **D — have been:** The plural perfect auxiliary 'have been' fails singular agreement with 'This pair'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'These leather shoes are comfortable' (plural) with 'This pair of leather shoes is comfortable' (singular head noun 'pair').

### Q023 · Subject-Verb Agreement

All the antique furniture in the reception hall ___ polished yesterday.

A. was
B. were
C. are
D. have been

**Answer:** A. was
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — was (Correct):** 'Furniture' is an uncountable singular noun (never pluralized as 'furnitures'), and 'yesterday' requires the past simple passive 'was'.
- **B — were:** The plural past verb 'were' wrongly treats the uncountable noun 'furniture' as plural.
- **C — are:** The plural present verb 'are' fails both singular uncountable agreement and past-time reference ('yesterday').
- **D — have been:** The plural present perfect form 'have been' disagrees in number and cannot pair with a definite past marker like 'yesterday'.
- **Exam Extra:** Common uncountable nouns in SSC exams: furniture, luggage, baggage, equipment, scenery, poetry, information, and advice — all take singular verbs.

### Q024 · Subject-Verb Agreement

The news from the flood-affected district ___ deeply encouraging today.

A. are
B. is
C. were
D. have been

**Answer:** B. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — is (Correct):** Despite ending in '-s', 'news' is an uncountable singular noun and takes the singular present verb 'is'.
- **A — are:** The plural verb 'are' is misled by the final '-s' in 'news'.
- **C — were:** The plural past verb 'were' disagrees with the singular subject 'news' and conflicts with 'today'.
- **D — have been:** The plural perfect form 'have been' cannot agree with the uncountable singular noun 'news'.
- **Exam Extra:** Other singular nouns ending in '-s' include news, innings, summons, billiards, measles, and mumps.

### Q025 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Every piece of confidential information ___ verified before the circular was issued.

A. were
B. are
C. have been
D. was

**Answer:** D. was
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — was (Correct):** 'Every piece of + uncountable noun' ('information') is singular and matches the past-tense context ('was issued'), so 'was' is correct.
- **A — were:** The plural past verb 'were' disagrees with the singular head phrase 'Every piece'.
- **B — are:** The plural present verb 'are' fails both singular agreement and past-tense consistency with 'was issued'.
- **C — have been:** The plural present perfect form 'have been' disagrees with 'Every piece' and clashes with the past clause.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Information' has no plural form 'informations'; quantify it with 'a piece of information' or 'pieces of information'.

### Q026 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Several useful pieces of advice ___ given to the interns during orientation.

A. was
B. is
C. were
D. has been

**Answer:** C. were
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — were (Correct):** While 'advice' itself is uncountable, the countable partitive 'Several useful pieces' is plural and pairs with the plural past verb 'were'.
- **A — was:** The singular past verb 'was' ignores the plural countable head noun 'pieces'.
- **B — is:** The singular present verb 'is' disagrees in number with 'Several useful pieces'.
- **D — has been:** The singular perfect form 'has been' fails plural agreement with 'pieces'.
- **Exam Extra:** Partitives let you count uncountable nouns: 'a piece of advice is...' vs 'several pieces of advice were...'.

### Q027 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Spot the error: More than one / senior official were / suspended following the / internal vigilance inquiry.

A. More than one
B. senior official were
C. suspended following the
D. internal vigilance inquiry.

**Answer:** B. senior official were
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — senior official were (Correct):** 'More than one' is followed by a singular noun ('senior official') and takes a singular verb, so 'senior official were' must be corrected to 'senior official was'.
- **A — More than one:** The opening determiner phrase 'More than one' is grammatically standard before a singular countable noun.
- **C — suspended following the:** The passive participle and preposition 'suspended following the' form a valid clause continuation.
- **D — internal vigilance inquiry.:** The concluding noun phrase 'internal vigilance inquiry.' properly serves as the object of 'following'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'More than one official WAS suspended' (singular) with 'More officials than one WERE suspended' (plural).

### Q028 · Subject-Verb Agreement

Spot the error: The breathtaking sceneries / of Kashmir Valley / captivated every photographer / during the autumn tour.

A. The breathtaking sceneries
B. of Kashmir Valley
C. captivated every photographer
D. during the autumn tour.

**Answer:** A. The breathtaking sceneries
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The breathtaking sceneries (Correct):** 'Scenery' is an uncountable noun and has no plural form 'sceneries'; the first segment should be 'The breathtaking scenery'.
- **B — of Kashmir Valley:** The prepositional modifier 'of Kashmir Valley' correctly specifies the location of the scenery.
- **C — captivated every photographer:** The past simple verb and object 'captivated every photographer' are grammatically sound and match the singular or uncountable subject.
- **D — during the autumn tour.:** The adverbial time phrase 'during the autumn tour.' appropriately completes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Never add '-s' or '-ies' to uncountable nouns like scenery, poetry, machinery, stationery, or crockery.

### Q029 · Tenses

Hardly had the train arrived at the platform ___ the passengers began to board.

A. when
B. than
C. then
D. while

**Answer:** A. when
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — when (Correct):** The negative adverb 'Hardly' (like 'Scarcely') pairs strictly with the conjunction 'when' (or 'before'), so 'when' is correct.
- **B — than:** The conjunction 'than' pairs with comparatives and 'No sooner', never with 'Hardly'.
- **C — then:** The adverb 'then' indicates sequence and cannot link correlative clauses after 'Hardly had'.
- **D — while:** The conjunction 'while' expresses simultaneous duration and does not complete the correlative pair with 'Hardly'.
- **Exam Extra:** Pair formula: 'Hardly / Scarcely + had + S + V3 + when + S + V2'.

### Q030 · Tenses

No sooner had the meeting begun ___ the power supply failed.

A. when
B. than
C. then
D. as

**Answer:** B. than
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — than (Correct):** Because 'sooner' is a comparative adverb, 'No sooner' must always pair with 'than', making 'than' the only valid choice.
- **A — when:** The conjunction 'when' belongs with 'Hardly' or 'Scarcely', not with comparative 'No sooner'.
- **C — then:** The time adverb 'then' is a common spelling/grammar trap for 'than' after 'No sooner'.
- **D — as:** The conjunction 'as' cannot complete the comparative structure initiated by 'No sooner'.
- **Exam Extra:** Pair formula: 'No sooner + had + S + V3 + than + S + V2' (or 'No sooner + did + S + V1 + than + S + V2').

### Q031 · Tenses

Spot the error: Scarcely I had / stepped out of / the house when / it started pouring.

A. Scarcely I had
B. stepped out of
C. the house when
D. it started pouring.

**Answer:** A. Scarcely I had
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Scarcely I had (Correct):** When a sentence begins with a negative adverb such as 'Scarcely', 'Hardly', or 'No sooner', subject-auxiliary inversion is mandatory: 'Scarcely had I' instead of 'Scarcely I had'.
- **B — stepped out of:** The past participle phrase 'stepped out of' correctly completes 'had ... stepped'.
- **C — the house when:** The noun and correlative conjunction 'the house when' properly link the two clauses.
- **D — it started pouring.:** The concluding clause 'it started pouring.' uses simple past tense accurately after 'when'.
- **Exam Extra:** Negative-adverb fronting triggers question-like word order: Auxiliary ('had'/'did') before Subject ('I').

### Q032 · Tenses

Spot the error: No sooner did / the referee blew / the whistle than / the players stopped.

A. No sooner did
B. the referee blew
C. the whistle than
D. the players stopped.

**Answer:** B. the referee blew
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — the referee blew (Correct):** After the past auxiliary 'did' in 'No sooner did', the main verb must be in its base form (V1), so 'the referee blew' should be 'the referee blow'.
- **A — No sooner did:** The opening inverted sequence 'No sooner did' is grammatically valid when followed by a subject and base verb.
- **C — the whistle than:** The noun and correlative conjunction 'the whistle than' correctly pairs with 'No sooner'.
- **D — the players stopped.:** The second clause 'the players stopped.' appropriately uses simple past tense.
- **Exam Extra:** Always check the verb form after inverted 'did': 'did + subject + V1', whereas 'had + subject + V3'.

### Q033 · Tenses

By next June, she ___ at this research institute for five years.

A. will work
B. works
C. will have worked
D. worked

**Answer:** C. will have worked
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — will have worked (Correct):** The time marker 'By next June' together with the duration 'for five years' indicates an action completed up to a future point, requiring Future Perfect ('will have worked').
- **A — will work:** The simple future 'will work' does not convey completion accumulated over five years up to next June.
- **B — works:** The simple present 'works' expresses a current habit and clashes with 'By next June ... for five years'.
- **D — worked:** The simple past 'worked' cannot describe a milestone occurring in the future ('By next June').
- **Exam Extra:** 'By + future time' ('by tomorrow', 'by next June', 'by 2030') is the classic signal for the Future Perfect tense ('will/shall have + V3').

### Q034 · Tenses

By this time next month, the engineering team ___ the bridge inspection.

A. will complete
B. completes
C. completed
D. will have completed

**Answer:** D. will have completed
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — will have completed (Correct):** 'By this time next month' specifies a future deadline by which the task will already be finished, requiring the Future Perfect 'will have completed'.
- **A — will complete:** The simple future 'will complete' indicates future action without expressing prior completion by the deadline.
- **B — completes:** The simple present 'completes' cannot denote a future completion milestone here.
- **C — completed:** The simple past 'completed' contradicts the future time marker 'By this time next month'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use Future Perfect ('will have + V3') when a sentence stresses completion before or by a stated future moment.

### Q035 · Tenses

She has been revising the syllabus ___ morning without taking a break.

A. for this
B. since this
C. from this
D. during this

**Answer:** B. since this
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — since this (Correct):** 'This morning' is a definite starting point of time with Present Perfect Continuous ('has been revising'), so 'since this' is required.
- **A — for this:** Using 'for this' is wrong because 'this morning' denotes a starting point rather than a duration.
- **C — from this:** The preposition 'from' is not used with Perfect Continuous tenses to mark an ongoing starting point; 'since' is required.
- **D — during this:** The preposition 'during' fits a completed event within a span, not an action continuing from a start point in Perfect Continuous.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'since' for a point of time (since 8 a.m., since Monday, since this morning) and 'for' for a period of time (for two hours, for five days).

### Q036 · Tenses

The elderly patient has been waiting in the reception lounge ___ two hours.

A. since
B. from
C. by
D. for

**Answer:** D. for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — for (Correct):** 'Two hours' represents a duration/period of time with Present Perfect Continuous ('has been waiting'), so 'for' is correct.
- **A — since:** The preposition 'since' requires a fixed starting point (such as 'since 10 a.m.'), not a duration like 'two hours'.
- **B — from:** The preposition 'from' cannot express ongoing elapsed duration in a Present Perfect Continuous clause.
- **C — by:** The preposition 'by' indicates a deadline or agency, not elapsed waiting time.
- **Exam Extra:** Duration markers like 'two hours', 'a long time', 'many years', and 'several weeks' always take 'for', never 'since'.

### Q037 · Tenses

The investigating officer asked me where I ___ the original property deed.

A. keep
B. have kept
C. had kept
D. will keep

**Answer:** C. had kept
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — had kept (Correct):** Because the reporting verb 'asked' is in the past tense, the embedded question shifts back into the past perfect ('had kept') to express an earlier action.
- **A — keep:** The simple present 'keep' violates the sequence of tenses following the past reporting verb 'asked'.
- **B — have kept:** The present perfect 'have kept' fails to backshift after the past reporting verb 'asked'.
- **D — will keep:** The future form 'will keep' breaks sequence of tenses and makes no sense for a document already placed somewhere.
- **Exam Extra:** In indirect/reported speech after a past reporting verb ('asked', 'said', 'told'), Simple Past / Present Perfect shifts to Past Perfect ('had + V3').

### Q038 · Tenses

Spot the error: The patient died / before the ambulance / could reach the / district hospital.

A. The patient died
B. before the ambulance
C. could reach the
D. district hospital.

**Answer:** A. The patient died
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The patient died (Correct):** When two actions happen in the past, the earlier action takes Past Perfect ('had + V3'); thus 'The patient died' should be 'The patient had died'.
- **B — before the ambulance:** The conjunction 'before the ambulance' properly introduces the later past clause.
- **C — could reach the:** The modal verb phrase 'could reach the' accurately expresses past ability in the later clause.
- **D — district hospital.:** The noun phrase 'district hospital.' correctly completes the object of 'reach'.
- **Exam Extra:** Classic two-past-action rule: Earlier action -> Past Perfect ('had + V3'); Later action -> Simple Past ('V2').

### Q039 · Tenses

Spot the error: I have received / your registered parcel / three days ago / at my residence.

A. I have received
B. your registered parcel
C. three days ago
D. at my residence.

**Answer:** A. I have received
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — I have received (Correct):** Specific finished past-time expressions like 'three days ago', 'yesterday', or 'last week' take Simple Past ('I received'), not Present Perfect ('I have received').
- **B — your registered parcel:** The noun phrase 'your registered parcel' serves properly as the direct object of the verb.
- **C — three days ago:** The past time marker 'three days ago' is correctly formed and sets a definite past point.
- **D — at my residence.:** The prepositional phrase 'at my residence.' accurately states the delivery location.
- **Exam Extra:** Never combine Present Perfect ('has/have + V3') with definite past adverbs such as 'ago', 'yesterday', 'last night', or 'in 2019'.

### Q040 · Tenses

It is high time the municipal authorities ___ strict action against illegal encroachments.

A. take
B. will take
C. have taken
D. took

**Answer:** D. took
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — took (Correct):** Expressions like 'It is time', 'It is high time', and 'It is about time' followed by a subject take the past subjunctive (V2), so 'took' is required.
- **A — take:** The present form 'take' fails the mandatory V2 rule after 'It is high time + subject'.
- **B — will take:** The future tense 'will take' is ungrammatical in 'It is high time' clauses.
- **C — have taken:** The present perfect 'have taken' violates the fixed V2 pattern following 'It is high time'.
- **Exam Extra:** Structure: 'It is high time + subject + V2', whereas without a subject it takes an infinitive ('It is high time to take action').

### Q041 · Tenses

Spot the error: My younger brother / is owning two / commercial apartments near / the metro station.

A. My younger brother
B. is owning two
C. commercial apartments near
D. the metro station.

**Answer:** B. is owning two
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — is owning two (Correct):** 'Own' is a stative verb of possession and is not normally used in continuous tenses; 'is owning two' should be 'owns two'.
- **A — My younger brother:** The singular subject 'My younger brother' is grammatically well-formed and expects a singular verb.
- **C — commercial apartments near:** The object phrase 'commercial apartments near' accurately describes the property and its location.
- **D — the metro station.:** The prepositional object 'the metro station.' cleanly completes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Stative verbs (own, belong, possess, know, believe, resemble, consist, seem) generally resist '-ing' progressive forms.

### Q042 · Tenses

Spot the error: Our geography teacher / explained to us / that the Earth / revolved around the Sun.

A. Our geography teacher
B. explained to us
C. that the Earth
D. revolved around the Sun.

**Answer:** D. revolved around the Sun.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — revolved around the Sun. (Correct):** Universal truths, scientific facts, and habitual realities remain in the Present Simple even when the reporting verb is in the past; 'revolved around the Sun.' should be 'revolves around the Sun.'.
- **A — Our geography teacher:** The subject noun phrase 'Our geography teacher' is valid and introduces the speaker cleanly.
- **B — explained to us:** The reporting verb and indirect object 'explained to us' correctly use 'to' after 'explain'.
- **C — that the Earth:** The conjunction and subject 'that the Earth' properly introduce the reported clause.
- **Exam Extra:** Exception to tense backshift: Universal truths, proverbs, and mathematical facts always stay in Simple Present.

### Q043 · Tenses

Spot the error: Ten years passed / since my uncle / shifted permanently to / his ancestral village.

A. Ten years passed
B. since my uncle
C. shifted permanently to
D. his ancestral village.

**Answer:** A. Ten years passed
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Ten years passed (Correct):** In 'Time-span + since + Simple Past', the main clause before 'since' takes Present Perfect, so 'Ten years passed' must be 'Ten years have passed'.
- **B — since my uncle:** The conjunction and subject 'since my uncle' correctly introduce the starting event clause.
- **C — shifted permanently to:** The simple past verb phrase 'shifted permanently to' is in the proper V2 form required after 'since'.
- **D — his ancestral village.:** The destination phrase 'his ancestral village.' accurately completes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Rule: 'Present Perfect (has/have + V3) + since + Simple Past (V2)' when measuring elapsed time up to the present.

### Q044 · Tenses

Spot the error: You had better / to consult a / specialist before taking / any strong medication.

A. You had better
B. to consult a
C. specialist before taking
D. any strong medication.

**Answer:** B. to consult a
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — to consult a (Correct):** Modal idioms 'had better', 'would rather', and 'had sooner' take a bare infinitive (V1 without 'to'), so 'to consult a' should be 'consult a'.
- **A — You had better:** The opening subject and modal idiom 'You had better' are properly formed for giving strong advice.
- **C — specialist before taking:** The object and participle clause 'specialist before taking' correctly use a gerund ('taking') after the preposition 'before'.
- **D — any strong medication.:** The concluding object 'any strong medication.' cleanly finishes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Always drop 'to' after 'had better' and 'would rather' ('You had better leave now', not 'to leave').

### Q045 · Conditionals

If I ___ the exact answer, I would tell you right away.

A. know
B. had known
C. will know
D. knew

**Answer:** D. knew
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — knew (Correct):** This is a Second Conditional (hypothetical present) sentence paired with 'would tell', so the 'if'-clause requires Simple Past ('knew').
- **A — know:** The simple present 'know' belongs to a First Conditional ('will tell'), clashing with 'would tell'.
- **B — had known:** The past perfect 'had known' belongs to a Third Conditional ('would have told'), not 'would tell'.
- **C — will know:** The future auxiliary 'will know' is ungrammatical inside an 'if'-condition clause.
- **Exam Extra:** Second Conditional formula: 'If + Subject + V2 / were, Subject + would/could/might + V1'.

### Q046 · Conditionals

If she ___ five minutes earlier, she would have caught the express bus.

A. left
B. leaves
C. had left
D. would leave

**Answer:** C. had left
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — had left (Correct):** The main clause 'would have caught' signals a Third Conditional (unreal past), which requires Past Perfect ('had left') in the 'if'-clause.
- **A — left:** The simple past 'left' pairs with 'would catch' (Second Conditional), not 'would have caught'.
- **B — leaves:** The simple present 'leaves' cannot express an unfulfilled past condition.
- **D — would leave:** The modal 'would leave' is incorrect inside a standard conditional 'if'-clause.
- **Exam Extra:** Third Conditional formula: 'If + Subject + had + V3, Subject + would/could/might + have + V3'.

### Q047 · Conditionals

Spot the error: If it will rain / heavily tonight, / we shall postpone / the outdoor rehearsal.

A. If it will rain
B. heavily tonight,
C. we shall postpone
D. the outdoor rehearsal.

**Answer:** A. If it will rain
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — If it will rain (Correct):** In a First Conditional sentence, the 'if'-clause takes Simple Present ('If it rains'), not 'will'; 'If it will rain' should be 'If it rains'.
- **B — heavily tonight,:** The adverbial phrase 'heavily tonight,' properly modifies the verb of raining.
- **C — we shall postpone:** The main clause 'we shall postpone' correctly uses future tense for the result of a First Conditional.
- **D — the outdoor rehearsal.:** The direct object 'the outdoor rehearsal.' completes the sentence cleanly.
- **Exam Extra:** Never use 'will' or 'shall' inside time/condition clauses introduced by 'if', 'unless', 'until', 'when', or 'as soon as'.

### Q048 · Conditionals

Spot the error: If I was / the finance minister, / I would simplify / the tax structure.

A. If I was
B. the finance minister,
C. I would simplify
D. the tax structure.

**Answer:** A. If I was
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — If I was (Correct):** In an unreal or hypothetical present condition ('I would simplify'), the subjunctive 'were' is required regardless of the subject, so 'If I was' should be 'If I were'.
- **B — the finance minister,:** The noun phrase 'the finance minister,' correctly serves as the subject complement.
- **C — I would simplify:** The main clause 'I would simplify' properly follows the Second Conditional result structure.
- **D — the tax structure.:** The object 'the tax structure.' completes the main verb accurately.
- **Exam Extra:** Formal SSC grammar always prefers 'If I were', 'If he were', and 'If she were' for counterfactual imagination.

### Q049 · Conditionals

Spot the error: If you would have / informed me earlier, / I would definitely / have helped you.

A. If you would have
B. informed me earlier,
C. I would definitely
D. have helped you.

**Answer:** A. If you would have
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — If you would have (Correct):** In a Third Conditional sentence, 'would have' must never appear in the 'if'-clause; 'If you would have' should be 'If you had'.
- **B — informed me earlier,:** The past participle and adverb 'informed me earlier,' fit properly after 'had'.
- **C — I would definitely:** The result clause 'I would definitely' uses the correct modal and adverb placement.
- **D — have helped you.:** The perfect infinitive 'have helped you.' accurately completes the Third Conditional result.
- **Exam Extra:** Avoid double 'would have': keep 'had + V3' in the 'if'-clause and 'would have + V3' only in the result clause.

### Q050 · Conditionals

___ the warning signs earlier, the mountaineers would not have ventured onto the glacier.

A. If they noticed
B. Did they notice
C. Were they noticing
D. Had they noticed

**Answer:** D. Had they noticed
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Had they noticed (Correct):** 'Had they noticed' is the inverted form of 'If they had noticed', matching the Third Conditional main clause 'would not have ventured'.
- **A — If they noticed:** The Second Conditional form 'If they noticed' cannot pair with 'would not have ventured'.
- **B — Did they notice:** The past interrogative 'Did they notice' cannot function as a conditional clause here.
- **C — Were they noticing:** The past continuous 'Were they noticing' does not form a valid inverted Third Conditional.
- **Exam Extra:** Conditional inversion drops 'if' and fronts the auxiliary: 'Had + S + V3' (3rd), 'Were + S + ...' (2nd), 'Should + S + V1' (1st).

### Q051 · Conditionals

Spot the error: Unless you / do not submit / the fee receipt, / your seat stands cancelled.

A. Unless you
B. do not submit
C. the fee receipt,
D. your seat stands cancelled.

**Answer:** B. do not submit
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — do not submit (Correct):** 'Unless' already carries a negative meaning ('if not'), so using 'do not' creates a double negative; 'do not submit' must be corrected to 'submit'.
- **A — Unless you:** The opening conjunction and subject 'Unless you' correctly initiate the negative condition.
- **C — the fee receipt,:** The object phrase 'the fee receipt,' properly completes the verb 'submit'.
- **D — your seat stands cancelled.:** The result clause 'your seat stands cancelled.' is grammatically accurate.
- **Exam Extra:** Both 'unless' and 'until' are inherently negative and must never be followed by 'not' or 'no' in the same clause.

### Q052 · Conditionals

If you heat pure water to 100 degrees Celsius at sea level, it ___.

A. boiled
B. would boil
C. has boiled
D. boils

**Answer:** D. boils
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — boils (Correct):** Scientific facts and universal laws follow the Zero Conditional ('If + Present Simple, Present Simple'), making 'boils' correct.
- **A — boiled:** The simple past 'boiled' clashes with a timeless scientific fact stated in the present tense.
- **B — would boil:** The conditional 'would boil' turns a factual law into a hypothetical situation.
- **C — has boiled:** The present perfect 'has boiled' does not match the general-truth structure of Zero Conditionals.
- **Exam Extra:** Zero Conditional pattern: 'If + S + V1/V5, S + V1/V5' for general truths and automatic cause-and-effect.

### Q053 · Conditionals

Spot the error: He behaves as / though he is / the sole owner / of this enterprise.

A. He behaves as
B. though he is
C. the sole owner
D. of this enterprise.

**Answer:** B. though he is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — though he is (Correct):** Clauses introduced by 'as if' or 'as though' expressing an unreal or contrary-to-fact claim require the past subjunctive 'were', so 'though he is' must be 'though he were'.
- **A — He behaves as:** The main clause 'He behaves as' correctly introduces the manner comparison.
- **C — the sole owner:** The complement 'the sole owner' is properly formed as a singular noun phrase.
- **D — of this enterprise.:** The prepositional phrase 'of this enterprise.' completes the sentence accurately.
- **Exam Extra:** 'As if / As though' takes 'were' (or V2) when the comparison is imaginary, and 'had + V3' when referring to an unreal past.

### Q054 · Conditionals

Spot the error: Suppose if she / refuses the transfer / order, what will / the department do?

A. Suppose if she
B. refuses the transfer
C. order, what will
D. the department do?

**Answer:** A. Suppose if she
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Suppose if she (Correct):** Using both 'Suppose' and 'if' together is superfluous; 'Suppose if she' should be either 'Suppose she' or 'If she'.
- **B — refuses the transfer:** The verb and object 'refuses the transfer' correctly agree with the singular subject 'she'.
- **C — order, what will:** The noun and question start 'order, what will' properly transition into the interrogative main clause.
- **D — the department do?:** The concluding question clause 'the department do?' correctly uses the base verb 'do' after 'will'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Suppose' and 'Supposing' already mean 'if', so never write 'Suppose if' or 'Supposing if' in formal English.

### Q055 · Pronouns

Between you and ___, this confidential tender looks poorly drafted.

A. I
B. myself
C. me
D. mine

**Answer:** C. me
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — me (Correct):** Prepositions such as 'between' always govern the objective case of pronouns, so 'me' is required after 'Between you and'.
- **A — I:** The nominative pronoun 'I' is ungrammatical as the object of the preposition 'Between'.
- **B — myself:** The reflexive pronoun 'myself' cannot replace the simple objective pronoun 'me' when no reflexive emphasis is needed.
- **D — mine:** The possessive pronoun 'mine' cannot function as the object of 'Between'.
- **Exam Extra:** Always use objective pronouns ('me, him, her, us, them') after prepositions like 'between', 'among', 'for', 'with', and 'except'.

### Q056 · Pronouns

Spot the error: One must always / keep his promises / if one wishes / to earn respect.

A. One must always
B. keep his promises
C. if one wishes
D. to earn respect.

**Answer:** B. keep his promises
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — keep his promises (Correct):** When the indefinite pronoun 'one' is the subject, subsequent possessive references must stay consistent with 'one's', so 'keep his promises' should be 'keep one's promises'.
- **A — One must always:** The opening clause 'One must always' correctly pairs the indefinite subject 'One' with the modal 'must'.
- **C — if one wishes:** The conditional clause 'if one wishes' maintains proper third-person indefinite consistency.
- **D — to earn respect.:** The infinitive complement 'to earn respect.' cleanly finishes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Maintain strict pronoun consistency: 'One' -> 'one' / 'one's' / 'oneself' (never shift to 'he/his/him' in SSC grammar).

### Q057 · Pronouns

The candidate ___ the panel interviewed first impressed everyone with her clarity.

A. who
B. whose
C. which
D. whom

**Answer:** D. whom
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — whom (Correct):** In '___ the panel interviewed first', the panel is the subject of 'interviewed' and the candidate is its object, so the objective relative pronoun 'whom' is required.
- **A — who:** The nominative pronoun 'who' is incorrect because the relative pronoun acts as the object of 'interviewed', not its subject.
- **B — whose:** The possessive pronoun 'whose' indicates ownership and does not fit as the object of 'interviewed'.
- **C — which:** The relative pronoun 'which' refers to things or animals, not a human candidate.
- **Exam Extra:** Quick test: substitute 'he/him' — if 'him' fits ('the panel interviewed him'), choose 'whom'; if 'he' fits, choose 'who'.

### Q058 · Pronouns

Spot the error: The journalist whom / broke the story / received a national / award last week.

A. The journalist whom
B. broke the story
C. received a national
D. award last week.

**Answer:** A. The journalist whom
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The journalist whom (Correct):** Inside the relative clause '___ broke the story', the pronoun serves as the subject of 'broke', so 'The journalist whom' must be 'The journalist who'.
- **B — broke the story:** The verb and object 'broke the story' properly express the past action performed by the journalist.
- **C — received a national:** The main verb and object start 'received a national' correctly use past simple tense.
- **D — award last week.:** The closing phrase 'award last week.' accurately completes the object and past time reference.
- **Exam Extra:** Count subjects and finite verbs in the relative clause: when a finite verb ('broke') lacks its own subject, use nominative 'who'.

### Q059 · Pronouns

Spot the error: Every employee should / avail of this / medical insurance scheme / before March ends.

A. Every employee should
B. avail of this
C. medical insurance scheme
D. before March ends.

**Answer:** B. avail of this
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — avail of this (Correct):** 'Avail' is a transitive verb in the pattern 'avail oneself of something', so 'avail of this' lacks a reflexive pronoun and must be 'avail himself/herself of this' (or 'avail themselves of this').
- **A — Every employee should:** The opening phrase 'Every employee should' correctly introduces a singular distributive subject with 'should'.
- **C — medical insurance scheme:** The noun phrase 'medical insurance scheme' serves properly as the object of 'of'.
- **D — before March ends.:** The time clause 'before March ends.' is grammatically sound in the present simple.
- **Exam Extra:** Verbs that commonly take a reflexive pronoun when no other direct object is present: avail, absent, acquit, enjoy, exert, pride, resign.

### Q060 · Pronouns

The five members of the panel congratulated ___ on completing the audit.

A. each other
B. themselves
C. one another
D. another

**Answer:** C. one another
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — one another (Correct):** Traditional SSC grammar reserves 'each other' for two people/things and 'one another' for more than two; since five members are involved, 'one another' is required.
- **A — each other:** The reciprocal pronoun 'each other' is restricted in prescriptive exam grammar to exactly two participants.
- **B — themselves:** Using 'themselves' changes the reciprocal action (A congratulating B, B congratulating A) into self-congratulation.
- **D — another:** The bare determiner 'another' does not function as a reciprocal pronoun after 'congratulated'.
- **Exam Extra:** Reciprocal rule: 'each other' = 2 entities; 'one another' = 3 or more entities.

### Q061 · Pronouns

Spot the error: I, you and / Rohan will jointly / organize the farewell / function next Friday.

A. I, you and
B. Rohan will jointly
C. organize the farewell
D. function next Friday.

**Answer:** A. I, you and
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — I, you and (Correct):** For positive or neutral joint actions, English pronoun order follows 2-3-1 (second person, third person, first person), so 'I, you and' must be 'You, Rohan and I' (shifting 'I' after 'Rohan').
- **B — Rohan will jointly:** The noun and verb phrase 'Rohan will jointly' correctly uses future tense with an adverb of manner.
- **C — organize the farewell:** The verb and object 'organize the farewell' pair cleanly in the base form after 'will'.
- **D — function next Friday.:** The concluding phrase 'function next Friday.' properly completes the object and future time marker.
- **Exam Extra:** Pronoun sequence: 2-3-1 ('You, he and I') for positive/normal statements; 1-2-3 ('I, you and he') when confessing a mistake or blame.

### Q062 · Pronouns

Spot the error: Let you and / I resolve this / minor misunderstanding before / the meeting begins.

A. Let you and
B. I resolve this
C. minor misunderstanding before
D. the meeting begins.

**Answer:** B. I resolve this
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — I resolve this (Correct):** The verb 'Let' takes pronouns in the objective case, so 'I resolve this' must be corrected to 'me resolve this'.
- **A — Let you and:** The opening sequence 'Let you and' is valid because 'you' has the same form in both nominative and objective cases.
- **C — minor misunderstanding before:** The object and conjunction 'minor misunderstanding before' properly connect the action to the time clause.
- **D — the meeting begins.:** The closing clause 'the meeting begins.' uses simple present tense accurately.
- **Exam Extra:** Objective case rule applies after 'let', 'like', 'unlike', 'except', and 'but' (when used as a preposition): 'Let you and me go'.

### Q063 · Pronouns

It is I who ___ answering your queries today.

A. is
B. are
C. were
D. am

**Answer:** D. am
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — am (Correct):** In 'It is I who ___', the verb after 'who' agrees with its antecedent 'I', requiring the first-person singular verb 'am'.
- **A — is:** The third-person singular verb 'is' wrongly agrees with the dummy subject 'It' instead of the antecedent 'I'.
- **B — are:** The plural verb 'are' does not agree with the singular first-person antecedent 'I'.
- **C — were:** The plural past verb 'were' disagrees in both person/number and present tense ('today').
- **Exam Extra:** In cleft sentences ('It is I who am...', 'It is you who are...'), match the verb in the relative clause to the personal pronoun before 'who'.

### Q064 · Pronouns

Spot the error: This is the / best documentary which / I have watched / on wildlife conservation.

A. This is the
B. best documentary which
C. I have watched
D. on wildlife conservation.

**Answer:** B. best documentary which
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — best documentary which (Correct):** After superlative adjectives ('the best'), 'that' is preferred over 'which' as the relative pronoun, so 'best documentary which' should be 'best documentary that'.
- **A — This is the:** The opening cleft phrase 'This is the' correctly introduces the superlative noun phrase.
- **C — I have watched:** The relative clause verb 'I have watched' accurately uses present perfect for a life experience.
- **D — on wildlife conservation.:** The prepositional phrase 'on wildlife conservation.' properly modifies 'documentary'.
- **Exam Extra:** Prefer relative pronoun 'that' after superlatives and words like 'all', 'only', 'same', 'none', 'nothing', and 'any'.

### Q065 · Pronouns

Spot the error: The climate of / Bengaluru is much / milder than / New Delhi.

A. The climate of
B. Bengaluru is much
C. milder than
D. New Delhi.

**Answer:** D. New Delhi.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — New Delhi. (Correct):** Comparisons must be between like things — climate must be compared with climate, not a city — so 'New Delhi.' should be 'that of New Delhi.'.
- **A — The climate of:** The opening phrase 'The climate of' correctly introduces the singular subject of comparison.
- **B — Bengaluru is much:** The verb and intensifier 'Bengaluru is much' properly pair 'much' with a comparative adjective.
- **C — milder than:** The comparative phrase 'milder than' is accurately formed for a two-city comparison.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'that of' for singular nouns ('the climate of X is milder than that of Y') and 'those of' for plural nouns ('the streets of X are wider than those of Y').

### Q066 · Pronouns

Spot the error: Neither of the / two sisters brought / their admit card / to the centre.

A. Neither of the
B. two sisters brought
C. their admit card
D. to the centre.

**Answer:** C. their admit card
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — their admit card (Correct):** 'Neither of the two sisters' is singular and feminine, so it requires a singular possessive adjective ('her admit card') rather than plural 'their admit card'.
- **A — Neither of the:** The opening phrase 'Neither of the' correctly initiates the negative distributive subject.
- **B — two sisters brought:** The noun and past verb 'two sisters brought' properly provide the plural noun after 'of' and the main verb.
- **D — to the centre.:** The destination phrase 'to the centre.' accurately completes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Singular distributives ('each', 'every', 'either', 'neither') take singular possessives ('his/her'), not plural 'their', in traditional SSC grammar.

### Q067 · Articles

Mr. Sharma is ___ honest officer who never compromises on integrity.

A. a
B. an
C. the
D. one

**Answer:** B. an
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — an (Correct):** Because 'honest' begins with a silent 'h' and starts with a vowel sound ('on-ist'), it takes the indefinite article 'an'.
- **A — a:** The article 'a' requires an initial consonant sound, whereas 'honest' begins with a vowel sound.
- **C — the:** The definite article 'the' is inappropriate here because 'officer' is being introduced generally, not uniquely specified.
- **D — one:** The numeral 'one' stresses numerical contrast ('one vs two') rather than simple class membership before 'officer who...'.
- **Exam Extra:** Choose 'a' vs 'an' strictly by the initial spoken sound, not the initial letter ('an honest man', 'an hour', 'an heir').

### Q068 · Articles

She secured admission to ___ university known for marine engineering.

A. an
B. the
C. a
D. any

**Answer:** C. a
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — a (Correct):** Although 'university' starts with the letter 'u', it is pronounced with a consonant 'y' sound ('yoo-ni-ver-si-ty'), so it requires 'a'.
- **A — an:** The article 'an' is wrong because 'university' begins with a consonant /j/ sound despite starting with the vowel letter 'u'.
- **B — the:** The definite article 'the' would refer to a previously identified university, whereas here an unspecified university is meant.
- **D — any:** The determiner 'any' does not normally pair with a singular affirmative statement of past admission.
- **Exam Extra:** Words starting with vowel letters that take 'a' due to a 'y' or 'w' consonant sound: 'a university', 'a European', 'a uniform', 'a one-rupee note'.

### Q069 · Articles

His father was elected ___ MLA from the western constituency.

A. a
B. the
C. some
D. an

**Answer:** D. an
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — an (Correct):** The initialism 'MLA' is pronounced 'em-el-ay', beginning with a vowel sound (/e/), so it takes 'an'.
- **A — a:** The article 'a' fails because 'MLA' starts with a vowel sound ('em'), not a consonant sound.
- **B — the:** The definite article 'the' is unnecessary when stating a general elective office/title without unique specification.
- **C — some:** The determiner 'some' cannot precede a singular countable noun phrase like 'MLA'.
- **Exam Extra:** Abbreviations beginning with F, H, L, M, N, R, S, or X usually start with a vowel sound and take 'an' (an FIR, an MP, an RTI, an X-ray).

### Q070 · Articles

Spot the error: Meera has been / learning to play / flute at the / academy since April.

A. Meera has been
B. learning to play
C. flute at the
D. academy since April.

**Answer:** C. flute at the
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — flute at the (Correct):** Before the name of a musical instrument used with 'play' or 'learn to play', the definite article 'the' is required, so 'flute at the' must be 'the flute at the'.
- **A — Meera has been:** The opening clause 'Meera has been' correctly starts the Present Perfect Continuous tense.
- **B — learning to play:** The verb phrase 'learning to play' properly links 'learning' to the infinitive 'to play'.
- **D — academy since April.:** The concluding phrase 'academy since April.' accurately gives the location and starting time marker.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'the' before musical instruments when referring to playing or learning them ('play the piano', 'play the sitar').

### Q071 · Articles

___ higher you climb up the mountain, the thinner the air becomes.

A. A
B. An
C. The
D. That

**Answer:** C. The
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The (Correct):** Parallel comparative constructions expressing proportional change follow the pattern 'The + comparative..., the + comparative...', requiring 'The' at the start.
- **A — A:** The indefinite article 'A' cannot initiate a parallel comparative 'the ... the ...' structure.
- **B — An:** The indefinite article 'An' is invalid both grammatically for this comparative idiom and phonetically before 'higher'.
- **D — That:** The demonstrative 'That' breaks the fixed double-comparative construction requiring 'The'.
- **Exam Extra:** Formula: 'The + comparative degree ..., the + comparative degree ...' (e.g., 'The sooner, the better'; 'The more you read, the more you learn').

### Q072 · Articles

Spot the error: The French is / widely spoken across / several countries in / West Africa.

A. The French is
B. widely spoken across
C. several countries in
D. West Africa.

**Answer:** A. The French is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The French is (Correct):** Names of languages do not take 'the' (adding 'the' before 'French' refers to the French people, which would also take a plural verb); 'The French is' should be 'French is'.
- **B — widely spoken across:** The passive verb phrase 'widely spoken across' accurately describes a language's usage.
- **C — several countries in:** The quantifier and noun 'several countries in' properly introduce the geographic region.
- **D — West Africa.:** The proper noun phrase 'West Africa.' correctly concludes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Rule: Zero article before languages ('English', 'French', 'Hindi'), whereas 'the + nationality' refers to the people of that nation.

### Q073 · Articles

Fortunately, ___ volunteers stayed back to clean the auditorium after the event.

A. few
B. little
C. a little
D. a few

**Answer:** D. a few
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — a few (Correct):** 'Fortunately' signals a positive outcome, and 'volunteers' is plural countable, so 'a few' (meaning some, enough to help) is correct.
- **A — few:** Using 'few' gives a negative sense ('hardly any'), which contradicts the positive adverb 'Fortunately'.
- **B — little:** The quantifier 'little' is strictly for uncountable nouns and cannot modify plural 'volunteers'.
- **C — a little:** The quantifier 'a little' applies only to uncountable nouns, not plural countable nouns like 'volunteers'.
- **Exam Extra:** Countable: 'few' (almost none), 'a few' (some), 'the few' (all the small number available). Uncountable: 'little', 'a little', 'the little'.

### Q074 · Articles

Spot the error: The Mount Everest is / the highest peak / in the Himalayan / mountain range.

A. The Mount Everest is
B. the highest peak
C. in the Himalayan
D. mountain range.

**Answer:** A. The Mount Everest is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The Mount Everest is (Correct):** Individual mountain peaks (such as Mount Everest, Kanchenjunga, Mount Abu) never take 'the', so 'The Mount Everest is' should be 'Mount Everest is'.
- **B — the highest peak:** The superlative phrase 'the highest peak' rightly uses 'the' before a superlative adjective.
- **C — in the Himalayan:** The prepositional phrase 'in the Himalayan' accurately introduces the adjective modifying 'mountain range'.
- **D — mountain range.:** The closing noun phrase 'mountain range.' cleanly completes the prepositional phrase.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'the' before mountain ranges ('the Himalayas', 'the Alps', 'the Andes'), but zero article before single mountain peaks ('Mount Everest').

### Q075 · Articles

Spot the error: The convicted smuggler / was promptly sent / to the prison / by the magistrate.

A. The convicted smuggler
B. was promptly sent
C. to the prison
D. by the magistrate.

**Answer:** C. to the prison
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — to the prison (Correct):** When someone is sent to prison, hospital, school, or church for its primary purpose (here, imprisonment), no article is used; 'to the prison' should be 'to prison'.
- **A — The convicted smuggler:** The opening subject 'The convicted smuggler' is grammatically well-formed.
- **B — was promptly sent:** The passive verb phrase 'was promptly sent' accurately uses past simple passive with proper adverb placement.
- **D — by the magistrate.:** The agent phrase 'by the magistrate.' correctly names the judicial authority.
- **Exam Extra:** Omit 'the' for primary purpose ('go to school to study', 'go to hospital for treatment', 'sent to prison'); use 'the' when visiting for another reason ('went to the prison to meet the warden').

### Q076 · Modifiers

Spot the error: Walking across the / dimly lit park, / a stray dog / bit the jogger.

A. Walking across the
B. dimly lit park,
C. a stray dog
D. bit the jogger.

**Answer:** A. Walking across the
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Walking across the (Correct):** A participial phrase at the start of a sentence must refer to the subject of the main clause; here 'Walking across the dimly lit park' illogically modifies 'a stray dog' instead of the jogger, creating a dangling modifier ('While the jogger was walking across the...').
- **B — dimly lit park,:** The participial object phrase 'dimly lit park,' properly forms a compound adjective before 'park'.
- **C — a stray dog:** The noun phrase 'a stray dog' is grammatically sound on its own, though it becomes the mismatched subject of the dangling opening phrase.
- **D — bit the jogger.:** The verb and object 'bit the jogger.' form a valid simple past predicate.
- **Exam Extra:** Always verify that the subject immediately following an introductory '-ing' or '-ed' participle phrase is the actual doer of that participle action.

### Q077 · Modifiers

Spot the error: Being a rainy / day, we decided / to cancel the / botanical garden trip.

A. Being a rainy
B. day, we decided
C. to cancel the
D. botanical garden trip.

**Answer:** A. Being a rainy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Being a rainy (Correct):** 'Being a rainy day' lacks its own dummy subject 'It' and therefore dangles onto 'we' (implying 'we' were a rainy day); it must be 'It being a rainy'.
- **B — day, we decided:** The transition 'day, we decided' properly separates the introductory clause from the main subject and verb.
- **C — to cancel the:** The infinitive introduction 'to cancel the' accurately follows the verb 'decided'.
- **D — botanical garden trip.:** The closing noun phrase 'botanical garden trip.' cleanly completes the object of 'cancel'.
- **Exam Extra:** Weather, time, and season phrases starting with 'Being...' require dummy 'It' upfront ('It being a hot day...', 'It being Sunday...').

### Q078 · Modifiers

Spot the error: The newly hired / intern is / enough mature to / handle client escalations.

A. The newly hired
B. intern is
C. enough mature to
D. handle client escalations.

**Answer:** C. enough mature to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — enough mature to (Correct):** When 'enough' modifies an adjective or adverb, it must follow that word rather than precede it; 'enough mature to' must be corrected to 'mature enough to'.
- **A — The newly hired:** The opening noun phrase 'The newly hired' properly uses an adverb to modify the participial adjective.
- **B — intern is:** The noun and linking verb 'intern is' correctly agree in singular number.
- **D — handle client escalations.:** The closing phrase 'handle client escalations.' accurately completes the infinitive 'to handle'.
- **Exam Extra:** Placement rule: 'adjective/adverb + enough' ('tall enough', 'fast enough'), but 'enough + noun' ('enough money', 'enough time').

### Q079 · Modifiers

Spot the error: Having completed the / annual financial audit, / the ledgers were / locked inside the vault.

A. Having completed the
B. annual financial audit,
C. the ledgers were
D. locked inside the vault.

**Answer:** C. the ledgers were
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — the ledgers were (Correct):** 'Having completed the annual financial audit' requires the person who completed it as the subject of the main clause, not 'the ledgers'; segment 3 should introduce the human subject (e.g., 'the accountant locked the ledgers').
- **A — Having completed the:** The opening perfect participle 'Having completed the' is grammatically well-formed.
- **B — annual financial audit,:** The noun phrase 'annual financial audit,' properly serves as the object of 'completed'.
- **D — locked inside the vault.:** The closing phrase 'locked inside the vault.' forms a valid passive participle phrase if preceded by an appropriate active subject or rephrased clause.
- **Exam Extra:** With 'Having + V3' at the start of a sentence, a passive main clause whose subject is an inanimate object almost always signals a dangling modifier.

### Q080 · Modifiers

The box was ___ heavy for a single porter to lift onto the truck.

A. so
B. very
C. much
D. too

**Answer:** D. too
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — too (Correct):** The correlative pattern 'too + adjective + (for someone) + to + V1' expresses excess preventing an action, making 'too' the only correct modifier here.
- **A — so:** The adverb 'so' requires a 'that'-clause ('so heavy that a porter could not lift it'), not an infinitive 'to lift'.
- **B — very:** The intensifier 'very' states high degree without linking to the negative result infinitive 'for a single porter to lift'.
- **C — much:** The adverb 'much' does not modify a positive-degree adjective like 'heavy' directly in this pattern.
- **Exam Extra:** Structure contrast: 'too + adj + to + V1' ('too weak to walk') vs 'so + adj + that + clause' ('so weak that he cannot walk').

### Q081 · Modifiers

Spot the error: The newly installed / air conditioner was / too much noisy / for the library.

A. The newly installed
B. air conditioner was
C. too much noisy
D. for the library.

**Answer:** C. too much noisy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — too much noisy (Correct):** 'Noisy' is an adjective, so it must be modified by 'much too' rather than 'too much' (which modifies nouns); 'too much noisy' must be 'much too noisy'.
- **A — The newly installed:** The opening modifier 'The newly installed' correctly pairs an adverb with a participle adjective.
- **B — air conditioner was:** The singular noun and verb 'air conditioner was' agree cleanly in number and tense.
- **D — for the library.:** The prepositional phrase 'for the library.' appropriately completes the sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Golden distinction: 'much too + adjective' ('much too painful', 'much too noisy') vs 'too much + uncountable noun' ('too much noise', 'too much pain').

### Q082 · Adjectives & Adverbs

The national sprinter runs noticeably ___ than his closest rivals.

A. faster
B. fast
C. fastest
D. more faster

**Answer:** A. faster
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — faster (Correct):** The conjunction 'than' signals a two-way comparison, requiring the comparative adverb 'faster'.
- **B — fast:** The positive degree 'fast' cannot pair with 'than' in a comparative statement.
- **C — fastest:** The superlative 'fastest' compares three or more with 'the', not a 'than' construction.
- **D — more faster:** The form 'more faster' is a double comparative and is always grammatically incorrect.
- **Exam Extra:** One-syllable adverbs like 'fast', 'hard', and 'high' form their comparative with '-er' ('faster', 'harder', 'higher'), never 'more + -er'.

### Q083 · Adjectives & Adverbs

She is senior ___ her immediate colleague by three years of service.

A. than
B. from
C. over
D. to

**Answer:** D. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — to (Correct):** Latin-origin comparative adjectives ending in '-ior' (senior, junior, superior, inferior, prior, anterior, posterior) take the preposition 'to', not 'than'.
- **A — than:** The comparative conjunction 'than' is invalid after Latin '-ior' adjectives like 'senior'.
- **B — from:** The preposition 'from' does not express seniority comparison in standard English.
- **C — over:** The preposition 'over' is incorrect after 'senior' when comparing rank or length of service.
- **Exam Extra:** Memorize the '-ior + to' set: senior, junior, superior, inferior, prior, anterior, posterior — plus 'elder' and 'preferable' — all take 'to'.

### Q084 · Adjectives & Adverbs

Spot the error: Today's weather is / more warmer than / it was during / the entire last week.

A. Today's weather is
B. more warmer than
C. it was during
D. the entire last week.

**Answer:** B. more warmer than
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — more warmer than (Correct):** 'Warmer' is already in the comparative degree, so adding 'more' creates an incorrect double comparative; 'more warmer than' must be 'warmer than'.
- **A — Today's weather is:** The opening subject and verb 'Today's weather is' are grammatically sound.
- **C — it was during:** The comparative reference 'it was during' properly introduces the past baseline.
- **D — the entire last week.:** The concluding time phrase 'the entire last week.' completes the sentence cleanly.
- **Exam Extra:** Never combine 'more'/'most' with '-er'/'-est' adjectives or adverbs ('more better', 'most fastest', 'more warmer').

### Q085 · Adjectives & Adverbs

Spot the error: The revised edition / of this textbook / is comparatively / easier to follow.

A. The revised edition
B. of this textbook
C. is comparatively
D. easier to follow.

**Answer:** D. easier to follow.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — easier to follow. (Correct):** Adverbs like 'comparatively' and 'relatively' already carry a comparative sense and must be followed by a positive-degree adjective, so 'easier to follow.' must be 'easy to follow.'.
- **A — The revised edition:** The opening noun phrase 'The revised edition' properly serves as a singular subject.
- **B — of this textbook:** The prepositional phrase 'of this textbook' accurately specifies the edition.
- **C — is comparatively:** The verb and adverb 'is comparatively' correctly match the singular subject and set up a positive adjective.
- **Exam Extra:** Rule: 'comparatively / relatively + positive degree' ('comparatively easy', 'relatively cheap'), never 'comparatively easier'.

### Q086 · Adjectives & Adverbs

Spot the error: Although she worked / hardly throughout the / semester, she missed / the top rank.

A. Although she worked
B. hardly throughout the
C. semester, she missed
D. the top rank.

**Answer:** B. hardly throughout the
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — hardly throughout the (Correct):** 'Hard' means diligently/with great effort, whereas 'hardly' means scarcely/almost not; here 'hardly throughout the' must be corrected to 'hard throughout the'.
- **A — Although she worked:** The concessive clause start 'Although she worked' is grammatically well-formed.
- **C — semester, she missed:** The noun and main clause start 'semester, she missed' accurately transitions to the contrasting result.
- **D — the top rank.:** The closing object 'the top rank.' completes the verb 'missed' cleanly.
- **Exam Extra:** Adverb pairs with different meanings: 'hard' (diligently) vs 'hardly' (scarcely); 'late' (tardy) vs 'lately' (recently).

### Q087 · Adjectives & Adverbs

Spot the error: Travelling by metro / is more preferable / to driving through / evening traffic jams.

A. Travelling by metro
B. is more preferable
C. to driving through
D. evening traffic jams.

**Answer:** B. is more preferable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — is more preferable (Correct):** 'Preferable' inherently carries comparative meaning and takes 'to' directly without 'more'; 'is more preferable' must be 'is preferable'.
- **A — Travelling by metro:** The gerund phrase 'Travelling by metro' acts properly as a singular subject.
- **C — to driving through:** The prepositional comparison 'to driving through' correctly uses 'to' after 'preferable'.
- **D — evening traffic jams.:** The closing noun phrase 'evening traffic jams.' cleanly completes 'driving through'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Prefer' and 'preferable' never take 'more' or 'than' — always write 'prefer A to B' and 'A is preferable to B'.

### Q088 · Adjectives & Adverbs

Before taking any legal action, the committee awaited ___ instructions from headquarters.

A. farther
B. further
C. farthest
D. more far

**Answer:** B. further
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — further (Correct):** 'Further' means additional or more ('further instructions'), whereas 'farther' refers strictly to physical distance.
- **A — farther:** The comparative 'farther' applies to literal spatial distance, not additional instructions.
