- **C — farthest:** The superlative 'farthest' also refers to physical distance and does not fit without 'the'.
- **D — more far:** The double form 'more far' is ungrammatical in standard English.
- **Exam Extra:** Distance vs Addition: 'farther' = physical distance ('walk farther'); 'further' = additional/subsequent ('further details', 'further delay').

### Q089 · Conjunctions & Prepositional Grammar

The regional manager insisted ___ reviewing the original dispatch records.

A. on
B. to
C. for
D. at

**Answer:** A. on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — on (Correct):** The verb 'insist' takes the fixed preposition 'on' followed by a noun or gerund ('insisted on reviewing'), making 'on' correct.
- **B — to:** Using 'to' after 'insisted' is incorrect; 'insist' requires 'on' before a gerund or a 'that'-clause.
- **C — for:** The preposition 'for' does not collocate with 'insisted' when introducing a gerund action.
- **D — at:** The preposition 'at' cannot link 'insisted' to the gerund phrase 'reviewing the original records'.
- **Exam Extra:** Key SSC 'verb + preposition + gerund' pairs: 'insist on', 'persist in', 'prevent from', 'abstain from', 'refrain from', and 'succeed in'.

### Q090 · Conjunctions & Prepositional Grammar

Spot the error: Although the terrain / was extremely steep, / but the trekkers / reached the summit.

A. Although the terrain
B. was extremely steep,
C. but the trekkers
D. reached the summit.

**Answer:** C. but the trekkers
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — but the trekkers (Correct):** 'Although' and 'Though' pair with 'yet' or a comma alone, never with 'but'; 'but the trekkers' must be corrected to 'yet the trekkers' (or simply 'the trekkers').
- **A — Although the terrain:** The opening concessive clause 'Although the terrain' is properly structured.
- **B — was extremely steep,:** The verb and intensifier 'was extremely steep,' agree with 'terrain' and end the subordinate clause properly.
- **D — reached the summit.:** The concluding predicate 'reached the summit.' is grammatically accurate in simple past tense.
- **Exam Extra:** Correlative rule: 'Although / Though ... yet' (or comma only) — never combine 'although/though' with 'but' in the same sentence.

---

## Chapter 2: Sentence Improvement & Completion

### Q091 · Sentence Completion: Correlative Conjunctions

Hardly had the train arrived ___ the passengers began to board.

A. than
B. then
C. when
D. while

**Answer:** C. when
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — when (Correct):** The correlative conjunction pair is 'hardly ... when', so 'when' correctly joins the inverted past perfect clause to the simple past clause.
- **A — than:** Using 'than' is incorrect because 'than' pairs with 'no sooner' or comparative adjectives, not with 'hardly'.
- **B — then:** Using 'then' is incorrect because 'then' is an adverb of time, not a correlative conjunction that links clauses after 'hardly'.
- **D — while:** Using 'while' is incorrect because 'while' indicates simultaneous ongoing actions rather than an immediate sequence after 'hardly'.
- **Exam Extra:** Both 'hardly' and 'scarcely' always pair with 'when' or 'before', never with 'than' or 'then'.

### Q092 · Sentence Completion: Correlative Conjunctions

No sooner had the bell rung ___ the class became silent.

A. when
B. than
C. then
D. that

**Answer:** B. than
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — than (Correct):** The correlative conjunction 'no sooner' is comparative in form and must be followed by 'than' to link the two sequential past actions.
- **A — when:** Using 'when' is incorrect because 'when' pairs with 'hardly' or 'scarcely', whereas 'no sooner' requires 'than'.
- **C — then:** Using 'then' is incorrect because 'then' is a time adverb and cannot replace the comparative conjunction 'than'.
- **D — that:** Using 'that' is incorrect because 'that' introduces noun or relative clauses and does not complete the 'no sooner' correlative structure.
- **Exam Extra:** When 'No sooner' begins a sentence, it triggers subject-auxiliary inversion: 'No sooner + had/did + subject + verb ... than'.

### Q093 · Sentence Completion: Conditionals

If she ___ earlier, she would have caught the bus.

A. left
B. would leave
C. had left
D. has left

**Answer:** C. had left
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — had left (Correct):** This is a third conditional sentence expressing an unreal past situation; the if-clause takes the past perfect ('had left') to match 'would have caught' in the main clause.
- **A — left:** Using 'left' (simple past) forms a second conditional, which pairs with 'would + V1' rather than 'would have + V3'.
- **B — would leave:** Using 'would leave' is incorrect because modal verbs like 'would' are generally not used in standard conditional 'if' clauses.
- **D — has left:** Using 'has left' (present perfect) clashes with the past unreal result 'would have caught'.
- **Exam Extra:** Third conditional formula: If + Subject + had + V3, Subject + would/could/might + have + V3.

### Q094 · Sentence Completion: Tense Sequence

By this time next month, the team ___ the project.

A. completes
B. will complete
C. has completed
D. will have completed

**Answer:** D. will have completed
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — will have completed (Correct):** The time marker 'By this time next month' points to an action that will be finished before a specific future deadline, requiring the future perfect tense ('will have completed').
- **A — completes:** Using 'completes' (simple present) denotes habitual actions or fixed timetables, not completion before a future reference point.
- **B — will complete:** Using 'will complete' (simple future) states a future action without emphasizing its prior completion by the given deadline.
- **C — has completed:** Using 'has completed' (present perfect) indicates an action already finished up to the present, contradicting 'next month'.
- **Exam Extra:** Time expressions starting with 'By + future time' (e.g., by tomorrow, by next year) typically take the future perfect: will/shall + have + V3.

### Q095 · Sentence Completion: Subject-Verb Agreement

The report, together with the annexures, ___ on the table.

A. is
B. are
C. were
D. have been

**Answer:** A. is
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — is (Correct):** When two nouns are joined by 'together with', the verb agrees with the first subject ('The report'), which is singular, so 'is' is correct.
- **B — are:** Using 'are' is incorrect because the verb must agree with the singular main subject 'The report', not the parenthetical noun 'annexures'.
- **C — were:** Using 'were' is incorrect because it is a plural verb and does not match the singular subject 'The report'.
- **D — have been:** Using 'have been' is incorrect because 'have' is plural and cannot agree with 'The report'.
- **Exam Extra:** Parenthetical connectors like 'together with', 'along with', 'as well as', and 'in addition to' do not make a singular subject plural.

### Q096 · Sentence Completion: Fixed Prepositions

She is senior ___ her colleague by three years.

A. than
B. to
C. from
D. with

**Answer:** B. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — to (Correct):** Comparative adjectives ending in '-ior' derived from Latin, such as 'senior', take the preposition 'to' rather than 'than'.
- **A — than:** Using 'than' is incorrect because Latin-origin '-ior' comparatives do not take 'than' in standard English grammar.
- **C — from:** Using 'from' is incorrect because 'senior' expresses relative rank or age toward someone using 'to', not separation with 'from'.
- **D — with:** Using 'with' is incorrect because 'with' indicates accompaniment rather than comparative seniority.
- **Exam Extra:** Adjectives like senior, junior, superior, inferior, prior, anterior, and posterior always take 'to', never 'than' or 'more'.

### Q097 · Sentence Completion: Fixed Prepositions

The manager insisted ___ reviewing the original records.

A. to
B. at
C. on
D. for

**Answer:** C. on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — on (Correct):** The verb 'insist' takes the fixed preposition 'on' (or 'upon') when followed by a noun or gerund ('reviewing').
- **A — to:** Using 'to' is incorrect because 'insist' does not take 'to' or an infinitive ('insist to review') in standard English.
- **B — at:** Using 'at' is incorrect because 'at' denotes target or location and never pairs with 'insist'.
- **D — for:** Using 'for' is incorrect because 'insist' governs its object with 'on', not 'for'.
- **Exam Extra:** Verb + fixed preposition + V-ing is a frequent SSC pattern: 'insist on', 'persist in', 'refrain from', and 'abstain from'.

### Q098 · Sentence Completion: Subject-Verb Agreement

The information ___ verified before the notice was issued.

A. were
B. was
C. are
D. have been

**Answer:** B. was
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — was (Correct):** 'Information' is an uncountable noun in English, so it takes a singular verb ('was') matching the past context of 'was issued'.
- **A — were:** Using 'were' is incorrect because 'were' is plural, whereas 'information' is an uncountable singular noun.
- **C — are:** Using 'are' is incorrect because it is both plural and present tense, clashing with 'information' and 'was issued'.
- **D — have been:** Using 'have been' is incorrect because 'have' is plural and present perfect, which fails agreement and tense consistency.
- **Exam Extra:** Uncountable nouns like information, advice, furniture, luggage, scenery, and equipment never take plural '-s' or plural verbs.

### Q099 · Sentence Completion: Prepositions of Time

The patient has been waiting ___ two hours.

A. since
B. for
C. from
D. during

**Answer:** B. for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — for (Correct):** 'For' is used with a duration or period of time ('two hours'), paired here with the present perfect continuous tense ('has been waiting').
- **A — since:** Using 'since' is incorrect because 'since' requires a fixed starting point in time (e.g., 'since 2 p.m.'), not a length of time.
- **C — from:** Using 'from' is incorrect because 'from' generally requires an endpoint ('from 2 to 4') and is not used with the present perfect continuous for duration.
- **D — during:** Using 'during' is incorrect because 'during' precedes a named event or period ('during the storm'), not a numerical count of hours with 'has been waiting'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'for' for a duration of time (two hours, five days) and 'since' for a specific starting point in time (9 a.m., Monday, 2020).

### Q100 · Sentence Completion: Tense Sequence

The officer asked me where I ___ the document.

A. had kept
B. have kept
C. keep
D. had been kept

**Answer:** A. had kept
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — had kept (Correct):** In indirect speech introduced by a past reporting verb ('asked'), the embedded question uses statement word order and shifts a past action further back to the past perfect ('had kept').
- **B — have kept:** Using 'have kept' (present perfect) violates the sequence of tenses after the past reporting verb 'asked'.
- **C — keep:** Using 'keep' (simple present) fails to backshift the tense under the past reporting verb 'asked'.
- **D — had been kept:** Using 'had been kept' turns the verb passive, implying the speaker ('I') was stored somewhere rather than the document.
- **Exam Extra:** Embedded questions in reported speech follow 'wh-word + subject + verb' order without 'do/did' inversion.

### Q101 · Sentence Completion: Prepositions of Time

The report must be submitted ___ Friday afternoon.

A. by
B. until
C. since
D. during

**Answer:** A. by
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — by (Correct):** 'By' indicates a deadline—meaning on or before Friday afternoon—which fits 'must be submitted'.
- **B — until:** Using 'until' is incorrect because 'until' describes an ongoing action continuing up to a time, whereas 'submitted' is a one-time deadline event.
- **C — since:** Using 'since' is incorrect because 'since' marks a starting point from the past up to now, not a future submission deadline.
- **D — during:** Using 'during' is incorrect because 'during' means throughout a time period rather than 'no later than' a specific deadline.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'by' for single-point completion deadlines ('finish by 5 p.m.') and 'until/till' for continuous states lasting up to a point ('stay until 5 p.m.').

### Q102 · Sentence Completion: Modifiers

The officer acted ___ and prevented further confusion.

A. prompt
B. promptly
C. promptness
D. prompted

**Answer:** B. promptly
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — promptly (Correct):** An adverb ('promptly') is required to modify the action verb 'acted', describing how the officer responded.
- **A — prompt:** Using 'prompt' is incorrect because 'prompt' is an adjective (or verb) here and cannot grammatically modify the verb 'acted'.
- **C — promptness:** Using 'promptness' is incorrect because 'promptness' is an abstract noun and cannot serve as a manner modifier for 'acted'.
- **D — prompted:** Using 'prompted' is incorrect because it is a past-tense/participle verb form, creating an ungrammatical double-verb sequence after 'acted'.
- **Exam Extra:** Action verbs like 'act', 'respond', and 'decide' take adverbs, whereas linking verbs like 'seem', 'appear', and 'look' take adjectives.

### Q103 · Sentence Completion: Conjunctions vs Prepositions

The train was delayed ___ dense fog.

A. because
B. because of
C. since
D. although

**Answer:** B. because of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — because of (Correct):** 'Dense fog' is a noun phrase, so it requires the prepositional phrase 'because of' rather than a subordinating conjunction.
- **A — because:** Using 'because' is incorrect because 'because' is a subordinating conjunction that must be followed by a full clause with a subject and verb.
- **C — since:** Using 'since' is incorrect because as a causal conjunction it requires a finite clause, and as a preposition it indicates time, not cause.
- **D — although:** Using 'although' is incorrect because it is a conjunction of contrast requiring a full clause, whereas the sentence expresses cause with a noun phrase.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Because of', 'owing to', and 'due to' introduce noun phrases; 'because', 'since', and 'as' introduce full clauses (subject + verb).

### Q104 · Sentence Completion: Word Forms

The principal asked the students to maintain ___ in the library.

A. silent
B. silently
C. silence
D. silenced

**Answer:** C. silence
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — silence (Correct):** The transitive verb 'maintain' requires a direct object, making the noun 'silence' the only grammatically valid choice.
- **A — silent:** Using 'silent' is incorrect because it is an adjective and cannot function as the direct object of 'maintain'.
- **B — silently:** Using 'silently' is incorrect because it is an adverb and does not supply the required noun object after 'maintain'.
- **D — silenced:** Using 'silenced' is incorrect because it is a past participle/verb form that cannot act as an uncountable abstract object here.
- **Exam Extra:** Verbs like 'maintain', 'ensure', and 'observe' require noun objects ('maintain silence', 'ensure discipline').

### Q105 · Sentence Completion: Contextual Vocabulary

The witness gave a ___ account of the incident.

A. reliably
B. reliance
C. reliable
D. rely

**Answer:** C. reliable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — reliable (Correct):** The noun 'account' needs an attributive adjective before it, so 'reliable' ('a reliable account') is correct.
- **A — reliably:** Using 'reliably' is incorrect because it is an adverb and cannot directly modify the noun 'account'.
- **B — reliance:** Using 'reliance' is incorrect because it is an abstract noun meaning dependence, which fails to modify 'account' properly here.
- **D — rely:** Using 'rely' is incorrect because it is a base verb and cannot stand between the article 'a' and the noun 'account'.
- **Exam Extra:** In the 'article + ___ + noun' slot, check whether an adjective is required to modify the head noun.

### Q106 · Sentence Completion: Contextual Vocabulary

The evidence was not ___ to support the claim.

A. sufficiently
B. suffice
C. sufficiency
D. sufficient

**Answer:** D. sufficient
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — sufficient (Correct):** After the linking verb 'was' (negated by 'not') and before the infinitive 'to support', the predicative adjective 'sufficient' is required.
- **A — sufficiently:** Using 'sufficiently' is incorrect because it is an adverb and does not complement the linking verb 'was' without a following adjective.
- **B — suffice:** Using 'suffice' is incorrect because it is a verb and cannot follow 'was not' to describe the noun 'evidence'.
- **C — sufficiency:** Using 'sufficiency' is incorrect because 'evidence' is not equated with the abstract noun 'sufficiency'; it needs the descriptive adjective.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Sufficient' is commonly followed by 'to + V1' or 'for + noun' to indicate adequacy for a purpose.

### Q107 · Sentence Completion: Confusable Words

The new rule will ___ all applicants equally.

A. effect
B. affect
C. effective
D. affection

**Answer:** B. affect
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — affect (Correct):** After the modal auxiliary 'will', a base verb is needed; 'affect' is the verb meaning 'to influence or have an impact on'.
- **A — effect:** Using 'effect' is incorrect here because 'effect' as a verb means 'to bring about or cause to happen' (e.g., 'effect a change'), not to influence applicants.
- **C — effective:** Using 'effective' is incorrect because it is an adjective and cannot serve as the main verb after 'will'.
- **D — affection:** Using 'affection' is incorrect because it is a noun meaning fondness and is ungrammatical after the modal 'will'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Affect' is primarily a verb (to influence), while 'effect' is primarily a noun (result), though 'effect' as a verb means 'to bring about'.

### Q108 · Sentence Completion: Word Forms

The committee reached a decision after a ___ discussion.

A. lengthy
B. length
C. lengthen
D. lengthily

**Answer:** A. lengthy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — lengthy (Correct):** Between the article 'a' and the singular noun 'discussion', an adjective is required; 'lengthy' properly describes a long discussion.
- **B — length:** Using 'length' is incorrect because 'length' is a noun and does not idiomaticallly modify 'discussion' in this slot.
- **C — lengthen:** Using 'lengthen' is incorrect because it is a verb meaning 'to make longer', which cannot modify the noun 'discussion'.
- **D — lengthily:** Using 'lengthily' is incorrect because it is an adverb and cannot modify a noun after an article.
- **Exam Extra:** Adjectives formed by adding '-y' to nouns (length -> lengthy, health -> healthy) modify nouns directly.

### Q109 · Sentence Completion: Gerunds & Prepositions

She is accustomed ___ early for work.

A. to rise
B. rising
C. to rising
D. for rising

**Answer:** C. to rising
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — to rising (Correct):** In 'be accustomed to', 'to' functions as a preposition rather than an infinitive marker, so it must be followed by a gerund ('rising').
- **A — to rise:** Using 'to rise' is incorrect because it treats 'to' as an infinitive marker, whereas 'accustomed to' requires a gerund or noun.
- **B — rising:** Using 'rising' is incorrect because it omits the required preposition 'to' after 'accustomed'.
- **D — for rising:** Using 'for rising' is incorrect because 'accustomed' pairs strictly with the preposition 'to', never 'for'.
- **Exam Extra:** Expressions ending in prepositional 'to'—'accustomed to', 'used to', 'look forward to', 'with a view to', 'object to'—take V-ing (gerund).

### Q110 · Sentence Completion: Question Tags

Let us go for a walk in the park, ___?

A. shall we
B. will we
C. shan't we
D. don't we

**Answer:** A. shall we
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — shall we (Correct):** Imperative sentences beginning with 'Let us' ('Let's') express a proposal or suggestion and always take the question tag 'shall we'.
- **B — will we:** Using 'will we' is incorrect because 'will' is not used in tags following first-person plural proposals with 'Let us'.
- **C — shan't we:** Using 'shan't we' is incorrect because 'Let us' takes a positive tag ('shall we'), not a negative tag.
- **D — don't we:** Using 'don't we' is incorrect because 'Let us' has no present-simple auxiliary 'do' to echo in the tag.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'Let us' (suggestion -> 'shall we?') with 'Let me/him/them' (permission -> 'will you?').

### Q111 · Sentence Completion: Question Tags

She seldom misses her morning classes, ___?

A. doesn't she
B. does she
C. isn't she
D. did she

**Answer:** B. does she
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — does she (Correct):** Adverbs such as 'seldom', 'hardly', 'scarcely', and 'rarely' give the statement a negative sense, so the question tag must be positive ('does she').
- **A — doesn't she:** Using 'doesn't she' is incorrect because 'seldom' already makes the statement negative, and a negative statement cannot take a negative tag.
- **C — isn't she:** Using 'isn't she' is incorrect both in polarity (negative instead of positive) and in auxiliary verb ('be' instead of 'do').
- **D — did she:** Using 'did she' is incorrect because the main verb 'misses' is in the simple present tense, not the simple past.
- **Exam Extra:** A negative-meaning adverb in the main clause requires an affirmative question tag matched to the main verb's tense and subject.

### Q112 · Sentence Completion: Question Tags

Nobody called during my absence, ___?

A. didn't they
B. did he
C. did they
D. didn't he

**Answer:** C. did they
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — did they (Correct):** Indefinite pronouns like 'nobody' and 'no one' take the plural pronoun 'they' in question tags, and because 'nobody' is negative, the tag must be positive ('did they').
- **A — didn't they:** Using 'didn't they' is incorrect because 'nobody' makes the main clause negative, so the tag must be affirmative.
- **B — did he:** Using 'did he' is incorrect because indefinite pronouns referring to people take 'they', not 'he', in standard question tags.
- **D — didn't he:** Using 'didn't he' is incorrect both because the tag must be positive after 'nobody' and because the tag pronoun must be 'they'.
- **Exam Extra:** Everybody, everyone, nobody, and no one take a singular verb in the statement but always use 'they' in the question tag.

### Q113 · Sentence Completion: Relative Clauses

The candidate ___ the panel interviewed yesterday has been selected.

A. who
B. whose
C. which
D. whom

**Answer:** D. whom
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — whom (Correct):** In the relative clause 'the panel interviewed ___', 'the candidate' is the object of the verb 'interviewed', so the objective relative pronoun 'whom' is required.
- **A — who:** Using 'who' is incorrect in formal exam grammar because 'who' is a subject pronoun, whereas 'the panel' is already the subject of 'interviewed'.
- **B — whose:** Using 'whose' is incorrect because 'whose' shows possession and must be followed directly by a noun, not a separate clause like 'the panel interviewed'.
- **C — which:** Using 'which' is incorrect because 'which' refers to things or animals, not to a person ('candidate').
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'who' when the relative pronoun acts as the subject of the verb, and 'whom' when it acts as the object of a verb or preposition.

### Q114 · Sentence Completion: Relative Clauses

The journalist ___ article won the award is visiting today.

A. who
B. whom
C. whose
D. which

**Answer:** C. whose
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — whose (Correct):** 'Whose' is the possessive relative pronoun linking 'the journalist' to 'article' (meaning 'the journalist's article').
- **A — who:** Using 'who' is incorrect because 'who' is a subjective pronoun and cannot show possession before the noun 'article'.
- **B — whom:** Using 'whom' is incorrect because 'whom' is an objective pronoun and cannot modify the noun 'article'.
- **D — which:** Using 'which' is incorrect because 'which' does not express possession and cannot refer to a person ('journalist').
- **Exam Extra:** 'Whose' can show possession for both people and inanimate objects ('a house whose roof was damaged').

### Q115 · Sentence Completion: Conjunctions

Walk carefully lest you ___ on the wet floor.

A. would slip
B. should slip
C. will not slip
D. might not slip

**Answer:** B. should slip
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — should slip (Correct):** The conjunction 'lest' (meaning 'for fear that') is always followed by 'should + V1' (or a bare subjunctive) and never takes 'not'.
- **A — would slip:** Using 'would slip' is incorrect because standard grammar pairs 'lest' with 'should', not 'would'.
- **C — will not slip:** Using 'will not slip' is incorrect both because 'lest' does not take 'will' and because adding 'not' creates a redundant double negative.
- **D — might not slip:** Using 'might not slip' is incorrect because 'not' is forbidden after 'lest' and 'should' is the standard modal after 'lest'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Lest' carries an inherent negative meaning ('so that ... not'), so using 'not' after 'lest' creates a double-negative error.

### Q116 · Sentence Completion: Subjunctive & Unreal Past

It is high time the authorities ___ strict action against illegal parking.

A. take
B. will take
C. took
D. have taken

**Answer:** C. took
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — took (Correct):** Expressions like 'It is time', 'It is high time', and 'It is about time' followed by a subject take the simple past tense ('took') to express an urgent present obligation.
- **A — take:** Using 'take' (simple present) is incorrect after 'It is high time + subject', which requires the unreal past subjunctive form.
- **B — will take:** Using 'will take' (simple future) is incorrect because 'It is high time' takes the simple past form to convey immediacy, not 'will'.
- **D — have taken:** Using 'have taken' (present perfect) is ungrammatical after 'It is high time', which mandates V2.
- **Exam Extra:** Formula: 'It is (high/about) time + Subject + V2 (Simple Past)', whereas 'It is time + to + V1' needs no separate subject.

### Q117 · Sentence Completion: Subjunctive & Unreal Past

He orders everyone around as if he ___ the owner of the company.

A. is
B. was
C. has been
D. were

**Answer:** D. were
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — were (Correct):** After 'as if' or 'as though' expressing a contrary-to-fact situation, the past subjunctive 'were' is used regardless of the subject's number.
- **A — is:** Using 'is' is incorrect because it states a real fact rather than an imaginary, contrary-to-fact comparison after 'as if'.
- **B — was:** Using 'was' is incorrect in formal SSC grammar because unreal comparisons with 'as if' require the subjunctive 'were'.
- **C — has been:** Using 'has been' is incorrect because the present perfect indicates factual past-to-present reality rather than a hypothetical scenario.
- **Exam Extra:** In hypothetical clauses with 'as if', 'as though', and 'I wish', always choose 'were' over 'was' for all subjects (he/she/it/I).

### Q118 · Sentence Completion: Subject-Verb Agreement

The number of applicants for the post ___ risen sharply this year.

A. has
B. have
C. are
D. were

**Answer:** A. has
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — has (Correct):** The phrase 'The number of + plural noun' refers to a single numerical figure and always takes a singular verb ('has').
- **B — have:** Using 'have' is incorrect because 'The number of' denotes one total count and requires a singular auxiliary verb.
- **C — are:** Using 'are' is incorrect both because it is plural and because 'be + risen' forms an invalid passive construction here.
- **D — were:** Using 'were' is incorrect because it is plural and cannot pair with 'risen' to express active rise in numbers.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'The number of + plural noun + singular verb' with 'A number of + plural noun + plural verb'.

### Q119 · Sentence Completion: Subject-Verb Agreement

Neither the captain nor the players ___ satisfied with the umpire's decision.

A. was
B. were
C. is
D. has been

**Answer:** B. were
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — were (Correct):** With 'neither ... nor', the verb agrees with the nearer subject; since 'the players' is plural and the sentence context fits past evaluation, 'were' is correct.
- **A — was:** Using 'was' is incorrect because the nearer subject 'the players' is plural, not singular.
- **C — is:** Using 'is' is incorrect because it is singular and cannot agree with the plural nearer subject 'the players'.
- **D — has been:** Using 'has been' is incorrect because 'has' is singular and fails to agree with 'the players'.
- **Exam Extra:** With 'neither...nor', 'either...or', and 'not only...but also', apply the proximity rule: match the verb to the closest subject.

### Q120 · Sentence Completion: Subject-Verb Agreement

She is one of the few authors who ___ consistently about rural life.

A. writes
B. is writing
C. write
D. has written

**Answer:** C. write
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — write (Correct):** In 'One of the + plural noun + who/that', the relative pronoun 'who' refers to its immediate antecedent ('the few authors'), which is plural, so the plural verb 'write' is required.
- **A — writes:** Using 'writes' is incorrect because the verb in the relative clause agrees with the plural antecedent 'authors', not with 'one'.
- **B — is writing:** Using 'is writing' is incorrect because 'is' is singular and continuous tense clashes with the habitual adverb 'consistently'.
- **D — has written:** Using 'has written' is incorrect because 'has' is singular and cannot agree with the plural antecedent 'authors'.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'One of the authors who write...' (plural verb) with 'The only one of the authors who writes...' (singular verb).

### Q121 · Sentence Completion: Conditionals

___ in your position, I would accept the offer immediately.

A. Was I
B. If I am
C. Had I been
D. Were I

**Answer:** D. Were I
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Were I (Correct):** 'Were I' is the inverted form of 'If I were', correctly introducing a second conditional hypothesis that pairs with 'would accept'.
- **A — Was I:** Using 'Was I' is incorrect because an inverted conditional requires the subjunctive 'Were I', and 'Was I...' forms a past question instead.
- **B — If I am:** Using 'If I am' is incorrect because a first-conditional real present clause does not pair with the hypothetical 'would accept'.
- **C — Had I been:** Using 'Had I been' forms a third conditional referring to the past, which would require 'would have accepted' instead of 'would accept'.
- **Exam Extra:** Conditional inversions replace 'if': 'Were I...' (Type 2) and 'Had I...' (Type 3) place the auxiliary before the subject.

### Q122 · Sentence Completion: Parallel Structure

The new software not only saves time ___ reduces manual errors.

A. but also
B. and also
C. as well as
D. rather than

**Answer:** A. but also
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — but also (Correct):** The correlative conjunction 'not only' must be paired with 'but also', maintaining parallel verb phrases ('saves time' and 'reduces manual errors').
- **B — and also:** Using 'and also' is incorrect because 'not only' specifically pairs with 'but also', not 'and also'.
- **C — as well as:** Using 'as well as' is incorrect because 'as well as' does not complete the correlative pair started by 'not only'.
- **D — rather than:** Using 'rather than' is incorrect because it expresses preference/exclusion, contradicting the additive meaning of 'not only'.
- **Exam Extra:** In 'not only X but also Y', X and Y must belong to the same grammatical category (two verbs, two nouns, or two clauses).

### Q123 · Sentence Completion: Parallel Structure

Her hobbies include sketching portraits, reading biographies, and ___ classical music.

A. to compose
B. composing
C. composed
D. compose

**Answer:** B. composing
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — composing (Correct):** Items in a series must be grammatically parallel; since 'sketching portraits' and 'reading biographies' are gerund phrases, the third item must also be a gerund phrase ('composing').
- **A — to compose:** Using 'to compose' breaks parallel structure by mixing a to-infinitive with two preceding gerund phrases.
- **C — composed:** Using 'composed' breaks parallelism because a past participle/past verb does not match the gerund objects of 'include'.
- **D — compose:** Using 'compose' is incorrect because a bare verb cannot serve as a parallel noun object alongside 'sketching' and 'reading'.
- **Exam Extra:** Check coordinate lists joined by 'and' or 'or' to ensure every element shares the same form (all V-ing, all nouns, or all to-infinitives).

### Q124 · Sentence Completion: Tense Sequence

While I ___ across the bridge, I noticed a flock of migratory birds.

A. walk
B. have walked
C. was walking
D. am walking

**Answer:** C. was walking
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — was walking (Correct):** An ongoing past action interrupted by a single past event ('I noticed') requires the past continuous tense ('was walking') after 'While'.
- **A — walk:** Using 'walk' (simple present) clashes with the past-tense main verb 'noticed'.
- **B — have walked:** Using 'have walked' (present perfect) cannot combine with a past-tense event ('noticed') in a 'while' clause.
- **D — am walking:** Using 'am walking' (present continuous) creates a tense mismatch with the past verb 'noticed'.
- **Exam Extra:** Pattern: 'While + Subject + was/were + V-ing, Subject + V2' expresses a background action interrupted by a shorter event.

### Q125 · Sentence Completion: Tense Sequence

Three years have passed since he ___ his hometown.

A. has left
B. leaves
C. had left
D. left

**Answer:** D. left
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — left (Correct):** When 'since' links a present perfect clause ('have passed') to a past point-in-time clause, the clause after 'since' must be in the simple past tense ('left').
- **A — has left:** Using 'has left' (present perfect) after 'since' is incorrect because 'since' marks a specific past event that started the duration.
- **B — leaves:** Using 'leaves' (simple present) clashes with the past starting point required after 'have passed since'.
- **C — had left:** Using 'had left' (past perfect) is incorrect here because the main clause is in the present perfect, not the past tense.
- **Exam Extra:** Formula: 'Present Perfect + since + Simple Past (V2)'—never use present perfect or past perfect right after 'since' in this construction.

### Q126 · Sentence Completion: Modifiers & Quantifiers

There is ___ hope of recovering the lost files without a backup.

A. little
B. a few
C. few
D. many

**Answer:** A. little
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — little (Correct):** 'Hope' is an uncountable noun, and the context ('without a backup') implies almost no hope, so the negative-sense uncountable quantifier 'little' fits best.
- **B — a few:** Using 'a few' is incorrect because 'few' only modifies plural countable nouns, whereas 'hope' is uncountable here.
- **C — few:** Using 'few' is incorrect because 'hope' is an uncountable noun and cannot be used with 'few'.
- **D — many:** Using 'many' is incorrect because 'many' requires a plural countable noun and also contradicts the pessimistic 'without a backup' context.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Little' / 'few' mean 'hardly any' (negative); 'a little' / 'a few' mean 'some' (positive); 'little' is for uncountable nouns and 'few' for countable plurals.

### Q127 · Sentence Completion: Fixed Prepositions

All employees must comply ___ the revised safety guidelines.

A. to
B. with
C. for
D. by

**Answer:** B. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — with (Correct):** The verb 'comply' always takes the fixed preposition 'with' to mean obeying rules, orders, or guidelines.
- **A — to:** Using 'to' is incorrect because 'adhere' and 'stick' take 'to', whereas 'comply' takes 'with'.
- **C — for:** Using 'for' is incorrect because 'comply' never governs its object with 'for'.
- **D — by:** Using 'by' is incorrect because 'abide' pairs with 'by', whereas 'comply' requires 'with'.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the preposition contrast: 'comply with' rules, 'abide by' rules, and 'adhere to' rules.

### Q128 · Sentence Completion: Phrasal Verbs

Due to heavy rain, the organizers had to ___ the outdoor concert.

A. call in
B. call on
C. call off
D. call out

**Answer:** C. call off
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — call off (Correct):** The phrasal verb 'call off' means to cancel an event, which matches 'Due to heavy rain'.
- **A — call in:** Using 'call in' is incorrect because it means to summon someone for help or ask someone to enter, not to cancel an event.
- **B — call on:** Using 'call on' is incorrect because it means to pay a brief visit to someone or formally appeal to someone.
- **D — call out:** Using 'call out' is incorrect because it means to shout aloud or summon into action, not to cancel a concert.
- **Exam Extra:** Key 'call' phrasal verbs: 'call off' (cancel), 'call on' (visit/appeal to), 'call in' (summon), and 'call for' (demand/require).

### Q129 · Sentence Completion: Modifiers & Degree

The box was ___ heavy for the child to lift alone.

A. so
B. very
C. enough
D. too

**Answer:** D. too
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — too (Correct):** The structure 'too + adjective + (for someone) + to + V1' expresses excess resulting in inability ('so heavy that the child could not lift it').
- **A — so:** Using 'so' is incorrect because 'so' pairs with a 'that'-clause ('so heavy that...'), not directly with 'for + noun + to + V1'.
- **B — very:** Using 'very' is incorrect because 'very' merely intensifies an adjective and does not grammatically pair with the result infinitive 'to lift'.
- **C — enough:** Using 'enough' is incorrect both in meaning (excess vs sufficiency) and in position ('enough' follows an adjective, e.g., 'heavy enough').
- **Exam Extra:** 'Too ... to' carries an inherent negative result; use 'so ... that' only when a full 'that'-clause follows instead of 'to + V1'.

### Q130 · Sentence Improvement: Modals & Infinitives

Improve the bracketed part: You [had better to consult] a doctor before taking this medicine.

A. had better consult
B. had better consulting
C. should better consult
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. had better consult
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — had better consult (Correct):** The modal idiom 'had better' is always followed directly by a bare infinitive (V1 without 'to'), so 'had better consult' is correct.
- **B — had better consulting:** Using 'had better consulting' is incorrect because 'had better' is a modal-like expression that takes a base verb, never a '-ing' form.
- **C — should better consult:** Using 'should better consult' is incorrect because 'should' and 'had better' cannot be blended into a single phrase.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because inserting 'to' after 'had better' violates the bare-infinitive rule.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Had better', 'would rather', and 'would sooner' always take a bare infinitive (V1) without 'to'.

### Q131 · Sentence Improvement: Causative Verbs

Improve the bracketed part: The security guard [made us to wait] outside the gate for twenty minutes.

A. made us waiting
B. make us to wait
C. made us wait
D. No improvement

**Answer:** C. made us wait
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — made us wait (Correct):** In the active voice, causative verbs such as 'make', 'let', and 'have' take an object followed by a bare infinitive ('made us wait').
- **A — made us waiting:** Using 'made us waiting' is incorrect because 'make' as a causative verb takes a bare infinitive, not a present participle.
- **B — make us to wait:** Using 'make us to wait' is incorrect because it shifts to present tense and still retains the erroneous 'to' before 'wait'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because active 'made' cannot be followed by a full 'to'-infinitive ('to wait').
- **Exam Extra:** Active 'make + object + V1' becomes passive 'be made + to + V1' ('We were made to wait').

### Q132 · Sentence Improvement: Dangling Modifiers

Improve the bracketed part: [Walking through the forest, a deer] suddenly crossed my path.

A. Walking through the forest, I saw a deer
B. Walking through the forest, there was a deer that
C. Having walked through the forest, a deer
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. Walking through the forest, I saw a deer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Walking through the forest, I saw a deer (Correct):** A participial phrase at the start of a sentence must share the same subject as the main clause; changing the main clause subject to 'I' ensures 'I' was walking through the forest.
- **B — Walking through the forest, there was a deer that:** Using 'there was a deer that' creates a dangling modifier because 'there' cannot perform the action of walking through the forest.
- **C — Having walked through the forest, a deer:** Using 'Having walked through the forest, a deer' still leaves 'a deer' as the subject of the main clause, repeating the dangling modifier error.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because the original wording illogically implies that the deer was walking through the forest and crossed 'my' path as the speaker.
- **Exam Extra:** Always verify that the implied subject of an opening '-ing' or '-ed' phrase matches the noun immediately following the comma.

### Q133 · Sentence Improvement: Parallel Comparison

Improve the bracketed part: The climate of Shimla is cooler [than Delhi].

A. than that of Delhi
B. than those of Delhi
C. to Delhi
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. than that of Delhi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — than that of Delhi (Correct):** Comparisons must compare like with like; 'that of' stands in for the singular noun 'the climate', properly comparing Shimla's climate with Delhi's climate.
- **B — than those of Delhi:** Using 'than those of Delhi' is incorrect because 'those' is plural, whereas its antecedent 'the climate' is singular.
- **C — to Delhi:** Using 'to Delhi' is incorrect because comparative adjectives like 'cooler' take 'than', and it still compares climate to a city.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because the original sentence illogically compares 'the climate of Shimla' directly to the city 'Delhi'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'that of' to replace a singular noun in comparisons and 'those of' to replace a plural noun ('The streets of Jaipur are wider than those of Agra').

### Q134 · Sentence Improvement: Adjectives & Comparatives

Improve the bracketed part: Experience has made him [more wiser] than before.

A. much wiser
B. most wise
C. more wise
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. much wiser
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — much wiser (Correct):** 'Wiser' is already a comparative adjective; adding 'more' creates a double comparative, whereas 'much' properly intensifies a comparative ('much wiser').
- **B — most wise:** Using 'most wise' is incorrect because the sentence compares two states ('than before'), requiring a comparative rather than a superlative.
- **C — more wise:** Using 'more wise' is incorrect because monosyllabic 'wise' forms its comparative by adding '-r' ('wiser'), not with 'more'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'more wiser' is a double-comparative error.
- **Exam Extra:** Never pair 'more' or 'most' with '-er'/'-est' forms; intensify comparatives with 'much', 'far', or 'even'.

### Q135 · Sentence Improvement: Pronoun Order

Improve the bracketed part: [I, you, and he] will jointly present the annual budget.

A. You, he, and I
B. He, you, and I
C. You, I, and he
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. You, he, and I
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — You, he, and I (Correct):** For positive or neutral actions, English pronoun etiquette follows the 2-3-1 order: second person ('You'), third person ('he'), and first person ('I').
- **B — He, you, and I:** Using 'He, you, and I' uses 3-2-1 order, violating the standard 2-3-1 sequence for positive/neutral actions.
- **C — You, I, and he:** Using 'You, I, and he' places the first-person pronoun 'I' before the third-person 'he', breaking both 2-3-1 and 1-2-3 rules.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 1-2-3 order ('I, you, and he') is reserved for negative admissions of fault, not presenting a budget.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 2-3-1 order (You, he, and I) for positive/neutral statements, and 1-2-3 order (I, you, and he) when admitting a mistake or guilt.

### Q136 · Sentence Improvement: Superfluous Prepositions

Improve the bracketed part: The committee [discussed about the] proposed reforms for hours.

A. discussed the
B. discussed on the
C. discussed over the
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. discussed the
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — discussed the (Correct):** 'Discuss' is a transitive verb meaning 'to talk about', so it takes its direct object directly without any preposition ('discussed the').
- **B — discussed on the:** Using 'discussed on the' is incorrect because 'on' is redundant after the transitive verb 'discuss'.
- **C — discussed over the:** Using 'discussed over the' is incorrect because 'discuss' directly governs its object without 'over'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'about' after 'discuss' is superfluous.
- **Exam Extra:** Active transitive verbs like 'discuss', 'comprise', 'describe', 'order', and 'emphasize' take no preposition before their direct object.

### Q137 · Sentence Improvement: Redundancy

Improve the bracketed part: Please [return back the library books] before Friday.

A. return the library books
B. return again the library books
C. give return the library books
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. return the library books
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — return the library books (Correct):** 'Return' already means 'give back' or 'come back', so 'back' is redundant; 'return the library books' is concise and correct.
- **B — return again the library books:** Using 'return again the library books' is incorrect both because 'again' is misplaced and because 'return' should not be paired redundantly unless repeated action is meant.
- **C — give return the library books:** Using 'give return the library books' is ungrammatical because 'return' is already a full verb and cannot combine with 'give' here.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'return back' is a classic redundancy error.
- **Exam Extra:** Avoid tautologies in SSC exams: 'return back', 'repeat again', 'revert back', 'enter into (a room)', and 'cousin brother/sister'.

### Q138 · Sentence Improvement: Inversion

Improve the bracketed part: [Seldom I have seen] such dedication from a young intern.

A. Seldom I saw
B. Seldom have I seen
C. Seldom did I seen
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. Seldom have I seen
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Seldom have I seen (Correct):** When a negative or restrictive adverb like 'seldom' opens a sentence, subject-auxiliary inversion is mandatory: 'Seldom + have + I + seen'.
- **A — Seldom I saw:** Using 'Seldom I saw' fails to invert the subject and auxiliary verb after the fronted negative adverb 'seldom'.
- **C — Seldom did I seen:** Using 'Seldom did I seen' is ungrammatical because 'did' must be followed by the base verb 'see', never the past participle 'seen'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'Seldom I have seen' uses normal statement order instead of mandatory inversion.
- **Exam Extra:** Negative front-positioned adverbs—Never, Seldom, Rarely, Hardly, Scarcely, Not only—always trigger 'Auxiliary + Subject + Main Verb' order.

### Q139 · Sentence Improvement: Gerunds & Infinitives

Improve the bracketed part: She avoids [to speak] in front of large crowds.

A. speaking
B. to speaking
C. spoke
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. speaking
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — speaking (Correct):** Verbs such as 'avoid', 'enjoy', 'mind', 'finish', and 'deny' take a gerund (V-ing) rather than an infinitive, making 'speaking' correct.
- **B — to speaking:** Using 'to speaking' is incorrect because 'avoid' takes a direct gerund object without the preposition 'to'.
- **C — spoke:** Using 'spoke' is incorrect because a finite past-tense verb cannot act as the object of 'avoids'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'avoid' cannot be followed by a 'to'-infinitive ('to speak').
- **Exam Extra:** Verbs always followed by V-ing (never to-infinitive): avoid, enjoy, admit, deny, mind, postpone, practice, risk, suggest.

### Q140 · Sentence Improvement: Modals & Infinitives

Improve the bracketed part: He [need not to worry] about the travel arrangements.

A. needs not worry
B. need not worry
C. need not worrying
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. need not worry
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — need not worry (Correct):** When used as a modal auxiliary in negative sentences, 'need not' does not take '-s' and is followed directly by a bare infinitive ('need not worry').
- **A — needs not worry:** Using 'needs not worry' is incorrect because modal 'need' never adds '-s' for third-person singular subjects.
- **C — need not worrying:** Using 'need not worrying' is incorrect because 'need not' requires the base verb (bare infinitive), not a gerund.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because modal 'need not' cannot be followed by 'to' before 'worry'.
- **Exam Extra:** Modal 'need not' / 'dare not' + V1 (no 'to', no '-s'); contrast main verb 'does not need + to + V1'.

### Q141 · Sentence Improvement: Fixed Prepositions

Improve the bracketed part: Most commuters prefer [metro trains than buses] during peak hours.

A. metro trains over than buses
B. metro trains to buses
C. metro trains from buses
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. metro trains to buses
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — metro trains to buses (Correct):** When comparing two nouns or gerunds with 'prefer', the correct preposition is 'to' ('prefer X to Y'), never 'than'.
- **A — metro trains over than buses:** Using 'metro trains over than buses' is ungrammatical because 'over' and 'than' cannot be combined after 'prefer'.
- **C — metro trains from buses:** Using 'metro trains from buses' is incorrect because 'prefer' expresses preference with 'to', not 'from'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'prefer' does not take 'than' between two noun phrases.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'prefer + noun/V-ing + to + noun/V-ing', but 'would prefer + to V1 + rather than + V1'.

### Q142 · Sentence Improvement: Subject-Verb Agreement

Improve the bracketed part: One of my closest friends [is moving] to Bengaluru next week.

A. are moving
B. were moving
C. have moved
D. No improvement

**Answer:** D. No improvement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — No improvement (Correct):** In 'One of + plural noun/pronoun', the grammatical subject is singular ('One'), so the singular verb 'is moving' is completely correct as written.
- **A — are moving:** Using 'are moving' is incorrect because the main subject is singular 'One', not 'friends'.
- **B — were moving:** Using 'were moving' is incorrect both because it is plural and because 'next week' indicates a planned future action, not past.
- **C — have moved:** Using 'have moved' is incorrect because 'have' is plural and present perfect conflicts with 'next week'.
- **Exam Extra:** Structure check: 'One of + plural noun + singular verb' ('One of the books is missing').

### Q143 · Sentence Improvement: Reflexive Pronouns

Improve the bracketed part: Employees should [avail this opportunity] to upgrade their skills.

A. avail themselves of this opportunity
B. avail to this opportunity
C. avail themselves with this opportunity
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. avail themselves of this opportunity
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — avail themselves of this opportunity (Correct):** 'Avail' is a transitive verb that requires a reflexive pronoun ('themselves') followed by the preposition 'of' ('avail themselves of').
- **B — avail to this opportunity:** Using 'avail to this opportunity' is incorrect because it omits the mandatory reflexive pronoun and uses the wrong preposition 'to' instead of 'of'.
- **C — avail themselves with this opportunity:** Using 'avail themselves with this opportunity' is incorrect because 'avail' pairs strictly with 'of', not 'with'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'avail' cannot take 'this opportunity' directly without a reflexive pronoun and 'of'.
- **Exam Extra:** Verbs requiring a reflexive pronoun when no other object is present: avail (oneself of), absent (oneself from), acquit (oneself), resign (oneself to).

### Q144 · Sentence Improvement: Reflexive Pronouns

Improve the bracketed part: Several members [absented from] the emergency meeting.

A. absented themselves from
B. absented them from
C. were absenting from
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. absented themselves from
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — absented themselves from (Correct):** When used as a verb meaning 'to stay away', 'absent' must take a reflexive pronoun matching the subject ('Several members absented themselves from').
- **B — absented them from:** Using 'absented them from' is incorrect because an action directed back at the subject requires the reflexive 'themselves', not 'them'.
- **C — were absenting from:** Using 'were absenting from' still omits the required reflexive pronoun 'themselves' after the verb 'absent'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because the verb 'absented' cannot be used intransitively without a reflexive object.
- **Exam Extra:** If 'absent' is used as an adjective ('were absent from'), no reflexive pronoun is needed; as an active verb ('absented'), a reflexive pronoun is mandatory.

### Q145 · Sentence Improvement: Prepositions & Conjunctions

Improve the bracketed part: [Despite of his injuries], the athlete finished the marathon.

A. In spite his injuries
B. Despite his injuries
C. Although his injuries
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. Despite his injuries
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Despite his injuries (Correct):** 'Despite' is never followed by 'of'; it directly precedes a noun phrase ('Despite his injuries'), meaning the same as 'In spite of'.
- **A — In spite his injuries:** Using 'In spite his injuries' is incorrect because 'in spite' cannot stand without the preposition 'of'.
- **C — Although his injuries:** Using 'Although his injuries' is incorrect because 'although' is a conjunction that requires a full clause with a verb, not just a noun phrase.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'Despite of' is an ungrammatical blend of 'despite' and 'in spite of'.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the equation: 'Despite = In spite of'. Never write 'despite of' or drop 'of' from 'in spite of'.

### Q146 · Sentence Improvement: Correlative Conjunctions

Improve the bracketed part: Although the exam was tough, [but she scored] above ninety percent.

A. yet she scored
B. and she scored
C. so she scored
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. yet she scored
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — yet she scored (Correct):** 'Although' and 'though' pair with 'yet' (or simply a comma), never with 'but', so 'yet she scored' is correct.
- **B — and she scored:** Using 'and she scored' is incorrect because 'and' joins parallel ideas rather than expressing the contrast introduced by 'Although'.
- **C — so she scored:** Using 'so she scored' is incorrect because 'so' shows result/consequence, contradicting the concessive contrast of 'Although'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'Although ... but' is a double-conjunction error in English grammar.
- **Exam Extra:** Never use 'but' to introduce the main clause after 'although' or 'though'; use 'yet' or just a comma.

### Q147 · Sentence Improvement: Modals & Tense Sequence

Improve the bracketed part: You [should inform] me earlier about the change in schedule.

A. should be informed
B. should have informed
C. would inform
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. should have informed
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — should have informed (Correct):** The time adverb 'earlier' indicates a missed past obligation, requiring the perfect modal structure 'should have + V3' ('should have informed').
- **A — should be informed:** Using 'should be informed' is incorrect because it makes 'You' passive (receiving information) and fails to express past obligation.
- **C — would inform:** Using 'would inform' is incorrect because 'would' expresses habitual past or conditional result, not an unfulfilled duty.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'should inform' refers to present or future advice, conflicting with the past reference 'earlier'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'should have + V3' to express regret, criticism, or unfulfilled advice about a past action.

### Q148 · Sentence Improvement: Gerunds & Prepositions

Improve the bracketed part: Living in a coastal town, they [are used to face] humid summers.

A. used to facing
B. are used to facing
C. are use to face
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. are used to facing
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — are used to facing (Correct):** When preceded by a form of 'be' ('are'), 'be used to' means 'accustomed to', where 'to' is a preposition requiring a gerund ('are used to facing').
- **A — used to facing:** Using 'used to facing' is incorrect because without 'be/get', 'used to' expresses a past habit and takes V1, not a gerund.
- **C — are use to face:** Using 'are use to face' is incorrect both for dropping '-d' in 'used' and for using a base verb instead of a gerund.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'are used to' treats 'to' as a preposition and cannot take the base verb 'face'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare: 'Subject + used to + V1' (discontinued past habit) vs 'Subject + be/get + used to + V-ing' (present/ongoing familiarity).

### Q149 · Sentence Improvement: Inversion & Conjunctions

Improve the bracketed part: [Scarcely had the chief guest arrived when] the auditorium erupted in applause.

A. Scarcely the chief guest arrived than
B. Scarcely had the chief guest arrived than
C. Scarcely did the chief guest arrived when
D. No improvement

**Answer:** D. No improvement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — No improvement (Correct):** The sentence correctly uses inverted past perfect ('Scarcely had the chief guest arrived') paired with the correlative conjunction 'when', so no improvement is needed.
- **A — Scarcely the chief guest arrived than:** Using 'Scarcely the chief guest arrived than' is wrong both for missing subject-auxiliary inversion and for using 'than' instead of 'when'.
- **B — Scarcely had the chief guest arrived than:** Using 'Scarcely had the chief guest arrived than' is incorrect because 'scarcely' pairs with 'when', never 'than'.
- **C — Scarcely did the chief guest arrived when:** Using 'Scarcely did the chief guest arrived when' is ungrammatical because 'did' must take the base verb 'arrive', not 'arrived'.
- **Exam Extra:** When 'Scarcely' or 'Hardly' starts a sentence: 'Scarcely/Hardly + had + Subject + V3 + when + Subject + V2'.

### Q150 · Sentence Improvement: Modifiers

Improve the bracketed part: He is [enough mature] to handle the responsibility independently.

A. mature enough
B. much mature
C. too mature
D. No improvement

**Answer:** A. mature enough
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — mature enough (Correct):** When 'enough' functions as an adverb modifying an adjective ('mature'), it must be placed after the adjective ('mature enough').
- **B — much mature:** Using 'much mature' is incorrect because 'much' generally modifies comparatives or past participles, not positive-degree adjectives like 'mature' before 'to + V1'.
- **C — too mature:** Using 'too mature' is incorrect because 'too ... to' implies negative excess (unable to handle), reversing the intended meaning.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'enough' cannot precede an adjective it modifies.
- **Exam Extra:** Placement rule: 'enough + noun' ('enough money'), but 'adjective/adverb + enough' ('tall enough', 'quickly enough').

### Q151 · Sentence Improvement: Pronouns & Gerunds

Improve the bracketed part: The coach objected to [me arriving] late for practice.

A. I arriving
B. my arriving
C. me to arrive
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. my arriving
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — my arriving (Correct):** A noun or pronoun placed directly before a gerund ('arriving') must be in the possessive case ('my arriving') in formal grammar.
- **A — I arriving:** Using 'I arriving' is incorrect because a subjective pronoun ('I') cannot modify a gerund after the preposition 'to'.
- **C — me to arrive:** Using 'me to arrive' is incorrect because 'object to' uses prepositional 'to' and takes a gerund, not an object + infinitive.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'me' is an objective pronoun rather than the possessive 'my' required before the gerund 'arriving'.
- **Exam Extra:** Always use possessive determiners (my, your, his, her, their, Ram's) before a gerund acting as the object of a verb or preposition.

### Q152 · Sentence Improvement: Confused Verbs

Improve the bracketed part: The weary traveller [laid down] under the shade of a banyan tree.

A. lied down
B. lay down
C. lain down
D. No improvement

**Answer:** B. lay down
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — lay down (Correct):** Intransitive 'lie' (to recline or rest) has the past tense 'lay' ('lay down'); 'laid' is the past tense of transitive 'lay' (to place something down).
- **A — lied down:** Using 'lied down' is incorrect because 'lied' is the past tense of 'lie' meaning 'to tell a falsehood', not to recline.
- **C — lain down:** Using 'lain down' is incorrect because 'lain' is a past participle and cannot act as a finite main verb without an auxiliary like 'had'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'laid' is transitive and requires a direct object (e.g., 'laid his bag down').
- **Exam Extra:** Verb forms: Lie–Lay–Lain (recline, no object); Lay–Laid–Laid (place something, needs object); Lie–Lied–Lied (tell an untruth).

### Q153 · Sentence Improvement: Confused Verbs

Improve the bracketed part: The framed certificate was [hanged on] the office wall.

A. hanging to
B. hunged on
C. hung on
D. No improvement

**Answer:** C. hung on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — hung on (Correct):** For suspending objects like pictures or certificates, the past participle of 'hang' is 'hung' ('was hung on'), whereas 'hanged' refers to execution.
- **A — hanging to:** Using 'hanging to' is incorrect because the preposition 'to' does not collocate with hanging an object against a wall ('on' is required).
- **B — hunged on:** Using 'hunged on' is incorrect because 'hunged' is not a valid English verb form.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'hanged' applies only to putting a person to death by hanging, not displaying a certificate.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember: 'Hang–Hung–Hung' for objects (clothes, frames) and 'Hang–Hanged–Hanged' for capital punishment.

### Q154 · Sentence Improvement: Relative Clauses

Improve the bracketed part: Give the award to [whomever deserves] it the most.

A. whomsoever deserves
B. No improvement
C. whoever deserve
D. whoever deserves

**Answer:** D. whoever deserves
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — whoever deserves (Correct):** Inside the clause '___ deserves it the most', the relative pronoun serves as the subject of 'deserves', so the subjective form 'whoever' is required (and takes singular 'deserves').
- **A — whomsoever deserves:** Using 'whomsoever deserves' is incorrect because 'whomsoever' is an objective pronoun and cannot act as the subject of 'deserves'.
- **B — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'whomever' is in the objective case, whereas the subject slot before 'deserves' requires 'whoever'.
- **C — whoever deserve:** Using 'whoever deserve' is incorrect because indefinite 'whoever' takes a singular verb ('deserves'), not plural 'deserve'.
- **Exam Extra:** Choose between 'whoever' and 'whomever' by checking its role inside its own clause, not whether a preposition precedes it.

### Q155 · Sentence Improvement: Subject-Verb Agreement

Improve the bracketed part: Many a student [have struggled] with this theorem.

A. are struggling
B. No improvement
C. were struggling
D. has struggled

**Answer:** D. has struggled
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — has struggled (Correct):** The quantifier 'Many a' is always followed by a singular countable noun ('student') and a singular verb ('has struggled').
- **A — are struggling:** Using 'are struggling' is incorrect because 'are' is a plural verb and cannot agree with the singular subject 'Many a student'.
- **B — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'have struggled' uses the plural auxiliary 'have' after 'Many a student'.
- **C — were struggling:** Using 'were struggling' is incorrect because 'were' is a plural verb, violating agreement with 'Many a student'.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'Many + plural noun + plural verb' with 'Many a/an + singular noun + singular verb'.

### Q156 · Sentence Improvement: Subject-Verb Agreement

Improve the bracketed part: A bouquet of fresh lilies [was placed] on the reception desk.

A. were placed
B. have been placed
C. are placed
D. No improvement

**Answer:** D. No improvement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — No improvement (Correct):** The head noun of the subject phrase is singular 'A bouquet' (not 'lilies'), so the singular passive verb 'was placed' is completely correct.
- **A — were placed:** Using 'were placed' is incorrect because the head noun 'bouquet' is singular, not plural.
- **B — have been placed:** Using 'have been placed' is incorrect because 'have' is plural and cannot agree with 'A bouquet'.
- **C — are placed:** Using 'are placed' is incorrect because 'are' is plural and fails to agree with the singular subject 'A bouquet'.
- **Exam Extra:** In 'Singular collective noun + of + plural noun', the verb agrees with the collective noun ('A bunch of keys is...', 'A herd of cattle was...').

### Q157 · Sentence Improvement: Stative Verbs

Improve the bracketed part: Mr. Verma [is owning] three bookstores in the city.

A. No improvement
B. has been owning
C. was owning
D. owns

**Answer:** D. owns
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — owns (Correct):** 'Own' is a stative verb expressing possession and is not normally used in continuous tenses; simple present 'owns' is required.
- **A — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because stative possession verbs like 'own' should not be used in the present continuous ('is owning').
- **B — has been owning:** Using 'has been owning' is incorrect because the present perfect continuous also treats 'own' as an ongoing progressive action.
- **C — was owning:** Using 'was owning' is incorrect because the past continuous likewise misuses the stative verb 'own'.
- **Exam Extra:** Stative verbs of possession, perception, and mental states (own, possess, know, belong, seem, believe) avoid '-ing' progressive forms.

### Q158 · Sentence Improvement: Subjunctive Mood

Improve the bracketed part: The doctor suggested that the patient [takes] complete rest for a week.

A. to take
B. taking
C. take
D. No improvement

**Answer:** C. take
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — take (Correct):** Verbs of recommendation or demand ('suggest', 'insist', 'recommend') followed by 'that' take the mandative subjunctive—a bare base verb ('take') regardless of subject or tense.
- **A — to take:** Using 'to take' is incorrect because a 'that'-clause requires a finite/subjunctive verb, not a 'to'-infinitive ('suggested that the patient to take').
- **B — taking:** Using 'taking' is incorrect because a participle alone cannot serve as the verb of the 'that'-clause ('suggested taking' drops 'that the patient').
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'takes' uses indicative '-s' instead of the mandative subjunctive 'take' after 'suggested that'.
- **Exam Extra:** Mandative subjunctive pattern: 'suggest/recommend/demand + that + Subject + base verb (V1)' without '-s'.

### Q159 · Sentence Improvement: Misplaced Modifiers

Improve the bracketed part: He [nearly studied] for eight hours before the mock test.

A. studied nearly for
B. No improvement
C. studied for nearly
D. nearly has studied

**Answer:** C. studied for nearly
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — studied for nearly (Correct):** Adverbs of degree/approximation such as 'nearly' should be placed immediately before the numeral or phrase they modify ('for nearly eight hours'), not before the verb 'studied'.
- **A — studied nearly for:** Using 'studied nearly for' separates 'nearly' from 'eight hours' by placing it before the preposition 'for' awkwardly.
- **B — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'nearly studied' implies he almost studied but did not actually study at all.
- **D — nearly has studied:** Using 'nearly has studied' keeps 'nearly' modifying the verb rather than 'eight hours' and misplaces it before 'has'.
- **Exam Extra:** Place limiting/focusing adverbs ('only', 'nearly', 'almost', 'even') right before the exact word or number they modify to prevent ambiguity.

### Q160 · Sentence Improvement: Correlative Prepositions

Improve the bracketed part: The train runs between Mumbai [to Pune] twice daily.

A. until Pune
B. through Pune
C. and Pune
D. No improvement

**Answer:** C. and Pune
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — and Pune (Correct):** The preposition 'between' pairs strictly with the conjunction 'and' ('between Mumbai and Pune'), never with 'to'.
- **A — until Pune:** Using 'until Pune' is incorrect because 'until' is a time preposition/conjunction and does not pair with 'between' for places.
- **B — through Pune:** Using 'through Pune' is incorrect because it changes the destination into a midpoint and fails to complete 'between'.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'between' must be paired with 'and', not 'to'.
- **Exam Extra:** Pair 'between ... and' for ranges and intervals, and 'from ... to' for start-to-end spans ('from Monday to Friday', 'between 9 a.m. and 5 p.m.').

### Q161 · Sentence Improvement: Parallel Structure

Improve the bracketed part: The workshop was [both informative and engaging] for all participants.

A. informative as well as engaging
B. both informative as well as engaging
C. neither informative and engaging
D. No improvement

**Answer:** D. No improvement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — No improvement (Correct):** 'Both ... and' correctly links two parallel predicative adjectives ('informative' and 'engaging'), so the sentence needs no improvement.
- **A — informative as well as engaging:** Using 'informative as well as engaging' drops 'both' unnecessarily and weakens the balanced correlative emphasis.
- **B — both informative as well as engaging:** Using 'both informative as well as engaging' is ungrammatical because 'both' must pair with 'and', never 'as well as'.
- **C — neither informative and engaging:** Using 'neither informative and engaging' is ungrammatical because 'neither' must pair with 'nor', not 'and'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Both' always pairs with 'and' (never 'as well as'), and 'both ... and' is not used in negative sentences (use 'neither ... nor').

### Q162 · Sentence Improvement: Prepositions

Improve the bracketed part: The prize money was divided equally [between the four] winners.

A. amidst the four
B. within the four
C. among the four
D. No improvement

**Answer:** C. among the four
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — among the four (Correct):** 'Among' is used when distributing or dividing something among more than two distinct entities ('the four winners'), whereas 'between' is normally for two.
- **A — amidst the four:** Using 'amidst the four' is incorrect because 'amidst' means 'surrounded by' in location or circumstances, not sharing equally.
- **B — within the four:** Using 'within the four' is incorrect because 'within' indicates inside boundaries or time limits, not distribution across recipients.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'between' is inappropriate for an equal division across four separate winners.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'between' for two items (or explicit reciprocal relationships) and 'among' for three or more items treated as a group.

### Q163 · Sentence Improvement: Tense Sequence

Improve the bracketed part: By the time we reached the theatre, the play [had already begun].

A. already began
B. has already begun
C. was already begun
D. No improvement

**Answer:** D. No improvement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — No improvement (Correct):** When one past action ('the play had already begun') happened before another past action ('we reached the theatre'), the earlier action takes the past perfect tense, so no improvement is required.
- **A — already began:** Using 'already began' (simple past) blurs the time sequence between the play starting and our arrival.
- **B — has already begun:** Using 'has already begun' (present perfect) clashes with the past-tense time clause 'By the time we reached'.
- **C — was already begun:** Using 'was already begun' creates an unnatural passive construction, as 'begin' is used intransitively here.
- **Exam Extra:** Classic two-past-action rule: Earlier action -> Past Perfect ('had + V3'); Later action -> Simple Past ('V2').

### Q164 · Sentence Improvement: Articles

Improve the bracketed part: She wants to pursue higher studies at [an European] university.

A. the European
B. one European
C. a European
D. No improvement

**Answer:** C. a European
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — a European (Correct):** Indefinite article selection depends on initial sound, not spelling; 'European' begins with a consonant /j/ ('yoo') sound, so it takes 'a European'.
- **A — the European:** Using 'the European' is incorrect because no specific European university has been identified yet, so an indefinite article is needed.
- **B — one European:** Using 'one European' changes the general indefinite sense into a strict numerical count.
- **D — No improvement:** Selecting 'No improvement' is incorrect because 'European' starts with a 'y' consonant sound, making 'an' wrong.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'a' before words starting with a consonant sound like 'university', 'European', 'unique', and 'one-rupee note'; use 'an' before silent-'h' words like 'honest' and 'heir'.

### Q165 · Sentence Improvement: Conjunctions

Improve the bracketed part: [Unless you submit] the form today, your application will not be processed.

A. Unless you do not submit
B. If you submit
C. Until you do not submit
D. No improvement

**Answer:** D. No improvement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — No improvement (Correct):** 'Unless' means 'if not' and is followed by an affirmative clause ('Unless you submit'), so the sentence is grammatically sound as written.
- **A — Unless you do not submit:** Using 'Unless you do not submit' creates an illogical double negative because 'unless' already means 'if not'.
- **B — If you submit:** Using 'If you submit' reverses the logical condition, meaning submitting the form prevents processing.
- **C — Until you do not submit:** Using 'Until you do not submit' is wrong both for the double negative 'until ... not' and for using a time conjunction where 'unless' (condition) is needed.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Unless' expresses a condition ('if not') and 'until' expresses time ('up to the time when'); neither takes 'not' or 'will/shall' in its clause.

---

## Chapter 3: Fixed Prepositions & Phrasal Fillers

### Q166 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The regional passport office remains closed ___ Saturdays and public holidays.

A. in
B. on
C. at
D. by

**Answer:** B. on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — on (Correct):** 'on' is used before specific days of the week and calendar dates, so 'on Saturdays and public holidays' is grammatically accurate.
- **A — in:** 'in' is used for longer periods such as months, seasons, years, or centuries (e.g., 'in July', 'in 2024'), not for individual days of the week.
- **C — at:** 'at' is used for exact clock times or fixed points such as 'at 9 a.m.' or 'at noon', not for days or public holidays.
- **D — by:** 'by' indicates a deadline meaning 'not later than' a particular time (e.g., 'by Friday'), rather than a recurring day when an office stays closed.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'on' for days and dates ('on Monday', 'on 15 August'), 'in' for months, years, and seasons, and 'at' for exact clock times.

### Q167 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The Rajdhani Express is scheduled to depart from New Delhi ___ 4:55 p.m. sharp.

A. on
B. in
C. during
D. at

**Answer:** D. at
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — at (Correct):** 'at' is the standard preposition for exact clock times such as 'at 4:55 p.m.' or 'at midnight'.
- **A — on:** 'on' is reserved for specific days and dates (e.g., 'on Tuesday', 'on 5 May'), not for exact clock hours.
- **B — in:** 'in' expresses broad intervals like months, years, or parts of the day ('in the afternoon'), rather than a precise clock time.
- **C — during:** 'during' denotes an action occurring throughout or within an event or period (e.g., 'during the summer'), not an instantaneous departure time.
- **Exam Extra:** In time expressions, 'at' pairs with precise moments: 'at 6 o'clock', 'at dawn', 'at dusk', 'at noon', and 'at night'.

### Q168 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The Archaeological Survey of India excavated the ancient Harappan site ___ 1921.

A. in
B. on
C. at
D. since

**Answer:** A. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — in (Correct):** 'in' is used before calendar years, months, decades, and centuries, making 'in 1921' the correct choice.
- **B — on:** 'on' requires a specific day or calendar date (e.g., 'on 12 March 1921'), whereas a standalone year takes 'in'.
- **C — at:** 'at' denotes a precise clock time or brief point in the day ('at noon'), not a full calendar year.
- **D — since:** 'since' marks a starting point for an action continuing up to a reference time with a perfect tense, whereas 'excavated' is a completed past action.
- **Exam Extra:** When only a year or month is given, use 'in' ('in 1921', 'in May'); once a specific day is added, switch to 'on' ('on 10 May 1921').

### Q169 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

Mr. Verma has been serving as the chief archivist at the museum ___ 2018.

A. for
B. from
C. since
D. during

**Answer:** C. since
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — since (Correct):** 'since' is used with perfect and perfect continuous tenses to indicate a definite starting point in the past ('since 2018').
- **A — for:** 'for' expresses a duration or length of time (e.g., 'for six years'), not a specific starting year like '2018'.
