- **B — from:** 'from' marks a starting point in non-perfect tenses or when paired with 'to/till' (e.g., 'from 2018 to 2022'), rather than with 'has been serving' alone.
- **D — during:** 'during' refers to an interval in which an event happens (e.g., 'during his tenure'), and does not link a past starting year to the present in a perfect continuous tense.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'since' for a fixed starting point ('since Monday', 'since 2018', 'since childhood') and 'for' for a total length of time ('for six years').

### Q170 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The candidates have been waiting outside the examination hall ___ over two hours.

A. since
B. for
C. from
D. by

**Answer:** B. for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — for (Correct):** 'for' is used to express a measurable duration or period of time ('for over two hours') in perfect continuous tenses.
- **A — since:** 'since' introduces an exact starting point in time (e.g., 'since 9 a.m.'), not a total duration like 'over two hours'.
- **C — from:** 'from' indicates a starting point when paired with an endpoint ('from 9 a.m. to 11 a.m.'), not a standalone period of waiting.
- **D — by:** 'by' signifies a deadline ('by 5 p.m.') or means, and cannot express how long an action has continued.
- **Exam Extra:** Time phrases with numbers and units ('two hours', 'ten days', 'five years') take 'for', whereas named starting points ('8 a.m.', '2020') take 'since'.

### Q171 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The winter workshop on data analytics will run ___ Monday to Friday next week.

A. from
B. since
C. for
D. at

**Answer:** A. from
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — from (Correct):** 'from' pairs naturally with 'to' ('from Monday to Friday') to mark the beginning and end of a scheduled span.
- **B — since:** 'since' is used for a past starting point continuing into the present with a perfect tense, and cannot pair with 'to' for a future workshop.
- **C — for:** 'for' indicates a total duration (e.g., 'for five days') and does not combine with 'to Friday' to express a range.
- **D — at:** 'at' specifies an exact point in time rather than the opening boundary of a multi-day range ending with 'to'.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the standard correlative pairs: 'from ... to', 'from ... till/until', and 'between ... and'—never mix 'from ... and' or 'between ... to'.

### Q172 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

All applicants must submit their verified documents to the registry ___ Friday evening.

A. until
B. since
C. for
D. by

**Answer:** D. by
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — by (Correct):** 'by' means 'not later than' and is used for deadlines by which a single action like submitting documents must be completed.
- **A — until:** 'until' denotes the continuation of a state or action up to a point in time, whereas submitting documents is a single completed event with a deadline.
- **B — since:** 'since' indicates a past starting point up to the present in a perfect tense, not a future submission deadline.
- **C — for:** 'for' measures a duration of time (e.g., 'for three days'), not a cut-off point on Friday evening.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'by' for a deadline of a one-time completion ('submit by Friday'), and 'until/till' for an ongoing state or continuous action ('wait until Friday').

### Q173 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The library reading room stays open ___ midnight during the university examination week.

A. by
B. within
C. until
D. since

**Answer:** C. until
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — until (Correct):** 'until' indicates that a continuous state—the reading room remaining open—persists up to midnight and stops there.
- **A — by:** 'by' marks a deadline for completing a discrete action ('finish by midnight'), not the continuous duration of staying open.
- **B — within:** 'within' means inside a span of time measured from now or another event ('within two hours'), not continuing up to a clock point.
- **D — since:** 'since' marks a starting point from the past into the present with a perfect tense, rather than a closing time.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Till' and 'until' share the same meaning for continuous actions; 'until' is preferred in formal writing and at the start of a sentence.

### Q174 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The courier company promised to deliver the parcel ___ forty-eight hours of dispatch.

A. within
B. until
C. since
D. on

**Answer:** A. within
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — within (Correct):** 'within' means 'before the end of a specified period', fitting 'within forty-eight hours of dispatch'.
- **B — until:** 'until' marks the point when an ongoing state ends and cannot be followed by a duration like 'forty-eight hours of dispatch'.
- **C — since:** 'since' introduces a past starting point in a perfect tense, not a delivery time limit.
- **D — on:** 'on' is used for specific days and dates, not for a measured time window like 'forty-eight hours'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'In forty-eight hours' means at the end of 48 hours, whereas 'within forty-eight hours' means at any time before 48 hours elapse.

### Q175 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

Several migratory birds visit the Chilika Lake sanctuary ___ the winter months.

A. until
B. during
C. since
D. by

**Answer:** B. during
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — during (Correct):** 'during' is a preposition used before a noun phrase representing a period or event ('during the winter months') to show when something takes place.
- **A — until:** 'until' indicates continuation up to a point in time, which clashes with the habitual action of birds visiting across the season.
- **C — since:** 'since' marks a starting point for a perfect tense, whereas 'visit' is in the simple present tense.
- **D — by:** 'by' indicates a deadline or proximity, not an ongoing seasonal timeframe.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish 'during' (preposition + noun phrase: 'during the winter') from 'while' (conjunction + clause: 'while it was snowing').

### Q176 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

The fire brigade reached the warehouse just ___ time to prevent the blaze from spreading.

A. on
B. at
C. by
D. in

**Answer:** D. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — in (Correct):** 'in time' is a fixed idiom meaning 'early enough before a crisis or deadline', especially when followed by an infinitive ('in time to prevent').
- **A — on:** 'on' forms 'on time', which means punctual according to a pre-set schedule rather than arriving early enough to stop a fire.
- **B — at:** 'at' does not form an idiomatic phrase with 'time' here; 'at times' means occasionally, and 'at the time' refers to a specific past moment.
- **C — by:** 'by' is used before a specific clock time or deadline ('by 5 p.m.'), not directly before the bare noun 'time' in this idiom.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'in time' (early enough to avoid missing or failing something) with 'on time' (punctual, strictly according to a timetable).

### Q177 · Time & Calendar Prepositions

India adopted its Constitution ___ 26 November 1949, though it came into force two months later.

A. in
B. at
C. on
D. by

**Answer:** C. on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — on (Correct):** 'on' is required whenever a specific calendar date with a day number is mentioned ('on 26 November 1949'), even if the month and year also appear.
- **A — in:** 'in' is used when only a month or year is stated ('in November 1949'), not when an exact date number ('26') is specified.
- **B — at:** 'at' indicates a clock time or brief point of the day, never a calendar date.
- **D — by:** 'by' signifies 'no later than' a deadline, whereas the sentence states the exact historical date of adoption.
- **Exam Extra:** Always check for the day number in date questions: 'in November 1949' takes 'in', but 'on 26 November 1949' takes 'on'.

### Q178 · Place & Position Prepositions

The spare keys to the archive cabinet are kept ___ the top drawer of the desk.

A. in
B. on
C. at
D. over

**Answer:** A. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — in (Correct):** 'in' indicates position inside a three-dimensional enclosed space such as a drawer, box, or room.
- **B — on:** 'on' denotes contact with an outer top surface (e.g., 'on the desk'), not inside an enclosed drawer.
- **C — at:** 'at' refers to a point or general location (e.g., 'at the entrance'), rather than inside a container.
- **D — over:** 'over' implies being positioned directly above or covering something, not contained inside it.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'in' for enclosed interiors ('in the drawer'), 'on' for flat surfaces ('on the desk'), and 'at' for specific reference points ('at the reception desk').

### Q179 · Place & Position Prepositions

The Shatabdi Express arrived ___ the Kanpur Central station precisely on schedule.

A. in
B. on
C. to
D. at

**Answer:** D. at
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — at (Correct):** 'arrive at' is the fixed collocation for reaching a specific point, building, or facility such as a railway station, airport, or office.
- **A — in:** 'in' follows 'arrive' only for large geographical areas like cities, states, or countries ('arrive in Delhi'), not a specific station.
- **B — on:** 'on' is used for surfaces or days, and does not collocate with 'arrive' for a railway station.
- **C — to:** 'to' is never used after the verb 'arrive' in standard English, even though 'go to' or 'travel to' takes 'to'.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the 'arrive' rule: 'arrive at' a small place or point (station, village, venue), 'arrive in' a large city or country, and never 'arrive to'.

### Q180 · Place & Position Prepositions

The foreign delegation arrived ___ Mumbai early this morning for the trade summit.

A. at
B. in
C. to
D. into

**Answer:** B. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — in (Correct):** 'arrive in' is used when the destination is a large city, state, or country such as Mumbai or India.
- **A — at:** 'at' follows 'arrive' for specific buildings, stations, or small towns, whereas a major metropolis like Mumbai takes 'in'.
- **C — to:** 'to' never follows 'arrive' in standard grammar; 'reach' also takes no preposition at all.
- **D — into:** 'into' denotes physical movement entering an interior space ('walked into the hall') and does not pair with 'arrive'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'arrive in Mumbai' (city) with 'arrive at Mumbai Airport' (specific terminal/facility).

### Q181 · Place & Position Prepositions

The project director divided the research responsibilities evenly ___ the five regional teams.

A. between
B. beside
C. among
D. across

**Answer:** C. among
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — among (Correct):** 'among' is used when distributing or locating something in relation to more than two distinct people or groups ('the five regional teams').
- **A — between:** 'between' is normally used for two entities or distinct one-to-one boundaries, whereas five teams require 'among'.
- **B — beside:** 'beside' means 'next to' or 'by the side of', which makes no sense for dividing work responsibilities.
- **D — across:** 'across' indicates movement or span from one side to the other, not distribution within five teams.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'between' for two items (or explicit pairwise relationships) and 'among' for three or more items treated collectively.

### Q182 · Place & Position Prepositions

A fierce debate broke out ___ the two finalists over the interpretation of the clause.

A. between
B. among
C. amid
D. within

**Answer:** A. between
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — between (Correct):** 'between' is the exact preposition for a relationship, contrast, or dispute involving two specific participants ('the two finalists').
- **B — among:** 'among' is used when referring collectively to three or more entities, not strictly two finalists.
- **C — amid:** 'amid' means surrounded by an uncountable mass, crowd, or atmosphere (e.g., 'amid chaos'), not two distinct debaters.
- **D — within:** 'within' expresses being inside physical bounds or a time limit, rather than an exchange between two people.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Between' can also be used for more than two items when each is named individually with 'and' (e.g., 'a treaty between India, Japan, and Australia').

### Q183 · Place & Position Prepositions

The keynote speaker delivered her address calmly ___ thunderous applause from the audience.

A. between
B. into
C. onto
D. amid

**Answer:** D. amid
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — amid (Correct):** 'amid' (or 'amidst') is used before uncountable nouns or collective surroundings such as 'thunderous applause', 'chaos', or 'heavy rain'.
- **A — between:** 'between' applies to two distinct entities or clearly separated points, not a continuous uncountable sound like applause.
- **B — into:** 'into' indicates inward physical motion into an enclosure, not the surrounding atmosphere during a speech.
- **C — onto:** 'onto' indicates movement onto a surface, which is incompatible with 'thunderous applause'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'among' before plural countable nouns ('among the students') and 'amid' before uncountable or abstract nouns ('amid applause').

### Q184 · Place & Position Prepositions

The old banyan tree stands quietly ___ the village temple, offering shade to travellers.

A. besides
B. beside
C. beyond
D. between

**Answer:** B. beside
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — beside (Correct):** 'beside' is a preposition of place meaning 'next to' or 'by the side of', fitting the tree standing next to the temple.
- **A — besides:** 'besides' means 'in addition to' or 'apart from', not physical proximity next to a building.
- **C — beyond:** 'beyond' means 'on the farther side of' or 'exceeding a limit', whereas the sentence describes standing right next to the temple.
- **D — between:** 'between' requires two reference points joined by 'and', whereas only 'the village temple' is named.
- **Exam Extra:** Never confuse 'beside' (next to, spatial) with 'besides' (in addition to, additive).

### Q185 · Place & Position Prepositions

___ fluency in English, the position requires working knowledge of two regional languages.

A. Beside
B. Between
C. Besides
D. Beyond

**Answer:** C. Besides
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Besides (Correct):** 'Besides' means 'in addition to' or 'apart from', introducing an extra job requirement alongside fluency in English.
- **A — Beside:** 'Beside' means 'by the side of' spatially and cannot mean 'in addition to' a qualification.
- **B — Between:** 'Between' links two items or choices and cannot introduce an additional qualification before a single noun phrase.
- **D — Beyond:** 'Beyond' means 'outside the range of' or 'further than', which reverses the intended additive meaning.
- **Exam Extra:** At the start of a clause, 'Besides + noun/gerund' adds another point, whereas 'Beside + noun' indicates physical location.

### Q186 · Place & Position Prepositions

Without a moment's hesitation, the lifeguard dived ___ the turbulent river to rescue the child.

A. in
B. on
C. at
D. into

**Answer:** D. into
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — into (Correct):** 'into' expresses physical movement from outside to inside an enclosed medium or space, matching the verb 'dived'.
- **A — in:** 'in' indicates a static position already inside something rather than the downward motion of diving from outside.
- **B — on:** 'on' refers to resting on or moving onto a surface, not plunging beneath the water's surface.
- **C — at:** 'at' marks a static point or target and does not express entering a body of water.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'in' for static location inside ('swimming in the river') and 'into' for motion across a boundary ('dived into the river').

### Q187 · Place & Position Prepositions

The villagers built a sturdy bamboo bridge ___ the swollen stream after the floods.

A. across
B. through
C. into
D. among

**Answer:** A. across
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — across (Correct):** 'across' means spanning or moving from one side to the opposite side of a flat surface or waterway like a stream.
- **B — through:** 'through' implies passing inside a three-dimensional enclosure such as a tunnel or pipe, whereas a bridge spans over a stream from bank to bank.
- **C — into:** 'into' denotes entering an interior, which does not describe a bridge connecting two banks.
- **D — among:** 'among' refers to being surrounded by three or more items, not spanning a single stream.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'across' (from one side to the other of a road, river, or field) with 'through' (inside a three-dimensional passage like a tunnel or forest).

### Q188 · Place & Position Prepositions

The expedition team trekked carefully ___ the dense rainforest before reaching the valley.

A. across
B. through
C. onto
D. beside

**Answer:** B. through
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — through (Correct):** 'through' conveys movement within and out the other side of a three-dimensional space such as a dense rainforest or tunnel.
- **A — across:** 'across' typically describes crossing a flat two-dimensional surface from edge to edge, whereas a dense rainforest surrounds travellers in three dimensions.
- **C — onto:** 'onto' denotes movement ending on top of a surface, not travelling inside a forest.
- **D — beside:** 'beside' means walking next to the edge of something rather than passing inside it.
- **Exam Extra:** Always choose 'through' for trees, crowds, tunnels, and fog, and 'across' for open surfaces like roads, bridges, and deserts.

### Q189 · Place & Position Prepositions

She spread a thick woollen blanket ___ the sleeping child to protect him from the chill.

A. above
B. under
C. over
D. among

**Answer:** C. over
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — over (Correct):** 'over' is used when something covers or is spread directly across the surface of another object ('spread a blanket over the child').
- **A — above:** 'above' indicates a higher elevation without touching or covering the object below, whereas a blanket physically covers the child.
- **B — under:** 'under' means beneath or below something, which makes no sense for spreading a blanket on top of a sleeping child.
- **D — among:** 'among' applies to being surrounded by three or more separate items, not covering one child.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'over' for covering contact or vertical position ('a sheet over the table', 'a bridge over the river') and 'above' purely for higher elevation without contact.

### Q190 · Agent, Instrument & Cause Prepositions

The acclaimed collection of short stories was written ___ Ruskin Bond in the hills of Mussoorie.

A. by
B. with
C. from
D. through

**Answer:** A. by
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — by (Correct):** 'by' introduces the human agent or doer of an action in a passive-voice sentence ('written by Ruskin Bond').
- **B — with:** 'with' introduces the physical tool or instrument used to perform an action, not the author who wrote the book.
- **C — from:** 'from' denotes origin or source (e.g., 'adapted from a novel'), rather than the passive agent.
- **D — through:** 'through' expresses an intermediary channel or process, not the direct human creator in a passive clause.
- **Exam Extra:** In passive constructions, use 'by' for the living agent ('written by the author') and 'with' for the non-living tool ('written with a fountain pen').

### Q191 · Agent, Instrument & Cause Prepositions

The ancient inscription on the temple pillar had been carved ___ a fine iron chisel.

A. by
B. from
C. of
D. with

**Answer:** D. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — with (Correct):** 'with' is used in passive sentences to identify the physical tool or instrument used to carry out an action ('carved with a chisel').
- **A — by:** 'by' introduces the human or active agent who performed the carving, whereas 'an iron chisel' is an inanimate tool.
- **B — from:** 'from' indicates the raw material or source ('carved from marble'), not the tool used by the sculptor.
- **C — of:** 'of' denotes composition or possession ('made of stone'), not the instrument used to carve.
- **Exam Extra:** A classic SSC pairing contrasts agent and instrument in one sentence: 'The letter was written by him with a blue pen.'

### Q192 · Agent, Instrument & Cause Prepositions

Before modern antibiotics were discovered, thousands of people died ___ tuberculosis every year.

A. from
B. of
C. with
D. by

**Answer:** B. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — of (Correct):** 'die of' is the standard preposition for death caused directly by a disease, illness, hunger, thirst, or grief ('died of tuberculosis').
- **A — from:** 'from' after 'die' is reserved for indirect or external causes such as wounds, exhaustion, or food poisoning, rather than a named disease.
- **C — with:** 'with' is not idiomatic after 'die' to state the medical cause of death.
- **D — by:** 'by' is used for violent means or agency ('die by the sword', 'die by suicide'), not an infectious disease.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the SSC distinction: 'die of' a disease/hunger/thirst, 'die from' an external wound/overwork/poisoning, and 'die for' a noble cause or country.

### Q193 · Agent, Instrument & Cause Prepositions

The injured stray dog tragically died ___ a deep wound inflicted by a speeding vehicle.

A. of
B. by
C. from
D. with

**Answer:** C. from
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — from (Correct):** 'die from' is used when death results from an external injury, wound, overwork, or exposure rather than an internal disease.
- **A — of:** 'of' follows 'die' when naming a disease, hunger, or thirst, whereas a deep vehicular wound is an external injury.
- **B — by:** 'by' refers to an intentional instrument or violence ('die by hanging'), not an accidental wound.
- **D — with:** 'with' does not express the cause of death after 'die' in standard English usage.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'die from' for wounds, snakebites, overeating, or exhaustion, and 'die in' for circumstances like 'die in battle' or 'die in harness'.

### Q194 · Agent, Instrument & Cause Prepositions

High-quality butter and cheese are made ___ fresh cow's milk at this dairy cooperative.

A. from
B. of
C. by
D. with

**Answer:** A. from
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — from (Correct):** 'made from' is used when the original raw material undergoes a chemical or structural transformation and is no longer visibly recognizable ('butter made from milk').
- **B — of:** 'of' after 'made' applies when the material retains its physical identity in the final object (e.g., 'a table made of teak'), unlike milk turned into butter.
- **C — by:** 'by' introduces the maker or process ('made by local farmers'), not the raw ingredient.
- **D — with:** 'with' is used when listing one of several added ingredients ('a curry made with fresh herbs'), rather than the sole transformed base substance.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'made of' (material remains visibly unchanged: 'a ring made of gold') with 'made from' (material changes form: 'paper made from wood').

### Q195 · Agent, Instrument & Cause Prepositions

___ heavy financial losses in the first quarter, the startup refused to lay off its staff.

A. Despite of
B. In spite
C. Although
D. Despite

**Answer:** D. Despite
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Despite (Correct):** 'Despite' is a preposition of concession that takes a noun phrase directly ('Despite heavy financial losses') without needing 'of'.
- **A — Despite of:** 'Despite of' is a common grammatical error because 'despite' never takes the preposition 'of' after it.
- **B — In spite:** 'In spite' is incomplete on its own and always requires 'of' before a noun phrase ('In spite of heavy financial losses').
- **C — Although:** 'Although' is a subordinating conjunction that must be followed by a full subject-verb clause ('Although it suffered losses'), not a bare noun phrase.
- **Exam Extra:** Memorize the exact equivalence: 'Despite + noun phrase' = 'In spite of + noun phrase'. Never write 'Despite of'!

### Q196 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Although she struggled with languages, Meera was exceptionally good ___ analytical geometry.

A. in
B. at
C. on
D. with

**Answer:** B. at
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — at (Correct):** 'good at' is the fixed adjective + preposition combination used to express skill or proficiency in a subject or activity.
- **A — in:** 'in' is used with 'weak in' or 'proficient in', but 'good' takes 'at' for skills and academic subjects; 'good in' is non-standard here.
- **C — on:** 'on' does not collocate with 'good' to denote academic ability.
- **D — with:** 'with' after 'good' refers to handling people or tools smoothly ('good with children'), not mastering a branch of mathematics.
- **Exam Extra:** Adjectives of skill take 'at': 'good at', 'bad at', 'clever at', 'slow at', and 'adept at' (though 'proficient' and 'weak' take 'in').

### Q197 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Although he joined the ministry later, Rajesh is senior ___ me in the administrative cadre.

A. than
B. from
C. to
D. over

**Answer:** C. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — to (Correct):** Latin-origin comparative adjectives ending in '-ior'—such as 'senior', 'junior', 'superior', 'inferior', 'prior', and 'anterior'—always take 'to', never 'than'.
- **A — than:** 'than' follows regular English comparatives like 'older than' or 'higher than', never Latin '-ior' adjectives like 'senior'.
- **B — from:** 'from' is used for separation or difference ('different from'), not rank comparison after 'senior'.
- **D — over:** 'over' expresses superiority in position or control ('authority over'), not comparison of seniority with 'senior'.
- **Exam Extra:** All '-ior' comparatives ('senior', 'junior', 'superior', 'inferior', 'prior', 'posterior') take 'to' instead of 'than' and never take 'more'.

### Q198 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Many seasoned commuters prefer travelling by metro rail ___ driving through congested roads.

A. to
B. than
C. over
D. from

**Answer:** A. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — to (Correct):** 'prefer' takes the preposition 'to' when comparing two nouns or gerunds ('prefer X to Y').
- **B — than:** 'than' is incorrect after 'prefer' when comparing two noun phrases or gerunds; 'prefer' strictly requires 'to'.
- **C — over:** 'over' is sometimes heard colloquially, but standard SSC grammar requires 'to' after 'prefer'.
- **D — from:** 'from' expresses separation or distinction ('differ from'), not preference between two options.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'prefer A to B' with nouns or gerunds ('prefer tea to coffee'), but use 'rather than' when comparing two 'to'-infinitives ('prefer to walk rather than drive').

### Q199 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

The internal audit team is directly responsible ___ verifying the quarterly expenditure statements.

A. to
B. with
C. of
D. for

**Answer:** D. for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — for (Correct):** 'responsible for' indicates accountability for a duty, task, or outcome ('responsible for verifying').
- **A — to:** 'to' follows 'responsible' only when naming the person or body to whom someone reports ('responsible to the board'), not the task itself.
- **B — with:** 'with' does not collocate with 'responsible' in standard English.
- **C — of:** 'of' is incorrect because 'responsible' never takes 'of' before an action or duty.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the dual pattern: a person is 'responsible to' a superior/authority, and 'responsible for' a task, duty, or mistake.

### Q200 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Conservative investors are generally averse ___ taking high risks in volatile equity markets.

A. of
B. to
C. from
D. against

**Answer:** B. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — to (Correct):** 'averse to' means strongly opposed or disinclined to something and is always followed by a noun or gerund ('averse to taking').
- **A — of:** 'of' does not pair with 'averse', even though adjectives like 'afraid of' or 'fearful of' take 'of'.
- **C — from:** 'from' follows verbs of avoidance like 'refrain from' or 'abstain from', not the adjective 'averse'.
- **D — against:** 'against' expresses opposition after verbs like 'warn against' or 'protest against', not after 'averse'.
- **Exam Extra:** Note that 'to' in 'averse to', 'addicted to', 'accustomed to', and 'prone to' is a preposition, so any verb following it must be in the '-ing' gerund form.

### Q201 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

The committee's preliminary report was completely devoid ___ any concrete statistical evidence.

A. of
B. from
C. with
D. by

**Answer:** A. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — of (Correct):** 'devoid of' means completely lacking or free from something ('devoid of evidence').
- **B — from:** 'from' follows 'free from' or 'exempt from', whereas 'devoid' strictly collocates with 'of'.
- **C — with:** 'with' indicates accompaniment or possession, the opposite of lacking something, and never follows 'devoid'.
- **D — by:** 'by' denotes agency or means and cannot follow 'devoid'.
- **Exam Extra:** Words denoting lack or deprivation—'devoid of', 'bereft of', 'short of', 'destitute of', and 'deprive of'—take 'of'.

### Q202 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

The revised examination pattern is markedly different ___ the one followed five years ago.

A. than
B. to
C. from
D. with

**Answer:** C. from
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — from (Correct):** In standard SSC grammar, 'different' takes the preposition 'from' ('different from the one followed five years ago').
- **A — than:** 'than' is used with comparative adjectives such as 'better than' or 'more complex than', and is treated as incorrect after 'different' in formal grammar.
- **B — to:** 'to' follows 'similar to' or 'identical to', whereas 'different' takes 'from' in prescriptive exam usage.
- **D — with:** 'with' pairs with 'differ' when two people disagree in opinion ('I differ with you'), not after the adjective 'different'.
- **Exam Extra:** Keep the pair straight: the adjective 'different' and the verb 'differ' (in qualities) both take 'from' ('different from', 'A differs from B').

### Q203 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

The coach was visibly angry ___ the captain for ignoring the team strategy during the match.

A. at
B. on
C. against
D. with

**Answer:** D. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — with (Correct):** 'angry with' is the fixed collocation when the object of anger is a person ('angry with the captain').
- **A — at:** 'at' follows 'angry' when referring to a non-living thing, event, or behaviour ('angry at the decision'), not a person like the captain.
- **B — on:** 'on' never follows 'angry' in standard English, though learners sometimes translate it literally from other languages.
- **C — against:** 'against' expresses opposition ('turn against', 'protest against'), not the target of 'angry'.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish 'angry with' a person ('angry with his brother') from 'angry at' a thing, remark, or situation ('angry at the delay').

### Q204 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

A conscientious leader cannot afford to be blind ___ the genuine grievances of junior staff.

A. of
B. to
C. with
D. from

**Answer:** B. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — to (Correct):** 'blind to' means unwilling or unable to notice a fault, danger, or problem ('blind to the genuine grievances').
- **A — of:** 'of' is incorrect because 'blind' takes 'in' for physical sight loss in one eye and 'to' for ignoring facts, never 'of'.
- **C — with:** 'with' indicates accompaniment or cause ('blind with rage'), not ignoring grievances.
- **D — from:** 'from' expresses separation or protection, not figurative blindness to an issue.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'blind in' one eye (physical blindness in a specific eye) with 'blind to' someone's faults (figurative disregard).

### Q205 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

To qualify for the diplomatic corps, candidates must be proficient ___ at least two foreign languages.

A. in
B. at
C. with
D. on

**Answer:** A. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — in (Correct):** 'proficient in' is the fixed collocation for being highly skilled or competent in a field or language.
- **B — at:** 'at' follows 'good' or 'clever', whereas 'proficient' always takes 'in' before a subject or skill.
- **C — with:** 'with' indicates handling tools or instruments ('skilled with tools'), not mastery of languages after 'proficient'.
- **D — on:** 'on' is used for topics of lectures or books ('an authority on'), not after 'proficient'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'proficient in', 'skilled in', 'versed in', and 'weak in' (all taking 'in') with 'good at' and 'adept at' (taking 'at').

### Q206 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Only graduates with two years of field experience are eligible ___ the senior fellowship.

A. to
B. of
C. for
D. with

**Answer:** C. for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — for (Correct):** 'eligible for' means qualified or entitled to apply for or receive a post, grant, or benefit ('eligible for the senior fellowship').
- **A — to:** 'to' is used after 'eligible' only as an infinitive marker before a verb ('eligible to vote'), not before a noun phrase like 'the senior fellowship'.
- **B — of:** 'of' pairs with 'worthy of' or 'capable of', not with 'eligible'.
- **D — with:** 'with' denotes association or instrument and never collocates with 'eligible'.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice the pattern: 'eligible for + noun' ('eligible for promotion'), whereas 'eligible to + verb' uses an infinitive ('eligible to apply').

### Q207 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

The scientist's selfless dedication to rural healthcare is truly worthy ___ the highest national honour.

A. for
B. to
C. with
D. of

**Answer:** D. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — of (Correct):** 'worthy of' is the fixed adjective + preposition pair meaning deserving of respect, praise, or an honour.
- **A — for:** 'for' follows 'fit for' or 'suitable for', whereas 'worthy' always takes 'of'.
- **B — to:** 'to' does not follow 'worthy' before a noun phrase; 'worthy' requires 'of'.
- **C — with:** 'with' expresses means or accompaniment and never pairs with 'worthy'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'worthy of + noun/gerund' ('worthy of praise'), while 'worth' takes a noun or gerund directly with no preposition ('worth praising').

### Q208 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Every new trainee must become thoroughly acquainted ___ the laboratory safety protocols.

A. with
B. to
C. of
D. in

**Answer:** A. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — with (Correct):** 'acquainted with' means familiar with or having personal knowledge of a person, rule, or procedure.
- **B — to:** 'to' pairs with 'known to' or 'familiar to' (when the subject is the thing known, e.g., 'the rule is known to all'), not with 'acquainted'.
- **C — of:** 'of' pairs with 'aware of' or 'ignorant of', never with 'acquainted'.
- **D — in:** 'in' indicates location or field ('proficient in') and does not follow 'acquainted'.
- **Exam Extra:** Parallel adjectives of familiarity: 'acquainted with', 'familiar with' (know well), and 'conversant with' all take 'with'.

### Q209 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

No responsible municipality can remain indifferent ___ the rising levels of urban air pollution.

A. of
B. to
C. for
D. with

**Answer:** B. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — to (Correct):** 'indifferent to' means unconcerned about or showing no interest in something ('indifferent to rising levels').
- **A — of:** 'of' is used with 'ignorant of' or 'neglectful of', never with 'indifferent'.
- **C — for:** 'for' indicates purpose or beneficiary and does not collocate with 'indifferent'.
- **D — with:** 'with' follows 'impatient with' or 'dissatisfied with', whereas 'indifferent' strictly takes 'to'.
- **Exam Extra:** Watch out for the trap: 'different' takes 'from', but 'indifferent' always takes 'to'!

### Q210 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Instead of celebrating her promotion, a few colleagues grew quietly jealous ___ her rapid rise.

A. from
B. with
C. over
D. of

**Answer:** D. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — of (Correct):** 'jealous of' is the fixed collocation meaning envious or resentful of someone else's success or possessions.
- **A — from:** 'from' expresses separation or origin and never follows 'jealous'.
- **B — with:** 'with' pairs with 'angry with' or 'pleased with', not with 'jealous'.
- **C — over:** 'over' follows verbs like 'brood over' or 'grieve over', whereas 'jealous' takes 'of'.
- **Exam Extra:** Adjectives of envy and suspicion—'jealous of', 'envious of', and 'suspicious of'—consistently take 'of'.

### Q211 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Under the new consumer law, online marketplaces are liable ___ defective goods sold on their platforms.

A. to
B. with
C. for
D. of

**Answer:** C. for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — for (Correct):** 'liable for' means legally responsible or answerable for something, such as defective goods or damages.
- **A — to:** 'to' after 'liable' means exposed or subject to a penalty or tendency ('liable to prosecution', 'liable to errors'), not legally responsible for goods.
- **B — with:** 'with' does not collocate with 'liable' in legal or standard usage.
- **D — of:** 'of' follows 'guilty of' or 'capable of', never 'liable'.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'liable for' (legally accountable for a debt, damage, or action) with 'liable to' (subject to a penalty or likely to suffer something, e.g., 'liable to a fine').

### Q212 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

A diet deficient ___ vitamin D and calcium can weaken bone density over time.

A. in
B. of
C. from
D. with

**Answer:** A. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — in (Correct):** 'deficient in' is the fixed collocation meaning lacking an adequate amount of a nutrient, quality, or resource.
- **B — of:** 'of' follows 'devoid of' or 'short of', whereas 'deficient' always requires 'in'.
- **C — from:** 'from' denotes source or separation and does not collocate with 'deficient'.
- **D — with:** 'with' indicates presence or accompaniment and never follows 'deficient'.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'deficient in' (adjective + 'in') with 'devoid of' / 'short of' (adjectives + 'of') and 'lack' (verb takes no preposition: 'lacks vitamin D').

### Q213 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Despite repeated setbacks, the young entrepreneur remained intent ___ launching her clean-energy startup.

A. to
B. on
C. for
D. with

**Answer:** B. on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — on (Correct):** 'intent on' means firmly resolved or determined to do something and takes a noun or gerund ('intent on launching').
- **A — to:** 'to' does not follow 'intent' before a '-ing' gerund; 'determined' takes 'to + base verb', while 'intent' takes 'on + gerund'.
- **C — for:** 'for' follows 'eager for' or 'ready for', not 'intent'.
- **D — with:** 'with' does not pair with the adjective 'intent' to show determination.
- **Exam Extra:** Related collocations of determination: 'bent on', 'intent on', and 'keen on' all take 'on' followed by a noun or '-ing' gerund.

### Q214 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

Make sure the upgraded software module is fully compatible ___ the existing operating system.

A. to
B. for
C. of
D. with

**Answer:** D. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — with (Correct):** 'compatible with' means able to exist, work, or function smoothly alongside another system or person.
- **A — to:** 'to' follows 'comparable to' or 'adaptable to', whereas 'compatible' takes 'with'.
- **B — for:** 'for' follows 'suitable for' or 'ready for', not 'compatible'.
- **C — of:** 'of' pairs with 'capable of' or 'characteristic of', never with 'compatible'.
- **Exam Extra:** Both 'compatible with' and its opposite 'incompatible with' always take the preposition 'with'.

### Q215 · Fixed Adjective + Preposition

No coastal town is completely immune ___ the devastating impact of severe tropical cyclones.

A. from
B. against
C. to
D. of

**Answer:** C. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — to (Correct):** 'immune to' is the standard collocation meaning protected against or unaffected by a disease, criticism, or hazard.
- **A — from:** 'from' follows 'safe from' or 'free from', whereas 'immune' standardly takes 'to' (not 'from') in exam English.
- **B — against:** 'against' collocates with 'protected against' or 'vaccinated against', not with 'immune'.
- **D — of:** 'of' does not pair with 'immune' in standard usage.
- **Exam Extra:** Adjectives of resistance or susceptibility—'immune to', 'resistant to', 'impervious to', and 'vulnerable to'—all take 'to'.

### Q216 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

The warehouse clerk was falsely accused ___ tampering with the inventory records.

A. of
B. for
C. with
D. about

**Answer:** A. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — of (Correct):** 'accuse someone of something' is the fixed verb + preposition pattern used to charge someone with an offence or wrongdoing.
- **B — for:** 'for' follows 'blame for' or 'punish for', whereas 'accuse' always takes 'of'.
- **C — with:** 'with' follows 'charge with' ('charged with tampering'), never 'accuse'.
- **D — about:** 'about' is used after 'complain about' or 'worry about', not after 'accuse'.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'accuse someone of' a crime with 'charge someone with' a crime and 'blame someone for' a mistake.

### Q217 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

The principal warmly congratulated the debate team ___ winning the national championship.

A. for
B. on
C. at
D. over

**Answer:** B. on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — on (Correct):** 'congratulate someone on something' is the mandatory collocation for praising someone for an achievement or success.
- **A — for:** 'for' is a frequent SSC distractor used with 'thank for' or 'praise for', whereas 'congratulate' strictly requires 'on'.
- **C — at:** 'at' follows 'rejoice at' or 'marvel at', not 'congratulate'.
- **D — over:** 'over' follows 'triumph over' or 'rejoice over', never 'congratulate'.
- **Exam Extra:** Never use 'congratulate for' in formal exams; both 'congratulate on' and 'compliment on' take 'on'.

### Q218 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Visitors are kindly requested to refrain ___ using flash photography inside the heritage gallery.

A. against
B. to
C. of
D. from

**Answer:** D. from
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — from (Correct):** 'refrain from' means to deliberately stop oneself from doing something and is followed by a noun or gerund ('refrain from using').
- **A — against:** 'against' follows 'warn against' or 'guard against', whereas 'refrain' takes 'from'.
- **B — to:** 'to' cannot follow 'refrain'; unlike 'refuse to + verb', 'refrain' takes 'from + gerund'.
- **C — of:** 'of' never pairs with 'refrain' in English grammar.
- **Exam Extra:** Verbs of avoidance and prevention—'refrain from', 'abstain from', 'desist from', 'prevent from', and 'prohibit from'—all take 'from + V-ing'.

### Q219 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

All member nations pledged to abide ___ the environmental standards set by the treaty.

A. with
B. to
C. by
D. on

**Answer:** C. by
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — by (Correct):** 'abide by' is a fixed phrasal verb meaning to accept and obey a rule, law, agreement, or decision.
- **A — with:** 'with' follows 'comply with' or 'agree with', whereas 'abide' always takes 'by' when meaning 'obey'.
- **B — to:** 'to' follows 'adhere to' or 'stick to', not 'abide'.
- **D — on:** 'on' follows 'insist on' or 'act on', never 'abide'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare obedience collocations: 'abide by' the rules, 'comply with' the rules, and 'adhere to' the rules.

### Q220 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Rural artisans had to quickly adapt ___ digital payment platforms after the market shift.

A. to
B. with
C. for
D. in

**Answer:** A. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — to (Correct):** 'adapt to' means to adjust oneself or change behaviour to fit new conditions or an environment.
- **B — with:** 'with' denotes accompaniment and does not follow 'adapt' to indicate adjusting to new platforms.
- **C — for:** 'for' follows 'adapt' only when modifying something for a new purpose ('adapted for television'), not when people adjust to a system.
- **D — in:** 'in' indicates location or field and does not pair with 'adapt' here.
- **Exam Extra:** Note the spelling and usage trio: 'adapt to' (adjust), 'adopt' (take up, no preposition), and 'adept at/in' (skilled).

### Q221 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Every pharmaceutical company must comply ___ the stringent quality norms issued by the regulator.

A. to
B. by
C. for
D. with

**Answer:** D. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — with (Correct):** 'comply with' is the fixed verb + preposition pattern meaning to act in accordance with a rule, order, or request.
- **A — to:** 'to' follows 'conform to' or 'submit to', whereas 'comply' always takes 'with'.
- **B — by:** 'by' pairs with 'abide by' or 'stand by', not with 'comply'.
- **C — for:** 'for' expresses purpose or benefit and never collocates with 'comply'.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the triad for following rules: 'comply with', 'abide by', and 'conform to/with'.

### Q222 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

The newly constituted selection panel consists ___ three retired judges and two senior academics.

A. in
B. of
C. with
D. from

**Answer:** B. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — of (Correct):** 'consist of' means to be made up of or composed of specific parts or members.
- **A — in:** 'in' changes the meaning to 'have as an essential nature' ('consist in contentment'), rather than listing constituent members.
- **C — with:** 'with' does not follow 'consist' to list component parts.
- **D — from:** 'from' indicates origin or transformation ('made from'), not composition after 'consist'.
- **Exam Extra:** Never use 'consist of' in the passive voice ('is consisted of' is wrong), and remember 'comprise' takes no 'of' in the active voice ('the panel comprises five members').

### Q223 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

According to the philosopher, true happiness consists ___ contentment rather than material wealth.

A. of
B. with
C. in
D. by

**Answer:** C. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — in (Correct):** 'consist in' means to lie in or have as its core essence or defining quality ('consists in contentment').
- **A — of:** 'of' is used with 'consist' to list tangible parts or members that compose a whole, not an abstract definition of happiness.
- **B — with:** 'with' never collocates with 'consist' in standard English.
- **D — by:** 'by' expresses agency or means and cannot follow 'consist'.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish 'consist of' (physical or countable components: 'water consists of hydrogen and oxygen') from 'consist in' (abstract essence: 'success consists in perseverance').

### Q224 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

For three generations, the family firm has dealt exclusively ___ handwoven Banarasi silk.

A. in
B. with
C. at
D. on

**Answer:** A. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — in (Correct):** 'deal in' means to buy and sell or trade in a specific commodity or product ('dealt in handwoven Banarasi silk').
- **B — with:** 'with' after 'deal' means to manage, handle, or do business with a person or issue, not to trade a commodity like silk.
- **C — at:** 'at' indicates location or rate and never collocates with 'deal' for trading goods.
- **D — on:** 'on' does not pair with 'deal' to describe commerce in a product.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'deal in' (trade in goods or stocks) with 'deal with' (handle a person, problem, or topic) and 'deal out' (distribute).

### Q225 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

The disaster management authority is well equipped to deal ___ sudden urban flooding.

A. in
B. with
C. by
D. to

**Answer:** B. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — with (Correct):** 'deal with' means to manage, handle, or take action regarding a problem, task, or situation ('deal with sudden urban flooding').
- **A — in:** 'in' follows 'deal' only when referring to trading commercial goods ('deal in spices'), not handling a crisis.
- **C — by:** 'by' denotes agency or method and does not form a phrasal verb with 'deal' here.
- **D — to:** 'to' never collocates with 'deal' to express handling a problem.
- **Exam Extra:** In essays and comprehension passages, 'deal with' can also mean 'be about a subject' ('this chapter deals with prepositions').

### Q226 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Despite mounting pressure from lobbyists, the honest official refused to yield ___ undue influence.

A. against
B. for
C. before
D. to

**Answer:** D. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — to (Correct):** 'yield to' means to give way, surrender, or submit to pressure, temptation, or authority.
- **A — against:** 'against' expresses resistance ('fight against'), the opposite of giving in after 'yield'.
- **B — for:** 'for' indicates purpose or benefit and never collocates with 'yield' in this sense.
- **C — before:** 'before' indicates position or time ('bow before'), whereas 'yield' requires 'to'.
- **Exam Extra:** Similar verbs of submission—'yield to', 'succumb to', 'submit to', and 'surrender to'—all take the preposition 'to'.

### Q227 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Extra coaching sessions were arranged to help first-year students cope ___ the demanding syllabus.

A. up with
B. against
C. with
D. for

**Answer:** C. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — with (Correct):** 'cope with' is the only grammatically correct form meaning to manage or handle a difficult situation successfully.
- **A — up with:** 'up with' creates the non-standard error 'cope up with'; 'cope' never takes 'up' before 'with'.
- **B — against:** 'against' expresses opposition ('compete against', 'struggle against'), whereas 'cope' strictly requires 'with'.
- **D — for:** 'for' denotes purpose or duration and does not collocate with 'cope'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Cope up with' is a classic Indian English error tested repeatedly in SSC CGL/CHSL; always drop 'up' and use 'cope with'.

### Q228 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

After reviewing the forensic evidence, the court acquitted the defendant ___ all criminal charges.

A. of
B. from
C. with
D. for

**Answer:** A. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — of (Correct):** 'acquit someone of a charge' is the fixed legal collocation meaning to declare someone not guilty of an offence.
- **B — from:** 'from' follows 'exempt from' or 'release from', whereas 'acquit' strictly requires 'of'.
- **C — with:** 'with' pairs with 'charge with' ('charged with fraud'), never with 'acquit'.
- **D — for:** 'for' follows 'punish for' or 'sentence for', not 'acquit'.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice that both 'accuse of' and its legal opposite 'acquit of' take the preposition 'of', while 'convict of' also takes 'of'.

### Q229 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Every journalist is expected to adhere strictly ___ the code of editorial ethics.

A. with
B. to
C. by
D. on

**Answer:** B. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — to (Correct):** 'adhere to' means to stick firmly to or faithfully follow a rule, principle, or code of conduct.
- **A — with:** 'with' pairs with 'comply with' or 'agree with', not with 'adhere'.
- **C — by:** 'by' follows 'abide by' or 'stand by', whereas 'adhere' always takes 'to'.
- **D — on:** 'on' indicates surface or reliance ('rely on') and never collocates with 'adhere'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'adhere to' for following rules or beliefs ('adhere to a schedule'); the related noun 'adherence' also takes 'to'.

### Q230 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Even in moments of severe distress, she could confide ___ only her elder sister.

A. to
B. on
C. with
D. in

**Answer:** D. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — in (Correct):** 'confide in' means to trust someone enough to share private thoughts or secrets with them.
- **A — to:** 'to' follows 'confide' only when a direct object such as 'a secret' comes immediately after the verb ('confide a secret to her'), not when naming the person directly.
- **B — on:** 'on' pairs with 'rely on' or 'depend on', never with 'confide'.
- **C — with:** 'with' follows 'share with' or 'entrust with', whereas 'confide' takes 'in' before a person.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish 'confide in' a trusted person ('confide in a friend') from 'confide something to' someone ('confide a secret to a friend').

### Q231 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Unchecked deforestation will gradually deprive countless tribal communities ___ their traditional livelihood.

A. from
B. with
C. of
D. by

**Answer:** C. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — of (Correct):** 'deprive someone of something' is the fixed pattern meaning to take away or deny someone a right, resource, or necessity.
- **A — from:** 'from' follows 'steal from' or 'snatch from', whereas 'deprive' strictly takes 'of'.
- **B — with:** 'with' expresses accompaniment or instrument and does not collocate with 'deprive'.
- **D — by:** 'by' denotes agent or means and cannot introduce what is taken away after 'deprive'.
- **Exam Extra:** Words meaning 'take away or strip'—'deprive of', 'rob of', and 'strip of'—all follow the pattern 'verb + object + of'.

### Q232 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

The safety inspector insisted ___ conducting a surprise drill before signing the clearance certificate.

A. on
B. to
C. for
D. at

**Answer:** A. on
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — on (Correct):** 'insist on' is the fixed verb + preposition pattern meaning to demand firmly or refuse to give up on a course of action.
- **B — to:** 'to' does not follow 'insist'; unlike 'demand to + verb', 'insist' takes 'on + gerund'.
- **C — for:** 'for' follows 'ask for' or 'call for', never 'insist'.
- **D — at:** 'at' indicates time or target and does not pair with 'insist'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Insist on' takes a noun or '-ing' gerund ('insisted on conducting'), whereas 'insist that' takes a finite clause.

### Q233 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

If the contractor persists ___ violating noise regulations, his licence will be suspended.

A. on
B. in
C. with
D. at

**Answer:** B. in
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — in (Correct):** 'persist in' means to continue stubbornly or steadfastly in an action or belief despite warnings or difficulty.
- **A — on:** 'on' collocates with 'insist on', whereas 'persist' always takes 'in' before a noun or gerund.
- **C — with:** 'with' may occasionally appear with an object like 'persist with a treatment', but standard grammar for continuing an action ('violating') requires 'persist in'.
- **D — at:** 'at' does not collocate with 'persist' in standard English.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast the pair: 'insist on' + V-ing, but 'persist in' + V-ing! Both are frequent SSC cloze-test items.

### Q234 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

The municipality has introduced strict penalties for factories that illegally dispose ___ untreated waste.

A. off
B. away
C. out
D. of

**Answer:** D. of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — of (Correct):** 'dispose of' (with a single 'f') is the fixed phrasal verb meaning to get rid of, discard, or settle something.
- **A — off:** 'off' is a classic spelling and usage trap; 'dispose off' is always incorrect in standard English.
- **B — away:** 'away' pairs with 'throw away' or 'give away', not with 'dispose'.
- **C — out:** 'out' follows 'phase out' or 'carry out', never 'dispose'.
- **Exam Extra:** Never write 'dispose off'; 'of' (single f) belongs to 'dispose of', while 'off' (double f) belongs to 'take off', 'put off', or 'write off'.

### Q235 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

After a prolonged battle with pneumonia, the veteran freedom fighter succumbed ___ the infection.

A. with
B. against
C. to
D. under

**Answer:** C. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — to (Correct):** 'succumb to' means to give in to superior force or pressure, or to die from an illness or injury.
- **A — with:** 'with' does not collocate with 'succumb'; use 'struggle with' for fighting an illness, but 'succumb to' for losing to it.
- **B — against:** 'against' follows 'fight against' or 'vaccinate against', not 'succumb'.
- **D — under:** 'under' follows 'collapse under' or 'buckle under', whereas 'succumb' requires 'to'.
- **Exam Extra:** Note the spelling of 'succumb' (double 'c', silent 'b') and remember it always takes 'to' ('succumb to pressure/illness').

### Q236 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

Even during severe financial hardship, the collector refused to part ___ his grandfather's pocket watch.

A. with
B. from
C. of
D. off

**Answer:** A. with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — with (Correct):** 'part with' means to give up or let go of a material possession or object ('part with his pocket watch').
- **B — from:** 'from' after 'part' is used when separating from a person ('parted from his friends at the station'), not when giving up an object.
- **C — of:** 'of' does not form a phrasal verb with 'part' to mean relinquishing a possession.
- **D — off:** 'off' never collocates with 'part' in standard English.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish 'part with' (give up a thing: 'part with money') from 'part from' (separate from a person: 'part from one's family').

### Q237 · Fixed Verb + Preposition

It took him several months to reconcile himself ___ the reality of his unexpected transfer.

A. with
B. to
C. for
D. against

**Answer:** B. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — to (Correct):** 'reconcile oneself to' means to bring oneself to accept an unwelcome or unavoidable situation ('reconcile himself to the reality').
- **A — with:** 'with' after 'reconcile' is used when two estranged people make peace with each other ('reconciled with his brother'), not when accepting an unwelcome fact.
- **C — for:** 'for' indicates purpose or beneficiary and does not collocate with 'reconcile'.
- **D — against:** 'against' expresses opposition and contradicts the idea of accepting a reality.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast 'reconcile oneself to' an unpleasant reality with 'reconcile with' a person (restore friendly relations).

### Q238 · Fixed Noun & Phrasal Prepositions

Consistent daily revision, rather than last-minute cramming, is the real key ___ clearing competitive exams.

A. of
B. for
C. to
D. in

**Answer:** C. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — to (Correct):** 'key to' is the fixed noun + preposition collocation meaning the crucial factor or method for achieving success ('key to clearing').
- **A — of:** 'of' indicates possession or composition (e.g., 'the key of the locker'), not the figurative secret to achieving a goal.
- **B — for:** 'for' is used with 'remedy for' or 'reason for', whereas figurative 'key' takes 'to'.
- **D — in:** 'in' does not collocate with 'the key' to denote the way to succeed in something.
- **Exam Extra:** Nouns expressing solutions or access—'key to', 'solution to', 'answer to', and 'access to'—all take 'to' (followed by a noun or gerund).

### Q239 · Fixed Noun & Phrasal Prepositions

Economists argued that targeted skill training is the only lasting remedy ___ structural unemployment.

A. of
B. to
C. against
D. for

**Answer:** D. for
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — for (Correct):** 'remedy for' is the standard noun + preposition pairing meaning a cure, treatment, or effective fix for an illness or problem.
- **A — of:** 'of' indicates possession or description, not the problem being solved after 'remedy'.
- **B — to:** 'to' pairs with 'solution to' or 'antidote to', whereas 'remedy' standardly takes 'for'.
- **C — against:** 'against' follows 'protection against' or 'measure against', not 'remedy'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'remedy for' and 'cure for' (both taking 'for') with 'antidote to' and 'solution to' (both taking 'to').

### Q240 · Fixed Noun & Phrasal Prepositions

There is rarely any exception ___ the rule that negligence in the lab invites disciplinary action.

A. to
B. of
C. for
D. from

**Answer:** A. to
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — to (Correct):** 'exception to' is the fixed collocation for a case that does not follow a general rule ('exception to the rule').
- **B — of:** 'of' appears in the phrase 'with the exception of X', but after 'an exception' referring to a rule, the required preposition is 'to'.
- **C — for:** 'for' indicates purpose or exception made for a person ('an exception for her'), not relation to a rule itself.
- **D — from:** 'from' follows 'exemption from', whereas 'exception' takes 'to'.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember 'exception to a rule', 'objection to a plan', and 'with the exception of' (when introducing the excluded item).

---

# Section II: Vocabulary Mastery

## Chapter 4: Synonyms & Antonyms

### Q241 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "credible".

A. incredible
B. believable
C. gullible
D. dubious

**Answer:** B. believable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — believable (Correct):** 'Credible' (adjective, positive/neutral tone) means able to be believed or convincing, which matches 'believable'.
- **A — incredible:** Meaning impossible to believe or extraordinary (synonym: unbelievable; negative/astonished tone), 'incredible' is the direct antonym of 'credible'.
- **C — gullible:** Meaning overly ready to believe things without proof (synonym: naive; negative tone), 'gullible' describes a person who trusts too easily rather than something trustworthy.
- **D — dubious:** Meaning doubtful or suspect (synonym: questionable; negative tone), 'dubious' implies uncertainty rather than trustworthiness.
- **Exam Extra:** From the Latin root 'credere' (to believe), which also gives 'creed', 'credit', and 'incredulous'.

### Q242 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "incessant".

A. intermittent
B. transient
C. tentative
D. continuous

**Answer:** D. continuous
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — continuous (Correct):** 'Incessant' (adjective, neutral/emphatic tone) means continuing without pause or interruption, which matches 'continuous'.
- **A — intermittent:** Meaning occurring at irregular intervals or stopping and starting (synonym: periodic; neutral tone), 'intermittent' is the opposite of 'incessant'.
- **B — transient:** Meaning lasting only for a short time (synonym: fleeting; neutral tone), 'transient' describes brief duration rather than uninterrupted continuation.
- **C — tentative:** Meaning hesitant or not definite (synonym: provisional; neutral tone), 'tentative' refers to uncertainty rather than nonstop action.
- **Exam Extra:** In SSC exams, 'incessant' is frequently paired with 'ceaseless' and 'unremitting' as synonyms and 'sporadic' or 'intermittent' as antonyms.

### Q243 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "assiduous".

A. meticulous
B. nonchalant
C. impulsive
D. perfunctory

**Answer:** A. meticulous
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — meticulous (Correct):** 'Assiduous' (adjective, positive tone) means showing great care, attention, and perseverance, which closely matches 'meticulous'.
- **B — nonchalant:** Meaning careless or lacking concern (synonym: negligent; negative tone), 'nonchalant' suggests casual indifference rather than careful effort.
- **C — impulsive:** Meaning acting suddenly without forethought (synonym: rash; negative tone), 'impulsive' contrasts with patient, thorough work.
- **D — perfunctory:** Meaning brief and lacking thoroughness (synonym: hasty; negative tone), 'perfunctory' describes work done with minimal effort.
- **Exam Extra:** Both 'assiduous' and 'sedulous' share the Latin root 'sedere' (to sit), evoking someone who sits steadily at their work.

### Q244 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "frugal".

A. extravagant
B. avaricious
C. thrifty
D. lavish

**Answer:** C. thrifty
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — thrifty (Correct):** 'Frugal' (adjective, positive tone) means economical with money or resources and avoiding waste, which matches 'thrifty'.
- **A — extravagant:** Meaning spending money recklessly and excessively (synonym: wasteful; negative tone), 'extravagant' is an antonym of 'frugal'.
- **B — avaricious:** Meaning having or showing extreme greed for wealth (synonym: covetous; negative tone), 'avaricious' implies hoarding rather than sensible saving.
- **D — lavish:** Meaning excessively generous or abundant (synonym: profuse; neutral/positive tone), 'lavish' suggests luxury and heavy spending.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the SSC tone distinction: 'frugal' and 'thrifty' are positive (wise economy), whereas 'miserly' and 'stingy' are negative (extreme greed).

### Q245 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "diligent".

A. indolent
B. indifferent
C. negligent
D. industrious

**Answer:** D. industrious
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — industrious (Correct):** 'Diligent' (adjective, positive tone) means showing persistent and careful effort in one's work, which matches 'industrious'.
- **A — indolent:** Meaning habitually lazy or avoiding activity (synonym: slothful; negative tone), 'indolent' is a direct antonym of 'diligent'.
- **B — indifferent:** Meaning unconcerned or lacking interest (synonym: apathetic; negative tone), 'indifferent' conveys a lack of care.
- **C — negligent:** Meaning careless or failing to take proper care (synonym: remiss; negative tone), 'negligent' contrasts with conscientious effort.
- **Exam Extra:** High-frequency SSC synonym cluster for 'diligent': 'industrious', 'assiduous', 'sedulous', and 'painstaking'.

### Q246 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "intricate".

A. complex
B. elementary
C. succinct
D. transparent

**Answer:** A. complex
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — complex (Correct):** 'Intricate' (adjective, neutral tone) means having many interrelated parts or details, which matches 'complex'.
- **B — elementary:** Meaning easily understood or uncomplicated (synonym: plain; neutral tone), 'elementary' is an antonym of 'intricate'.
- **C — succinct:** Meaning brief, clear, and to the point (synonym: terse; positive/neutral tone), 'succinct' describes economy of words rather than complexity.
- **D — transparent:** Meaning clear and easily seen through (synonym: obvious; neutral tone), 'transparent' denotes clarity rather than tangled detail.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Intricate' comes from the Latin 'intricare' (to entangle); its common SSC synonyms include 'convoluted' and 'elaborate'.

### Q247 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "arduous".

A. facile
B. trivial
C. difficult
D. serene

**Answer:** C. difficult
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — difficult (Correct):** 'Arduous' (adjective, neutral/negative tone) means involving strenuous effort and hard to accomplish, which matches 'difficult'.
- **A — facile:** Meaning requiring little effort or skill (synonym: effortless; positive/neutral tone), 'facile' is an antonym of 'arduous'.
- **B — trivial:** Meaning light-hearted, insignificant, or lacking seriousness (synonym: frivolous; negative tone), 'trivial' refers to minor importance rather than difficulty.
- **D — serene:** Meaning calm, peaceful, and undisturbed (synonym: tranquil; positive tone), 'serene' describes stillness rather than struggle.
- **Exam Extra:** From the Latin 'arduus' (steep, high), 'arduous' originally described a steep uphill climb before extending to any tough task.

### Q248 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "hamper".

A. facilitate
B. hinder
C. expedite
D. aid

**Answer:** B. hinder
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — hinder (Correct):** 'Hamper' (verb, negative/neutral tone) means to obstruct or slow down progress, which matches 'hinder'.
- **A — facilitate:** Meaning to make an action or process easier (synonym: assist; positive tone), 'facilitate' is an antonym of 'hamper'.
- **C — expedite:** Meaning to speed up or accelerate a process (synonym: hasten; neutral tone), 'expedite' implies removing obstacles rather than creating them.
- **D — aid:** Meaning to provide help or encouragement (synonym: support; positive tone), 'aid' conveys assistance rather than obstruction.
- **Exam Extra:** As a noun, a 'hamper' is a lidded basket; as a verb in SSC exams, it consistently means 'impede', 'obstruct', or 'hinder'.

### Q249 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "eminent".

A. obscure
B. mediocre
C. distinguished
D. notorious

**Answer:** C. distinguished
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — distinguished (Correct):** 'Eminent' (adjective, positive tone) means famous, respected, and standing above others in a profession or field, which matches 'distinguished'.
- **A — obscure:** Meaning not well known or unclear (synonym: unknown; neutral/negative tone), 'obscure' is the opposite of 'eminent'.
- **B — mediocre:** Meaning ordinary and lacking any distinctive features (synonym: average; neutral/negative tone), 'mediocre' implies unremarkable quality.
- **D — notorious:** Meaning well known for a bad quality or deed (synonym: disreputable; negative tone), 'notorious' carries negative fame rather than respect.
- **Exam Extra:** Do not confuse 'eminent' (famous, respected), 'imminent' (about to happen soon), and 'immanent' (inherent or present throughout) — a classic SSC homophone trio.

### Q250 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "prudent".

A. rash
B. wise
C. inane
D. audacious

**Answer:** B. wise
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — wise (Correct):** 'Prudent' (adjective, positive tone) means acting with care, foresight, and sound judgement, which matches 'wise'.
- **A — rash:** Meaning acting hastily without considering consequences (synonym: reckless; negative tone), 'rash' is an antonym of 'prudent'.
- **C — inane:** Meaning foolishly senseless or absurd (synonym: silly; negative tone), 'inane' denotes emptiness of thought rather than good judgement.
- **D — audacious:** Meaning overly bold or lacking respect (synonym: impudent; negative tone), 'audacious' suggests daring rather than caution.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Prudent' comes from Latin 'providens' (foreseeing), linking foresight and wisdom; its common antonym is 'imprudent' or 'reckless'.

### Q251 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "daunt".

A. encourage
B. console
C. inspire
D. intimidate

**Answer:** D. intimidate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — intimidate (Correct):** 'Daunt' (verb, negative/neutral tone) means to make someone feel afraid or discouraged, which matches 'intimidate'.
- **A — encourage:** Meaning to give support, confidence, or hope (synonym: hearten; positive tone), 'encourage' is the direct antonym of 'daunt'.
- **B — console:** Meaning to calm or soothe someone's distress (synonym: pacify; positive tone), 'console' relieves fear instead of causing it.
- **C — inspire:** Meaning to rouse to action or excitement (synonym: motivate; positive tone), 'inspire' uplifts rather than frightens.
- **Exam Extra:** The adjective 'undaunted' (meaning fearless or not discouraged) is built directly from 'daunt' + 'un-'.

### Q252 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "lucid".

A. clear
B. ambiguous
C. muddled
D. arcane

**Answer:** A. clear
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — clear (Correct):** 'Lucid' (adjective, positive tone) means expressed clearly and easy to understand, which matches 'clear'.
- **B — ambiguous:** Meaning uncertain or open to more than one interpretation (synonym: vague; negative/neutral tone), 'ambiguous' is an antonym of 'lucid'.
- **C — muddled:** Meaning confusing, complicated, or hard to follow (synonym: murky; negative tone), 'muddled' denotes disorder rather than clarity.
- **D — arcane:** Meaning hidden or hard to understand (synonym: cryptic; neutral/negative tone), 'arcane' refers to secret or obscure knowledge.
- **Exam Extra:** Derived from the Latin 'lux' (light); related words include 'elucidate' (to clarify) and 'translucent' (allowing light through).

### Q253 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "genuine".

A. authentic
B. counterfeit
C. spurious
D. feigned

**Answer:** A. authentic
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — authentic (Correct):** 'Genuine' (adjective, positive tone) means truly what something is said to be, real, and not fake, which matches 'authentic'.
- **B — counterfeit:** Meaning made in exact imitation of something valuable to deceive (synonym: fake; negative tone), 'counterfeit' is an antonym of 'genuine'.
- **C — spurious:** Meaning not being what it purports to be (synonym: bogus; negative tone), 'spurious' denotes falsehood rather than realness.
- **D — feigned:** Meaning false or simulated (synonym: artificial; negative tone), 'feigned' describes pretended emotions or qualities.
- **Exam Extra:** SSC often tests 'genuine' alongside 'bona fide' and 'veritable' as synonyms, and 'counterfeit' or 'spurious' as antonyms.

### Q254 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "plausible".

A. absurd
B. incredible
C. reasonable
D. preposterous

**Answer:** C. reasonable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — reasonable (Correct):** 'Plausible' (adjective, positive/neutral tone) means seeming reasonable, believable, or probable, which matches 'reasonable'.
- **A — absurd:** Meaning wildly unreasonable or contrary to common sense (synonym: ridiculous; negative tone), 'absurd' is an antonym of 'plausible'.
- **B — incredible:** Meaning impossible to believe or accept (synonym: implausible; negative tone), 'incredible' expresses disbelief rather than likelihood.
- **D — preposterous:** Meaning bizarre, strange, or extremely unrealistic (synonym: outlandish; negative tone), 'preposterous' indicates complete absurdity.
- **Exam Extra:** Note the nuance: 'plausible' means seemingly valid or believable on the surface, though not yet proven with certainty.

### Q255 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "ephemeral".

A. perpetual
B. temporary
C. enduring
D. indelible

**Answer:** B. temporary
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — temporary (Correct):** 'Ephemeral' (adjective, neutral tone) means lasting for a very short time, which matches 'temporary'.
- **A — perpetual:** Meaning lasting forever or without end (synonym: eternal; neutral/positive tone), 'perpetual' is an antonym of 'ephemeral'.
- **C — enduring:** Meaning continuing for a long time or constantly recurring (synonym: chronic; neutral tone), 'enduring' denotes longevity rather than brevity.
- **D — indelible:** Meaning impossible to erase or forget (synonym: permanent; positive/neutral tone), 'indelible' implies lasting impact.
- **Exam Extra:** From Greek 'ephemeros' (lasting only a day); top SSC synonyms are 'transient', 'fleeting', 'evanescent', and 'momentary'.

### Q256 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "lethargic".

A. vivacious
B. energetic
C. alert
D. sluggish

**Answer:** D. sluggish
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — sluggish (Correct):** 'Lethargic' (adjective, negative tone) means affected by sluggishness, apathy, or lack of energy, which matches 'sluggish'.
- **A — vivacious:** Meaning full of energy, liveliness, and spirit (synonym: lively; positive tone), 'vivacious' is an antonym of 'lethargic'.
- **B — energetic:** Meaning quick, energetic, and active (synonym: brisk; positive tone), 'energetic' describes high vitality rather than tiredness.
- **C — alert:** Meaning quick to notice and respond (synonym: attentive; positive tone), 'alert' contrasts with drowsy sluggishness.
- **Exam Extra:** Rooted in Greek 'lethe' (forgetfulness/drowsiness); 'torpid' and 'languid' are its close formal synonyms in SSC CGL.

### Q257 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "immense".

A. diminutive
B. enormous
C. minute
D. negligible

**Answer:** B. enormous
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — enormous (Correct):** 'Immense' (adjective, neutral/emphatic tone) means extremely large or great in scale, which matches 'enormous'.
- **A — diminutive:** Meaning very small or tiny in size (synonym: miniature; neutral tone), 'diminutive' is an antonym of 'immense'.
- **C — minute:** Meaning so small as to be barely noticeable (synonym: microscopic; neutral tone), 'minute' denotes tiny scale rather than vastness.
- **D — negligible:** Meaning insignificant or barely worth mentioning (synonym: paltry; negative tone), 'negligible' emphasizes smallness.
- **Exam Extra:** Literally from Latin 'immensus' (immeasurable); common SSC synonyms include 'colossal', 'gargantuan', and 'mammoth'.

### Q258 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "valiant".

A. brave
B. timid
C. pusillanimous
D. trepidatious

**Answer:** A. brave
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — brave (Correct):** 'Valiant' (adjective, positive tone) means possessing or showing courage and determination, which matches 'brave'.
- **B — timid:** Meaning lacking courage and easily frightened (synonym: craven; negative tone), 'timid' is an antonym of 'valiant'.
- **C — pusillanimous:** Meaning excessively fearful or unwilling to take risks (synonym: cowardly; negative tone), 'pusillanimous' denotes faint-heartedness.
- **D — trepidatious:** Meaning full of fear or apprehension (synonym: anxious; negative tone), 'trepidatious' expresses nervousness rather than boldness.
- **Exam Extra:** From Latin 'valere' (to be strong), the same root behind 'valour', 'valid', and 'valiant'.

### Q259 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "exemplary".

A. deplorable
B. inferior
C. reprehensible
D. commendable

**Answer:** D. commendable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — commendable (Correct):** 'Exemplary' (adjective, positive tone) means serving as a desirable model and worthy of praise, which matches 'commendable'.
- **A — deplorable:** Meaning deserving strong disapproval or condemnation (synonym: shameful; negative tone), 'deplorable' is an antonym of 'exemplary'.
- **B — inferior:** Meaning lower in rank, status, or quality (synonym: substandard; negative tone), 'inferior' falls short of a model standard.
- **C — reprehensible:** Meaning deserving blame or censure (synonym: blameworthy; negative tone), 'reprehensible' indicates misconduct rather than excellence.
- **Exam Extra:** Be careful: 'exemplary' can also mean serving as a warning (as in 'exemplary punishment'), though SSC almost always tests its primary meaning 'model/commendable'.

### Q260 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "reluctant".

A. eager
B. enthusiastic
C. unwilling
D. willing

**Answer:** C. unwilling
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — unwilling (Correct):** 'Reluctant' (adjective, negative/neutral tone) means hesitant and disinclined to do something, which matches 'unwilling'.
- **A — eager:** Meaning wanting to do or have something very much (synonym: keen; positive tone), 'eager' is an antonym of 'reluctant'.
- **B — enthusiastic:** Meaning showing intense and eager enjoyment (synonym: ardent; positive tone), 'enthusiastic' reflects eagerness rather than hesitation.
- **D — willing:** Meaning ready and happy to act without hesitation (synonym: amenable; positive tone), 'willing' is the direct opposite of 'unwilling'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Reluctant' pairs with 'loath' and 'averse' in SSC synonym questions; its prepositional usage in grammar is usually followed by an infinitive ('reluctant to go').

### Q261 · Synonyms

Select the most appropriate synonym of "abate".

A. aggravate
B. escalate
C. augment
D. lessen

**Answer:** D. lessen
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — lessen (Correct):** 'Abate' (verb, neutral tone) means to become less intense or widespread, which matches 'lessen'.
- **A — aggravate:** Meaning to make a problem or bad feeling worse (synonym: worsen; negative tone), 'aggravate' is an antonym of 'abate'.
- **B — escalate:** Meaning to increase in force, degree, or strength (synonym: heighten; neutral tone), 'escalate' means to grow stronger rather than subside.
