D. compliant

**Answer:** D. compliant
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — compliant (Correct):** 'Obdurate' (adjective, negative tone) means stubbornly refusing to change one's opinion or course of action; its opposite is 'compliant' (inclined to agree with others or obey rules).
- **A — stubborn:** Meaning having or showing dogged determination not to change one's attitude (synonym: headstrong; negative tone), 'stubborn' is a synonym of 'obdurate'.
- **B — obstinate:** Meaning stubbornly refusing to change one's opinion (synonym: inflexible; negative tone), 'obstinate' reinforces rigid refusal.
- **C — unbending:** Meaning unyielding and impossible to persuade (synonym: rigid; negative/emphatic tone), 'unbending' resists compliance.
- **Exam Extra:** From Latin 'ob-' (against) + 'durare' (to harden); frequent SSC synonyms include 'stubborn', 'callous', and 'unyielding'.

### Q349 · Antonyms

Select the most appropriate antonym of "precarious".

A. perilous
B. hazardous
C. secure
D. uncertain

**Answer:** C. secure
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — secure (Correct):** 'Precarious' (adjective, negative tone) means not securely held or in position and dangerously likely to fall or collapse; its opposite is 'secure' (fixed or fastened so as not to give way, become loose, or be lost).
- **A — perilous:** Meaning not reliable and dangerously unstable (synonym: risky; negative tone), 'perilous' is a synonym of 'precarious'.
- **B — hazardous:** Meaning full of danger or risk (synonym: unsafe; negative tone), 'hazardous' conveys instability and risk.
- **D — uncertain:** Meaning not known or reliable and liable to change (synonym: shaky; negative tone), 'uncertain' reflects precariousness rather than security.
- **Exam Extra:** Originally meant 'obtained by begging/entreaty' (Latin 'precarius'), hence dependent on someone else's will and uncertain; antonyms: 'secure', 'stable'.

### Q350 · Antonyms

Select the most appropriate antonym of "quell".

A. suppress
B. subdue
C. pacify
D. incite

**Answer:** D. incite
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — incite (Correct):** 'Quell' (verb, neutral/emphatic tone) means to put an end to a rebellion or other disorder, typically by force, or to suppress a feeling; its opposite is 'incite' (encourage or stir up violent or unlawful behaviour).
- **A — suppress:** Meaning to forcibly put an end to (synonym: crush; neutral/negative tone), 'suppress' is a synonym of 'quell'.
- **B — subdue:** Meaning to overcome, quieten, or bring under control (synonym: conquer; neutral tone), 'subdue' aligns with suppressing disorder.
- **C — pacify:** Meaning to bring peace to or calm anger and agitation (synonym: appease; positive/neutral tone), 'pacify' ends unrest rather than sparking it.
- **Exam Extra:** In SSC exams, 'quell' is commonly paired with 'suppress/subdue/pacify' (synonyms) and 'incite/instigate/foment' (antonyms).

---

## Chapter 5: Idioms & Phrases

### Q351 · Everyday Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of the idiom "keep an eye on".

A. deliberately ignore a fault
B. attract someone's attention
C. judge something at first glance
D. watch carefully and attentively

**Answer:** D. watch carefully and attentively
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — watch carefully and attentively (Correct):** "Keep an eye on" means to watch someone or something carefully to ensure safety, accuracy, or proper behaviour.
- **A — deliberately ignore a fault:** Closing one's eyes to something describes "turn a blind eye", which means deliberately ignoring a fault rather than monitoring it.
- **B — attract someone's attention:** Attracting someone's attention is expressed by "catch someone's eye", not "keep an eye on".
- **C — judge something at first glance:** Evaluating something casually at first sight relates to "at first glance" rather than continuous careful observation.
- **Exam Extra:** Related idiom: "keep a sharp lookout" means staying alert for danger or changes; contrast with "turn a blind eye", which means ignoring wrongdoing.

### Q352 · Everyday Idioms

In the sentence "Rohan skipped practice today because he was feeling a bit under the weather," what does "under the weather" mean?

A. caught in a sudden rainstorm
B. feeling slightly ill or unwell
C. facing severe financial loss
D. working outdoors in harsh conditions

**Answer:** B. feeling slightly ill or unwell
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — feeling slightly ill or unwell (Correct):** "Under the weather" means feeling slightly ill, unwell, or low in energy.
- **A — caught in a sudden rainstorm:** Being caught in a sudden downpour is a literal weather trap; the idiom refers to personal health rather than actual rain.
- **C — facing severe financial loss:** Facing severe financial loss is expressed by "in the red" or "on the rocks", not "under the weather".
- **D — working outdoors in harsh conditions:** Working outdoors in harsh conditions is a literal misreading of "weather", whereas the phrase figuratively describes mild sickness.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Sailors used to go below deck ("under the weather rail") when seasick or unwell to shelter from rough weather.

### Q353 · Animal & Nature Idioms

What does the idiom "hold your horses" mean?

A. wait and be patient before acting
B. control one's anger during a debate
C. refuse to change an established opinion
D. protect valuable property from theft

**Answer:** A. wait and be patient before acting
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — wait and be patient before acting (Correct):** "Hold your horses" is an idiomatic command meaning wait, slow down, and be patient before acting.
- **B — control one's anger during a debate:** Controlling anger in a dispute corresponds to "hold one's temper" or "keep one's cool", whereas "hold your horses" specifically urges patience against rushing.
- **C — refuse to change an established opinion:** Refusing to change an opinion describes "stick to one's guns", not pausing to wait patiently.
- **D — protect valuable property from theft:** Taking care of expensive possessions is a literal trap tied to owning horses rather than the figurative call to slow down.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Comes from 19th-century horse-drawn carriages and riding, where pulling back the reins kept horses from bolting prematurely.

### Q354 · Work & Effort Idioms

Select the correct meaning of the idiom "call it a day".

A. celebrate a memorable occasion
B. postpone a plan until next week
C. stop working for the day
D. start an assignment early in the morning

**Answer:** C. stop working for the day
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — stop working for the day (Correct):** "Call it a day" means to stop working on a task for the rest of the day, usually after accomplishing enough or feeling tired.
- **A — celebrate a memorable occasion:** Celebrating a major milestone or happy occasion is denoted by "red-letter day", not "call it a day".
- **B — postpone a plan until next week:** Postponing a meeting to the following week corresponds to "put off", while "call it a day" means concluding today's work.
- **D — start an assignment early in the morning:** Beginning an urgent assignment early in the morning is the opposite of winding up work for the day.
- **Exam Extra:** Related expression: "call it quits" means ending an activity, partnership, or effort permanently, whereas "call it a day" is temporary until the next day.

### Q355 · Work & Effort Idioms

What does the idiom "hit the nail on the head" mean?

A. describe the exact cause or point accurately
B. injure oneself while doing manual work
C. react angrily to minor criticism
D. finish a difficult task ahead of schedule

**Answer:** A. describe the exact cause or point accurately
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — describe the exact cause or point accurately (Correct):** "Hit the nail on the head" means to describe or identify the exact cause of a situation or say something completely accurate.
- **B — injure oneself while doing manual work:** Harming oneself while working carelessly is a literal carpentry trap; the idiom praises precision in speech or judgement.
- **C — react angrily to minor criticism:** Reacting angrily to minor criticism describes "fly off the handle", not making an exact diagnosis.
- **D — finish a difficult task ahead of schedule:** Completing a difficult job ahead of schedule refers to efficiency rather than pinpointing the exact truth.
- **Exam Extra:** SSC tip: Frequently paired in exams with "miss the mark", which means failing to reach the right conclusion.

### Q356 · Everyday Idioms

Select the meaning of the idiom "piece of cake".

A. a small share of a larger profit
B. a brief moment of comfort
C. an unexpected reward for hard work
D. something very easy to accomplish

**Answer:** D. something very easy to accomplish
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — something very easy to accomplish (Correct):** "A piece of cake" refers to a task or activity that is effortless and very easy to accomplish.
- **A — a small share of a larger profit:** A small share of a larger profit corresponds to "a slice of the pie", not "a piece of cake".
- **B — a brief moment of comfort:** A brief period of comfort describes a temporary respite, whereas "piece of cake" denotes extreme ease.
- **C — an unexpected reward for hard work:** An unexpected reward for hard work is unrelated to ease of execution; the idiom never implies difficulty or reward.
- **Exam Extra:** Synonym idioms often tested in SSC CGL/CHSL: "a walk in the park" and "child's play" carry the same meaning.

### Q357 · Everyday Idioms

In the sentence "The host played a short quiz to break the ice among the guests," what does "break the ice" mean?

A. end a long-standing friendship
B. cause sudden panic in a meeting
C. start a friendly conversation to ease tension
D. reject a proposal without discussion

**Answer:** C. start a friendly conversation to ease tension
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — start a friendly conversation to ease tension (Correct):** "Break the ice" means to say or do something to relieve awkwardness and begin a friendly conversation among strangers.
- **A — end a long-standing friendship:** Ending a long-standing friendship permanently corresponds to "burn one's bridges", the opposite of initiating friendly contact.
- **B — cause sudden panic in a meeting:** Causing sudden panic in a calm meeting describes creating tension rather than easing social awkwardness.
- **D — reject a proposal without discussion:** Rejecting a proposal without discussion is expressed by "turn down cold" or "shoot down", not "break the ice".
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Derived from icebreaker ships clearing frozen waterways so other vessels could pass smoothly.

### Q358 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "get cold feet" mean?

A. fall ill from walking in winter air
B. become nervous and hesitant before an action
C. walk away calmly after finishing a job
D. gradually lose interest in a hobby

**Answer:** B. become nervous and hesitant before an action
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — become nervous and hesitant before an action (Correct):** "Get cold feet" means to become suddenly nervous, anxious, or fearful just before undertaking an important action.
- **A — fall ill from walking in winter air:** Falling ill due to exposure to winter air is a literal temperature trap; the phrase describes psychological hesitation.
- **C — walk away calmly after finishing a job:** Walking away calmly after completing a job expresses composure, not pre-action anxiety.
- **D — gradually lose interest in a hobby:** Losing interest in a hobby over time refers to waning enthusiasm rather than last-minute fear before an event.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast: "keep a cool head" means staying calm under pressure, whereas "get cold feet" means losing nerve before acting.

### Q359 · Work & Effort Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of the idiom "back to square one".

A. moving rapidly to the final stage
B. returning to the starting point after a setback
C. visiting one's childhood home again
D. winning a contest on the first attempt

**Answer:** B. returning to the starting point after a setback
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — returning to the starting point after a setback (Correct):** "Back to square one" means having to return to the very beginning of a project or process because previous efforts failed.
- **A — moving rapidly to the final stage:** Moving rapidly to the final stage describes swift progress ("by leaps and bounds"), the reverse of restarting from zero.
- **C — visiting one's childhood home again:** Returning to a familiar childhood neighbourhood is a literal place interpretation, not a setback in a task.
- **D — winning a contest on the first attempt:** Winning a contest on the very first attempt is unrelated to restarting after a failed plan.
- **Exam Extra:** Etymology: Often linked to early board games or BBC radio football commentary grids where "square one" marked the starting zone.

### Q360 · Everyday Idioms

What does the idiom "beat around the bush" mean?

A. search thoroughly for lost property
B. criticise someone harshly in secret
C. avoid speaking directly about the main point
D. make loud noise to frighten animals away

**Answer:** C. avoid speaking directly about the main point
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — avoid speaking directly about the main point (Correct):** "Beat around the bush" means to avoid speaking directly about the main issue, often using vague or indirect remarks.
- **A — search thoroughly for lost property:** Searching thoroughly for lost property describes "leave no stone unturned" or physical searching, not evasive speech.
- **B — criticise someone harshly in secret:** Criticising someone harshly behind their back corresponds to "backbite", whereas "beat around the bush" refers to avoiding a topic while speaking.
- **D — make loud noise to frighten animals away:** Celebrating noisily in a garden is a literal misunderstanding of "beat" and "bush".
- **Exam Extra:** Antonym idiom: "come straight to the point" or "call a spade a spade" means speaking plainly and directly.

### Q361 · Everyday Idioms

In the sentence "Her coding skills improved by leaps and bounds after joining the bootcamp," what does "by leaps and bounds" mean?

A. slowly and with great caution
B. irregularly and without a plan
C. beyond legal limits and rules
D. very rapidly and remarkably

**Answer:** D. very rapidly and remarkably
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — very rapidly and remarkably (Correct):** "By leaps and bounds" means progressing or growing very rapidly and noticeably.
- **A — slowly and with great caution:** Moving cautiously step by step describes "at a snail's pace" or slow, measured advancement, the opposite of rapid growth.
- **B — irregularly and without a plan:** Acting unpredictably without a plan describes erratic behaviour ("by fits and starts"), not steady rapid progress.
- **C — beyond legal limits and rules:** Exceeding legal limits is a literal trap on "bounds"; the idiom refers to speed and scale of progress.
- **Exam Extra:** SSC usage tip: Almost always appears with verbs of growth or improvement such as "progress", "grow", "increase", or "improve".

### Q362 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

What does the idiom "a blessing in disguise" mean?

A. an apparent misfortune that results in something good
B. a hidden danger disguised as something harmless
C. an unexpected gift received on a birthday
D. a generous act carried out in secret

**Answer:** A. an apparent misfortune that results in something good
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — an apparent misfortune that results in something good (Correct):** "A blessing in disguise" is an apparent misfortune or difficulty that later turns out to produce something beneficial.
- **B — a hidden danger disguised as something harmless:** A hidden danger pretending to be harmless describes "a wolf in sheep's clothing", the opposite of a misfortune that proves helpful.
- **C — an unexpected gift received on a birthday:** An unexpected gift received on a special day lacks the element of an initial setback or hardship.
- **D — a generous act carried out in secret:** A generous act done secretly for charity is anonymous benevolence rather than an unwelcome event yielding good results.
- **Exam Extra:** Exam pair: Contrast with "a wolf in sheep's clothing", which is the exact reverse—something dangerous disguised as harmless.

### Q363 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "the last straw".

A. the best remaining opportunity in a crisis
B. a weak excuse offered to escape blame
C. the final problem that makes a situation unbearable
D. the smallest contribution toward a charity drive

**Answer:** C. the final problem that makes a situation unbearable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — the final problem that makes a situation unbearable (Correct):** "The last straw" refers to the final minor problem in a series of troubles that makes a situation completely unbearable.
- **A — the best remaining opportunity in a crisis:** The single best opportunity left corresponds to "one's last shot", not an accumulation of annoyances.
- **B — a weak excuse offered to escape blame:** A fragile excuse offered to avoid blame describes "a lame excuse" or "clutching at straws", whereas "the last straw" breaks patience.
- **D — the smallest contribution toward a charity drive:** The oldest member of a committee has no connection to the tipping point of tolerance.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Shortened from the proverb "It is the last straw that breaks the camel's back."

### Q364 · Everyday Idioms

What does the idiom "spill the beans" mean?

A. reveal a secret prematurely
B. waste food through carelessness
C. spoil a celebration by arriving late
D. make a minor error in financial accounts

**Answer:** A. reveal a secret prematurely
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — reveal a secret prematurely (Correct):** "Spill the beans" means to reveal secret or confidential information, often accidentally or prematurely.
- **B — waste food through carelessness:** Wasting food due to carelessness is a literal kitchen trap rather than disclosing a secret.
- **C — spoil a celebration by arriving late:** Spoiling a celebration by arriving late refers to bad manners or timing, not divulging hidden facts.
- **D — make a minor error in financial accounts:** Making a small mistake in accounts relates to carelessness with numbers, whereas the idiom strictly concerns secrets.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Linked to an ancient Greek voting custom where coloured beans were dropped into a jar; spilling the jar revealed the vote count early.

### Q365 · Work & Effort Idioms

In the sentence "Aspirants often burn the midnight oil in the weeks before SSC CGL," what does "burn the midnight oil" mean?

A. waste fuel through poor planning
B. work or study late into the night
C. destroy old documents to hide evidence
D. wake up before sunrise to exercise

**Answer:** B. work or study late into the night
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — work or study late into the night (Correct):** "Burn the midnight oil" means to stay up and work or study late into the night.
- **A — waste fuel through poor planning:** Wasting fuel carelessly through poor planning is a literal interpretation of burning oil.
- **C — destroy old documents to hide evidence:** Destroying old records to hide evidence corresponds to a cover-up rather than diligent late-night effort.
- **D — wake up before sunrise to exercise:** Waking up before sunrise to exercise describes "rising with the lark", not working late at night.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Dates back to when people used oil lamps for light to read or work after dark.

### Q366 · Color & Number Idioms

Which phrase best explains the idiom "once in a blue moon"?

A. regularly every month
B. only during a solar eclipse
C. at the beginning of every season
D. very rarely or almost never

**Answer:** D. very rarely or almost never
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — very rarely or almost never (Correct):** "Once in a blue moon" means something happens very rarely or almost never.
- **A — regularly every month:** Happening regularly every month describes a routine event, the exact opposite of rarity.
- **B — only during a solar eclipse:** Occurring only during a solar eclipse is overly literal and ties the idiom to astronomy instead of general infrequency.
- **C — at the beginning of every season:** Taking place at the start of every season describes periodic regularity rather than extreme rarity.
- **Exam Extra:** Astronomy link: A "blue moon" usually refers to the second full moon in a single calendar month, which occurs only once every two to three years.

### Q367 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "cost an arm and a leg" mean?

A. be extremely expensive
B. require dangerous physical labour
C. be sold at a heavy discount
D. take many months to repair

**Answer:** A. be extremely expensive
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — be extremely expensive (Correct):** "Cost an arm and a leg" means to be extremely expensive or require a very high price.
- **B — require dangerous physical labour:** Requiring dangerous physical labour relates to risk rather than monetary price.
- **C — be sold at a heavy discount:** Being sold at a heavy seasonal discount is the exact opposite of exorbitant cost.
- **D — take many months to repair:** Taking several months to repair deals with time delay rather than high financial expense.
- **Exam Extra:** Related idiom: "pay through the nose" also implies paying an excessively high price, often unfairly.

### Q368 · Work & Effort Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "cut corners".

A. redesign a building to look modern
B. do something cheaply or carelessly by skipping steps
C. reduce unnecessary personal luxuries wisely
D. choose a longer scenic route while travelling

**Answer:** B. do something cheaply or carelessly by skipping steps
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — do something cheaply or carelessly by skipping steps (Correct):** "Cut corners" means to do something cheaply, hastily, or carelessly by skipping important steps to save time or money.
- **A — redesign a building to look modern:** Redesigning a room to look modern is a literal carpentry/interior trap unrelated to skimping on standards.
- **C — reduce unnecessary personal luxuries wisely:** Trimming excess expenses wisely describes prudent budgeting ("tighten one's belt"), whereas "cut corners" implies sacrificing quality or rules.
- **D — choose a longer scenic route while travelling:** Taking a scenic route on a journey means extending travel time, the reverse of skipping steps to finish fast.
- **Exam Extra:** Exam note: Unlike "take a shortcut" (which can simply mean a faster route), "cut corners" usually carries a negative sense of lowering quality or safety.

### Q369 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

In the sentence "The clerk landed in hot water after misplacing the confidential file," what does "in hot water" mean?

A. relaxing comfortably after hard work
B. debating politics with great passion
C. working in a hot and humid kitchen
D. in serious trouble or difficulty

**Answer:** D. in serious trouble or difficulty
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — in serious trouble or difficulty (Correct):** "In hot water" means to be in serious trouble, difficulty, or disfavour with someone in authority.
- **A — relaxing comfortably after hard work:** Relaxing in a warm bath after work is a literal comfort trap, the opposite of facing trouble.
- **B — debating politics with great passion:** Arguing passionately about politics describes a heated debate ("hot under the collar"), not being in trouble.
- **C — working in a hot and humid kitchen:** Working in a humid kitchen environment misreads the phrase literally as physical heat.
- **Exam Extra:** Related idiom: "in a fix" or "in a tight spot" also describes being in a difficult predicament.

### Q370 · Color & Number Idioms

What does the idiom "on cloud nine" mean?

A. daydreaming and out of touch with reality
B. travelling frequently by aeroplane
C. extremely happy and elated
D. feeling dizzy at a high altitude

**Answer:** C. extremely happy and elated
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — extremely happy and elated (Correct):** "On cloud nine" means feeling extremely happy, delighted, or elated.
- **A — daydreaming and out of touch with reality:** Daydreaming without paying attention describes "head in the clouds", a common SSC distractor trap!
- **B — travelling frequently by aeroplane:** Travelling frequently by air is a literal reading of "cloud", not a state of euphoria.
- **D — feeling dizzy at a high altitude:** Feeling dizzy at a high altitude confuses emotional elation with physical vertigo.
- **Exam Extra:** Synonym idioms: "over the moon" and "in seventh heaven" express the same state of extreme joy.

### Q371 · Everyday Idioms

Select the correct meaning of "through thick and thin".

A. while walking through a dense forest
B. by changing opinions repeatedly
C. through both good and bad times
D. by comparing things of different sizes

**Answer:** C. through both good and bad times
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — through both good and bad times (Correct):** "Through thick and thin" means remaining loyal or steadfast through both good times and bad times, under all circumstances.
- **A — while walking through a dense forest:** Navigating through a dense forest is a literal misreading of "thick" (thicket) and "thin".
- **B — by changing opinions repeatedly:** Changing one's mind repeatedly describes "blow hot and cold", the opposite of steadfast loyalty.
- **D — by comparing things of different sizes:** Comparing two objects of different sizes confuses a figurative pledge of loyalty with physical measurement.
- **Exam Extra:** Grammar note: Often follows verbs like "support", "stand by", or "stay together".

### Q372 · Everyday Idioms

What does the idiom "make ends meet" mean?

A. join two broken ends of a wire together
B. help two quarrelling friends reconcile
C. complete two separate tasks at the same time
D. manage basic expenses within one's income

**Answer:** D. manage basic expenses within one's income
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — manage basic expenses within one's income (Correct):** "Make ends meet" means to earn just enough money to cover one's basic living expenses.
- **A — join two broken ends of a wire together:** Connecting two pieces of torn wire is a literal mechanical reading of "ends".
- **B — help two quarrelling friends reconcile:** Reconciling two quarrelling friends corresponds to "patch up" or "bury the hatchet", not managing finances.
- **C — complete two separate tasks at the same time:** Finishing two projects at the same hour concerns scheduling rather than living within one's income.
- **Exam Extra:** Exam tip: Frequently appears with verbs like "struggle to", "barely", or "manage to" in SSC fill-in-the-blank and idiom questions.

### Q373 · Everyday Idioms

Which meaning accurately matches the idiom "miss the boat"?

A. arrive early at a busy harbour
B. lose an opportunity by acting too late
C. decline a risky venture out of caution
D. forget to pack luggage before a trip

**Answer:** B. lose an opportunity by acting too late
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — lose an opportunity by acting too late (Correct):** "Miss the boat" means to lose a valuable opportunity by failing to act quickly enough.
- **A — arrive early at a busy harbour:** Arriving early at a harbour is a literal travel detail and the opposite of being too late.
- **C — decline a risky venture out of caution:** Refusing to take an unnecessary risk describes cautious restraint, whereas "miss the boat" implies regret over lost timing.
- **D — forget to pack luggage before a trip:** Forgetting to pack essentials for a trip relates to absent-mindedness rather than losing an opportunity.
- **Exam Extra:** Synonym idiom: "miss the bus" carries the same meaning in British and Indian English.

### Q374 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

Which idiom means "make an already bad situation worse"? Wait—no, let's check the question stem: What does the idiom "add fuel to the fire" mean?

A. make an already bad situation worse
B. calm an angry crowd with kind words
C. invest extra funds in a growing business
D. prepare a meal quickly over high heat

**Answer:** A. make an already bad situation worse
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — make an already bad situation worse (Correct):** "Add fuel to the fire" means to say or do something that makes an already bad conflict or situation even worse.
- **B — calm an angry crowd with kind words:** Calming an angry crowd quickly describes "pour oil on troubled waters", the exact opposite of inflaming a conflict.
- **C — invest extra funds in a growing business:** Providing extra funds to a growing startup relates to helpful investment rather than worsening a problem.
- **D — prepare a meal quickly over high heat:** Cooking dinner quickly on high heat is a literal stove trap with no figurative connection.
- **Exam Extra:** Synonym idiom: "fan the flames" has the exact same figurative meaning.

### Q375 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of the idiom "a storm in a teacup".

A. a sudden disaster affecting many people
B. great excitement or fuss over a trivial matter
C. a quiet morning ruined by spilled tea
D. a secret plan discussed over refreshments

**Answer:** B. great excitement or fuss over a trivial matter
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — great excitement or fuss over a trivial matter (Correct):** "A storm in a teacup" refers to great anger, panic, or excitement over a small, trivial matter.
- **A — a sudden disaster affecting many people:** A sudden disaster affecting an entire nation describes a real crisis ("a bolt from the blue" or catastrophe), not a trivial fuss.
- **C — a quiet morning ruined by spilled tea:** A quiet morning ruined by spoiled food is a literal beverage reading of "teacup".
- **D — a secret plan discussed over refreshments:** A secret plan discussed at a cafe refers to covert plotting rather than exaggerated outrage over something minor.
- **Exam Extra:** US equivalent: "a tempest in a teapot"; SSC exams almost always use the British form "a storm in a teacup".

### Q376 · Animal & Nature Idioms

In the sentence "The grand stadium became a white elephant for the small town," what does "a white elephant" mean?

A. a costly possession that is useless or burdensome to maintain
B. a rare achievement that brings great pride to its owner
C. a loyal companion who helps during financial hardship
D. a harmless prank played among office colleagues

**Answer:** A. a costly possession that is useless or burdensome to maintain
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — a costly possession that is useless or burdensome to maintain (Correct):** "A white elephant" describes a costly, burdensome possession that is of little practical use or profit to its owner.
- **B — a rare achievement that brings great pride to its owner:** A rare achievement that brings great pride corresponds to "a feather in one's cap", not an expensive burden.
- **C — a loyal companion who helps during financial hardship:** A loyal friend who helps in hardship describes a true ally, unrelated to costly upkeep.
- **D — a harmless prank played among office colleagues:** A harmless prank played on colleagues is a lighthearted joke rather than an impractical liability.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Kings of Siam (Thailand) allegedly gifted rare sacred white elephants to courtiers they wished to ruin financially, as the animals were expensive to feed and could not be put to work.

### Q377 · Animal & Nature Idioms

What does the idiom "a dark horse" mean?

A. a dishonest person pretending to be noble
B. a stubborn opponent who refuses to yield
C. an unexpected winner with hidden abilities
D. a timid competitor who withdraws early

**Answer:** C. an unexpected winner with hidden abilities
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — an unexpected winner with hidden abilities (Correct):** "A dark horse" is an unexpected winner or a little-known competitor who surprises everyone by succeeding or revealing hidden talent.
- **A — a dishonest person pretending to be noble:** A dishonest person pretending to be noble describes "a wolf in sheep's clothing", whereas a dark horse simply has underestimated ability.
- **B — a stubborn opponent who refuses to yield:** A stubborn opponent who refuses to compromise refers to obstinacy rather than surprising success.
- **D — a timid competitor who withdraws early:** A fearful rider who avoids dangerous contests describes timidity, the opposite of an unexpected victor.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Horse-racing slang for a horse whose true speed was kept secret from bettors until the race.

### Q378 · Everyday Idioms

Select the correct meaning of "a feather in one's cap".

A. a foolish error that invites public ridicule
B. a showy decoration worn to impress others
C. a minor duty assigned to a new trainee
D. an achievement to be proud of

**Answer:** D. an achievement to be proud of
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — an achievement to be proud of (Correct):** "A feather in one's cap" signifies a notable achievement or honour of which someone can be proud.
- **A — a foolish error that invites public ridicule:** A foolish mistake that invites ridicule corresponds to "a blot on one's escutcheon" or embarrassment, the reverse of an honour.
- **B — a showy decoration worn to impress others:** An unnecessary ornament worn for show is a literal clothing reading that misses the pride of accomplishment.
- **C — a minor duty assigned to a new trainee:** A minor task assigned to a beginner refers to trivial duty rather than a distinguished success.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Historically, warriors in several cultures added a feather to their headgear for each notable feat or victory.

### Q379 · Animal & Nature Idioms

What does the idiom "kill two birds with one stone" mean?

A. harm innocent creatures without reason
B. spend double effort on a simple task
C. abandon one plan to pursue another
D. achieve two aims with a single effort

**Answer:** D. achieve two aims with a single effort
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — achieve two aims with a single effort (Correct):** "Kill two birds with one stone" means accomplishing two different aims with a single effort or action.
- **A — harm innocent creatures without reason:** Harming innocent creatures deliberately is a literal reading that misses the figurative sense of efficiency.
- **B — spend double effort on a simple task:** Wasting double effort on a simple job is the exact opposite of achieving two goals at once.
- **C — abandon one plan to pursue another:** Abandoning one plan to chase another describes inconsistency rather than dual success.
- **Exam Extra:** SSC tip: Remember this expresses efficiency (2 goals, 1 effort), unlike "between two stools", which means failing between two choices.

### Q380 · Animal & Nature Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "barking up the wrong tree".

A. shouting loudly to frighten away rivals
B. complaining endlessly about minor rules
C. pursuing a mistaken line of thought or accusation
D. climbing carefully to get a better view

**Answer:** C. pursuing a mistaken line of thought or accusation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — pursuing a mistaken line of thought or accusation (Correct):** "Barking up the wrong tree" means pursuing a mistaken line of thought, accusation, or effort toward the wrong target.
- **A — shouting loudly to frighten away rivals:** Shouting loudly to intimidate rivals describes bluster ("barking worse than one's bite"), not misdirected effort.
- **B — complaining endlessly about minor rules:** Complaining endlessly about minor rules refers to constant grumbling rather than making a false assumption.
- **D — climbing carefully to get a better view:** Climbing cautiously to inspect a view is a literal woodland image with no connection to misdirected blame.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Hunting dogs sometimes barked at the base of a tree after the raccoon or prey had already leaped to a neighbouring tree.

### Q381 · Animal & Nature Idioms

What does the idiom "a snake in the grass" mean?

A. a treacherous person pretending to be a friend
B. a shy stranger who avoids all company
C. a strict official who enforces minor rules
D. a coward who flees at the first danger

**Answer:** A. a treacherous person pretending to be a friend
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — a treacherous person pretending to be a friend (Correct):** "A snake in the grass" refers to a treacherous, deceitful person who pretends to be a friend while secretly plotting harm.
- **B — a shy stranger who avoids all company:** A shy stranger who avoids social gatherings describes an introvert, not a deceitful betrayer.
- **C — a strict official who enforces minor rules:** A strict supervisor who enforces every rule refers to rigidity rather than covert treachery.
- **D — a coward who flees at the first danger:** A coward who runs away at the first sign of danger corresponds to "showing the white feather", not hidden malice.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Coined by the Roman poet Virgil ("Latet anguis in herba" — a snake lurks in the grass) to warn of hidden danger.

### Q382 · Animal & Nature Idioms

Select the correct meaning of the idiom "crocodile tears".

A. sudden terror triggered by a dangerous creature
B. insincere or hypocritical display of sorrow
C. deep regret felt long after a mistake
D. tears of joy upon reuniting with friends

**Answer:** B. insincere or hypocritical display of sorrow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — insincere or hypocritical display of sorrow (Correct):** "Crocodile tears" means an insincere, pretended, or hypocritical display of grief or sympathy.
- **A — sudden terror triggered by a dangerous creature:** Sudden panic caused by a wild animal is a literal fear trap rather than fake sorrow.
- **C — deep regret felt long after a mistake:** Bitter regret felt years after a mistake describes genuine remorse, whereas crocodile tears are fake.
- **D — tears of joy upon reuniting with friends:** Joyful crying upon meeting an old friend represents sincere happiness, not hypocrisy.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Medieval folklore claimed crocodiles wept false tears while devouring their prey to lure or mock victims.

### Q383 · Animal & Nature Idioms

What does the idiom "cry wolf" mean?

A. raise a false alarm repeatedly
B. confront a fierce enemy without fear
C. request help politely during a shortage
D. hunt wild animals after sunset

**Answer:** A. raise a false alarm repeatedly
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — raise a false alarm repeatedly (Correct):** "Cry wolf" means to raise a false alarm repeatedly, causing others to stop believing you when real danger occurs.
- **B — confront a fierce enemy without fear:** Facing a fierce enemy bravely corresponds to "take the bull by the horns", not sounding fake warnings.
- **C — request help politely during a shortage:** Asking politely for help during a shortage refers to sincere appeal rather than false alarms.
- **D — hunt wild animals after sunset:** Hunting dangerous animals at night is a literal wilderness trap.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: From Aesop's fable "The Boy Who Cried Wolf", a classic SSC favourite.

### Q384 · Animal & Nature Idioms

In the sentence "The senior partner claimed the lion's share of the annual bonus," what does "the lion's share" mean?

A. the smallest leftover portion of a meal
B. an exactly equal division among partners
C. the largest or best portion of something
D. a risky duty assigned only to leaders

**Answer:** C. the largest or best portion of something
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — the largest or best portion of something (Correct):** "The lion's share" means the largest or best portion of something divided among people.
- **A — the smallest leftover portion of a meal:** The smallest leftover piece corresponds to "scraps" or a pittance, the opposite of the largest share.
- **B — an exactly equal division among partners:** An equal division among all partners describes fair sharing, whereas the lion's share is disproportionately huge.
- **D — a risky duty assigned only to leaders:** A dangerous task reserved for leaders relates to "bell the cat" rather than taking the biggest portion.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Another Aesop's fable reference—a lion hunts with other animals and claims almost all the prey for himself.

### Q385 · Animal & Nature Idioms

What does the idiom "bell the cat" mean?

A. train a domestic pet to follow commands
B. take the lead in a dangerous or difficult task
C. shift responsibility onto someone else
D. spread harmless rumours among neighbours

**Answer:** B. take the lead in a dangerous or difficult task
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — take the lead in a dangerous or difficult task (Correct):** "Bell the cat" means to take the lead in performing a dangerous or risky task for the benefit of a group.
- **A — train a domestic pet to follow commands:** Training a pet to obey simple commands is a literal domestic trap with no sense of peril.
- **C — shift responsibility onto someone else:** Avoiding responsibility by blaming others corresponds to "pass the buck", the opposite of stepping up to face danger.
- **D — spread harmless rumours among neighbours:** Spreading harmless gossip among neighbours relates to idle chatter rather than courageous leadership.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: From the fable where mice agree a bell should be tied around a cat's neck, but none dares to actually do it.

### Q386 · Animal & Nature Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "a wild goose chase".

A. a carefully planned and profitable expedition
B. a thrilling race won by a surprise outsider
C. a peaceful stroll through open countryside
D. a foolish and futile search for something unattainable

**Answer:** D. a foolish and futile search for something unattainable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — a foolish and futile search for something unattainable (Correct):** "A wild goose chase" means a foolish, futile, or hopeless search for something unattainable.
- **A — a carefully planned and profitable expedition:** A well-planned expedition that yields treasure describes a fruitful quest, the exact opposite of a futile search.
- **B — a thrilling race won by a surprise outsider:** A fast race won by an outsider corresponds to "a dark horse" victory, not a pointless pursuit.
- **C — a peaceful stroll through open countryside:** An enjoyable weekend walk in nature misses the frustration and futility implied by the idiom.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Originally a 16th-century horse race in which riders had to follow a leader erratically, resembling wild geese in flight.

### Q387 · Animal & Nature Idioms

What does the idiom "rain cats and dogs" mean?

A. drizzle lightly throughout the afternoon
B. rain very heavily in torrents
C. shelter stray animals during a storm
D. quarrel noisily inside a small house

**Answer:** B. rain very heavily in torrents
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — rain very heavily in torrents (Correct):** "Rain cats and dogs" means to rain extremely heavily or pour down in torrents.
- **A — drizzle lightly throughout the afternoon:** Drizzling lightly throughout a cool evening describes gentle rain, the opposite of a heavy downpour.
- **C — shelter stray animals during a storm:** Stray animals sheltering on porches during storms is a literal reading of "cats and dogs".
- **D — quarrel noisily inside a small house:** Arguing noisily indoors while stuck inside corresponds to "fighting like cats and dogs", a completely different idiom!
- **Exam Extra:** Grammar watch: In SSC error-spotting, never write "raining like cats and dogs"—the idiom takes no "like"!

### Q388 · Animal & Nature Idioms

In the sentence "Traffic moved at a snail's pace because of the waterlogged road," what does "at a snail's pace" mean?

A. quietly to avoid being noticed
B. erratically with sudden turns
C. halting work completely due to fatigue
D. very slowly

**Answer:** D. very slowly
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — very slowly (Correct):** "At a snail's pace" means moving or progressing extremely slowly.
- **A — quietly to avoid being noticed:** Moving silently to avoid detection describes stealth rather than extreme slowness.
- **B — erratically with sudden turns:** Changing direction unpredictably at every turn refers to erratic movement, not slow steady pace.
- **C — halting work completely due to fatigue:** Stopping work completely due to fatigue corresponds to "call it a day" or halting, not slow ongoing progress.
- **Exam Extra:** Antonym idiom: "by leaps and bounds" means progressing very rapidly.

### Q389 · Animal & Nature Idioms

Which idiom means "confront a difficult situation boldly and directly"? Wait—let's keep stem uniform: What does the idiom "take the bull by the horns" mean?

A. escape from trouble until it subsides
B. wait passively for others to intervene
C. confront a difficult situation boldly and directly
D. compromise quietly to keep everyone pleased

**Answer:** C. confront a difficult situation boldly and directly
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — confront a difficult situation boldly and directly (Correct):** "Take the bull by the horns" means to confront a difficult or dangerous problem boldly and directly.
- **A — escape from trouble until it subsides:** Running away from trouble until it passes describes cowardice or avoidance, the opposite of bold confrontation.
- **B — wait passively for others to intervene:** Waiting patiently for someone else to intervene describes passivity rather than decisive action.
- **D — compromise quietly to keep everyone pleased:** Compromising secretly to please everyone relates to appeasement, not facing a challenge head-on.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast: "beat around the bush" means avoiding a tough point indirectly; "take the bull by the horns" means tackling it head-on.

### Q390 · Animal & Nature Idioms

Select the correct meaning of the idiom "smell a rat".

A. suspect trickery or foul play
B. notice a foul smell in a damp room
C. trust a stranger's promise without question
D. panic over an imaginary threat

**Answer:** A. suspect trickery or foul play
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — suspect trickery or foul play (Correct):** "Smell a rat" means to suspect that something is wrong, dishonest, or deceitful.
- **B — notice a foul smell in a damp room:** Detecting a foul odour in an old room is a literal olfactory trap rather than suspecting fraud.
- **C — trust a stranger's promise without question:** Trusting a stranger's story immediately is the exact opposite of becoming suspicious.
- **D — panic over an imaginary threat:** Panicking over an imaginary danger corresponds to excessive fear rather than sharp suspicion of foul play.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Dogs used for rat-catching sniffed out hidden pests before anyone could see them, giving rise to the sense of detecting hidden trickery.

### Q391 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "turn a deaf ear" mean?

A. mishear words due to loud background noise
B. listen sympathetically to someone's troubles
C. refuse to listen to complaints or advice
D. speak loudly to silence everyone else

**Answer:** C. refuse to listen to complaints or advice
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — refuse to listen to complaints or advice (Correct):** "Turn a deaf ear" means to deliberately refuse to listen to advice, complaints, or requests.
- **A — mishear words due to loud background noise:** Mishearing instructions because of background noise implies accidental difficulty, whereas turning a deaf ear is intentional refusal.
- **B — listen sympathetically to someone's troubles:** Listening sympathetically to a friend's troubles corresponds to "lend an ear", the exact opposite.
- **D — speak loudly to silence everyone else:** Speaking loudly to dominate a conversation describes overbearing speech rather than ignoring appeals.
- **Exam Extra:** Exam pair: "turn a deaf ear" = refuse to listen; "turn a blind eye" = refuse to notice wrongdoing. Both appear frequently in SSC CHSL!

### Q392 · Body & Action Idioms

In the sentence "The warden chose to turn a blind eye to the late arrivals," what does "turn a blind eye" mean?

A. deliberately ignore wrongdoing or a rule violation
B. watch every single movement carefully
C. miss an error accidentally due to haste
D. recover lost vision after treatment

**Answer:** A. deliberately ignore wrongdoing or a rule violation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — deliberately ignore wrongdoing or a rule violation (Correct):** "Turn a blind eye" means to intentionally ignore wrongful behaviour or rules being broken, pretending not to notice.
- **B — watch every single movement carefully:** Observing every detail carefully corresponds to "keep an eye on", the opposite of ignoring wrongdoing.
- **C — miss an error accidentally due to haste:** Failing to spot an error because of haste implies accidental oversight, whereas "turn a blind eye" is deliberate.
- **D — recover lost vision after treatment:** Recovering eyesight after medical treatment is a literal medical trap.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Famously linked to Admiral Horatio Nelson holding a telescope to his blind eye at the Battle of Copenhagen (1801) to ignore a signal to withdraw.

### Q393 · Body & Action Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "bite the bullet".

A. retreat quietly to avoid discomfort
B. speak harshly to hurt someone's feelings
C. make a hasty choice without thinking
D. endure an unpleasant or painful situation bravely

**Answer:** D. endure an unpleasant or painful situation bravely
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — endure an unpleasant or painful situation bravely (Correct):** "Bite the bullet" means to force oneself to endure a painful, unpleasant, or unavoidable situation with courage.
- **A — retreat quietly to avoid discomfort:** Retreating quietly to avoid discomfort describes backing out ("get cold feet"), the opposite of enduring bravely.
- **B — speak harshly to hurt someone's feelings:** Speaking harshly to hurt someone's feelings corresponds to "bite someone's head off", not enduring hardship.
- **C — make a hasty choice without thinking:** Making a rash decision without thinking relates to impulsiveness ("on the spur of the moment") rather than stoic acceptance.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Before modern anaesthetics, wounded soldiers reportedly bit on a lead bullet to cope with pain during battlefield surgery.

### Q394 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "bite off more than one can chew" mean?

A. eat too quickly at a formal dinner
B. take on a task too big to handle
C. refuse all extra duties out of laziness
D. finish a complex project effortlessly

**Answer:** B. take on a task too big to handle
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — take on a task too big to handle (Correct):** "Bite off more than one can chew" means to take on a commitment or task that is far too large or difficult to handle.
- **A — eat too quickly at a formal dinner:** Eating greedily at a banquet is a literal dining trap unrelated to overcommitting at work.
- **C — refuse all extra duties out of laziness:** Refusing every extra duty out of laziness describes shirking responsibility, the opposite of overreaching.
- **D — finish a complex project effortlessly:** Completing a complex project effortlessly corresponds to finding it a "piece of cake", not being overwhelmed.
- **Exam Extra:** Related idiom: "spread oneself too thin" describes taking on too many tasks simultaneously.

### Q395 · Body & Action Idioms

Select the correct meaning of the idiom "see eye to eye".

A. stare angrily at a rival
B. meet someone face to face after years
C. inspect fine measurements for accuracy
D. agree completely with someone

**Answer:** D. agree completely with someone
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — agree completely with someone (Correct):** "See eye to eye" means to hold the exact same opinion or agree completely with someone.
- **A — stare angrily at a rival:** Staring angrily at a rival corresponds to "lock horns" or hostility, not agreement.
- **B — meet someone face to face after years:** Meeting someone face to face after years is a literal reading of eye contact rather than shared opinion.
- **C — inspect fine measurements for accuracy:** Checking measurements for accuracy relates to visual inspection, not mutual agreement.
- **Exam Extra:** Grammar note: Very often used in the negative ("They don't see eye to eye on budget cuts") in SSC sentence-improvement questions.

### Q396 · Body & Action Idioms

In the sentence "Don't take him seriously; he's only pulling your leg," what does "pulling your leg" mean?

A. help someone climb a difficult slope
B. tease or joke with someone playfully
C. sabotage a colleague's promotion secretly
D. trip someone accidentally in a hallway

**Answer:** B. tease or joke with someone playfully
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — tease or joke with someone playfully (Correct):** "Pull someone's leg" means to tease, joke with, or playfully deceive someone in a lighthearted way.
- **A — help someone climb a difficult slope:** Helping someone climb a steep path describes physical assistance rather than humorous teasing.
- **C — sabotage a colleague's promotion secretly:** Deliberately hindering a rival's promotion corresponds to "put a spoke in someone's wheel", which is malicious rather than joking.
- **D — trip someone accidentally in a hallway:** Tripping someone in a crowded hallway is a literal physical prank rather than verbal teasing.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinction: Unlike "stab someone in the back" (malicious betrayal), "pull someone's leg" is always playful and harmless.

### Q397 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "keep one's fingers crossed" mean?

A. hope for good luck and a favourable outcome
B. break a solemn promise secretly
C. refuse to lend money to relatives
D. practise a manual skill repeatedly

**Answer:** A. hope for good luck and a favourable outcome
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — hope for good luck and a favourable outcome (Correct):** "Keep one's fingers crossed" means to hope strongly for good luck or a successful outcome.
- **B — break a solemn promise secretly:** Breaking a solemn promise secretly corresponds to crossing fingers behind one's back to invalidate an oath—a distinct cultural nuance, not the standard idiom for hoping for luck.
- **C — refuse to lend money to relatives:** Refusing to lend money to a relative describes stinginess ("tight-fisted"), not wishing for success.
- **D — practise a manual skill repeatedly:** Practising typing skills repeatedly is a literal hand exercise unrelated to hopeful anticipation.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Believed to trace back to early symbolic gestures meant to invoke protection or ward off bad luck before an uncertain result.

### Q398 · Body & Action Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "put one's foot down".

A. step off a moving vehicle hastily
B. yield easily to unreasonable demands
C. take a firm stand and refuse strictly
D. say something embarrassing by mistake

**Answer:** C. take a firm stand and refuse strictly
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — take a firm stand and refuse strictly (Correct):** "Put one's foot down" means to use one's authority firmly to stop something or insist strictly on a decision.
- **A — step off a moving vehicle hastily:** Stepping off a train hastily is a literal motion trap.
- **B — yield easily to unreasonable demands:** Yielding easily to unreasonable demands describes "giving in" or weakness, the exact opposite of a firm stand.
- **D — say something embarrassing by mistake:** Saying something embarrassing by accident corresponds to "put one's foot in one's mouth", a frequent SSC confusion trap!
- **Exam Extra:** Usage note: Contrast with "put one's foot in one's mouth", which means saying something tactless or embarrassing!

### Q399 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "lend an ear" mean?

A. listen attentively and sympathetically
B. spread private gossip to neighbours
C. refuse to hear any explanation
D. borrow money urgently from a friend

**Answer:** A. listen attentively and sympathetically
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — listen attentively and sympathetically (Correct):** "Lend an ear" means to listen attentively and sympathetically to what someone has to say.
- **B — spread private gossip to neighbours:** Spreading confidential gossip describes "spill the beans" or loose talk, not attentive listening.
- **C — refuse to hear any explanation:** Refusing to hear an explanation corresponds to "turn a deaf ear", the exact opposite.
- **D — borrow money urgently from a friend:** Borrowing a small sum urgently relates to money ("pass the hat"), not offering attention.
- **Exam Extra:** Literary echo: Shakespeare's Mark Antony says "Friends, Romans, countrymen, lend me your ears" to ask the crowd for attentive listening.

### Q400 · Body & Action Idioms

In the sentence "Overcome with guilt, the suspect decided to make a clean breast of the theft," what does "make a clean breast of" mean?

A. hide incriminating evidence before an audit
B. blame a junior colleague for one's error
C. prepare a simple healthy meal at home
D. confess openly and honestly about a fault

**Answer:** D. confess openly and honestly about a fault
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — confess openly and honestly about a fault (Correct):** "Make a clean breast of" means to confess openly and honestly about a fault or secret wrongdoing.
- **A — hide incriminating evidence before an audit:** Hiding evidence before an audit is the exact opposite of full confession.
- **B — blame a junior colleague for one's error:** Blaming a junior colleague for one's error corresponds to "pass the buck", not owning up honestly.
- **C — prepare a simple healthy meal at home:** Preparing a healthy meal at home is a literal culinary misreading of "breast".
- **Exam Extra:** Exam tip: In SSC idioms, "clean" often signals honesty or a fresh start (compare "clean slate" and "come clean").

### Q401 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "head over heels" most commonly mean?

A. walking upright with excessive pride
B. deeply and completely in love
C. exhausted after running a long race
D. undecided about which career to pick

**Answer:** B. deeply and completely in love
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — deeply and completely in love (Correct):** "Head over heels" means completely and deeply in love (or occasionally utterly overwhelmed/upset depending on context, though SSC predominantly tests "deeply in love").
- **A — walking upright with excessive pride:** Walking upright with great pride describes haughtiness ("high and mighty"), not deep affection.
- **C — exhausted after running a long race:** Exhausted after running a long marathon is a literal physical reading of head and heels.
- **D — undecided about which career to pick:** Confused about which career to pick corresponds to "at sea" or "at a loss", not intense infatuation.
- **Exam Extra:** Word history: Originally "heels over head" (meaning upside down/somersaulting), which inverted over time into "head over heels".

### Q402 · Body & Action Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "keep at arm's length".

A. embrace a close friend warmly
B. measure wooden furniture accurately
C. avoid becoming too friendly or intimate with someone
D. protect a child closely from danger

**Answer:** C. avoid becoming too friendly or intimate with someone
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — avoid becoming too friendly or intimate with someone (Correct):** "Keep someone at arm's length" means to avoid becoming too friendly, intimate, or involved with someone.
- **A — embrace a close friend warmly:** Embracing a friend warmly upon arrival describes intimacy, the exact opposite of maintaining distance.
- **B — measure wooden furniture accurately:** Measuring furniture accurately before buying is a literal measurement trap.
- **D — protect a child closely from danger:** Protecting a child closely from danger implies nearness and shielding, not emotional or social detachment.
- **Exam Extra:** Legal/business use: An "arm's length transaction" is one in which buyer and seller act independently without personal favouritism.

### Q403 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

What does the idiom "face the music" mean?

A. enjoy a live concert on the weekend
B. escape quietly before being questioned
C. accept the unpleasant consequences of one's actions
D. praise a talented performer enthusiastically

**Answer:** C. accept the unpleasant consequences of one's actions
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — accept the unpleasant consequences of one's actions (Correct):** "Face the music" means to accept and deal with the unpleasant consequences, criticism, or punishment for one's actions.
- **A — enjoy a live concert on the weekend:** Enjoying a live concert on the weekend is a literal entertainment trap.
- **B — escape quietly before being questioned:** Running away before being questioned corresponds to absconding or shirking accountability, the opposite of facing consequences.
- **D — praise a talented performer enthusiastically:** Praising a performer enthusiastically describes admiration rather than enduring criticism.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Often linked to disgraced officers being drummed out of a military regiment, or stage actors stepping forward to face the orchestra pit before a tough audience.

### Q404 · Work & Effort Idioms

In the sentence "Be careful of his advice; he clearly has an axe to grind," what does "have an axe to grind" mean?

A. own valuable tools for carpentry work
B. have a selfish motive or personal grievance
C. help neighbours selflessly without reward
D. work tirelessly to sharpen professional skills

**Answer:** B. have a selfish motive or personal grievance
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — have a selfish motive or personal grievance (Correct):** "Have an axe to grind" means to have a selfish ulterior motive, personal grievance, or private agenda behind one's actions or words.
- **A — own valuable tools for carpentry work:** Possessing valuable tools for farming is a literal hardware reading with no figurative sense.
- **C — help neighbours selflessly without reward:** Helping neighbours selflessly without reward describes altruism ("a labour of love"), the exact opposite of a selfish motive.
- **D — work tirelessly to sharpen professional skills:** Working tirelessly to sharpen professional skills refers to dedicated self-improvement rather than a hidden agenda.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Attributed to a story by Benjamin Franklin (or Charles Miner) about a man who flattered a boy to trick him into turning a grindstone to sharpen his axe.

### Q405 · Color & Number Idioms

What does the idiom "caught red-handed" mean?

A. caught in the very act of committing a wrongdoing
B. blushing deeply out of shyness in public
C. accused falsely without any solid evidence
D. injured slightly while painting a wooden fence

**Answer:** A. caught in the very act of committing a wrongdoing
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — caught in the very act of committing a wrongdoing (Correct):** "Caught red-handed" means to be apprehended in the very act of committing a crime or wrongdoing.
- **B — blushing deeply out of shyness in public:** Blushing deeply out of shyness relates to embarrassment ("red in the face"), not being caught mid-crime.
- **C — accused falsely without any solid evidence:** Suspected falsely without any proof describes an unfounded accusation, the opposite of unmistakable guilt in the act.
- **D — injured slightly while painting a wooden fence:** Injured while painting a fence is a literal colour trap on "red".
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Originated in old Scottish law, referring to a poacher or murderer caught with literal blood still on their hands.

### Q406 · Color & Number Idioms

Select the correct meaning of "in the pink of health".

A. recovering slowly after a severe fever
B. dressed in bright festive clothing
C. feeling embarrassed after a public mistake
D. in excellent physical health and fitness

**Answer:** D. in excellent physical health and fitness
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — in excellent physical health and fitness (Correct):** "In the pink of health" means to be in an excellent, vigorous state of physical health and fitness.
- **A — recovering slowly after a severe fever:** Recovering slowly after a long fever describes convalescence ("on the mend"), not peak fitness.
- **B — dressed in bright festive clothing:** Wearing bright clothes for a festival is a literal colour trap on "pink".
- **C — feeling embarrassed after a public mistake:** Feeling embarrassed after a mistake corresponds to turning red in the face, not robust health.
- **Exam Extra:** Antonym idiom: "under the weather" (slightly ill) or "off colour" (unwell).

### Q407 · Color & Number Idioms

In the sentence "The manager insisted on having the terms in black and white," what does "in black and white" mean?

A. photographed using an old monochrome camera
B. in writing or printed clearly as proof
C. dressed formally for an official ceremony
D. agreed upon verbally without any witnesses

**Answer:** B. in writing or printed clearly as proof
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — in writing or printed clearly as proof (Correct):** "In black and white" means written or printed down officially, leaving clear proof and no room for ambiguity.
- **A — photographed using an old monochrome camera:** Photographed using an old camera is a literal photography reading of monochrome colours.
- **C — dressed formally for an official ceremony:** Dressed formally for a court hearing refers to attire rather than written documentation.
- **D — agreed upon verbally without any witnesses:** Agreed upon privately without witnesses describes a verbal/informal pact, the opposite of a written record.
- **Exam Extra:** Exam note: Don't confuse with "see things in black and white", which means viewing issues overly simplistically as all good or all bad.

### Q408 · Color & Number Idioms

What does the idiom "a bolt from the blue" mean?

A. a minor delay that everyone expected
B. a weather warning issued days in advance
C. a sudden and completely unexpected shock
D. a rusted metal latch on an old door

**Answer:** C. a sudden and completely unexpected shock
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — a sudden and completely unexpected shock (Correct):** "A bolt from the blue" means a sudden, completely unexpected shock or surprising piece of news.
- **A — a minor delay that everyone expected:** A minor delay that everyone predicted is expected and gradual, the opposite of a sudden shock.
- **B — a weather warning issued days in advance:** A steady storm warning issued in advance gives prior notice, unlike a bolt from the blue.
- **D — a rusted metal latch on an old door:** An old lock that jams in winter is a literal mechanical reading of "bolt".
- **Exam Extra:** Image: Like a lightning bolt striking unexpectedly out of a clear blue sky with no storm clouds in sight.

### Q409 · Color & Number Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "green with envy".

A. extremely jealous of someone else's good fortune
B. inexperienced at handling a new job
C. deeply committed to protecting forests
D. feeling seasick during a rough voyage

**Answer:** A. extremely jealous of someone else's good fortune
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — extremely jealous of someone else's good fortune (Correct):** "Green with envy" means feeling intensely jealous of another person's success, possessions, or good fortune.
- **B — inexperienced at handling a new job:** Inexperienced at a new job corresponds to "greenhorn" or simply "green", not jealousy.
- **C — deeply committed to protecting forests:** Concerned deeply about forest conservation relates to "going green", an environmental term rather than envy.
- **D — feeling seasick during a rough voyage:** Nauseous while sailing at sea describes turning green from seasickness, a physical symptom rather than envy.
- **Exam Extra:** Literary origin: Shakespeare popularized green as the colour of jealousy in Othello ("the green-eyed monster").

### Q410 · Color & Number Idioms

What does the idiom "at sixes and sevens" mean?

A. arranged neatly in numerical order
B. working early morning shifts regularly
C. in complete agreement after a brief talk
D. in a state of confusion and disorder

**Answer:** D. in a state of confusion and disorder
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — in a state of confusion and disorder (Correct):** "At sixes and sevens" means in a state of complete confusion, disorder, or disagreement.
- **A — arranged neatly in numerical order:** Arranged neatly in ascending numerical order is the exact opposite of total disorder.
- **B — working early morning shifts regularly:** Working shift hours early in the morning is a literal clock-time trap (6 or 7 o'clock).
- **C — in complete agreement after a brief talk:** Unanimous after a brief discussion describes harmony, whereas "at sixes and sevens" denotes confusion or disunity.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Thought to stem from an old dice game ("cinque and sice") where risking high numbers meant leaving things in disarray.

### Q411 · Color & Number Idioms

In the sentence "The rescue team arrived at the eleventh hour to save the stranded hikers," what does "at the eleventh hour" mean?

A. well ahead of the scheduled deadline
B. precisely at eleven o'clock in the morning
C. after the submission window has closed
D. at the very last moment before a deadline

**Answer:** D. at the very last moment before a deadline
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — at the very last moment before a deadline (Correct):** "At the eleventh hour" means at the very last possible moment just before a deadline or crucial event.
- **A — well ahead of the scheduled deadline:** Long before the scheduled deadline corresponds to "ahead of time", the opposite of last-minute action.
- **B — precisely at eleven o'clock in the morning:** Precisely at eleven in the morning is a literal clock trap; the idiom works regardless of the actual time of day.
- **C — after the submission window has closed:** After the deadline has already expired describes being too late ("miss the boat"), whereas the eleventh hour is just in time.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Biblical parable of the vineyard workers hired at the eleventh hour of a twelve-hour workday, just before dusk.

### Q412 · Everyday Idioms

What does the idiom "on the spur of the moment" mean?

A. impulsively, without prior planning
B. after months of careful preparation
C. only when forced by strict regulations
D. with painful hesitation and self-doubt

**Answer:** A. impulsively, without prior planning
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — impulsively, without prior planning (Correct):** "On the spur of the moment" means acting suddenly and impulsively, without advance planning or thought.
- **B — after months of careful preparation:** After months of careful preparation describes deliberate planning, the opposite of spontaneous action.
- **C — only when forced by strict regulations:** Only when forced by strict rules implies external compulsion rather than sudden impulse.
- **D — with painful hesitation and self-doubt:** With painful hesitation and doubt corresponds to "get cold feet", not quick spontaneous action.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: From riding horses—a rider using spurs urges the horse to sprint suddenly right at that instant.

### Q413 · Color & Number Idioms

Select the correct meaning of "in the red" in business contexts.

A. earning record quarterly profits
B. furious over an unfair decision
C. owing money or operating at a financial loss
D. caught committing an unlawful act

**Answer:** C. owing money or operating at a financial loss
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — owing money or operating at a financial loss (Correct):** "In the red" means owing money, having an overdrawn account, or operating at a financial loss.
- **A — earning record quarterly profits:** Earning record quarterly profits corresponds to "in the black", the exact opposite of being in debt.
- **B — furious over an unfair decision:** Furious over an unfair decision describes "seeing red", an anger idiom rather than a financial one.
- **D — caught committing an unlawful act:** Caught committing an unlawful act corresponds to "caught red-handed", not operating at a loss.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Bookkeepers traditionally recorded financial losses and debts in red ink and profits in black ink ("in the black").

### Q414 · Color & Number Idioms

What does the idiom "a red-letter day" mean?

A. a day of severe financial losses
B. a very important, happy, or memorable day
C. an ordinary day of dull routine work
D. a deadline for sending urgent letters

**Answer:** B. a very important, happy, or memorable day
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — a very important, happy, or memorable day (Correct):** "A red-letter day" is a remarkably happy, significant, or memorable day.
- **A — a day of severe financial losses:** A day marked by severe budget cuts corresponds to a dark or gloomy period, not a joyful milestone.
- **C — an ordinary day of dull routine work:** An ordinary weekday spent on routine chores is the opposite of a standout memorable date.
- **D — a deadline for sending urgent letters:** A deadline when urgent letters must be mailed is a literal postal trap on "letter".
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Medieval church calendars printed major feast days and saints' holidays in red ink to stand out from ordinary black-ink days.

### Q415 · Work & Effort Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "leave no stone unturned".

A. make every possible effort to achieve something
B. give up after facing the first obstacle
C. collect rare minerals from rocky hills
D. keep old quarrels alive indefinitely

**Answer:** A. make every possible effort to achieve something
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — make every possible effort to achieve something (Correct):** "Leave no stone unturned" means to try every conceivable method and make every possible effort to find something or achieve a goal.
- **B — give up after facing the first obstacle:** Giving up after the first minor obstacle corresponds to "throw in the towel", the opposite of exhaustive effort.
- **C — collect rare minerals from rocky hills:** Collecting rare minerals as a hobby is a literal geology reading of "stone".
- **D — keep old quarrels alive indefinitely:** Keeping old quarrels alive forever describes holding a grudge, not thorough effort.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Linked to an ancient Greek oracle telling Polycrates to "turn every stone" at a battlefield site to uncover hidden Persian treasure.

### Q416 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

In the sentence "Resigning rudely will only burn your bridges with the industry," what does "burn your bridges" mean?

A. mend broken ties with former colleagues
B. destroy all chances of returning to a previous state
C. retire peacefully to a remote island
D. postpone a difficult career decision

**Answer:** B. destroy all chances of returning to a previous state
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — destroy all chances of returning to a previous state (Correct):** "Burn one's bridges" means to take an irreversible action that destroys any possibility of returning to a former relationship, job, or position.
- **A — mend broken ties with former colleagues:** Repairing damaged ties with former rivals describes building bridges ("mend fences"), the exact opposite.
- **C — retire peacefully to a remote island:** Travelling to a remote island to retire is a literal travel reading of bridges.
- **D — postpone a difficult career decision:** Postponing a difficult career move implies keeping options open rather than cutting off retreat.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Similar to "burn one's boats"—military commanders occasionally destroyed their own crossings so soldiers had no option to retreat.

### Q417 · Everyday Idioms

What does the idiom "read between the lines" mean?

A. skim a lengthy document hurriedly
B. proofread an article for spelling errors
C. understand the hidden or implied meaning
D. read a speech aloud to an audience

**Answer:** C. understand the hidden or implied meaning
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — understand the hidden or implied meaning (Correct):** "Read between the lines" means to infer or understand an unstated, hidden, or implied meaning beyond the literal words.
- **A — skim a lengthy document hurriedly:** Skimming a long book hurriedly to finish early describes "skimming" rather than uncovering hidden intent.
- **B — proofread an article for spelling errors:** Checking every spelling mistake carefully refers to proofreading, not reading implied meaning.
- **D — read a speech aloud to an audience:** Reading aloud slowly to a large audience describes public recitation rather than subtle interpretation.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Derived from cryptography and invisible ink, where secret messages were written in the blank spaces between visible lines of text.

### Q418 · Work & Effort Idioms

Which idiom means "admit defeat and give up"? Wait—let's keep the direct stem: What does the idiom "throw in the towel" mean?

A. prepare energetically for a tough contest
B. complain about unfair rules after losing
C. tidy up carefully after physical exercise
D. admit defeat and give up

**Answer:** D. admit defeat and give up
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — admit defeat and give up (Correct):** "Throw in the towel" means to admit defeat and give up an effort or struggle.
- **A — prepare energetically for a tough contest:** Preparing energetically for a tough match describes gearing up ("roll up one's sleeves"), the opposite of quitting.
- **B — complain about unfair rules after losing:** Blaming the referee after a close loss corresponds to being a sore loser rather than conceding defeat.
- **C — tidy up carefully after physical exercise:** Cleaning up carefully after a workout is a literal gym trap on "towel".
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Boxing—when a fighter's corner tosses a towel into the ring, the referee stops the bout immediately.

### Q419 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "sit on the fence".

A. guard a property carefully through the night
B. join the winning side enthusiastically
C. remain neutral and avoid taking sides
D. mock both parties during an argument

**Answer:** C. remain neutral and avoid taking sides
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — remain neutral and avoid taking sides (Correct):** "Sit on the fence" means to remain neutral and avoid taking a side or making a clear decision in a dispute.
- **A — guard a property carefully through the night:** Guarding one's property throughout the night is a literal yard-watch reading of "fence".
- **B — join the winning side enthusiastically:** Supporting the winning side enthusiastically corresponds to "jump on the bandwagon", the opposite of neutrality.
- **D — mock both parties during an argument:** Mocking both sides loudly in a debate describes cynical provocation rather than cautious non-commitment.
- **Exam Extra:** Noun form: Someone who habitually avoids taking sides is called a "fence-sitter".

### Q420 · Work & Effort Idioms
