What does the idiom "pass the buck" mean?

A. share prize money fairly with teammates
B. lend small change to an acquaintance
C. accept full blame for a team's failure
D. shift blame or responsibility onto someone else

**Answer:** D. shift blame or responsibility onto someone else
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — shift blame or responsibility onto someone else (Correct):** "Pass the buck" means to shift blame or responsibility for a problem onto someone else instead of handling it oneself.
- **A — share prize money fairly with teammates:** Sharing prize money fairly with teammates relates to generosity with cash ("buck" as slang for dollar), a classic SSC trap!
- **B — lend small change to an acquaintance:** Lending small change to a friend is another literal dollar trap.
- **C — accept full blame for a team's failure:** Accepting full accountability for a failure is the exact opposite—that is where "the buck stops".
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: From 19th-century poker, where a marker ("buck") passed to the next player to show whose turn it was to deal; US President Truman kept a sign reading "The Buck Stops Here."

### Q421 · Work & Effort Idioms

In the sentence "With only a month left for Tier 2, you must pull up your socks," what does "pull up your socks" mean?

A. make a greater effort to improve
B. dress smartly before a formal interview
C. relax comfortably after finishing exams
D. complain about unfair exam standards

**Answer:** A. make a greater effort to improve
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — make a greater effort to improve (Correct):** "Pull up one's socks" means to make a greater effort to improve one's performance or attitude.
- **B — dress smartly before a formal interview:** Dressing neatly for a job interview is a literal wardrobe trap on "socks".
- **C — relax comfortably after finishing exams:** Relaxing comfortably after completing exams corresponds to "put one's feet up", the opposite of doubling effort.
- **D — complain about unfair exam standards:** Complaining about difficult test papers shifts focus outward instead of improving one's own effort.
- **Exam Extra:** Usage note: Usually used as encouragement or warning when someone has been slacking ("You need to pull up your socks before the mock test").

### Q422 · Work & Effort Idioms

What does the idiom "go the extra mile" mean?

A. take a longer detour to avoid heavy traffic
B. make more effort than is expected
C. do only the minimum required to pass
D. walk daily to improve physical stamina

**Answer:** B. make more effort than is expected
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — make more effort than is expected (Correct):** "Go the extra mile" means to put in more effort, care, or time than is strictly required or expected.
- **A — take a longer detour to avoid heavy traffic:** Taking a longer route to avoid traffic describes detouring rather than extraordinary dedication.
- **C — do only the minimum required to pass:** Doing only the bare minimum to avoid trouble corresponds to "coast along" or minimal compliance, the opposite of extra effort.
- **D — walk daily to improve physical stamina:** Walking daily for cardiovascular fitness is a literal exercise trap on "mile".
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Traces back to the Sermon on the Mount (Matthew 5:41), advising going two miles when compelled to go one.

### Q423 · Animal & Nature Idioms

Select the correct meaning of the idiom "nip in the bud".

A. allow a minor conflict to grow freely
B. prune tall garden trees in late winter
C. celebrate the first signs of spring
D. stop a problem at a very early stage

**Answer:** D. stop a problem at a very early stage
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — stop a problem at a very early stage (Correct):** "Nip in the bud" means to stop a problem, bad habit, or undesirable situation at a very early stage before it can grow.
- **A — allow a minor conflict to grow freely:** Allowing a minor issue to grow unchecked is the exact opposite of early intervention.
- **B — prune tall garden trees in late winter:** Pruning mature trees in late winter is a literal horticultural reading that misses stopping trouble at inception.
- **C — celebrate the first signs of spring:** Celebrating the first signs of spring relates to seasonal joy rather than halting an incipient problem.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Gardening practice of pinching off unwanted flower buds early so the plant redirects energy or stops spreading.

### Q424 · Everyday Idioms

What does the idiom "blow one's own trumpet" mean?

A. practise a brass instrument in private
B. deliver bad news tactfully to friends
C. boast about one's own achievements
D. praise a rival's talent generously

**Answer:** C. boast about one's own achievements
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — boast about one's own achievements (Correct):** "Blow one's own trumpet" means to boast proudly about one's own abilities or achievements.
- **A — practise a brass instrument in private:** Practising a musical instrument alone at home is a literal music trap.
- **B — deliver bad news tactfully to friends:** Announcing bad news tactfully to friends has no connection to self-praise.
- **D — praise a rival's talent generously:** Praising a rival's talent generously is humility and generosity, the opposite of boasting.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Medieval tournaments where heralds blew trumpets to announce arriving knights; doing it yourself seemed shameless bragging.

### Q425 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

In the sentence "Water sharing has long been a bone of contention between the two states," what does "a bone of contention" mean?

A. a fair compromise that satisfies both sides
B. a subject of dispute or disagreement
C. a distant memory from early childhood
D. a painful injury suffered in sports

**Answer:** B. a subject of dispute or disagreement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — a subject of dispute or disagreement (Correct):** "A bone of contention" means a subject or issue over which people argue or disagree.
- **A — a fair compromise that satisfies both sides:** A generous compromise that satisfies everyone ends a dispute rather than causing one.
- **C — a distant memory from early childhood:** A long-forgotten memory from childhood lacks active disagreement.
- **D — a painful injury suffered in sports:** A painful fracture from a sports injury is a literal medical trap on "bone".
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Comes from the sight of two dogs fighting fiercely over a single bone; synonym idiom in SSC exams: "an apple of discord".

### Q426 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "the apple of one's eye" mean?

A. someone or something cherished above all others
B. a cause of bitter jealousy among rivals
C. a small flaw spoiling a good plan
D. a nutritious habit followed every morning

**Answer:** A. someone or something cherished above all others
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — someone or something cherished above all others (Correct):** "The apple of one's eye" refers to a person or thing that someone loves and cherishes above all others.
- **B — a cause of bitter jealousy among rivals:** A source of constant envy among rivals describes "an apple of discord", a frequent confusion pair with "apple of one's eye"!
- **C — a small flaw spoiling a good plan:** A minor flaw in an otherwise fine plan corresponds to "a fly in the ointment", not deep affection.
- **D — a nutritious habit followed every morning:** A healthy snack eaten every morning is a literal fruit trap.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Historically, the "apple" referred to the pupil of the eye—vital and fiercely protected against harm.

### Q427 · Everyday Idioms

Select the most appropriate meaning of "a bed of roses".

A. a delicate gift that breaks easily
B. a peaceful garden full of sweet fragrance
C. a hidden danger masked by beauty
D. an easy and comfortable situation

**Answer:** D. an easy and comfortable situation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — an easy and comfortable situation (Correct):** "A bed of roses" describes a life, job, or situation that is entirely easy, comfortable, and free from hardship.
- **A — a delicate gift that breaks easily:** A fragile ornament that breaks easily misses the sense of effortless comfort.
- **B — a peaceful garden full of sweet fragrance:** A garden filled with sweet fragrance is a literal floral reading of "roses".
- **C — a hidden danger masked by beauty:** A dangerous trap hidden under beauty describes deceptive allure ("a gilded cage" or "snake in the grass"), whereas "bed of roses" means pure ease.
- **Exam Extra:** Usage note: Most commonly used negatively ("Life is not a bed of roses") in SSC CHSL and CGL questions.

### Q428 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

What does the idiom "a hard nut to crack" mean?

A. a costly delicacy served at royal feasts
B. a cheerful companion who jokes constantly
C. a difficult problem or person to deal with
D. a simple riddle meant for young children

**Answer:** C. a difficult problem or person to deal with
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — a difficult problem or person to deal with (Correct):** "A hard nut to crack" refers to a difficult problem to solve or a stubborn/challenging person to understand or deal with.
- **A — a costly delicacy served at royal feasts:** An expensive delicacy served at banquets is a literal food trap on "nut".
- **B — a cheerful companion who jokes constantly:** A cheerful friend who jokes constantly describes an easygoing person, the opposite of tough to deal with.
- **D — a simple riddle meant for young children:** A minor riddle meant for young children implies simplicity, whereas this idiom stresses stubborn difficulty.
- **Exam Extra:** Antonym idiom: "a piece of cake" (something very easy) or "an open book" (someone easy to understand).

### Q429 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

Select the correct meaning of "between the devil and the deep blue sea".

A. sailing across calm waters on a cruise
B. caught between two equally unpleasant choices
C. choosing freely among attractive job offers
D. escaping unharmed from a fierce storm

**Answer:** B. caught between two equally unpleasant choices
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — caught between two equally unpleasant choices (Correct):** "Between the devil and the deep blue sea" means being trapped between two equally dangerous, difficult, or unpleasant alternatives.
- **A — sailing across calm waters on a cruise:** Enjoying a thrilling cruise across calm oceans is a literal maritime trap.
- **C — choosing freely among attractive job offers:** Choosing freely among several attractive offers describes an enviable abundance, the opposite of a grim dilemma.
- **D — escaping unharmed from a fierce storm:** Surviving a natural storm safely describes escaping danger rather than facing two bad choices.
- **Exam Extra:** Synonym idioms: "between Scylla and Charybdis" and "between a rock and a hard place".

### Q430 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

In the sentence "With two overs left and ten runs needed, the match hung in the balance," what does "hung in the balance" mean?

A. remain uncertain at a critical moment
B. stay physically steady during exercise
C. settle all outstanding debts early
D. cancel a contract permanently

**Answer:** A. remain uncertain at a critical moment
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — remain uncertain at a critical moment (Correct):** "Hang in the balance" means to remain uncertain or undecided at a critical moment, where outcomes could go either way.
- **B — stay physically steady during exercise:** Maintaining calm poise during yoga is a literal physical reading of "balance".
- **C — settle all outstanding debts early:** Settling every debt ahead of schedule relates to financial closure ("in the black"), not critical uncertainty.
- **D — cancel a contract permanently:** Cancelling an agreement permanently corresponds to "call off", not hovering in suspense.
- **Exam Extra:** Image: Like weighing scales hovering evenly before tipping toward victory or defeat.

### Q431 · Everyday Idioms

What does the idiom "kick the bucket" mean?

A. lose one's temper over a minor chore
B. spill water accidentally while cleaning
C. die (informal expression)
D. begin a new career with great zeal

**Answer:** C. die (informal expression)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — die (informal expression) (Correct):** "Kick the bucket" is a well-known informal or humorous idiom meaning to die.
- **A — lose one's temper over a minor chore:** Losing one's temper over a trivial chore corresponds to "fly off the handle", not dying.
- **B — spill water accidentally while cleaning:** Spilling water carelessly while mopping is a literal household trap on "bucket".
- **D — begin a new career with great zeal:** Starting a new job with enthusiasm relates to fresh beginnings, the opposite of life's end.
- **Exam Extra:** Synonym idioms in SSC exams: "bite the dust" and "give up the ghost" also mean to die (or fail/cease functioning).

### Q432 · Situation & Conflict Idioms

Which idiom means "end a quarrel and make peace"? Wait—keep stem direct: Select the most appropriate meaning of "bury the hatchet".

A. hide a weapon for a future ambush
B. misplace garden tools in a shed
C. start a bitter dispute over property
D. end a quarrel and make peace

**Answer:** D. end a quarrel and make peace
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — end a quarrel and make peace (Correct):** "Bury the hatchet" means to end an old quarrel, forgive grievances, and make peace.
- **A — hide a weapon for a future ambush:** Hiding a weapon for a surprise attack implies treacherous plotting, the exact opposite of making peace.
- **B — misplace garden tools in a shed:** Forgetting where one stored garden tools is a literal shed trap on "hatchet".
- **C — start a bitter dispute over property:** Starting a bitter dispute over land corresponds to "digging up the hatchet", the reverse of burying it.
- **Exam Extra:** Origin: Associated with Native American peace ceremonies where rival tribes symbolically buried weapons in the ground to mark the end of war.

### Q433 · Body & Action Idioms

What does the idiom "grease someone's palm" mean?

A. apply soothing oil to tired hands
B. bribe someone to gain a favour
C. shake hands warmly after an agreement
D. present an award publicly to a winner

**Answer:** B. bribe someone to gain a favour
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — bribe someone to gain a favour (Correct):** "Grease someone's palm" means to give someone a bribe, typically money, in exchange for an illegal or unfair favour.
- **A — apply soothing oil to tired hands:** Applying lotion to rough hands is a literal skincare trap on "grease" and "palm".
- **C — shake hands warmly after an agreement:** Shaking hands warmly after a meeting signals polite agreement, not bribery.
- **D — present an award publicly to a winner:** Rewarding a winner publicly with a trophy describes legitimate honour, unlike covert bribery.
- **Exam Extra:** Related idiom: "under the table" refers to payments made secretly and illegally.

### Q434 · Everyday Idioms

Select the correct meaning of "a man of straw".

A. a person of weak character or no real substance
B. a hardworking farmer during harvest season
C. a strict judge respected by everyone
D. a wealthy merchant owning large estates

**Answer:** A. a person of weak character or no real substance
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — a person of weak character or no real substance (Correct):** "A man of straw" refers to a weak, spineless person of no real substance, influence, or financial standing (or an easily refuted imaginary opponent).
- **B — a hardworking farmer during harvest season:** A diligent farmer who harvests wheat is a literal agricultural trap on "straw".
- **C — a strict judge respected by everyone:** A strict judge respected across the town describes authority and integrity, the opposite of a weak character.
- **D — a wealthy merchant owning large estates:** A wealthy merchant with vast estates denotes financial substance, whereas a man of straw lacks weight or reliability.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast: "a man of letters" means a scholarly writer, and "a man of his word" means someone completely trustworthy.

### Q435 · Phrasal Expressions

In the sentence "The cricket board had to call off the match due to persistent rain," what does "call off" mean?

A. postpone an event until next month
B. invite someone warmly to a gathering
C. cancel a planned event or activity
D. announce a name loudly in public

**Answer:** C. cancel a planned event or activity
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — cancel a planned event or activity (Correct):** "Call off" is a separable phrasal verb meaning to cancel a planned event, strike, match, or operation.
- **A — postpone an event until next month:** Postpone an event until next month corresponds to "put off", not "call off".
- **B — invite someone warmly to a gathering:** Invite guests warmly to a ceremony corresponds to "call over" or "call in", not cancellation.
- **D — announce a name loudly in public:** Announce the winner loudly over a microphone describes "call out", not calling off.
- **Exam Extra:** SSC trap: "call off" means cancel permanently, whereas "put off" means postpone to a later time!

---

## Chapter 6: One-Word Substitution

### Q436 · Phobias

Fear of confined or enclosed spaces

A. agoraphobia
B. hydrophobia
C. claustrophobia
D. nyctophobia

**Answer:** C. claustrophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — claustrophobia (Correct):** Claustrophobia comes from the Latin 'claustrum' (confined place) and Greek 'phobos' (fear), meaning an irrational fear of confined or enclosed spaces.
- **A — agoraphobia:** An irrational fear of open spaces or situations where escape might be difficult.
- **B — hydrophobia:** An extreme or irrational fear of water.
- **D — nyctophobia:** An extreme fear of darkness or the night.
- **Exam Extra:** The opposite of claustrophobia is agoraphobia, the fear of open or crowded public spaces.

### Q437 · Phobias

Extreme fear of heights

A. acrophobia
B. aquaphobia
C. ailurophobia
D. arachnophobia

**Answer:** A. acrophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — acrophobia (Correct):** Acrophobia combines Greek 'akron' (summit, peak) and 'phobos' (fear), meaning an extreme fear of heights.
- **B — aquaphobia:** An intense fear of water.
- **C — ailurophobia:** A persistent and irrational fear of cats.
- **D — arachnophobia:** An intense fear of spiders and other arachnids.
- **Exam Extra:** Do not confuse acrophobia (fear of heights) with aerophobia (fear of flying or air drafts).

### Q438 · Phobias

Fear of open or crowded public places

A. monophobia
B. zoophobia
C. pyrophobia
D. agoraphobia

**Answer:** D. agoraphobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — agoraphobia (Correct):** Agoraphobia derives from the Greek 'agora' (marketplace/public square), meaning a fear of open, crowded, or public places from which escape feels difficult.
- **A — monophobia:** An acute fear of being alone or isolated.
- **B — zoophobia:** An irrational fear of animals.
- **C — pyrophobia:** An extreme fear of fire.
- **Exam Extra:** In ancient Greece, the 'agora' was the central public gathering space, which helps link the root to crowds and open places.

### Q439 · Phobias

Extreme fear of water

A. photophobia
B. hydrophobia
C. hemophobia
D. chronophobia

**Answer:** B. hydrophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — hydrophobia (Correct):** Hydrophobia comes from Greek 'hydor' (water) and 'phobos' (fear), denoting an extreme fear of water (and historically a symptom of rabies).
- **A — photophobia:** An abnormal sensitivity to or fear of bright light.
- **C — hemophobia:** An extreme fear of blood.
- **D — chronophobia:** A persistent fear of time or the passage of time.
- **Exam Extra:** In chemistry, 'hydrophobic' describes substances that repel water rather than dissolve in it.

### Q440 · Phobias

Fear or dislike of strangers or foreigners

A. androphobia
B. gynophobia
C. xenophobia
D. ochlophobia

**Answer:** C. xenophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — xenophobia (Correct):** Xenophobia derives from Greek 'xenos' (stranger, foreigner), meaning a fear, dislike, or prejudice against strangers or people from other countries.
- **A — androphobia:** An abnormal fear or hatred of men.
- **B — gynophobia:** An irrational fear or hatred of women.
- **D — ochlophobia:** An extreme fear of crowds or mobs.
- **Exam Extra:** The antonym 'xenophile' refers to a person attracted to foreign peoples, cultures, or customs.

### Q441 · Phobias

Fear of darkness or night

A. nyctophobia
B. necrophobia
C. nosophobia
D. nomophobia

**Answer:** A. nyctophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — nyctophobia (Correct):** Nyctophobia comes from Greek 'nyx' (night), meaning an extreme fear of darkness or the night.
- **B — necrophobia:** An irrational fear of dead bodies or things associated with death.
- **C — nosophobia:** An unreasonable fear of contracting a specific disease.
- **D — nomophobia:** The fear of being without access to a working mobile phone.
- **Exam Extra:** The related root 'noct-' appears in 'nocturnal' (active at night).

### Q442 · Phobias

Fear of fire

A. astraphobia
B. pyrophobia
C. thermophobia
D. anemophobia

**Answer:** B. pyrophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — pyrophobia (Correct):** Pyrophobia comes from Greek 'pyr' (fire), meaning an intense, irrational fear of fire.
- **A — astraphobia:** An extreme fear of thunder and lightning.
- **C — thermophobia:** An intolerance of or fear of high heat.
- **D — anemophobia:** An irrational fear of wind or air drafts.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'pyr-' also gives us 'pyromania' (obsession with setting fires) and 'pyre' (a heap of wood for burning).

### Q443 · Phobias

Fear or hatred of books

A. graphophobia
B. logophobia
C. pedophobia
D. bibliophobia

**Answer:** D. bibliophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — bibliophobia (Correct):** Bibliophobia combines Greek 'biblion' (book) and 'phobos' (fear), meaning a fear or hatred of books.
- **A — graphophobia:** An extreme fear or aversion to writing.
- **B — logophobia:** A fear of words or of talking.
- **C — pedophobia:** An irrational fear or dislike of children.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice the root 'biblio-' across SSC vocabulary: bibliophile (book lover), bibliomania (book obsession), and bibliography (list of books).

### Q444 · Phobias

Fear of dogs

A. ophidiophobia
B. ornithophobia
C. cynophobia
D. entomophobia

**Answer:** C. cynophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — cynophobia (Correct):** Cynophobia derives from Greek 'kyon' (dog), meaning an abnormal fear of dogs.
- **A — ophidiophobia:** An extreme fear of snakes.
- **B — ornithophobia:** An irrational fear of birds.
- **D — entomophobia:** A persistent fear of insects.
- **Exam Extra:** The same Greek root 'kyon' gives us 'cynic' (originally a philosophical school nicknamed after dogs).

### Q445 · Phobias

Intense fear of death or dying

A. thanatophobia
B. gerontophobia
C. algophobia
D. pathophobia

**Answer:** A. thanatophobia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — thanatophobia (Correct):** Thanatophobia comes from Greek 'thanatos' (death), meaning an intense fear of death or the dying process.
- **B — gerontophobia:** An irrational fear of aging or of elderly people.
- **C — algophobia:** An abnormal fear of pain.
- **D — pathophobia:** A morbid fear of disease.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish thanatophobia (fear of death itself) from necrophobia (fear of corpses and things related to death).

### Q446 · Manias

An irresistible urge to steal

A. pyromania
B. kleptomania
C. dipsomania
D. megalomania

**Answer:** B. kleptomania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — kleptomania (Correct):** Kleptomania comes from Greek 'kleptes' (thief) and 'mania' (madness), describing a recurrent, irresistible urge to steal without economic motive.
- **A — pyromania:** An obsessive impulse to set things on fire.
- **C — dipsomania:** An uncontrollable craving for alcoholic drinks.
- **D — megalomania:** A delusion of one's own extreme power, wealth, or greatness.
- **Exam Extra:** In SSC exams, 'kleptomaniac' denotes the person suffering from this compulsion, while 'kleptomania' is the condition itself.

### Q447 · Manias

An extreme obsession with collecting books

A. logomania
B. graphomania
C. mythomania
D. bibliomania

**Answer:** D. bibliomania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — bibliomania (Correct):** Bibliomania is an extreme obsession with collecting and possessing books, derived from Greek 'biblion' (book) and 'mania'.
- **A — logomania:** Excessive talkativeness or an obsession with words.
- **B — graphomania:** A compulsive urge to write continuously.
- **C — mythomania:** An abnormal compulsion to lie or exaggerate.
- **Exam Extra:** A bibliomaniac collects books compulsively, whereas a bibliophile simply loves reading and cherishing books.

### Q448 · Manias

A delusion of one's own extreme greatness or power

A. monomania
B. plutomania
C. megalomania
D. melomania

**Answer:** C. megalomania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — megalomania (Correct):** Megalomania derives from Greek 'megas' (great, large), meaning an obsession with grandiose schemes or a delusion of one's own extreme power and importance.
- **A — monomania:** An obsessive preoccupation with a single idea or subject.
- **B — plutomania:** An intense obsession with wealth and money.
- **D — melomania:** An obsessive fondness for music.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'megalo-' means 'great' or 'large', also seen in 'megalith' (huge stone) and 'megacity'.

### Q449 · Manias

An obsessive desire to set things on fire

A. pyromania
B. dromomania
C. ergomania
D. trichotillomania

**Answer:** A. pyromania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — pyromania (Correct):** Pyromania comes from Greek 'pyr' (fire) and 'mania' (obsession), referring to an obsessive impulse to start fires.
- **B — dromomania:** An uncontrollable urge to wander or travel.
- **C — ergomania:** An excessive obsession with work.
- **D — trichotillomania:** A compulsive urge to pull out one's own hair.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike arson (a deliberate criminal act for gain or malice), pyromania is an impulse-control obsession.

### Q450 · Manias

An uncontrollable craving for alcoholic drinks

A. sitomania
B. dipsomania
C. narcomania
D. pharmacomania

**Answer:** B. dipsomania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — dipsomania (Correct):** Dipsomania derives from Greek 'dipsa' (thirst), meaning an uncontrollable craving for alcoholic liquor.
- **A — sitomania:** An abnormal craving for food.
- **C — narcomania:** A morbid craving for or addiction to narcotics.
- **D — pharmacomania:** An obsession with taking medicines or drugs.
- **Exam Extra:** Pair 'dipsomania' (craving for alcohol) with 'teetotaler' (one who completely abstains from alcohol).

### Q451 · Manias

An abnormal propensity for lying or exaggerating

A. nostomania
B. xenomania
C. mythomania
D. theomania

**Answer:** C. mythomania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — mythomania (Correct):** Mythomania comes from Greek 'mythos' (story, fable), meaning a pathological propensity to lie, fabricate, or exaggerate.
- **A — nostomania:** An extreme longing to return to one's home or native place.
- **B — xenomania:** An excessive fondness for foreign customs, fashions, or things.
- **D — theomania:** A delusion that one is a deity or an obsession with religion.
- **Exam Extra:** A person affected by mythomania is called a mythomaniac or pathological liar.

### Q452 · Manias

An obsessive fondness for music

A. choreomania
B. iconomania
C. verbomania
D. melomania

**Answer:** D. melomania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — melomania (Correct):** Melomania comes from Greek 'melos' (song, tune), meaning an obsessive fondness for music.
- **A — choreomania:** An irresistible impulse to dance wildly.
- **B — iconomania:** An obsession with collecting or venerating images and icons.
- **C — verbomania:** An obsession with words or excessive talking.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'melos' also gives us 'melody' and 'melodrama'.

### Q453 · Manias

An obsession with a single idea or subject

A. monomania
B. anthomania
C. demomania
D. plutomania

**Answer:** A. monomania
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — monomania (Correct):** Monomania combines Greek 'monos' (single) and 'mania' (madness), meaning an excessive preoccupation with a single idea or subject.
- **B — anthomania:** An extraordinary passion or obsession for flowers.
- **C — demomania:** An intense fondness for or obsession with crowds.
- **D — plutomania:** An obsessive desire for wealth.
- **Exam Extra:** Captain Ahab's obsession with the white whale in 'Moby-Dick' is a classic literary example of monomania.

### Q454 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of a king or monarch

A. homicide
B. patricide
C. regicide
D. tyrannicide

**Answer:** C. regicide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — regicide (Correct):** Regicide derives from Latin 'rex, regis' (king) and '-cida' (killer/killing), meaning the act of killing a king or monarch.
- **A — homicide:** The killing of one human being by another.
- **B — patricide:** The killing of one's own father.
- **D — tyrannicide:** The killing of a tyrant or oppressive ruler.
- **Exam Extra:** The Latin root 'reg-' appears in 'regal' (royal), 'reign', and 'regent'.

### Q455 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of one's own brother

A. parricide
B. fratricide
C. sororicide
D. uxoricide

**Answer:** B. fratricide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — fratricide (Correct):** Fratricide comes from Latin 'frater' (brother), meaning the killing of one's own brother (or, more broadly, a comrade).
- **A — parricide:** The killing of one's parent or other close relative.
- **C — sororicide:** The killing of one's own sister.
- **D — uxoricide:** The killing of one's own wife.
- **Exam Extra:** Related Latin family roots: 'fraternal' (brotherly), 'sororal' (sisterly), 'paternal' (fatherly), and 'maternal' (motherly).

### Q456 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of one's own wife

A. mariticide
B. matricide
C. filicide
D. uxoricide

**Answer:** D. uxoricide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — uxoricide (Correct):** Uxoricide comes from Latin 'uxor' (wife) and '-cide' (killing), meaning the murder of one's own wife.
- **A — mariticide:** The killing of one's own husband.
- **B — matricide:** The killing of one's own mother.
- **C — filicide:** The killing of one's own child.
- **Exam Extra:** The adjective 'uxorious' describes a husband who is excessively fond of or submissive to his wife.

### Q457 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of one's own mother

A. matricide
B. patricide
C. parricide
D. infanticide

**Answer:** A. matricide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — matricide (Correct):** Matricide comes from Latin 'mater' (mother) and '-cide' (killing), meaning the killing of one's own mother.
- **B — patricide:** The killing of one's own father.
- **C — parricide:** The killing of a parent or close relative.
- **D — infanticide:** The killing of an infant or newborn baby.
- **Exam Extra:** In Greek mythology, Orestes famously committed matricide by killing Clytemnestra.

### Q458 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of one's own father

A. avunculicide
B. genocide
C. patricide
D. regicide

**Answer:** C. patricide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — patricide (Correct):** Patricide comes from Latin 'pater' (father) and '-cide' (killing), meaning the killing of one's own father.
- **A — avunculicide:** The killing of one's own uncle.
- **B — genocide:** The deliberate mass killing of a particular national or ethnic group.
- **D — regicide:** The killing of a king or monarch.
- **Exam Extra:** Note the distinction: 'patricide' is specifically killing one's father, whereas 'parricide' covers killing either parent or a close relative.

### Q459 · Killings (-cide)

The deliberate killing of an entire ethnic, racial, or national group

A. homicide
B. genocide
C. suicide
D. feticide

**Answer:** B. genocide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — genocide (Correct):** Genocide combines Greek 'genos' (race, tribe) and Latin '-cide' (killing), meaning the deliberate extermination of a national, ethnic, racial, or religious group.
- **A — homicide:** The killing of a human being by another.
- **C — suicide:** The act of intentionally taking one's own life.
- **D — feticide:** The destruction or abortion of a fetus.
- **Exam Extra:** The term 'genocide' was coined in 1944 by jurist Raphael Lemkin.

### Q460 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of an infant or newborn child

A. filicide
B. feticide
C. parricide
D. infanticide

**Answer:** D. infanticide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — infanticide (Correct):** Infanticide comes from Latin 'infans' (infant, young child), meaning the intentional killing of a newborn or infant.
- **A — filicide:** The killing of one's own son or daughter regardless of age.
- **B — feticide:** The destruction of a fetus in the womb.
- **C — parricide:** The killing of a parent or close relative.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'infanticide' (killing a newborn/infant) with 'filicide' (killing one's own child of any age).

### Q461 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of one's own husband

A. mariticide
B. uxoricide
C. sororicide
D. amicicide

**Answer:** A. mariticide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — mariticide (Correct):** Mariticide comes from Latin 'maritus' (husband) and '-cide' (killing), meaning the killing of one's own husband.
- **B — uxoricide:** The killing of one's own wife.
- **C — sororicide:** The killing of one's own sister.
- **D — amicicide:** The killing of a friend.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'marit-' is also found in 'marital' (relating to marriage).

### Q462 · Killings (-cide)

The killing of one's own sister

A. matricide
B. uxoricide
C. sororicide
D. nepoticide

**Answer:** C. sororicide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — sororicide (Correct):** Sororicide comes from Latin 'soror' (sister) and '-cide' (killing), meaning the murder of one's own sister.
- **A — matricide:** The killing of one's own mother.
- **B — uxoricide:** The killing of one's own wife.
- **D — nepoticide:** The killing of one's own nephew.
- **Exam Extra:** The word 'sorority' (a society for female students) shares the same Latin root 'soror'.

### Q463 · Killings (-cide)

A chemical substance used to destroy unwanted plants or weeds

A. fungicide
B. herbicide
C. insecticide
D. germicide

**Answer:** B. herbicide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — herbicide (Correct):** Herbicide combines Latin 'herba' (plant, grass) and '-cide' (killer), meaning a chemical substance used to destroy unwanted plants or weeds.
- **A — fungicide:** A chemical agent used to destroy fungi or fungal spores.
- **C — insecticide:** A substance used to kill insects.
- **D — germicide:** An agent used to destroy disease-causing germs or microorganisms.
- **Exam Extra:** In SSC exams, non-human '-cide' words like herbicide, fungicide, and insecticide frequently appear alongside family '-cide' words.

### Q464 · Systems of Governance

A government by the people

A. democracy
B. autocracy
C. theocracy
D. oligarchy

**Answer:** A. democracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — democracy (Correct):** Democracy derives from Greek 'demos' (people) and 'kratos' (power, rule), meaning a system of government by the people, directly or through elected representatives.
- **B — autocracy:** A system of government by one person holding absolute power.
- **C — theocracy:** A system of government where priests rule in the name of God.
- **D — oligarchy:** A form of government controlled by a small, privileged group.
- **Exam Extra:** Other 'demos' words include 'demography' (population study) and 'demagogue' (a leader who appeals to popular prejudices).

### Q465 · Systems of Governance

A government by one person with absolute power

A. aristocracy
B. bureaucracy
C. plutocracy
D. autocracy

**Answer:** D. autocracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — autocracy (Correct):** Autocracy derives from Greek 'autos' (self) and 'kratos' (power), meaning a system of government in which one person possesses unlimited power.
- **A — aristocracy:** Government by the nobility or privileged upper class.
- **B — bureaucracy:** A system of administration run by non-elected state officials.
- **C — plutocracy:** Government by the wealthy class.
- **Exam Extra:** A ruler in an autocracy is an autocrat; 'autocratic' describes a domineering, absolute manner.

### Q466 · Systems of Governance

A government by the wealthy

A. meritocracy
B. kleptocracy
C. plutocracy
D. ochlocracy

**Answer:** C. plutocracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — plutocracy (Correct):** Plutocracy comes from Greek 'ploutos' (wealth) and 'kratos' (rule), meaning government or rule by the wealthy.
- **A — meritocracy:** A system in which power is held according to ability and talent.
- **B — kleptocracy:** A corrupt government where rulers steal public funds.
- **D — ochlocracy:** Government by the mob or disorderly crowd.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember: 'pluto-' refers to wealth (from Plutus, the Greek god of wealth), as seen in 'plutocrat' and 'plutomania'.

### Q467 · Systems of Governance

A government by religious leaders in the name of God

A. monarchy
B. theocracy
C. gerontocracy
D. technocracy

**Answer:** B. theocracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — theocracy (Correct):** Theocracy comes from Greek 'theos' (God) and 'kratos' (rule), meaning a system of government in which priests or religious authorities rule in the name of God.
- **A — monarchy:** A form of government with a king or queen at the head.
- **C — gerontocracy:** A state or society governed by old people.
- **D — technocracy:** A system of governance led by technical or scientific experts.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'theo-' (god) also appears in 'theology' (study of religion) and 'atheist' (one who denies God's existence).

### Q468 · Systems of Governance

A government by a small group of powerful people

A. diarchy
B. anarchy
C. panarchy
D. oligarchy

**Answer:** D. oligarchy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — oligarchy (Correct):** Oligarchy derives from Greek 'oligos' (few) and 'arkhein' (to rule), meaning a system in which power is held by a small group of people.
- **A — diarchy:** A form of government shared jointly by two rulers or authorities.
- **B — anarchy:** A state of lawlessness due to the absence of government.
- **C — panarchy:** A system of universal rule or governance encompassing all.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'oligarchy' (rule by a few) with 'monarchy' (rule by one) and 'diarchy' (rule by two).

### Q469 · Systems of Governance

A government by the nobility or upper class

A. aristocracy
B. timocracy
C. stratocracy
D. bureaucracy

**Answer:** A. aristocracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — aristocracy (Correct):** Aristocracy comes from Greek 'aristos' (best) and 'kratos' (rule), meaning government by the nobility or hereditary upper class.
- **B — timocracy:** A form of government in which possession of property or honor qualifies one to rule.
- **C — stratocracy:** A form of government headed by military chiefs.
- **D — bureaucracy:** Administration managed by departments of state officials.
- **Exam Extra:** Although 'aristos' literally means 'the best', in practice aristocracy refers to rule by noble birth and rank.

### Q470 · Systems of Governance

Absence of government and law in a country

A. monarchy
B. anarchy
C. autarchy
D. hierarchy

**Answer:** B. anarchy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — anarchy (Correct):** Anarchy comes from Greek 'an-' (without) and 'arkhos' (ruler), meaning the absence of government, law, or political authority.
- **A — monarchy:** A form of government with a hereditary sovereign as head of state.
- **C — autarchy:** Absolute sovereignty or rule by a single autocratic leader.
- **D — hierarchy:** A system in which people or groups are ranked one above the other by status or authority.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice '-archy' means 'rule/ruler' (monarchy, hierarchy, anarchy), whereas '-cracy' means 'power/rule' (democracy, autocracy).

### Q471 · Systems of Governance

A government run by state officials rather than elected representatives

A. technocracy
B. meritocracy
C. bureaucracy
D. kakistocracy

**Answer:** C. bureaucracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — bureaucracy (Correct):** Bureaucracy combines French 'bureau' (office/desk) and Greek '-kratos' (rule), meaning a system of government operated largely by state officials rather than elected representatives.
- **A — technocracy:** Rule or control by scientific and technical experts.
- **B — meritocracy:** A system where advancement and power depend on individual merit.
- **D — kakistocracy:** Government by the least qualified or worst citizens.
- **Exam Extra:** In everyday usage, 'bureaucracy' often carries a negative sense of excessive official rules and red tape.

### Q472 · Systems of Governance

A government or state ruled by old people

A. neocracy
B. gynecocracy
C. pedocracy
D. gerontocracy

**Answer:** D. gerontocracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — gerontocracy (Correct):** Gerontocracy comes from Greek 'geron' (old man) and 'kratos' (rule), meaning a state or society governed by elderly people.
- **A — neocracy:** Government by new, young, or inexperienced rulers.
- **B — gynecocracy:** Government or social dominance by women.
- **C — pedocracy:** Rule or government by children.
- **Exam Extra:** The same root 'geron-' appears in 'gerontology' (study of aging) and 'geriatrics' (medical care of the elderly).

### Q473 · Systems of Governance

A government by the worst or least competent citizens

A. kakistocracy
B. kleptocracy
C. ochlocracy
D. stratocracy

**Answer:** A. kakistocracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — kakistocracy (Correct):** Kakistocracy derives from Greek 'kakistos' (worst) and 'kratos' (rule), meaning a government run by the worst, least qualified, or most unscrupulous citizens.
- **B — kleptocracy:** A government whose corrupt leaders use power to steal national wealth.
- **C — ochlocracy:** Government by a disorderly crowd or mob.
- **D — stratocracy:** A form of government led by the military.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'kakos' (bad/evil) also appears in 'cacophony' (harsh, unpleasant mixture of sounds).

### Q474 · Sciences & Studies

The study of human societies and social behavior

A. anthropology
B. sociology
C. ethnology
D. psychology

**Answer:** B. sociology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — sociology (Correct):** Sociology combines Latin 'socius' (companion, society) and Greek '-logia' (study), meaning the scientific study of human societies, institutions, and social relationships.
- **A — anthropology:** The study of human origins, physical development, and cultures.
- **C — ethnology:** The comparative study of the characteristics and divisions of different peoples and cultures.
- **D — psychology:** The scientific study of the human mind and behavior.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish 'sociology' (modern human societies and social structures) from 'anthropology' (holistic study of human evolution and cultures).

### Q475 · Sciences & Studies

The study of the origin and history of words

A. philology
B. lexicology
C. etymology
D. semantics

**Answer:** C. etymology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — etymology (Correct):** Etymology comes from Greek 'etymon' (true sense of a word) and '-logia' (study), meaning the study of the origin and historical development of words.
- **A — philology:** The study of language in historical written texts.
- **B — lexicology:** The branch of linguistics dealing with the vocabulary and meaning of words.
- **D — semantics:** The branch of linguistics and logic concerned with meaning.
- **Exam Extra:** Do not confuse 'etymology' (word origins) with 'entomology' (the study of insects)—a classic SSC distractor pair!

### Q476 · Sciences & Studies

The study of human origins, evolution, and cultures

A. archaeology
B. genealogy
C. paleontology
D. anthropology

**Answer:** D. anthropology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — anthropology (Correct):** Anthropology derives from Greek 'anthropos' (human being) and '-logia' (study), meaning the comprehensive study of humanity, human origins, and cultures.
- **A — archaeology:** The study of past human activity through the excavation and analysis of artifacts.
- **B — genealogy:** The study of family lineages and ancestral histories.
- **C — paleontology:** The scientific study of life in the geological past through fossils.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'anthrop-' appears in 'philanthropist' (lover of mankind) and 'misanthrope' (hater of mankind).

### Q477 · Sciences & Studies

The study of human history through excavation of sites and artifacts

A. archaeology
B. geology
C. epigraphy
D. numismatics

**Answer:** A. archaeology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — archaeology (Correct):** Archaeology comes from Greek 'arkhaios' (ancient) and '-logia' (study), meaning the study of past human cultures through the recovery and analysis of material remains and artifacts.
- **B — geology:** The scientific study of the Earth's physical structure, rocks, and history.
- **C — epigraphy:** The study and interpretation of ancient inscriptions.
- **D — numismatics:** The study or collection of coins, banknotes, and medals.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice the difference: archaeology studies human artifacts and ruins, whereas paleontology studies fossils of ancient organisms.

### Q478 · Sciences & Studies

The scientific study of fossil animals and plants

A. petrology
B. paleontology
C. mineralogy
D. speleology

**Answer:** B. paleontology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — paleontology (Correct):** Paleontology derives from Greek 'palaios' (ancient) and 'onta' (beings), meaning the scientific study of prehistoric life through fossils of plants and animals.
- **A — petrology:** The branch of geology that studies the origin, composition, and structure of rocks.
- **C — mineralogy:** The scientific study of minerals, their crystal structure, and properties.
- **D — speleology:** The scientific study and exploration of caves.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'paleo-' (ancient/old) also appears in 'paleography' (ancient writing) and 'Paleolithic' (Old Stone Age).

### Q479 · Sciences & Studies

The scientific study of earthquakes

A. meteorology
B. volcanology
C. seismology
D. hydrology

**Answer:** C. seismology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — seismology (Correct):** Seismology derives from Greek 'seismos' (earthquake), meaning the scientific study of earthquakes and seismic waves.
- **A — meteorology:** The scientific study of the atmosphere and weather forecasting.
- **B — volcanology:** The scientific study of volcanoes, lava, and related geological phenomena.
- **D — hydrology:** The science dealing with the occurrence, circulation, and properties of water on Earth.
- **Exam Extra:** An instrument that detects and records earthquake waves is called a 'seismograph'.

### Q480 · Sciences & Studies

The study and interpretation of ancient inscriptions

A. paleography
B. calligraphy
C. cryptography
D. epigraphy

**Answer:** D. epigraphy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — epigraphy (Correct):** Epigraphy comes from Greek 'epigraphe' (inscription), meaning the study, deciphering, and interpretation of ancient inscriptions on stone, metal, or wood.
- **A — paleography:** The study of ancient handwriting and historical manuscripts.
- **B — calligraphy:** The art of producing decorative handwriting or lettering.
- **C — cryptography:** The art or science of writing and decoding secret messages.
- **Exam Extra:** Pair 'epigraphy' (study of inscriptions) with 'numismatics' (study of coins)—the two core sources of ancient Indian history.

### Q481 · Sciences & Studies

The scientific study of old age and the aging process

A. gerontology
B. pathology
C. oncology
D. etiology

**Answer:** A. gerontology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — gerontology (Correct):** Gerontology derives from Greek 'geron' (old man), meaning the scientific study of old age, aging, and the problems of the elderly.
- **B — pathology:** The scientific study of the causes and effects of diseases.
- **C — oncology:** The branch of medicine concerned with the study and treatment of tumors and cancer.
- **D — etiology:** The study of the causes or origins of a disease or condition.
- **Exam Extra:** Do not confuse 'gerontology' (study of aging) with 'geriatrics' (the branch of medicine treating elderly patients).

### Q482 · Sciences & Studies

The scientific study of reptiles and amphibians

A. ichthyology
B. herpetology
C. mammalogy
D. arachnology

**Answer:** B. herpetology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — herpetology (Correct):** Herpetology comes from Greek 'herpeton' (creeping animal), meaning the branch of zoology devoted to the study of reptiles and amphibians.
- **A — ichthyology:** The branch of zoology concerned with the study of fishes.
- **C — mammalogy:** The branch of zoology that studies mammals.
- **D — arachnology:** The scientific study of spiders and related arachnids.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice how zoology splits into specialized '-logy' branches: herpetology (reptiles/amphibians), ichthyology (fish), ornithology (birds), entomology (insects).

### Q483 · Sciences & Studies

The branch of zoology concerned with the study of fishes

A. conchology
B. malacology
C. ichthyology
D. cetology

**Answer:** C. ichthyology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — ichthyology (Correct):** Ichthyology derives from Greek 'ikhthys' (fish), meaning the branch of zoology dedicated to the study of fishes.
- **A — conchology:** The scientific study of mollusk shells.
- **B — malacology:** The branch of zoology dealing with mollusks such as snails and octopuses.
- **D — cetology:** The branch of marine mammal science that studies whales, dolphins, and porpoises.
- **Exam Extra:** The silent 'ch' / 'kh' spelling ('ichthy-') is frequently tested in both One-Word Substitution and Spelling sections.

### Q484 · Sciences & Studies

The study of ancient writing systems and historical manuscripts

A. orthography
B. bibliography
C. choreography
D. paleography

**Answer:** D. paleography
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — paleography (Correct):** Paleography comes from Greek 'palaios' (ancient) and 'graphein' (to write), meaning the study of ancient writing systems and historical manuscripts.
- **A — orthography:** The conventional spelling system of a language.
- **B — bibliography:** A systematic list of books and writings, or the study of books as physical objects.
- **C — choreography:** The art of designing sequences of movements for dances.
- **Exam Extra:** Distinguish 'paleography' (ancient handwriting/scripts on manuscripts) from 'epigraphy' (inscriptions carved on hard surfaces).

### Q485 · Sciences & Studies

The statistical study of elections and voting trends

A. psephology
B. demography
C. criminology
D. penology

**Answer:** A. psephology
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — psephology (Correct):** Psephology derives from Greek 'psephos' (pebble, used by ancient Greeks to cast votes), meaning the statistical study of elections and voting behavior.
- **B — demography:** The statistical study of human populations, births, deaths, and migration.
- **C — criminology:** The scientific study of crime, criminals, and criminal behavior.
- **D — penology:** The study of the punishment of crime and prison management.
- **Exam Extra:** A person who analyzes election results and voting trends is called a 'psephologist'.

### Q486 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A person who compiles dictionaries

A. calligrapher
B. lexicographer
C. stenographer
D. choreographer

**Answer:** B. lexicographer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — lexicographer (Correct):** Lexicographer comes from Greek 'lexikon' (dictionary) and 'graphein' (to write), meaning a person who compiles or edits dictionaries.
- **A — calligrapher:** A person skilled in the art of decorative handwriting.
- **C — stenographer:** A person trained to write rapidly in shorthand, often transcribing speech.
- **D — choreographer:** A person who designs and arranges dance steps and movements.
- **Exam Extra:** Dr. Samuel Johnson famously defined a lexicographer as 'a writer of dictionaries, a harmless drudge.'

### Q487 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A person who studies birds

A. herpetologist
B. ichthyologist
C. ornithologist
D. anthropologist

**Answer:** C. ornithologist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — ornithologist (Correct):** Ornithologist derives from Greek 'ornis, ornithos' (bird), meaning a scientist or specialist who studies birds.
- **A — herpetologist:** A zoologist who specializes in the study of reptiles and amphibians.
- **B — ichthyologist:** A zoologist who specializes in the study of fishes.
- **D — anthropologist:** A specialist who studies human origins, cultures, and societies.
- **Exam Extra:** Dr. Salim Ali is celebrated as the 'Birdman of India' and was a renowned ornithologist.

### Q488 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A person who studies insects

A. botanist
B. zoologist
C. mycologist
D. entomologist

**Answer:** D. entomologist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — entomologist (Correct):** Entomologist comes from Greek 'entomon' (insect, literally 'cut into segments'), meaning a scientist who studies insects.
- **A — botanist:** A scientist who studies plants and plant life.
- **B — zoologist:** A scientist who studies animals and animal life in general.
- **C — mycologist:** A biologist who specializes in the study of fungi.
- **Exam Extra:** Keep 'entomologist' (insects) distinct from 'etymologist' (word origins)—one of SSC's favorite traps.

### Q489 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A person who collects coins and medals

A. numismatist
B. philatelist
C. notaphilist
D. cartophilist

**Answer:** A. numismatist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — numismatist (Correct):** Numismatist comes from Latin 'numisma' (coin), meaning a person who collects or studies coins, tokens, and medals.
- **B — philatelist:** A person who collects or studies postage stamps.
- **C — notaphilist:** A person who collects or studies paper banknotes.
- **D — cartophilist:** A person who collects picture cards such as cigarette or trading cards.
- **Exam Extra:** Related collector terms: 'numismatist' (coins), 'philatelist' (stamps), 'notaphilist' (banknotes).

### Q490 · Professions, Collectors & Places

One who collects or studies postage stamps

A. connoisseur
B. philatelist
C. antiquarian
D. numismatist

**Answer:** B. philatelist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — philatelist (Correct):** Philatelist derives from Greek 'philos' (loving) and 'ateleia' (exemption from tax, referring to prepaid postage), meaning one who collects or studies postage stamps.
- **A — connoisseur:** An expert judge in matters of taste, art, or fine food.
- **C — antiquarian:** A person who studies, collects, or deals in rare historical objects and antiques.
- **D — numismatist:** A person who collects or studies coins and medals.
- **Exam Extra:** The hobby itself is called 'philately', and the adjective is 'philatelic'.

### Q491 · Professions, Collectors & Places

One who draws or makes maps

A. topographer
B. cosmographer
C. cartographer
D. demographer

**Answer:** C. cartographer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — cartographer (Correct):** Cartographer combines Latin 'charta' (map, paper) and Greek 'graphein' (to draw/write), meaning a person who draws or produces maps.
- **A — topographer:** One who maps or describes the surface features and elevation of a region.
- **B — cosmographer:** One who describes or maps the general features of the universe or Earth.
- **D — demographer:** One who studies human population statistics such as size, density, and distribution.
- **Exam Extra:** The art and science of mapmaking is called 'cartography'.

### Q492 · Professions, Collectors & Places

One who writes the story of another person's life

A. autobiographer
B. chronographer
C. hagiographer
D. biographer

**Answer:** D. biographer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — biographer (Correct):** Biographer comes from Greek 'bios' (life) and 'graphein' (to write), meaning one who writes an account of another person's life.
- **A — autobiographer:** A person who writes the story of their own life.
- **B — chronographer:** A person who records historical events in chronological order.
- **C — hagiographer:** A writer of the lives of saints, or of an uncritically flattering biography.
- **Exam Extra:** If a person writes their own life story, they are an 'autobiographer' ('auto-' = self).

### Q493 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A place where books are kept for reading or borrowing

A. library
B. archive
C. museum
D. observatory

**Answer:** A. library
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — library (Correct):** Library derives from Latin 'liber' (book), meaning a building or room containing collections of books, periodicals, and media for reading, study, or borrowing.
- **B — archive:** A place where historical documents and official records are preserved.
- **C — museum:** A building in which objects of historical, scientific, or artistic interest are stored and exhibited.
- **D — observatory:** A building equipped with telescopes for observing astronomical or meteorological phenomena.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'library' (books for reading/borrowing) with 'archive' (historical public documents and records).

### Q494 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A place where bees are kept

A. aviary
B. apiary
C. aquarium
D. terrarium

**Answer:** B. apiary
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — apiary (Correct):** Apiary derives from Latin 'apis' (bee), meaning a place or collection of hives where bees are kept for honey and pollination.
- **A — aviary:** A large cage, building, or enclosure for keeping birds.
- **C — aquarium:** A transparent tank of water in which live fish and aquatic plants are kept.
- **D — terrarium:** A sealed or enclosed glass container for keeping small land plants or reptiles.
- **Exam Extra:** Never confuse 'apiary' (place for bees, Latin 'apis') with 'aviary' (place for birds, Latin 'avis').

### Q495 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A large enclosure where birds are kept

A. kennel
B. stable
C. aviary
D. menagerie

**Answer:** C. aviary
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — aviary (Correct):** Aviary comes from Latin 'avis' (bird), meaning a large enclosure or building in which birds are kept and allowed to fly.
- **A — kennel:** A small shelter or boarding place for dogs.
- **B — stable:** A building set apart and adapted for keeping horses.
- **D — menagerie:** A collection of wild animals kept in captivity for exhibition.
- **Exam Extra:** The root 'avis' (bird) also gives us 'aviation' and 'avian'.

### Q496 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A person in charge of a museum or collection

A. archivist
B. librarian
C. warden
D. curator

**Answer:** D. curator
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — curator (Correct):** Curator derives from Latin 'curare' (to care for), meaning the keeper, custodian, or manager of a museum, art gallery, or collection.
- **A — archivist:** A person trained to preserve and organize historical documents and records.
- **B — librarian:** A person who works in or manages a library.
- **C — warden:** A person responsible for supervising a particular place, prison, or wildlife sanctuary.
- **Exam Extra:** A curator oversees acquisitions and exhibitions, whereas an archivist specifically manages historical records.

### Q497 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A person employed to drive a private or hired motorcar

A. chauffeur
B. steward
C. valet
D. concierge

**Answer:** A. chauffeur
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — chauffeur (Correct):** Chauffeur is a French loanword originally meaning 'stoker', now meaning a person employed to drive a private or hired automobile.
- **B — steward:** An attendant who looks after passengers on a ship, aircraft, or train, or manages an estate.
- **C — valet:** A male servant who attends to a man's personal needs and clothing.
- **D — concierge:** A hotel employee whose job is to assist guests with bookings, directions, and services.
- **Exam Extra:** Watch the spelling of 'chauffeur' (ch-a-u-f-f-e-u-r), as it is a frequent SSC spelling question too!

### Q498 · Professions, Collectors & Places

A person skilled in the art of beautiful handwriting

A. stenographer
B. calligrapher
C. typographer
D. lithographer

**Answer:** B. calligrapher
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — calligrapher (Correct):** Calligrapher comes from Greek 'kallos' (beauty) and 'graphein' (to write), meaning a person skilled in the art of decorative handwriting.
- **A — stenographer:** A person skilled in writing rapidly in shorthand.
- **C — typographer:** A specialist who designs, sets, and arranges printed type.
- **D — lithographer:** A printmaker who creates prints using flat stone or metal plates.
- **Exam Extra:** The opposite of calligraphy (beautiful handwriting) is 'cacography' (bad handwriting or poor spelling).

### Q499 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

One who speaks or knows many languages

A. bilingual
B. linguist
C. polyglot
D. orator

**Answer:** C. polyglot
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — polyglot (Correct):** Polyglot derives from Greek 'polys' (many) and 'glotta' (tongue, language), meaning a person who knows and is able to speak or use several languages.
- **A — bilingual:** Able to speak two languages fluently.
- **B — linguist:** A person skilled in foreign languages or one who studies linguistics scientifically.
- **D — orator:** A skilled and eloquent public speaker.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'bilingual' (two languages), 'multilingual' (many languages, usually adjective), and 'polyglot' (person knowing many languages).

### Q500 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

One who walks in sleep

A. somniloquist
B. insomniac
C. hypnotist
D. somnambulist

**Answer:** D. somnambulist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — somnambulist (Correct):** Somnambulist comes from Latin 'somnus' (sleep) and 'ambulare' (to walk), meaning a person who walks while asleep.
- **A — somniloquist:** A person who talks while asleep.
- **B — insomniac:** A person who is regularly unable to sleep.
- **C — hypnotist:** A person who practices hypnosis on others.
- **Exam Extra:** The act of sleepwalking is 'somnambulism'; compare with 'somniloquist' (one who talks in sleep).

### Q501 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

A remedy for all diseases or difficulties

A. panacea
B. antidote
C. placebo
D. elixir

**Answer:** A. panacea
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — panacea (Correct):** Panacea comes from Greek 'pan' (all) and 'akos' (remedy), meaning a universal cure or solution for all diseases or difficulties.
- **B — antidote:** A medicine given specifically to counteract a particular poison or disease.
- **C — placebo:** A harmless, inactive substance given to satisfy a patient or serve as a control in trials.
- **D — elixir:** A magical or medicinal potion believed to prolong life or cure ills.
- **Exam Extra:** In Greek mythology, Panacea was the goddess of universal healing, daughter of Asclepius.

### Q502 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

One who loves mankind and works for human welfare

A. misogynist
B. philanthropist
C. hedonist
D. ascetic

**Answer:** B. philanthropist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — philanthropist (Correct):** Philanthropist comes from Greek 'philos' (loving) and 'anthropos' (mankind), meaning a person who seeks to promote human welfare, especially through generous donations.
- **A — misogynist:** A person who dislikes, despises, or is prejudiced against women.
- **C — hedonist:** A person who believes that pleasure is the highest good and primary aim of life.
- **D — ascetic:** A person who practices severe self-discipline and abstains from worldly pleasures.
- **Exam Extra:** Its direct antonym is 'misanthrope' (one who hates or distrusts humankind).

### Q503 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

One who hates or distrusts mankind

A. misogamist
B. egoist
C. misanthrope
D. cynic

**Answer:** C. misanthrope
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — misanthrope (Correct):** Misanthrope derives from Greek 'misein' (to hate) and 'anthropos' (mankind), meaning a person who dislikes humankind and avoids human society.
- **A — misogamist:** A person who hates or is strongly opposed to marriage.
- **B — egoist:** A self-centered person preoccupied with their own interests.
- **D — cynic:** A person who believes people are motivated purely by selfishness.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice the trio of 'mis-' words in SSC: misanthrope (hates mankind), misogynist (hates women), misogamist (hates marriage).

### Q504 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

One who can use both hands equally well

A. dexterous
B. versatile
C. bipartisan
D. ambidextrous

**Answer:** D. ambidextrous
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — ambidextrous (Correct):** Ambidextrous combines Latin 'ambi-' (both) and 'dexter' (right-handed, skillful), meaning able to use both hands with equal skill and ease.
- **A — dexterous:** Showing skill and grace, especially with the hands.
- **B — versatile:** Able to adapt or be adapted to many different functions or activities.
- **C — bipartisan:** Involving the agreement or cooperation of two political parties.
- **Exam Extra:** The Latin 'dexter' meant 'right' (considered favorable), while 'sinister' meant 'left'.

### Q505 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

A speech or performance delivered without prior preparation

A. impromptu
B. verbatim
C. soliloquy
D. monologue

**Answer:** A. impromptu
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — impromptu (Correct):** Impromptu derives from Latin 'in promptu' (in readiness), meaning done, spoken, or performed without prior preparation or rehearsal.
- **B — verbatim:** In exactly the same words as were used originally.
- **C — soliloquy:** A speech in a play in which a character speaks thoughts aloud while alone.
- **D — monologue:** A long speech delivered by one actor in a play or conversation.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Extempore' is a close SSC synonym for an unprepared speech, though 'impromptu' emphasizes spontaneity on the spur of the moment.

### Q506 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

A person who looks at the bright side of things

A. pessimist
B. optimist
C. idealist
D. realist

**Answer:** B. optimist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — optimist (Correct):** Optimist derives from Latin 'optimus' (best), meaning a person who tends to be hopeful and looks at the bright side of things.
- **A — pessimist:** A person who tends to see the worst aspect of things or believe the worst will happen.
- **C — idealist:** A person guided more by high ideals than by practical considerations.
- **D — realist:** A person who accepts a situation as it is and deals with it practically.
- **Exam Extra:** Pair 'optimist' (bright side) with its antonym 'pessimist' (dark/worst side, from Latin 'pessimus').

### Q507 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

One who doubts accepted beliefs or claims

A. atheist
B. agnostic
C. skeptic
D. heretic

**Answer:** C. skeptic
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — skeptic (Correct):** Skeptic (also spelled 'sceptic' in British English) comes from Greek 'skeptesthai' (to examine, inquire), meaning one who habitually doubts or questions accepted beliefs and claims.
- **A — atheist:** A person who denies or disbelieves in the existence of God.
- **B — agnostic:** A person who believes nothing is known or can be known about the existence of God.
- **D — heretic:** A person holding religious or ideological beliefs contrary to orthodox doctrine.
- **Exam Extra:** In SSC exams, 'skeptic' (US) and 'sceptic' (UK) are the same word; 'skepticism' is the noun for the attitude of doubt.

### Q508 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

That which cannot be avoided

A. invincible
B. irrevocable
C. infallible
D. inevitable

**Answer:** D. inevitable
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — inevitable (Correct):** Inevitable derives from Latin 'in-' (not) and 'evitare' (to avoid), meaning certain to happen and impossible to avoid or prevent.
- **A — invincible:** Too powerful to be defeated or overcome.
- **B — irrevocable:** Impossible to change, reverse, or call back.
- **C — infallible:** Incapable of making mistakes or being wrong.
- **Exam Extra:** Remember the negative prefix 'in-' + 'evitable' (avoidable) = unavoidable.

### Q509 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

A person who is new to a profession or activity

A. novice
B. veteran
C. virtuoso
D. mentor

**Answer:** A. novice
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — novice (Correct):** Novice comes from Latin 'novicius' (new), meaning a person who is new to and inexperienced in a profession, skill, or situation.
- **B — veteran:** A person who has had long experience in a particular field or occupation.
- **C — virtuoso:** A person highly skilled in music or another artistic pursuit.
- **D — mentor:** An experienced and trusted adviser or guide.
- **Exam Extra:** Common SSC synonyms for 'novice' include 'tyro', 'neophyte', and 'rookie'; its antonym is 'veteran'.

### Q510 · Personality, Habits & General Terms

A person who believes in fate and that events cannot be changed

A. fanatic
B. fatalist
C. stoic
D. epicurean

**Answer:** B. fatalist
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — fatalist (Correct):** Fatalist derives from Latin 'fatum' (fate), meaning a person who believes that all events are predetermined by fate and humans are powerless to alter them.
- **A — fanatic:** A person filled with excessive, single-minded zeal, especially in religion or politics.
- **C — stoic:** A person who can endure pain or hardship without showing feelings or complaining.
- **D — epicurean:** A person devoted to sensual enjoyment, especially derived from fine food and drink.
- **Exam Extra:** Do not confuse 'fatalist' (believer in fate) with 'stoic' (one who endures pain or hardship without showing emotion).

---

# Section III: Voice & Narration

## Chapter 7: Active & Passive Voice

### Q511 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct passive form of: "The gardener waters the plants every morning."

A. The plants are watered by the gardener every morning.
B. The plants were watered by the gardener every morning.
C. The plants are being watered by the gardener every morning.
D. The plants have been watered by the gardener every morning.

**Answer:** A. The plants are watered by the gardener every morning.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The plants are watered by the gardener every morning. (Correct):** Simple Present active ('waters') changes to 'is/am/are + V3' in passive voice. Since the new subject 'The plants' is plural, 'are watered' is correct: "The plants are watered by the gardener every morning."
- **B — The plants were watered by the gardener every morning.:** Using 'were watered' shifts the Simple Present tense to Simple Past passive.
- **C — The plants are being watered by the gardener every morning.:** Using 'are being watered' adds 'being', incorrectly shifting Simple Present to Present Continuous passive.
- **D — The plants have been watered by the gardener every morning.:** Using 'have been watered' shifts Simple Present to Present Perfect passive.
- **Exam Extra:** Simple Present Passive formula: Object + is/am/are + V3 + by + Subject (retain time adverbs like 'every morning' at the end).

### Q512 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct passive form of: "Carelessness causes many road accidents."

A. Many road accidents is caused by carelessness.
B. Many road accidents are caused by carelessness.
C. Many road accidents were caused by carelessness.
D. Many road accidents are being caused by carelessness.

**Answer:** B. Many road accidents are caused by carelessness.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Many road accidents are caused by carelessness. (Correct):** Simple Present active ('causes') converts to 'is/am/are + V3'. With the plural subject 'Many road accidents', 'are caused' is required.
- **A — Many road accidents is caused by carelessness.:** Using singular 'is caused' creates a subject-verb agreement error with the plural subject 'Many road accidents'.
- **C — Many road accidents were caused by carelessness.:** Using 'were caused' shifts the sentence from Simple Present to Simple Past passive.
- **D — Many road accidents are being caused by carelessness.:** Using 'are being caused' incorrectly introduces 'being', changing Simple Present to Present Continuous passive.
- **Exam Extra:** Always match the passive auxiliary verb (is/are, was/were, has/have) to the number of the new passive subject, not the original active subject.

### Q513 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct active form of: "English is spoken all over the world."

A. People were speaking English all over the world.
B. People spoke English all over the world.
C. People speak English all over the world.
D. People have spoken English all over the world.

**Answer:** C. People speak English all over the world.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — People speak English all over the world. (Correct):** 'is spoken' is Simple Present passive without a stated agent, so the implied general subject 'People' is supplied with Simple Present active 'speak'.
- **A — People were speaking English all over the world.:** Using 'were speaking' converts Simple Present passive into Past Continuous active instead of Simple Present active.
- **B — People spoke English all over the world.:** Using 'spoke' shifts the tense from Simple Present to Simple Past active.
- **D — People have spoken English all over the world.:** Using 'have spoken' shifts the tense from Simple Present to Present Perfect active.
- **Exam Extra:** When an agent like 'by people' or 'by someone' is omitted in passive voice, restore an appropriate indefinite subject ('People', 'Someone', 'They') when converting to active voice.

### Q514 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct passive form of: "He does not waste his time."

A. His time was not wasted by him.
B. His time is not being wasted by him.
C. His time has not been wasted by him.
D. His time is not wasted by him.

**Answer:** D. His time is not wasted by him.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — His time is not wasted by him. (Correct):** Negative Simple Present ('does not waste') transforms into 'is/am/are + not + V3'. With the singular uncountable subject 'His time', 'is not wasted' is correct.
- **A — His time was not wasted by him.:** Using 'was not wasted' shifts the Simple Present negative to Simple Past passive.
- **B — His time is not being wasted by him.:** Using 'is not being wasted' adds 'being', shifting Simple Present to Present Continuous passive.
- **C — His time has not been wasted by him.:** Using 'has not been wasted' shifts Simple Present to Present Perfect passive.
- **Exam Extra:** Active auxiliaries 'do' and 'does' disappear in passive voice and are replaced by 'is', 'am', or 'are'.

### Q515 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct passive form of: "The clerk prepared the schedule."

A. The schedule is prepared by the clerk.
B. The schedule had been prepared by the clerk.
C. The schedule was being prepared by the clerk.
D. The schedule was prepared by the clerk.

**Answer:** D. The schedule was prepared by the clerk.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The schedule was prepared by the clerk. (Correct):** Simple Past active ('prepared') transforms into 'was/were + V3'. For singular 'The schedule', the correct passive form is 'was prepared'.
- **A — The schedule is prepared by the clerk.:** Using 'is prepared' shifts the Simple Past tense back to Simple Present passive.
- **B — The schedule had been prepared by the clerk.:** Using 'had been prepared' shifts Simple Past into Past Perfect passive.
- **C — The schedule was being prepared by the clerk.:** Using 'was being prepared' inserts 'being', changing Simple Past to Past Continuous passive.
- **Exam Extra:** Simple Past Passive formula: Object + was/were + V3 + by + Subject.

### Q516 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct passive form of: "The clerk filed the report."

A. The report was filed by the clerk.
B. The report is filed by the clerk.
C. The report has been filed by the clerk.
D. The report was being filed by the clerk.

**Answer:** A. The report was filed by the clerk.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The report was filed by the clerk. (Correct):** Simple Past active ('filed') becomes 'was/were + V3' in passive voice. Since 'The report' is singular, 'was filed' is accurate.
- **B — The report is filed by the clerk.:** Using 'is filed' shifts the Simple Past action into Simple Present passive.
- **C — The report has been filed by the clerk.:** Using 'has been filed' changes Simple Past to Present Perfect passive.
- **D — The report was being filed by the clerk.:** Using 'was being filed' adds 'being', turning Simple Past into Past Continuous passive.
- **Exam Extra:** Never add 'been' or 'being' in Simple Past passive; 'was/were + V3' alone expresses the completed past action.

### Q517 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct passive form of: "The mechanic repaired the engine."

A. The engine is repaired by the mechanic.
B. The engine had been repaired by the mechanic.
C. The engine was repaired by the mechanic.
D. The engine was repairing by the mechanic.

**Answer:** C. The engine was repaired by the mechanic.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The engine was repaired by the mechanic. (Correct):** Simple Past active ('repaired') changes to 'was/were + V3'. With singular 'The engine', 'was repaired' is correct.
- **A — The engine is repaired by the mechanic.:** Using 'is repaired' shifts Simple Past to Simple Present passive.
- **B — The engine had been repaired by the mechanic.:** Using 'had been repaired' shifts Simple Past to Past Perfect passive.
- **D — The engine was repairing by the mechanic.:** Using 'was repairing' keeps the verb in active Past Continuous instead of passive Simple Past ('was repaired').
- **Exam Extra:** In SSC exams, a frequent distractor uses 'was/were + V-ing' (active Past Continuous) in place of 'was/were + V3' (passive Simple Past).

### Q518 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct active form of: "The broken glass was swept up by the janitor."

A. The janitor sweeps up the broken glass.
B. The janitor swept up the broken glass.
C. The janitor had swept up the broken glass.
D. The janitor was sweeping up the broken glass.

**Answer:** B. The janitor swept up the broken glass.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The janitor swept up the broken glass. (Correct):** 'was swept up' is Simple Past passive, so the active form uses the Simple Past verb 'swept up' with 'The janitor' as the subject.
- **A — The janitor sweeps up the broken glass.:** Using 'sweeps up' changes Simple Past passive to Simple Present active.
- **C — The janitor had swept up the broken glass.:** Using 'had swept up' changes Simple Past passive to Past Perfect active.
- **D — The janitor was sweeping up the broken glass.:** Using 'was sweeping up' changes Simple Past passive to Past Continuous active.
- **Exam Extra:** Keep verb particles like 'up', 'out', or 'off' attached to the verb when switching between active and passive voice.

### Q519 · Simple Tenses

Select the correct passive form of: "The officer will issue the certificate."

A. The certificate is issued by the officer.
B. The certificate will be issued by the officer.
C. The certificate will have been issued by the officer.
