- **C — Priya will say that I have never seen such a vibrant painting.:** Fails to change 'I' to 'she' to match the speaker Priya.
- **Exam Extra:** A future reporting verb ('will say') blocks tense backshift; only pronouns and verb agreement with the new subject adjust.

### Q584 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Neha said, "I can solve this complex puzzle within ten minutes."

A. Neha said that she can solve this complex puzzle within ten minutes.
B. Neha said that she could solve this complex puzzle within ten minutes.
C. Neha said that she could solve that complex puzzle within ten minutes.
D. Neha told that she could solve that complex puzzle within ten minutes.

**Answer:** C. Neha said that she could solve that complex puzzle within ten minutes.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Neha said that she could solve that complex puzzle within ten minutes. (Correct):** In indirect speech, 'can' changes to 'could', 'this' changes to 'that', and 'my' changes to 'her' to match the speaker Neha.
- **A — Neha said that she can solve this complex puzzle within ten minutes.:** Fails to change the modal 'can' to 'could' after a past reporting verb.
- **B — Neha said that she could solve this complex puzzle within ten minutes.:** Retains the near-demonstrative 'this' instead of converting it to 'that'.
- **D — Neha told that she could solve that complex puzzle within ten minutes.:** Wrongly changes 'said' to 'told' without an object and retains 'my' instead of 'her'.
- **Exam Extra:** Modal shift rule: can -> could, may -> might, will -> would, shall -> should/would.

### Q585 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The manager said to the staff, "We may launch the product tonight."

A. The manager told the staff that they might launch the product that night.
B. The manager told the staff that they may launch the product that night.
C. The manager told the staff that they might launch the product tonight.
D. The manager said to the staff that they would launch the product that night.

**Answer:** A. The manager told the staff that they might launch the product that night.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The manager told the staff that they might launch the product that night. (Correct):** 'Said to' becomes 'told', 'may' shifts to 'might', 'we' changes to 'they', and 'tonight' changes to 'that night'.
- **B — The manager told the staff that they may launch the product that night.:** Keeps 'may' unchanged instead of shifting it to 'might'.
- **C — The manager told the staff that they might launch the product tonight.:** Fails to convert 'tonight' into 'that night'.
- **D — The manager said to the staff that they would launch the product that night.:** Mistakenly replaces 'may' with 'would' and leaves 'said to' unconverted.
- **Exam Extra:** Time-marker shift: 'tonight' becomes 'that night' and 'today' becomes 'that day' in indirect speech.

### Q586 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Vikram said to his friend, "I must submit my passport now."

A. Vikram told his friend that he must submit his passport now.
B. Vikram told his friend that he had to submit my passport then.
C. Vikram told to his friend that he has to submit his passport then.
D. Vikram told his friend that he had to submit his passport then.

**Answer:** D. Vikram told his friend that he had to submit his passport then.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Vikram told his friend that he had to submit his passport then. (Correct):** When 'must' expresses immediate personal obligation or compulsion at the moment of speaking, it converts to 'had to' in indirect speech; 'now' also changes to 'then' and 'my' to 'his'.
- **A — Vikram told his friend that he must submit his passport now.:** Uses 'must' unchanged despite 'now -> then' marking a specific past situational obligation, and leaves 'now' unconverted.
- **B — Vikram told his friend that he had to submit my passport then.:** Fails to change 'my' to 'his' to match the speaker Vikram.
- **C — Vikram told to his friend that he has to submit his passport then.:** Changes 'said to' to 'told' incorrectly by adding 'to' after 'told' and uses 'has to'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Must' becomes 'had to' when expressing immediate necessity/obligation, whereas it remains 'must' for permanent rules or prohibitions.

### Q587 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into direct speech: Ananya told me that she had visited her grandmother the day before.

A. Ananya said to me, "I will visit my grandmother yesterday."
B. Ananya said to me, "I visited my grandmother yesterday."
C. Ananya said to me, "I visited my grandmother the day before."
D. Ananya said to me, "I am visiting my grandmother yesterday."

**Answer:** B. Ananya said to me, "I visited my grandmother yesterday."
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Ananya said to me, "I visited my grandmother yesterday." (Correct):** Converting back to direct speech reverses 'had visited' to simple past 'visited' (because 'the day before' indicates a specific past time -> 'yesterday'), 'she' to 'I', 'her' to 'my', and 'told me' to 'said to me'.
- **A — Ananya said to me, "I will visit my grandmother yesterday.":** Uses future tense 'will visit' with 'yesterday', which is chronologically impossible.
- **C — Ananya said to me, "I visited my grandmother the day before.":** Keeps 'the day before' instead of reverting it to 'yesterday' in direct speech.
- **D — Ananya said to me, "I am visiting my grandmother yesterday.":** Uses present continuous 'am visiting' which conflicts with 'yesterday'.
- **Exam Extra:** When changing indirect speech back to direct speech, 'the day before' / 'the previous day' signals 'yesterday' with a simple past verb.

### Q588 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into direct speech: The students told the principal that they would attend the seminar the following week.

A. The students said to the principal, "We will attend the seminar next week."
B. The students said to the principal, "We would attend the seminar next week."
C. The students said to the principal, "We will attend the seminar the following week."
D. The students said to the principal, "We attended the seminar next week."

**Answer:** A. The students said to the principal, "We will attend the seminar next week."
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The students said to the principal, "We will attend the seminar next week." (Correct):** Reversing indirect speech to direct speech converts 'would attend' back to 'will attend', 'they' to 'we', and 'the following week' back to 'next week', while 'told the principal' becomes 'said to the principal'.
- **B — The students said to the principal, "We would attend the seminar next week.":** Leaves 'would' un-reverted instead of changing it back to 'will' for a future seminar next week.
- **C — The students said to the principal, "We will attend the seminar the following week.":** Retains 'the following week' instead of reverting it to 'next week' inside quotation marks.
- **D — The students said to the principal, "We attended the seminar next week.":** Uses past simple 'attended' with 'next week', creating a tense-time mismatch.
- **Exam Extra:** In indirect-to-direct conversion, 'the following week/month/year' reverts to 'next week/month/year'.

### Q589 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Tarun said, "I could not afford the tuition fee for his course."

A. Tarun said that he had not could afford the tuition fee for his course.
B. Tarun told that he cannot afford the tuition fee for his course.
C. Tarun said that he could not afford the tuition fee for his course.
D. Tarun said that he was not able afford the tuition fee for my course.

**Answer:** C. Tarun said that he could not afford the tuition fee for his course.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Tarun said that he could not afford the tuition fee for his course. (Correct):** 'Said' without an object stays 'said', 'could not' remains 'could not' (modals like could, would, should, might do not backshift further), and 'his' (already 3rd person) stays 'his'.
- **A — Tarun said that he had not could afford the tuition fee for his course.:** Incorrectly uses 'had not could', which is ungrammatical because modal auxiliaries never take 'had'.
- **B — Tarun told that he cannot afford the tuition fee for his course.:** Wrongly changes 'could not' to 'cannot' and uses 'told' without an object.
- **D — Tarun said that he was not able afford the tuition fee for my course.:** Changes 'could not' to 'was not able' missing 'to', and changes 'his' unnecessarily to 'my'.
- **Exam Extra:** Past modals (could, would, should, might) do not undergo any further tense change in indirect speech.

### Q590 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: He said, "Friends, I need your support during this crisis."

A. He said that friends, he need their support during this crisis.
B. He told his friends that he needed their support during that crisis.
C. He told his friends that he needed your support during that crisis.
D. He told his friends that he has needed their support during that crisis.

**Answer:** B. He told his friends that he needed their support during that crisis.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — He told his friends that he needed their support during that crisis. (Correct):** When a vocative noun like 'Friends' appears inside direct speech, it becomes the object of the reporting verb ('told his friends') or is introduced with 'Addressing them as friends, he said that...'; 'your' changes to 'their' and 'need' backshifts to 'needed'.
- **A — He said that friends, he need their support during this crisis.:** Keeps 'Friends' as a subject inside the 'that'-clause and leaves 'need' in the present tense.
- **C — He told his friends that he needed your support during that crisis.:** Fails to change 'your' to 'their' after making 'his friends' the object.
- **D — He told his friends that he has needed their support during that crisis.:** Retains 'have needed' (present perfect) instead of simple past 'needed'.
- **Exam Extra:** Vocatives inside quotes (e.g., "Friends, ...", "Boys, ...") are pulled out as the object of 'told' or introduced via 'Addressing ... as ...'.

### Q591 · Assertive Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The father said to his son, "You had better revise your notes carefully."

A. The father told his son that he would have better revise his notes carefully.
B. The father told his son that he had better revise your notes carefully.
C. The father told his son that he has better revise his notes carefully.
D. The father told his son that he had better revise his notes carefully.

**Answer:** D. The father told his son that he had better revise his notes carefully.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The father told his son that he had better revise his notes carefully. (Correct):** Expressions like 'had better', 'would rather', and 'used to' do not change their form in indirect speech; only 'you' changes to 'he' (matching 'his son') and 'your' changes to 'his'.
- **A — The father told his son that he would have better revise his notes carefully.:** Wrongly converts 'had better' into 'would have better', altering the modal idiom.
- **B — The father told his son that he had better revise your notes carefully.:** Fails to change the possessive pronoun 'your' to 'his'.
- **C — The father told his son that he has better revise his notes carefully.:** Changes 'had better' to 'has better', which is ungrammatical in English.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Had better', 'would rather', and 'used to' remain unchanged in indirect speech even when the reporting verb is in the past tense.

### Q592 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into indirect speech: The chemistry teacher explained, "Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius."

A. The chemistry teacher explained that water boiled at 100 degrees Celsius.
B. The chemistry teacher explained that water boils at 100 degrees Celsius.
C. The chemistry teacher explained that water was boiling at 100 degrees Celsius.
D. The chemistry teacher explained that water would boil at 100 degrees Celsius.

**Answer:** B. The chemistry teacher explained that water boils at 100 degrees Celsius.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The chemistry teacher explained that water boils at 100 degrees Celsius. (Correct):** Scientific laws and universal truths remain in the simple present tense ('boils') even when the reporting verb ('explained') is in the past tense.
- **A — The chemistry teacher explained that water boiled at 100 degrees Celsius.:** Wrongly backshifts 'boils' to 'boiled', violating the universal truth exception.
- **C — The chemistry teacher explained that water was boiling at 100 degrees Celsius.:** Uses past continuous 'was boiling', which turns a general scientific law into a one-time past event.
- **D — The chemistry teacher explained that water would boil at 100 degrees Celsius.:** Uses 'would boil', altering the meaning from a universal fact to a conditional outcome.
- **Exam Extra:** Universal truths, scientific facts, and mathematical equations never undergo tense backshift in indirect speech.

### Q593 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into indirect speech: The teacher said to the class, "The earth revolves around the sun."

A. The teacher told the class that the earth revolved around the sun.
B. The teacher told to the class that the earth had revolved around the sun.
C. The teacher told the class that the earth is revolving around the sun.
D. The teacher told the class that the earth revolves around the sun.

**Answer:** D. The teacher told the class that the earth revolves around the sun.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The teacher told the class that the earth revolves around the sun. (Correct):** Astronomical truths do not change tense in indirect speech, so 'revolves' stays in the simple present tense; 'said to the class' changes to 'told the class'.
- **A — The teacher told the class that the earth revolved around the sun.:** Backshifts 'revolves' to 'revolved', which is incorrect for a permanent astronomical fact.
- **B — The teacher told to the class that the earth had revolved around the sun.:** Uses 'told to the class' (erroneous 'to' after 'told') and shifts the verb to 'had revolved'.
- **C — The teacher told the class that the earth is revolving around the sun.:** Converts the simple present fact into present continuous 'is revolving'.
- **Exam Extra:** Natural phenomena and permanent astronomical facts always retain the simple present tense in reported speech.

### Q594 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into indirect speech: My grandfather said, "I go for a brisk walk every morning."

A. My grandfather said that he goes for a brisk walk every morning.
B. My grandfather said that he go for a brisk walk every morning.
C. My grandfather said that he went for a brisk walk every morning.
D. My grandfather told that he was going for a brisk walk every morning.

**Answer:** A. My grandfather said that he goes for a brisk walk every morning.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — My grandfather said that he goes for a brisk walk every morning. (Correct):** Habitual actions expressed with 'every morning' remain in the simple present tense in indirect speech; only the pronoun 'I' changes to 'he' and 'go' adjusts to 'goes' for third-person singular agreement.
- **B — My grandfather said that he go for a brisk walk every morning.:** Fails to add the third-person singular '-s' ending ('go' instead of 'goes') after changing 'I' to 'he'.
- **C — My grandfather said that he went for a brisk walk every morning.:** Wrongly backshifts the habitual action to simple past 'went'.
- **D — My grandfather told that he was going for a brisk walk every morning.:** Converts the habitual present into past continuous 'was going' and uses 'told' without an object.
- **Exam Extra:** Habitual facts and daily routines (marked by 'every day', 'always', 'usually') keep their simple present tense, though the verb must still agree with the new pronoun.

### Q595 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into indirect speech: Gandhiji said, "Honesty is the best policy."

A. Gandhiji said that honesty was the best policy.
B. Gandhiji said that honesty had been the best policy.
C. Gandhiji said that honesty is the best policy.
D. Gandhiji said honesty would be the best policy.

**Answer:** C. Gandhiji said that honesty is the best policy.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Gandhiji said that honesty is the best policy. (Correct):** Proverbs and moral sayings ('Honesty is the best policy') represent timeless truths and retain the simple present verb 'is' in indirect speech.
- **A — Gandhiji said that honesty was the best policy.:** Incorrectly backshifts 'is' to 'was', treating a timeless proverb as a past event.
- **B — Gandhiji said that honesty had been the best policy.:** Uses 'had been' instead of preserving 'is' for a universal moral maxim.
- **D — Gandhiji said honesty would be the best policy.:** Omits the conjunction 'that' improperly and changes 'is' to 'would be'.
- **Exam Extra:** Well-known proverbs, idioms, and maxims never backshift their tense when reported.

### Q596 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into indirect speech: The historian said, "India gained independence in 1947."

A. The historian said that India had gained independence in 1947.
B. The historian said that India gains independence in 1947.
C. The historian said that India was gained independence in 1947.
D. The historian said that India gained independence in 1947.

**Answer:** D. The historian said that India gained independence in 1947.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The historian said that India gained independence in 1947. (Correct):** Established historical facts with specific dates ('in 1947') generally do not shift from simple past to past perfect in standard SSC grammar; 'gained' remains 'gained'.
- **A — The historian said that India had gained independence in 1947.:** Shifts the historical simple past 'gained' unnecessarily to past perfect 'had gained'.
- **B — The historian said that India gains independence in 1947.:** Wrongly converts a historical past event into present simple 'gains'.
- **C — The historian said that India was gained independence in 1947.:** Uses 'was gained' (passive voice error), changing the grammatical structure of the original sentence.
- **Exam Extra:** Historical events stated in the simple past tense remain in the simple past in indirect speech rather than shifting to past perfect.

### Q597 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into indirect speech: Sita said, "While I was reading, my brother was practicing the violin."

A. Sita said that while she was reading, her brother was practicing the violin.
B. Sita said that while she had been reading, her brother had been practicing the violin.
C. Sita said that while she was reading, my brother was practicing the violin.
D. Sita said that while I had been reading, her brother was practicing the violin.

**Answer:** A. Sita said that while she was reading, her brother was practicing the violin.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Sita said that while she was reading, her brother was practicing the violin. (Correct):** Two simultaneous past actions joined by 'while' do not backshift from past continuous to past perfect continuous; only pronouns change ('I' -> 'she', 'my' -> 'her').
- **B — Sita said that while she had been reading, her brother had been practicing the violin.:** Wrongly shifts both past continuous verbs to past perfect continuous ('had been reading' and 'had been cooking').
- **C — Sita said that while she was reading, my brother was practicing the violin.:** Fails to update the possessive pronoun 'my' to 'her' to match the speaker Sita.
- **D — Sita said that while I had been reading, her brother was practicing the violin.:** Shifts only the first verb to past perfect continuous and omits the pronoun shift on 'I'.
- **Exam Extra:** Two simultaneous actions in the past continuous joined by 'while' or 'when' remain in the past continuous in indirect speech.

### Q598 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into indirect speech: The boy said, "If I were a bird, I would fly across the ocean."

A. The boy said that if he had been a bird, he would have flown across the ocean.
B. The boy said that if he were a bird, he would fly across the ocean.
C. The boy said that if he was a bird, he will fly across the ocean.
D. The boy said that if he had been a bird, he would fly across the ocean.

**Answer:** B. The boy said that if he were a bird, he would fly across the ocean.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The boy said that if he were a bird, he would fly across the ocean. (Correct):** Hypothetical or unreal past conditions ('If I were...') do not undergo any tense shift in indirect speech; only the pronouns change ('I' -> 'he', 'I' -> 'he') and 'would' remains 'would'.
- **A — The boy said that if he had been a bird, he would have flown across the ocean.:** Incorrectly shifts 'were' to 'had been' and 'would' to 'would have', turning a present unreal condition into a third conditional.
- **C — The boy said that if he was a bird, he will fly across the ocean.:** Changes the subjunctive 'were' to 'was' and drops 'would' to 'will'.
- **D — The boy said that if he had been a bird, he would fly across the ocean.:** Uses 'had been' instead of keeping the subjunctive 'were' intact.
- **Exam Extra:** Unreal past situations using 'If I were...' or 'I wish I knew...' remain unchanged in tense during narration conversion.

### Q599 · Universal Truths & Habitual Facts

Convert into direct speech: The physicist told the students that light travels faster than sound.

A. The physicist said to the students, "Light travelled faster than sound."
B. The physicist said to the students, "Light will travel faster than sound."
C. The physicist said to the students, "Light travels faster than sound."
D. The physicist said the students, "Light travels faster than sound."

**Answer:** C. The physicist said to the students, "Light travels faster than sound."
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The physicist said to the students, "Light travels faster than sound." (Correct):** Reverting the indirect universal truth to direct speech keeps 'travels' in the simple present tense and restores 'told the students' to 'said to the students'.
- **A — The physicist said to the students, "Light travelled faster than sound.":** Incorrectly puts the universal truth in past simple 'travelled' inside direct speech.
- **B — The physicist said to the students, "Light will travel faster than sound.":** Uses future tense 'will travel' instead of the timeless simple present 'travels'.
- **D — The physicist said the students, "Light travels faster than sound.":** Uses 'said the students' without the required preposition 'to' before the object.
- **Exam Extra:** In both direct and indirect speech, universal physical truths like the speed of light retain the simple present tense.

### Q600 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: She said to me, "Do you like classical music?"

A. She asked me if I liked classical music.
B. She asked me whether I did like classical music.
C. She asked me that if I like classical music.
D. She said to me if did I like classical music.

**Answer:** A. She asked me if I liked classical music.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — She asked me if I liked classical music. (Correct):** A Yes/No interrogative takes 'asked' + 'if' (or 'whether'), drops the dummy auxiliary 'do', shifts the verb to simple past 'liked', and uses assertive word order (Subject + Verb) ending with a full stop.
- **B — She asked me whether I did like classical music.:** Retains 'did' in an assertive clause ('whether I did like') instead of merging it into simple past 'liked'.
- **C — She asked me that if I like classical music.:** Redundantly inserts 'that' before 'if' and fails to backshift 'like' to 'liked'.
- **D — She said to me if did I like classical music.:** Keeps interrogative inversion ('did I like') and uses 'said to' instead of 'asked'.
- **Exam Extra:** Never use 'that' before 'if' or 'whether', and always drop 'do/does/did' when converting an interrogative to assertive indirect order.

### Q601 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: My friend said to me, "Did you attend the workshop yesterday?"

A. My friend asked me if I attended the workshop the previous day.
B. My friend asked me that whether I had attended the workshop yesterday.
C. My friend asked me whether I had attended the workshop the previous day.
D. My friend asked me if had I attended the workshop the previous day?

**Answer:** C. My friend asked me whether I had attended the workshop the previous day.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — My friend asked me whether I had attended the workshop the previous day. (Correct):** A Yes/No question in the simple past ('Did you attend...') converts using 'asked' + 'if/whether', drops 'did', and backshifts the verb to past perfect ('had attended'), changing 'yesterday' to 'the previous day'.
- **A — My friend asked me if I attended the workshop the previous day.:** Leaves the verb in simple past 'attended' instead of shifting 'did attend' to past perfect 'had attended'.
- **B — My friend asked me that whether I had attended the workshop yesterday.:** Incorrectly puts 'that' before 'whether' and retains 'yesterday'.
- **D — My friend asked me if had I attended the workshop the previous day?:** Keeps interrogative word order ('had I attended') and ends with a question mark instead of assertive order with a full stop.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Did + V1' in a direct question always converts to 'had + V3' in indirect speech.

### Q602 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: They said to me, "Are you coming with us or not?"

A. They told me whether I am coming with them or not.
B. They asked me whether I was coming with them or not.
C. They asked me that whether I was coming with us or not.
D. They asked me whether was I coming with us or not.

**Answer:** B. They asked me whether I was coming with them or not.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — They asked me whether I was coming with them or not. (Correct):** When a Yes/No question presents an explicit alternative with 'or not', 'whether' is the standard conjunction; 'are coming' backshifts to 'were coming' (or 'was coming' depending on singular/plural 'you'), 'us' changes to 'them', and 'said to' becomes 'asked'.
- **A — They told me whether I am coming with them or not.:** Uses 'told' instead of 'asked' for a question and leaves 'are coming' unshifted.
- **C — They asked me that whether I was coming with us or not.:** Retains the direct pronoun 'us' instead of changing it to 'them' and uses 'that whether'.
- **D — They asked me whether was I coming with us or not.:** Keeps interrogative inversion ('was I coming') and forgets to shift 'us' to 'them'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'whether' rather than 'if' when the sentence contains 'or not' or offers two explicit choices.

### Q603 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Deepa said to Rahul, "Can you lend me your dictionary?"

A. Deepa asked Rahul if could he lend her his dictionary.
B. Deepa asked Rahul if he can lend me his dictionary.
C. Deepa told Rahul that if he could lend her his dictionary.
D. Deepa asked Rahul if he could lend her his dictionary.

**Answer:** D. Deepa asked Rahul if he could lend her his dictionary.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Deepa asked Rahul if he could lend her his dictionary. (Correct):** 'Said to' becomes 'asked', 'if' introduces the Yes/No question, 'can' shifts to 'could', 'you' changes to 'he', 'me' changes to 'her' (speaker Deepa), and 'your' changes to 'his'.
- **A — Deepa asked Rahul if could he lend her his dictionary.:** Keeps the modal 'could' before the subject 'he' (interrogative inversion) instead of assertive order.
- **B — Deepa asked Rahul if he can lend me his dictionary.:** Fails to shift 'can' to 'could' and leaves 'me' unchanged instead of 'her'.
- **C — Deepa told Rahul that if he could lend her his dictionary.:** Uses 'told' instead of 'asked' and 'that if' together.
- **Exam Extra:** In interrogatives with modals (Can/Will/May), convert to 'if/whether + Subject + could/would/might + V1'—never keep the modal before the subject.

### Q604 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The policeman said to the girl, "Where are you going so late at night?"

A. The policeman asked the girl where was she going so late at night.
B. The policeman asked the girl that where she was going so late at night.
C. The policeman asked the girl where she was going so late at night.
D. The policeman asked the girl where she is going so late at night.

**Answer:** C. The policeman asked the girl where she was going so late at night.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The policeman asked the girl where she was going so late at night. (Correct):** In a Wh-question, the Wh-word ('where') itself acts as the connector without 'that' or 'if', word order becomes assertive ('she was going'), 'said to' becomes 'asked', and 'you' changes to 'she'.
- **A — The policeman asked the girl where was she going so late at night.:** Keeps interrogative inversion ('was she going') instead of assertive Subject + Verb order.
- **B — The policeman asked the girl that where she was going so late at night.:** Wrongly inserts 'that' before the Wh-word 'where'.
- **D — The policeman asked the girl where she is going so late at night.:** Leaves the verb in present continuous 'is going' instead of backshifting it to 'was going'.
- **Exam Extra:** In Wh-questions, never insert 'that', 'if', or 'whether' before the Wh-word, and always place the subject before the verb.

### Q605 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The officer said to the clerk, "Why have you not submitted the file?"

A. The officer asked the clerk why he had not submitted the file.
B. The officer asked the clerk why had he not submitted the file.
C. The officer asked the clerk that why he have not submitted the file.
D. The officer asked the clerk why he did not submit the file.

**Answer:** A. The officer asked the clerk why he had not submitted the file.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The officer asked the clerk why he had not submitted the file. (Correct):** 'Said to' becomes 'asked', 'why' links the clauses directly, 'you' changes to 'he' (matching 'the clerk'), and present perfect 'have not submitted' shifts to past perfect 'had not submitted' in assertive order.
- **B — The officer asked the clerk why had he not submitted the file.:** Keeps the negative auxiliary before the subject ('had he not submitted'), violating assertive word order.
- **C — The officer asked the clerk that why he have not submitted the file.:** Uses 'that why' and leaves 'have not submitted' unshifted.
- **D — The officer asked the clerk why he did not submit the file.:** Incorrectly drops 'had' and shifts the verb to simple past 'did not submit' instead of past perfect.
- **Exam Extra:** Negative Wh-questions follow the exact same assertive rule in indirect speech: Wh-word + Subject + auxiliary + not + main verb.

### Q606 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The angry mother said, "Who broke this porcelain vase?"

A. The angry mother asked who broke this porcelain vase.
B. The angry mother asked that who had broken this porcelain vase.
C. The angry mother asked who did break that porcelain vase.
D. The angry mother asked who had broken that porcelain vase.

**Answer:** D. The angry mother asked who had broken that porcelain vase.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The angry mother asked who had broken that porcelain vase. (Correct):** 'Who' acts as both connector and subject; simple past 'broke' shifts to past perfect 'had broken', and 'this' changes to 'that'. 'Demanded' or 'asked' fits the angry inquiry.
- **A — The angry mother asked who broke this porcelain vase.:** Leaves 'broke' in the simple past instead of backshifting to past perfect 'had broken', and keeps 'this'.
- **B — The angry mother asked that who had broken this porcelain vase.:** Wrongly adds 'that' before 'who' and retains 'this'.
- **C — The angry mother asked who did break that porcelain vase.:** Uses 'did break' in an indirect clause instead of past perfect 'had broken'.
- **Exam Extra:** When 'who' is the subject of a simple past question ('Who broke...?'), it converts cleanly to 'who had broken...' in indirect speech.

### Q607 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The librarian said to me, "Which book do you prefer?"

A. The librarian asked me which book did I prefer.
B. The librarian asked me which book I preferred.
C. The librarian asked me that which book I prefer.
D. The librarian asked me which book did I prefer?

**Answer:** B. The librarian asked me which book I preferred.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The librarian asked me which book I preferred. (Correct):** 'Which book' stays together as the Wh-phrase connector; 'do' is dropped, 'you' changes to 'I', and simple present 'prefer' shifts to simple past 'preferred'.
- **A — The librarian asked me which book did I prefer.:** Keeps 'did I prefer' instead of dropping the dummy auxiliary and using simple past 'preferred'.
- **C — The librarian asked me that which book I prefer.:** Places 'that' before 'which book' and fails to backshift 'prefer'.
- **D — The librarian asked me which book did I prefer?:** Retains interrogative word order ('did I prefer') and ends with a question mark.
- **Exam Extra:** Phrasal Wh-interrogatives ('which book', 'how many pens', 'whose bag') move as one unit before the subject in indirect speech.

### Q608 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The helper said, "Where shall I keep this heavy crate?"

A. The helper asked where he should keep that heavy crate.
B. The helper asked where he would keep this heavy crate.
C. The helper asked where should he keep that heavy crate.
D. The helper asked that where he shall keep that heavy crate.

**Answer:** A. The helper asked where he should keep that heavy crate.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The helper asked where he should keep that heavy crate. (Correct):** When 'Shall I/we...?' asks for instruction, advice, or suggestion (not simple future), 'shall' changes to 'should' in indirect speech; 'this' also becomes 'that'.
- **B — The helper asked where he would keep this heavy crate.:** Uses 'would' instead of 'should' even though the speaker is asking for instruction/advice, and retains 'this'.
- **C — The helper asked where should he keep that heavy crate.:** Keeps interrogative inversion ('should he keep') instead of assertive order.
- **D — The helper asked that where he shall keep that heavy crate.:** Wrongly inserts 'that' before 'where' and uses 'shall' unshifted.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Shall' becomes 'would' for pure future statements, but becomes 'should' when asking for advice, permission, or instructions.

### Q609 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into direct speech: He asked me when the train would arrive.

A. He said to me, "When the train will arrive?"
B. He said to me, "When would the train arrive?"
C. He asked to me, "When the train would arrive?"
D. He said to me, "When will the train arrive?"

**Answer:** D. He said to me, "When will the train arrive?"
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — He said to me, "When will the train arrive?" (Correct):** Reverting the indirect Wh-question to direct speech restores 'asked me' to 'said to me', 'would arrive' to 'will arrive', and places the auxiliary 'will' before the subject 'the train' with a question mark.
- **A — He said to me, "When the train will arrive?":** Keeps assertive word order ('the train will arrive') inside a direct question instead of inverting 'will' before the subject.
- **B — He said to me, "When would the train arrive?":** Retains 'would' instead of reverting it to 'will' inside the direct question.
- **C — He asked to me, "When the train would arrive?":** Uses 'asked to me' (ungrammatical 'to' after 'asked') and omits the auxiliary inversion.
- **Exam Extra:** When converting indirect questions back to direct speech, always restore Subject-Auxiliary inversion and end with a question mark.

### Q610 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into direct speech: She inquired whether I had seen her keys anywhere.

A. She said to me, "Had you seen her keys anywhere?"
B. She said to me, "Have you seen my keys anywhere?"
C. She said to me, "Whether you saw my keys anywhere?"
D. She told to me, "You have seen my keys anywhere."

**Answer:** B. She said to me, "Have you seen my keys anywhere?"
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — She said to me, "Have you seen my keys anywhere?" (Correct):** 'Inquired whether I had seen her keys anywhere' reverts to 'said to me, "Have you seen my keys anywhere?"' (or simple past 'Did you see'), with 'whether' dropped, 'I' -> 'you', 'her' -> 'my', and auxiliary inversion restored.
- **A — She said to me, "Had you seen her keys anywhere?":** Keeps 'had' in past perfect and fails to revert 'her' to 'my'.
- **C — She said to me, "Whether you saw my keys anywhere?":** Retains 'whether' inside the direct quotation and omits inversion.
- **D — She told to me, "You have seen my keys anywhere.":** Uses 'told to me' instead of 'said to me' and keeps assertive order without a question mark.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Whether/if' is always removed and replaced by an inverted Yes/No question mark structure when converting back to direct speech.

### Q611 · Interrogative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The mother said to the child, "Have you finished your lunch?" "Yes," he replied.

A. The mother asked the child if he had finished your lunch and he said yes.
B. The mother asked the child if he have finished his lunch and he replied in the negative.
C. The mother asked the child if he had finished his lunch and he replied in the affirmative.
D. The mother asked the child that if he had finished his lunch and he answered yes.

**Answer:** C. The mother asked the child if he had finished his lunch and he replied in the affirmative.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The mother asked the child if he had finished his lunch and he replied in the affirmative. (Correct):** Short replies 'Yes' or 'No' following a question are reported as 'replied in the affirmative' / 'replied in the negative' (or 'replied that he had/had not'); here 'Have you finished...' converts to 'asked if he had finished his lunch' and '"Yes."' becomes 'and he replied in the affirmative'.
- **A — The mother asked the child if he had finished your lunch and he said yes.:** Keeps the direct word 'yes' unidiomatically and fails to shift 'your' to 'his'.
- **B — The mother asked the child if he have finished his lunch and he replied in the negative.:** Leaves 'have finished' unshifted and writes 'replied in the negative' which contradicts 'Yes'.
- **D — The mother asked the child that if he had finished his lunch and he answered yes.:** Wrongly uses 'that if' and omits conversion of the 'Yes' response into indirect phrasing.
- **Exam Extra:** In two-part dialogues, '"Yes"' converts to 'replied in the affirmative' and '"No"' converts to 'replied in the negative'.

### Q612 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The guard said to the intruder, "Leave this premises immediately."

A. The guard ordered the intruder that he should leave this premises immediately.
B. The guard ordered the intruder to leave that premises immediately.
C. The guard said to the intruder to leave this premises immediately.
D. The guard ordered to the intruder leave that premises immediately.

**Answer:** B. The guard ordered the intruder to leave that premises immediately.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The guard ordered the intruder to leave that premises immediately. (Correct):** An authoritative command converts 'said to' into 'ordered', links the imperative verb with 'to + V1' ('to leave'), and changes 'this' to 'that'.
- **A — The guard ordered the intruder that he should leave this premises immediately.:** Uses a 'that'-clause with 'should' incorrectly and leaves 'this' unchanged.
- **C — The guard said to the intruder to leave this premises immediately.:** Retains 'said to' and places 'to' improperly, failing to use an imperative reporting verb.
- **D — The guard ordered to the intruder leave that premises immediately.:** Uses 'ordered to the intruder' (erroneous 'to' after 'ordered') and omits the infinitive marker 'to' before 'leave'.
- **Exam Extra:** Imperative sentences use 'to + V1' (infinitive) instead of 'that' when reported with verbs like ordered, requested, or advised.

### Q613 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Anita said to me, "Please help me carry this heavy suitcase."

A. Anita requested me to help her carry that heavy suitcase.
B. Anita requested me to please help her carry that heavy suitcase.
C. Anita requested me to help me carry this heavy suitcase.
D. Anita requested to me that I help her carry that heavy suitcase.

**Answer:** A. Anita requested me to help her carry that heavy suitcase.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Anita requested me to help her carry that heavy suitcase. (Correct):** 'Please' indicates a polite request, so 'said to' becomes 'requested', the word 'please' is dropped, the imperative connects via 'to help', 'me' changes to 'her', and 'this' becomes 'that'.
- **B — Anita requested me to please help her carry that heavy suitcase.:** Redundantly retains 'please' alongside 'requested', which is a classic SSC error.
- **C — Anita requested me to help me carry this heavy suitcase.:** Fails to change 'me' to 'her' and 'this' to 'that'.
- **D — Anita requested to me that I help her carry that heavy suitcase.:** Uses 'requested to me' (wrong preposition after 'requested') and replaces 'to' with 'that'.
- **Exam Extra:** Words of politeness like 'please' and 'kindly' are always omitted in indirect speech once 'requested' is used.

### Q614 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The gardener said to the children, "Do not pluck these roses."

A. The gardener forbade the children not to pluck those roses.
B. The gardener forbade the children do not pluck these roses.
C. The gardener said to the children to not pluck those roses.
D. The gardener forbade the children to pluck those roses.

**Answer:** D. The gardener forbade the children to pluck those roses.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The gardener forbade the children to pluck those roses. (Correct):** Negative imperatives can use 'forbade + object + to + V1'; because 'forbade' already carries a negative meaning, 'not' must NOT be used after it, and 'these' changes to 'those'.
- **A — The gardener forbade the children not to pluck those roses.:** Commits a double-negative error by using 'not' after 'forbade'.
- **B — The gardener forbade the children do not pluck these roses.:** Retains the direct demonstrative 'these' instead of changing it to 'those' and uses 'do not' inside indirect speech.
- **C — The gardener said to the children to not pluck those roses.:** Places 'to' before 'not' improperly ('to not pluck') and uses 'said to' instead of an imperative verb.
- **Exam Extra:** Never use 'not' after 'forbade' or 'prohibited'; write 'forbade them to pluck', NOT 'forbade them not to pluck'.

### Q615 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The electrician said to the apprentice, "Don't touch this exposed wire."

A. The electrician forbade the apprentice not to touch that exposed wire.
B. The electrician warned the apprentice not touch that exposed wire.
C. The electrician warned the apprentice not to touch that exposed wire.
D. The electrician told the apprentice do not touch this exposed wire.

**Answer:** C. The electrician warned the apprentice not to touch that exposed wire.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The electrician warned the apprentice not to touch that exposed wire. (Correct):** A warning imperative in the negative converts to 'warned + object + not to + V1', placing 'not' strictly before 'to touch' and converting 'this' to 'that'.
- **A — The electrician forbade the apprentice not to touch that exposed wire.:** Uses 'forbade ... not to', creating a forbidden double negative.
- **B — The electrician warned the apprentice not touch that exposed wire.:** Incorrectly puts 'not' after 'to' or omits 'to' entirely ('not touch').
- **D — The electrician told the apprentice do not touch this exposed wire.:** Uses 'told' with 'do not touch' directly instead of converting to an infinitive phrase.
- **Exam Extra:** In negative imperatives with 'advised', 'ordered', or 'warned', the correct order is 'not to + V1', never 'to not + V1' or 'forbade ... not to'.

### Q616 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The doctor said to the patient, "Take your medicines regularly."

A. The doctor advised the patient to take his medicines regularly.
B. The doctor advised to the patient to take your medicines regularly.
C. The doctor advised the patient that he takes his medicines regularly.
D. The doctor ordered the patient to take your medicines regularly.

**Answer:** A. The doctor advised the patient to take his medicines regularly.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The doctor advised the patient to take his medicines regularly. (Correct):** Medical or helpful guidance converts 'said to' into 'advised', removes quotation marks, uses 'to take', and changes 'your' to 'his' (matching 'the patient').
- **B — The doctor advised to the patient to take your medicines regularly.:** Uses 'advised to the patient' (erroneous 'to' after 'advised') and forgets to change 'your' to 'his'.
- **C — The doctor advised the patient that he takes his medicines regularly.:** Uses a 'that'-clause with present tense 'takes' instead of the infinitive 'to take'.
- **D — The doctor ordered the patient to take your medicines regularly.:** Wrongly converts advice into a harsh command ('ordered') and retains 'your'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Advised' is the preferred reporting verb when a doctor, elder, or teacher gives beneficial counsel.

### Q617 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Kavita said to her cousins, "Do attend my brother's wedding."

A. Kavita requested her cousins to do attend her brother's wedding.
B. Kavita told her cousins that do attend my brother's wedding.
C. Kavita ordered her cousins to attend my brother's wedding.
D. Kavita urged her cousins to attend her brother's wedding.

**Answer:** D. Kavita urged her cousins to attend her brother's wedding.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Kavita urged her cousins to attend her brother's wedding. (Correct):** An emphatic imperative beginning with 'Do' conveys strong persuasion or urging, so 'said to' becomes 'urged' (or 'insisted'/'requested'), 'do' is dropped, and 'my' changes to 'her'.
- **A — Kavita requested her cousins to do attend her brother's wedding.:** Retains the emphatic 'do' inside the infinitive ('to do attend'), which is ungrammatical.
- **B — Kavita told her cousins that do attend my brother's wedding.:** Fails to change 'my' to 'her' and keeps 'do' inside a 'that'-clause.
- **C — Kavita ordered her cousins to attend my brother's wedding.:** Uses 'ordered' inappropriately for an invitation and leaves 'my' unchanged.
- **Exam Extra:** Emphatic 'Do + V1' in direct imperatives drops 'do' in indirect speech and uses reporting verbs like 'urged', 'pressed', or 'entreated'.

### Q618 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The customer said to the waiter, "Could you please bring a glass of water?"

A. The customer asked the waiter if he could please bring a glass of water.
B. The customer requested the waiter to bring a glass of water.
C. The customer requested the waiter to please bring a glass of water.
D. The customer ordered the waiter to please bring a glass of water.

**Answer:** B. The customer requested the waiter to bring a glass of water.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The customer requested the waiter to bring a glass of water. (Correct):** Sentences structured as polite questions ('Could you please...?') function semantically as requests, not literal inquiries, so they convert to 'requested + object + to + V1' ('requested the waiter to bring...').
- **A — The customer asked the waiter if he could please bring a glass of water.:** Treats the polite request literally as a Yes/No question and keeps 'please' redundantly.
- **C — The customer requested the waiter to please bring a glass of water.:** Retains 'please' inside the infinitive phrase after 'requested'.
- **D — The customer ordered the waiter to please bring a glass of water.:** Uses 'ordered' instead of 'requested' despite 'Could you please' and keeps 'please'.
- **Exam Extra:** Polite question-requests ('Could you please...', 'Would you mind...') are reported as imperatives using 'requested', not as interrogatives with 'if'.

### Q619 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The major said to the platoon, "March forward without fear!"

A. The major told the platoon march forward without fear.
B. The major commanded the platoon that to march forward without fear.
C. The major commanded the platoon to march forward without fear.
D. The major commanded to the platoon that they marches forward without fear.

**Answer:** C. The major commanded the platoon to march forward without fear.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The major commanded the platoon to march forward without fear. (Correct):** Military commands use 'commanded' + object + 'to + V1', so 'said to the platoon' becomes 'commanded the platoon to march'.
- **A — The major told the platoon march forward without fear.:** Uses 'told' with a direct bare infinitive 'march' without 'to'.
- **B — The major commanded the platoon that to march forward without fear.:** Incorrectly inserts 'that' before 'to march'.
- **D — The major commanded to the platoon that they marches forward without fear.:** Uses 'commanded to the platoon' (ungrammatical 'to' after 'commanded') and keeps present tense 'marches'.
- **Exam Extra:** In military or official contexts, 'commanded' is the most accurate reporting verb for orders.

### Q620 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into direct speech: The doctor forbade the patient to eat oily food.

A. The doctor said to the patient, "Eat oily food."
B. The doctor said to the patient, "Do not eat oily food."
C. The doctor forbade the patient, "Not to eat oily food."
D. The doctor said to the patient, "You did not eat oily food."

**Answer:** B. The doctor said to the patient, "Do not eat oily food."
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The doctor said to the patient, "Do not eat oily food." (Correct):** Converting 'forbade the patient to eat' back to direct speech restores a negative imperative ('Do not eat'), and 'forbade' becomes 'said to'.
- **A — The doctor said to the patient, "Eat oily food.":** Omits 'Do not', turning a prohibition into a positive instruction to eat oily food.
- **C — The doctor forbade the patient, "Not to eat oily food.":** Uses 'forbade' inside direct speech as a reporting verb and keeps 'not to eat'.
- **D — The doctor said to the patient, "You did not eat oily food.":** Incorrectly turns the imperative into an assertive statement with 'must' instead of a direct imperative.
- **Exam Extra:** When reversing 'forbade + object + to + V1' into direct speech, always restore the negative imperative 'Do not + V1'.

### Q621 · Imperative Sentences

Convert into direct speech: The beggar implored the passerby to give him a morsel of food.

A. The beggar said to the passerby, "Please give me a morsel of food."
B. The beggar said to the passerby, "Please give him a morsel of food."
C. The beggar said to the passerby, "Please gave me a morsel of food."
D. The beggar asked the passerby, "Give him a morsel of food?"

**Answer:** A. The beggar said to the passerby, "Please give me a morsel of food."
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The beggar said to the passerby, "Please give me a morsel of food." (Correct):** 'Implored' signals an earnest plea/entreaty ('Please' or 'For heaven's sake'), 'to give him' reverts to 'give me' (matching the speaker, the beggar), and 'implored the passerby' becomes 'said to the passerby'.
- **B — The beggar said to the passerby, "Please give him a morsel of food.":** Retains third-person 'him' instead of reverting it to first-person 'me'.
- **C — The beggar said to the passerby, "Please gave me a morsel of food.":** Uses a past verb 'gave' inside an imperative quote instead of base verb 'give'.
- **D — The beggar asked the passerby, "Give him a morsel of food?":** Drops 'me' and writes 'give him' with a question mark instead of an imperative plea.
- **Exam Extra:** Reporting verbs like 'implored', 'begged', and 'entreated' revert to 'said to' with polite/earnest markers like 'Please' in direct speech.

### Q622 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The players said, "Hurrah! We have won the championship."

A. The players exclaimed with joy that Hurrah, they have won the championship.
B. The players exclaimed with sorrow that they had won the championship.
C. The players exclaimed with joy that they had won the championship.
D. The players said that we had won the championship.

**Answer:** C. The players exclaimed with joy that they had won the championship.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The players exclaimed with joy that they had won the championship. (Correct):** 'Hurrah!' expresses joy, so 'said' becomes 'exclaimed with joy', the exclamation mark and 'Hurrah!' are dropped, 'we' changes to 'they', and 'have won' backshifts to 'had won'.
- **A — The players exclaimed with joy that Hurrah, they have won the championship.:** Retains 'Hurrah!' inside indirect speech and leaves 'have won' unshifted.
- **B — The players exclaimed with sorrow that they had won the championship.:** Uses 'exclaimed with sorrow' which contradicts the joyful interjection 'Hurrah!'.
- **D — The players said that we had won the championship.:** Fails to change 'we' to 'they' and uses 'said' instead of an exclamatory reporting verb.
- **Exam Extra:** Interjection mapping: Hurrah! -> exclaimed with joy; Alas! -> exclaimed with sorrow; Oh!/Good heavens! -> exclaimed with surprise.

### Q623 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The old woman said, "Alas! My loyal dog is no more."

A. The old woman exclaimed with sorrow that her loyal dog is no more.
B. The old woman exclaimed with sorrow that alas her loyal dog was no more!
C. The old woman exclaimed with wonder that my loyal dog was no more.
D. The old woman exclaimed with sorrow that her loyal dog was no more.

**Answer:** D. The old woman exclaimed with sorrow that her loyal dog was no more.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The old woman exclaimed with sorrow that her loyal dog was no more. (Correct):** 'Alas!' conveys grief, so 'said' becomes 'exclaimed with sorrow', 'my' changes to 'her' (for the old woman), and 'is' backshifts to 'was'.
- **A — The old woman exclaimed with sorrow that her loyal dog is no more.:** Leaves 'is' in the present tense instead of backshifting it to 'was'.
- **B — The old woman exclaimed with sorrow that alas her loyal dog was no more!:** Keeps the interjection 'Alas' inside the reported clause and ends with an exclamation mark.
- **C — The old woman exclaimed with wonder that my loyal dog was no more.:** Fails to convert 'my' to 'her' and wrongly uses 'exclaimed with wonder'.
- **Exam Extra:** In exclamatory indirect speech, always convert the exclamation into an assertive 'that'-clause ending with a full stop.

### Q624 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The judge said to him, "Bravo! You have performed brilliantly."

A. The judge applauded him, saying that he had performed brilliantly.
B. The judge told him that bravo he had performed brilliantly.
C. The judge applauded him, saying that he have performed brilliantly.
D. The judge exclaimed with sorrow that you had performed brilliantly.

**Answer:** A. The judge applauded him, saying that he had performed brilliantly.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The judge applauded him, saying that he had performed brilliantly. (Correct):** 'Bravo!' expresses praise/applause, so 'said to him' becomes 'applauded him, saying that', 'you' changes to 'he', and 'have performed' backshifts to 'had performed'.
- **B — The judge told him that bravo he had performed brilliantly.:** Retains 'Bravo' inside the indirect clause and uses 'told' instead of 'applauded'.
- **C — The judge applauded him, saying that he have performed brilliantly.:** Leaves 'have performed' in the present perfect instead of shifting it to 'had performed'.
- **D — The judge exclaimed with sorrow that you had performed brilliantly.:** Fails to change 'you' to 'he' and misuses 'exclaimed with sorrow'.
- **Exam Extra:** Sentences starting with 'Bravo!' convert uniquely using 'applauded + object + saying that...'.

### Q625 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The tourist said, "What a magnificent monument it is!"

A. The tourist exclaimed with wonder that what a magnificent monument it was.
B. The tourist exclaimed with wonder that it was a very magnificent monument.
C. The tourist exclaimed with wonder that it is a very magnificent monument!
D. The tourist exclaimed with sorrow that it was very magnificent monument.

**Answer:** B. The tourist exclaimed with wonder that it was a very magnificent monument.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The tourist exclaimed with wonder that it was a very magnificent monument. (Correct):** 'What a magnificent monument it is!' converts to an assertive clause using 'exclaimed with wonder/admiration that it was a very magnificent monument', backshifting 'is' to 'was' and replacing 'What a' with 'a very'.
- **A — The tourist exclaimed with wonder that what a magnificent monument it was.:** Keeps the exclamatory word 'what' and verb-final order instead of converting to assertive structure.
- **C — The tourist exclaimed with wonder that it is a very magnificent monument!:** Leaves 'is' unshifted in the present tense and ends with an exclamation mark.
- **D — The tourist exclaimed with sorrow that it was very magnificent monument.:** Uses 'exclaimed with sorrow' inappropriately and omits the article 'a' before 'very magnificent monument'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'What a/an + adjective + noun!' becomes 'a very + adjective + noun' in assertive indirect speech, while 'What a + noun!' (without an adjective) uses 'a great + noun'.

### Q626 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Nisha said, "How foolish I have been!"

A. Nisha exclaimed with regret that how foolish she had been.
B. Nisha exclaimed with regret that she have been very foolish.
C. Nisha exclaimed with regret that how foolish I had been.
D. Nisha exclaimed with regret that she had been very foolish.

**Answer:** D. Nisha exclaimed with regret that she had been very foolish.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Nisha exclaimed with regret that she had been very foolish. (Correct):** 'How foolish I have been!' converts 'How + adjective' into 'very + adjective' in assertive Subject-Verb order ('she had been very foolish'), with 'I' -> 'she' and 'have been' -> 'had been'.
- **A — Nisha exclaimed with regret that how foolish she had been.:** Retains 'how' inside the indirect clause instead of replacing it with 'very'.
- **B — Nisha exclaimed with regret that she have been very foolish.:** Leaves 'have been' unshifted instead of backshifting it to 'had been'.
- **C — Nisha exclaimed with regret that how foolish I had been.:** Fails to change 'I' to 'she' and keeps 'how foolish' at the start of the clause.
- **Exam Extra:** Exclamations starting with 'How + adjective/adverb' transform into 'Subject + verb + very + adjective/adverb' in indirect speech.

### Q627 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: She said to him, "Fie! You have betrayed your own friend."

A. She exclaimed with joy that he had betrayed his own friend.
B. She told him that fie he had betrayed your own friend.
C. She exclaimed with disgust that he had betrayed his own friend.
D. She exclaimed with disgust that he have betrayed his own friend.

**Answer:** C. She exclaimed with disgust that he had betrayed his own friend.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — She exclaimed with disgust that he had betrayed his own friend. (Correct):** 'Fie!' expresses disgust or contempt, so 'said to' becomes 'exclaimed with disgust/contempt that', 'you' changes to 'he', 'have betrayed' shifts to 'had betrayed', and 'your' changes to 'his'.
- **A — She exclaimed with joy that he had betrayed his own friend.:** Misinterprets 'Fie!' as joy instead of disgust/contempt.
- **B — She told him that fie he had betrayed your own friend.:** Retains 'Fie' inside the reported clause and fails to change 'your' to 'his'.
- **D — She exclaimed with disgust that he have betrayed his own friend.:** Leaves 'have betrayed' unshifted in the present perfect tense.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Fie!' or 'Pooh!' maps to 'exclaimed with disgust/contempt' in indirect narration.

### Q628 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The bystander said, "Good heavens! The old roof has collapsed."

A. The bystander exclaimed with surprise that the old roof had collapsed.
B. The bystander exclaimed with surprise that the old roof has collapsed.
C. The bystander exclaimed that good heavens the old roof had collapsed.
D. The bystander prayed that the old roof had collapsed.

**Answer:** A. The bystander exclaimed with surprise that the old roof had collapsed.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The bystander exclaimed with surprise that the old roof had collapsed. (Correct):** 'Good heavens!' expresses sudden shock or surprise, so 'said' becomes 'exclaimed with surprise/shock', and present perfect 'has collapsed' backshifts to past perfect 'had collapsed'.
- **B — The bystander exclaimed with surprise that the old roof has collapsed.:** Leaves 'has collapsed' in the present perfect instead of shifting it to 'had collapsed'.
- **C — The bystander exclaimed that good heavens the old roof had collapsed.:** Retains 'Good heavens' inside the indirect 'that'-clause.
- **D — The bystander prayed that the old roof had collapsed.:** Wrongly uses 'prayed' as if it were an optative sentence instead of an exclamation of shock.
- **Exam Extra:** Phrases like 'Good heavens!' or 'My God!' convert to 'exclaimed with surprise/shock' and are dropped from the 'that'-clause.

### Q629 · Exclamatory Sentences

Convert into direct speech: She exclaimed with sorrow that she had lost her diamond ring.

A. She said, "Hurrah! I have lost my diamond ring."
B. She said, "Alas! I have lost my diamond ring."
C. She said, "Alas! She had lost her diamond ring."
D. She said, "Bravo! I had lost my diamond ring."

**Answer:** B. She said, "Alas! I have lost my diamond ring."
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — She said, "Alas! I have lost my diamond ring." (Correct):** Reverting 'exclaimed with sorrow that she had lost her diamond ring' to direct speech restores 'Alas!' (matching sorrow), converts 'had lost' back to 'have lost' (or 'lost'), 'she' to 'I', and 'her' to 'my'.
- **A — She said, "Hurrah! I have lost my diamond ring.":** Uses 'Hurrah!' (joy) which contradicts 'exclaimed with sorrow'.
- **C — She said, "Alas! She had lost her diamond ring.":** Retains 'she' and 'her' instead of reverting the pronouns to 'I' and 'my'.
- **D — She said, "Bravo! I had lost my diamond ring.":** Uses 'Bravo!' (applause) and keeps the verb in past perfect 'had lost'.
- **Exam Extra:** In indirect-to-direct exclamatory conversion, match the reporting emotion phrase ('with sorrow', 'with joy') back to the right interjection ('Alas!', 'Hurrah!').

### Q630 · Optative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The saint said to the young man, "May God bless you with wisdom!"

A. The saint prayed that God may bless the young man with wisdom.
B. The saint prayed that might God bless him with wisdom.
C. The saint prayed that God might bless him with wisdom.
D. The saint ordered that God might bless you with wisdom.

**Answer:** C. The saint prayed that God might bless him with wisdom.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The saint prayed that God might bless him with wisdom. (Correct):** Optative prayers invoke God using 'May God...', which converts to 'prayed that God might + V1'; 'you' changes to 'him' (matching 'the young man') and the exclamation mark becomes a full stop.
- **A — The saint prayed that God may bless the young man with wisdom.:** Leaves 'may' unshifted instead of changing it to 'might'.
- **B — The saint prayed that might God bless him with wisdom.:** Keeps the optative inversion ('might God bless') instead of assertive order ('God might bless').
- **D — The saint ordered that God might bless you with wisdom.:** Fails to change 'you' to 'him' and uses 'ordered' instead of 'prayed'.
- **Exam Extra:** Optative sentences with 'May God...' take 'prayed that God might...' (not 'could' or 'may') in indirect speech.

### Q631 · Optative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The grandmother said to him, "May you live long!"

A. The grandmother wished that he might live long.
B. The grandmother wished that he may live long.
C. The grandmother wished that might he live long.
D. The grandmother wished that you would live long.

**Answer:** A. The grandmother wished that he might live long.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The grandmother wished that he might live long. (Correct):** An optative wish without invoking God ('May you live long!') converts using 'wished that' + Subject ('he') + 'might live long', dropping the exclamation mark.
- **B — The grandmother wished that he may live long.:** Keeps 'may' unshifted instead of converting it to 'might'.
- **C — The grandmother wished that might he live long.:** Preserves the optative inversion ('might he live') instead of assertive order ('he might live').
- **D — The grandmother wished that you would live long.:** Wrongly replaces 'might' with 'would' and keeps the pronoun 'you' unchanged.
- **Exam Extra:** In optative sentences, invert 'May + Subject + V1!' into assertive 'Subject + might + V1.' after 'wished that' or 'prayed that'.

### Q632 · Optative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The victim said to the culprits, "May your wicked plans fail!"

A. The victim blessed that their wicked plans may fail.
B. The victim cursed that your wicked plans may fail.
C. The victim exclaimed with joy that might their wicked plans fail.
D. The victim cursed that their wicked plans might fail.

**Answer:** D. The victim cursed that their wicked plans might fail.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The victim cursed that their wicked plans might fail. (Correct):** An ill-wish or malediction ('May your wicked plans fail!') converts using 'cursed that' + Subject ('their wicked plans') + 'might fail', with 'your' shifting to 'their'.
- **A — The victim blessed that their wicked plans may fail.:** Uses 'blessed' which has the opposite meaning of an ill-wish, and keeps 'may'.
- **B — The victim cursed that your wicked plans may fail.:** Fails to change 'your' to 'their' and leaves 'may' unshifted.
- **C — The victim exclaimed with joy that might their wicked plans fail.:** Uses 'exclaimed with joy' instead of 'cursed' and keeps optative inversion.
- **Exam Extra:** Optative sentences expressing ill-will or ruin use 'cursed that ... might ...' in indirect narration.

### Q633 · Optative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: The student said to his teacher, "Good morning, Sir!"

A. The student respectfully bade his teacher good morning.
B. The student respectfully wished his teacher good morning.
C. The student wished his teacher that good morning, Sir.
D. The student told his teacher good morning.

**Answer:** B. The student respectfully wished his teacher good morning.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The student respectfully wished his teacher good morning. (Correct):** Greetings like 'Good morning' convert using 'wished' + object + 'good morning' (no 'that'), and respectful titles like 'Sir' become 'respectfully' before 'wished'.
- **A — The student respectfully bade his teacher good morning.:** Uses 'bade' which is reserved for farewells like 'Goodbye' or 'Good night', not arrival greetings like 'Good morning'.
- **C — The student wished his teacher that good morning, Sir.:** Includes 'Sir' inside the indirect sentence and wrongly inserts 'that' before 'good morning'.
- **D — The student told his teacher good morning.:** Uses 'told' instead of 'wished' and omits the respectful sense of 'Sir'.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Sir/Madam' in direct speech is converted into 'respectfully' in indirect speech, and greetings use 'wished', never 'bade'.

### Q634 · Optative Sentences

Convert into indirect speech: Ramesh said, "Goodbye, my friends!"

A. Ramesh wished his friends my goodbye.
B. Ramesh bidded his friends that goodbye.
C. Ramesh bade his friends goodbye.
D. Ramesh told his friends goodbye, my friends.

**Answer:** C. Ramesh bade his friends goodbye.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Ramesh bade his friends goodbye. (Correct):** Parting phrases like 'Goodbye', 'Farewell', or 'Good night' use 'bade' (past tense of 'bid') + object + 'goodbye', changing 'my' to 'his'.
- **A — Ramesh wished his friends my goodbye.:** Uses 'wished' instead of 'bade' for a farewell expression and keeps 'my'.
- **B — Ramesh bidded his friends that goodbye.:** Uses the ungrammatical past form 'bidded' instead of 'bade' and wrongly inserts 'that'.
- **D — Ramesh told his friends goodbye, my friends.:** Uses 'told' instead of 'bade' and fails to change 'my' to 'his'.
- **Exam Extra:** Use 'wished' for meeting greetings (Good morning/afternoon/evening) and 'bade' for parting expressions (Goodbye/Farewell/Good night).

### Q635 · Optative Sentences

Convert into direct speech: The old woman blessed him that he might prosper in life.

A. The old woman said to him, "Might you prosper in life!"
B. The old woman said to him, "He might prosper in life."
C. The old woman said to him, "Should you prosper in life?"
D. The old woman said to him, "May you prosper in life!"

**Answer:** D. The old woman said to him, "May you prosper in life!"
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The old woman said to him, "May you prosper in life!" (Correct):** Reverting 'blessed him that he might prosper in life' to direct speech restores the optative formula 'May you prosper in life!' with 'said to him'.
- **A — The old woman said to him, "Might you prosper in life!":** Keeps 'might' inside direct speech instead of reverting to optative 'May'.
- **B — The old woman said to him, "He might prosper in life.":** Retains assertive third-person 'he' instead of second-person 'you' and omits 'May'.
- **C — The old woman said to him, "Should you prosper in life?":** Uses 'Should you prosper' (interrogative conditional) instead of optative 'May you prosper'.
- **Exam Extra:** When converting an indirect optative ('wished/blessed/prayed that ... might ...') back to direct speech, always start the quote with 'May + subject + V1!'.

### Q636 · Let's / Let & Time/Place Markers

Convert into indirect speech: Karan said to his teammates, "Let's plan a trekking trip."

A. Karan suggested to his teammates that they should plan a trekking trip.
B. Karan suggested his teammates that they should plan a trekking trip.
C. Karan ordered his teammates that let's plan a trekking trip.
D. Karan suggested his teammates to plan a trekking trip.

**Answer:** A. Karan suggested to his teammates that they should plan a trekking trip.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Karan suggested to his teammates that they should plan a trekking trip. (Correct):** 'Let us' / 'Let's' expressing a joint suggestion converts to 'suggested to' / 'proposed to' + object + 'that they should + V1'; note that 'suggested' and 'proposed' always take 'to' before a person object.
- **B — Karan suggested his teammates that they should plan a trekking trip.:** Omits the mandatory preposition 'to' after 'suggested' before 'his teammates'.
- **C — Karan ordered his teammates that let's plan a trekking trip.:** Uses 'ordered' instead of 'suggested/proposed' and leaves 'let's' inside indirect speech.
- **D — Karan suggested his teammates to plan a trekking trip.:** Uses 'to' infinitive after 'suggested' ('suggested his teammates to plan') instead of a 'that ... should' clause.
- **Exam Extra:** Always write 'suggested to + object' or 'proposed to + object'—never omit 'to' before an indirect object with these two verbs.

### Q637 · Let's / Let & Time/Place Markers

Convert into indirect speech: I said to my sister, "Let us donate these old clothes."

A. I proposed to my sister that they should donate these old clothes.
B. I proposed my sister that we shall donate those old clothes.
C. I said to my sister that let us donate those old clothes.
D. I proposed to my sister that we should donate those old clothes.

**Answer:** D. I proposed to my sister that we should donate those old clothes.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — I proposed to my sister that we should donate those old clothes. (Correct):** When the speaker is 'I' ('I said to my sister, "Let us..."'), the listener and speaker together remain first-person plural 'we' in indirect speech: 'proposed to my sister that we should...'.
- **A — I proposed to my sister that they should donate these old clothes.:** Changes 'us' to 'they' even though the speaker is 'I', excluding the speaker incorrectly.
- **B — I proposed my sister that we shall donate those old clothes.:** Omits 'to' after 'proposed' and uses 'shall' instead of 'should'.
- **C — I said to my sister that let us donate those old clothes.:** Retains 'let us' verbatim inside the indirect clause.
- **Exam Extra:** With 'Let us', use 'they should' when neither speaker nor listener is 'I/we', and use 'we should' when either the speaker or listener is 'I/we'.

### Q638 · Let's / Let & Time/Place Markers

Convert into indirect speech: The boy said to the teacher, "Let me explain my side of the story."

A. The boy proposed to the teacher that they should explain his side of the story.
B. The boy requested the teacher to let him explain his side of the story.
C. The boy requested the teacher to let me explain my side of the story.
D. The boy requested to the teacher let him explain his side of the story.

**Answer:** B. The boy requested the teacher to let him explain his side of the story.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The boy requested the teacher to let him explain his side of the story. (Correct):** 'Let me' expresses a personal wish or request for permission (not a joint proposal), so it converts to 'requested + object + to let him/her + V1' (or 'wished that he might be allowed to...'), changing 'me' to 'him' and 'my' to 'his'.
- **A — The boy proposed to the teacher that they should explain his side of the story.:** Wrongly treats 'Let me' as a joint proposal using 'proposed to ... that they should'.
- **C — The boy requested the teacher to let me explain my side of the story.:** Fails to change 'me' to 'him' and 'my' to 'his'.
- **D — The boy requested to the teacher let him explain his side of the story.:** Uses 'requested to the teacher' (invalid 'to' after 'requested') and omits 'to' before 'let'.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike 'Let us' (proposal -> 'should'), 'Let me' expresses permission/wish and converts to 'requested ... to let him/her' or 'might be allowed to'.

### Q639 · Let's / Let & Time/Place Markers

Convert into indirect speech: The judge said, "Let the witness be called into the courtroom now."

A. The judge ordered that the witness should be called into the courtroom now.
B. The judge suggested that the witness should be called into the courtroom then.
C. The judge ordered that the witness should be called into the courtroom then.
D. The judge ordered that the witness will be called into the courtroom now.

**Answer:** C. The judge ordered that the witness should be called into the courtroom then.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The judge ordered that the witness should be called into the courtroom then. (Correct):** 'Let the witness be called...' is an official command rather than a suggestion, so it converts to 'ordered that the witness should be called' (or 'ordered them to let the witness be called'), with 'now' changing to 'then'.
- **A — The judge ordered that the witness should be called into the courtroom now.:** Retains the direct time marker 'now' instead of converting it to 'then'.
- **B — The judge suggested that the witness should be called into the courtroom then.:** Wrongly uses 'suggested' for a judge's authoritative courtroom order.
- **D — The judge ordered that the witness will be called into the courtroom now.:** Uses 'will be called' instead of 'should be called' and keeps 'now'.
- **Exam Extra:** When 'Let + noun/third person' expresses an authority's order, report it using 'ordered that ... should + V1'.

### Q640 · Let's / Let & Time/Place Markers

Convert into indirect speech: Varun said, "My uncle returned from London two weeks ago last night."

A. Varun said that his uncle had returned from London two weeks ago the previous night.
B. Varun said that his uncle had returned from London two weeks before the previous night.
C. Varun said that his uncle returned from London two weeks before last night.
D. Varun said that his uncle had returned from London two weeks ago that night.

**Answer:** B. Varun said that his uncle had returned from London two weeks before the previous night.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Varun said that his uncle had returned from London two weeks before the previous night. (Correct):** Multiple time markers shift together: 'last night' changes to 'the previous night' (or 'the night before'), 'ago' changes to 'before', and simple past 'returned' shifts to past perfect 'had returned'.
- **A — Varun said that his uncle had returned from London two weeks ago the previous night.:** Leaves 'ago' unchanged instead of converting it to 'before'.
- **C — Varun said that his uncle returned from London two weeks before last night.:** Retains 'last night' and leaves 'returned' in simple past instead of past perfect.
- **D — Varun said that his uncle had returned from London two weeks ago that night.:** Converts 'last night' to 'that night' (which corresponds to 'tonight', not 'last night') and keeps 'ago'.
- **Exam Extra:** Key time-marker conversions: 'ago' -> 'before', 'last night' -> 'the previous night', 'yesterday' -> 'the previous day'.

# Section IV: Spellings, Para Jumbles & Comprehension

## Chapter 9: Spellings & Para Jumbles (PQRS)

### Q641 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. supercede
B. supersede
C. superseed
D. superceed

**Answer:** B. supersede
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — supersede (Correct):** 'Supersede' is the only English word in the '-sede' family, derived from Latin 'supersedere' (super + sedere, to sit). Note the single 's' before 'e-d-e' and never 'c'.
- **A — supercede:** The spelling 'supercede' wrongly applies the '-cede' ending used in words like 'precede' and 'concede'.
- **C — superseed:** The variant 'superseed' phonetically alters the Latin root '-sedere' into '-seed', which is invalid in English.
