- **D — superceed:** The form 'superceed' mistakenly borrows the '-ceed' pattern restricted solely to 'exceed', 'proceed', and 'succeed'.
- **Exam Extra:** Rule of '-sede / -ceed / -cede': Only 'supersede' ends in '-sede'; only three words end in '-ceed' (exceed, proceed, succeed); all others (concede, precede, recede, intercede) take '-cede'.

### Q642 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. embarassment
B. embarrasment
C. embarrassment
D. embarasment

**Answer:** C. embarrassment
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — embarrassment (Correct):** 'Embarrassment' retains both doubled consonants from its root verb 'embarrass' (double 'r' and double 's') before adding the noun suffix '-ment'.
- **A — embarassment:** The spelling 'embarassment' drops one 'r' from the mandatory double 'rr' after 'emba-'.
- **B — embarrasment:** The variant 'embarrasment' incorrectly reduces the double 'ss' of 'embarrass' to a single 's' before '-ment'.
- **D — embarasment:** The form 'embarasment' omits a letter from both consonant pairs, leaving single 'r' and single 's'.
- **Exam Extra:** Double-letter check: Words ending in double consonants like 'embarrass' and 'harass' (single 'r', double 's') keep their consonant counts when adding '-ment' (embarrassment, harassment).

### Q643 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. accommodate
B. accomodate
C. acommodate
D. acomodate

**Answer:** A. accommodate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — accommodate (Correct):** 'Accommodate' requires both double 'c' and double 'm' ('ac-com-mod-ate'), followed by '-ate'.
- **B — accomodate:** The spelling 'accomodate' keeps the double 'c' but mistakenly drops the second 'm'.
- **C — acommodate:** The variant 'acommodate' preserves the double 'm' while omitting the second 'c' after the prefix 'ac-'.
- **D — acomodate:** The form 'acomodate' reduces both consonant pairs to single letters, creating an invalid spelling.
- **Exam Extra:** Mnemonic: 'Accommodate' is large enough to hold two 'c's and two 'm's—just like 'recommendation' has one 'c' and two 'm's.

### Q644 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. bureaucrasy
B. buereaucracy
C. burocracy
D. bureaucracy

**Answer:** D. bureaucracy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — bureaucracy (Correct):** 'Bureaucracy' combines the French loanword 'bureau' (desk/office) with the Greek governance suffix '-cracy' (rule by).
- **A — bureaucrasy:** The spelling 'bureaucrasy' wrongly replaces the suffix '-cracy' (rule) with '-crasy' (confused with 'idiosyncrasy').
- **B — buereaucracy:** The variant 'buereaucracy' inserts an extra 'e' before 'u' in the French root 'bureau'.
- **C — burocracy:** The form 'burocracy' improperly simplifies the three-vowel sequence 'eau' into a single 'o'.
- **Exam Extra:** Look closely at the vowel cluster 'e-a-u' in French-origin English words like 'bureaucracy', 'plateau', and 'chateau'.

### Q645 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. millenium
B. milennium
C. millennium
D. millenniam

**Answer:** C. millennium
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — millennium (Correct):** 'Millennium' comes from Latin 'mille' (thousand, double 'l') + 'annus' (year, double 'n'), so it carries both double 'l' and double 'n' and ends in '-ium'.
- **A — millenium:** The spelling 'millenium' retains the double 'l' from 'mille' but drops the second 'n' from 'annus'.
- **B — milennium:** The variant 'milennium' keeps the double 'n' while reducing the double 'l' to a single 'l'.
- **D — millenniam:** The form 'millenniam' preserves both double consonants but corrupts the Latin neuter ending '-ium' into '-iam'.
- **Exam Extra:** Related rule: 'Perennial' and 'centennial' also carry double 'n' from Latin 'annus' (year), whereas 'annual' drops nothing.

### Q646 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. consientious
B. conscientious
C. consciencious
D. conscientous

**Answer:** B. conscientious
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — conscientious (Correct):** 'Conscientious' is built from 'con-' + 'scient-' (from Latin 'sciens', knowing, as in 'science') + '-ious', meaning guided by conscience or thorough.
- **A — consientious:** The spelling 'consientious' drops the silent 'c' from the root 'scient' (as found in 'science').
- **C — consciencious:** The variant 'consciencious' retains the 'c' of 'conscience' instead of changing it to 't' before '-ious'.
- **D — conscientous:** The form 'conscientous' omits the 'i' in the suffix '-ious', distorting the standard adjectival ending.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice that 'conscience' uses '-ce', whereas the adjective 'conscientious' switches 'c' to 't' before '-ious' (parallel to 'essence' -> 'essential').

### Q647 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. diarrhoea
B. diarhoea
C. diarrheoa
D. dairrhoea

**Answer:** A. diarrhoea
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — diarrhoea (Correct):** In British and SSC standard English, 'diarrhoea' is spelt with double 'r' followed by the Greek vowel digraph 'oe' before the final 'a' ('di-a-rr-hoe-a').
- **B — diarhoea:** The spelling 'diarhoea' mistakenly drops one 'r' from the mandatory double 'rr' after 'dia-'.
- **C — diarrheoa:** The variant 'diarrheoa' reverses the 'oe' digraph into 'eo', which is never used in Greek-derived medical terms.
- **D — dairrhoea:** The form 'dairrhoea' transposes the opening vowels 'ia' into 'ai', breaking the Greek prefix 'dia-' (through).
- **Exam Extra:** Greek medical roots containing 'rrhoia' (flow) keep 'rrh' and 'oe' in British English, as seen in 'diarrhoea' and 'gonorrhoea'.

### Q648 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. liason
B. liasion
C. liaision
D. liaison

**Answer:** D. liaison
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — liaison (Correct):** 'Liaison' contains the distinctive four-letter vowel-consonant pattern 'l-i-a-i' followed by '-son' (from French 'lier', to bind).
- **A — liason:** The spelling 'liason' omits the second 'i' after 'a' in the 'liai-' cluster.
- **B — liasion:** The variant 'liasion' places the second 'i' after the 's' instead of between 'a' and 's'.
- **C — liaision:** The form 'liaision' adds a third, redundant 'i' after the 's' while keeping the first two.
- **Exam Extra:** Break 'liaison' into 'liai-son'—there are two 'i's flanking the 'a' in the first syllable, never after the 's'.

### Q649 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. vaccuum
B. vaccum
C. vacuum
D. vacum

**Answer:** C. vacuum
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — vacuum (Correct):** 'Vacuum' is one of the very few English words with a doubled 'u' ('uu') and a single 'c' ('vac-u-um').
- **A — vaccuum:** The spelling 'vaccuum' unnecessarily doubles the 'c' in addition to keeping the double 'u'.
- **B — vaccum:** The variant 'vaccum' wrongly shifts the doubling from 'u' to 'c'.
- **D — vacum:** The form 'vacum' omits the second 'u', leaving neither consonant nor vowel doubled.
- **Exam Extra:** Words with 'uu' are rare in English; besides 'vacuum', notable examples are 'continuum' and 'residuum' (both Latin '-uum' nouns).

### Q650 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. questionaire
B. questionnaire
C. questionnare
D. questtionnaire

**Answer:** B. questionnaire
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — questionnaire (Correct):** 'Questionnaire' comes from French and keeps double 'n' between 'question' and '-aire' ('question-n-aire').
- **A — questionaire:** The spelling 'questionaire' drops the second 'n' required before the '-aire' suffix.
- **C — questionnare:** The variant 'questionnare' keeps double 'n' but omits the 'i' in the '-aire' ending.
- **D — questtionnaire:** The form 'questtionnaire' erroneously doubles the 't' in the base word 'question'.
- **Exam Extra:** Compare 'questionnaire' (double 'n') with 'millionaire' and 'billionaire' (single 'n'): French nouns ending in '-ion' double the 'n' before '-aire'.

### Q651 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. entreprenuer
B. entrepreneuer
C. entreprenour
D. entrepreneur

**Answer:** D. entrepreneur
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — entrepreneur (Correct):** 'Entrepreneur' follows the French structure 'entre-' (between) + 'pren-' (take) + '-eur' (agent suffix), ending strictly in '-eur'.
- **A — entreprenuer:** The spelling 'entreprenuer' transposes 'eu' into 'ue' in the French suffix '-eur'.
- **B — entrepreneuer:** The variant 'entrepreneuer' inserts an extra 'e' before 'u' in the final syllable.
- **C — entreprenour:** The form 'entreprenour' substitutes the English/Anglo-Norman '-our' ending for the French '-eur'.
- **Exam Extra:** Agent nouns borrowed directly from French—such as 'entrepreneur', 'chauffeur', 'connoisseur', and 'saboteur'—end in '-eur', not '-uer' or '-our'.

### Q652 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. maintainance
B. maintenance
C. maintanance
D. maintenence

**Answer:** B. maintenance
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — maintenance (Correct):** Although derived from the verb 'maintain', the noun 'maintenance' shifts the middle vowel from '-tain-' to '-ten-' and ends in '-ance'.
- **A — maintainance:** The spelling 'maintainance' mechanically copies 'maintain' without making the mandatory vowel change to '-ten-'.
- **C — maintanance:** The variant 'maintanance' drops the 'i' from 'maintain' and substitutes 'a' instead of 'e' in the middle syllable.
- **D — maintenence:** The form 'maintenence' uses the correct '-ten-' stem but replaces the '-ance' suffix with '-ence'.
- **Exam Extra:** Vowel-shift pair: 'maintain' -> 'maintenance' mirrors 'abstain' -> 'abstinence' and 'sustain' -> 'sustenance' (the '-ai-' weakens to 'e' in the noun).

### Q653 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. lieutenant
B. leutenant
C. lieutanant
D. liefenant

**Answer:** A. lieutenant
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — lieutenant (Correct):** 'Lieutenant' combines French 'lieu' (place, spelt 'l-i-e-u') and 'tenant' (holder, spelt 't-e-n-a-n-t'), meaning placeholder.
- **B — leutenant:** The spelling 'leutenant' omits the 'i' from the French root 'lieu' (as in 'in lieu of').
- **C — lieutanant:** The variant 'lieutanant' replaces the 'e' in 'tenant' with 'a', distorting the root meaning 'holder'.
- **D — liefenant:** The form 'liefenant' phonetically spells the British pronunciation 'lef-' instead of the standard 'lieu-'.
- **Exam Extra:** Despite the British military pronunciation sounding like 'lef-tenant', the standard spelling in both British and American English is 'lieutenant'.

### Q654 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. guarentee
B. garantee
C. guarantee
D. guarante

**Answer:** C. guarantee
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — guarantee (Correct):** 'Guarantee' opens with 'gu-' followed by '-aran-' (only 'a's) and ends with double 'e' ('-tee').
- **A — guarentee:** The spelling 'guarentee' wrongly replaces the second 'a' in 'guaran-' with an 'e'.
- **B — garantee:** The variant 'garantee' drops the silent 'u' after 'g' required in 'gua-'.
- **D — guarante:** The form 'guarante' omits the final 'e' from the mandatory '-ee' ending.
- **Exam Extra:** Both 'guarantee' and 'guarantor' begin with 'gua-' and use 'a' in the second syllable ('guaran-'), never 'e'.

### Q655 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. occurence
B. ocurrence
C. occurrance
D. occurrence

**Answer:** D. occurrence
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — occurrence (Correct):** Because 'occur' ends in a single stressed vowel + single consonant ('-ur'), the final 'r' doubles before '-ence', giving 'occurrence' (double 'c', double 'r', '-ence').
- **A — occurence:** The spelling 'occurence' keeps the double 'c' of 'occur' but fails to double the final 'r' before '-ence'.
- **B — ocurrence:** The variant 'ocurrence' doubles the 'r' properly while omitting the second 'c' in 'occur'.
- **C — occurrance:** The form 'occurrance' preserves both double consonants but replaces the '-ence' suffix with '-ance'.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice that 'occur' -> 'occurrence' and 'concur' -> 'concurrence' take '-ence', whereas 'endure' -> 'endurance' takes '-ance'.

### Q656 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. renaisance
B. renaissance
C. rennaisance
D. rennaissance

**Answer:** B. renaissance
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — renaissance (Correct):** 'Renaissance' combines French 're-' + 'naissance' (birth), featuring a single 'n', the vowel pair 'ai', and a double 's' before '-ance'.
- **A — renaisance:** The spelling 'renaisance' drops one 's' from the required double 'ss' in '-naissance'.
- **C — rennaisance:** The variant 'rennaisance' mistakenly doubles the 'n' while leaving the 's' single.
- **D — rennaissance:** The form 'rennaissance' keeps the double 'ss' properly but adds an unwanted second 'n'.
- **Exam Extra:** In 'renaissance', only the 's' is doubled ('re-nais-sance'); the 'n' is always single, and the ending is '-ance'.

### Q657 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. surveillance
B. surveilance
C. survellance
D. surviellance

**Answer:** A. surveillance
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — surveillance (Correct):** 'Surveillance' comes from French 'surveiller' (to watch over): it takes 'ei' after 'v', double 'l', and the noun suffix '-ance'.
- **B — surveilance:** The spelling 'surveilance' keeps the 'ei' vowel pair but drops one 'l' from the mandatory double 'll'.
- **C — survellance:** The variant 'survellance' omits the 'i' from '-veil-' altogether despite retaining the double 'l'.
- **D — surviellance:** The form 'surviellance' reverses 'ei' into 'ie' by misapplying the 'i before e' rule.
- **Exam Extra:** Note the exception to 'i before e': in 'surveillance', the 'ei' follows 'v' (not 'c') because it comes from French 'veiller' (meaning to stay awake/watch).

### Q658 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. mischeivous
B. mischievious
C. mischievous
D. mischevious

**Answer:** C. mischievous
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — mischievous (Correct):** 'Mischievous' is formed from 'mischief' (changing 'f' to 'v') + '-ous', producing a three-syllable word ('mis-chie-vous') with no 'i' after 'v'.
- **A — mischeivous:** The spelling 'mischeivous' transposes the 'ie' from 'mischief' into 'ei'.
- **B — mischievious:** The variant 'mischievious' inserts an extra 'i' after 'v', mimicking words like 'previous' or 'envious'.
- **D — mischevious:** The form 'mischevious' commits both errors at once by omitting the 'i' before 'e' and inserting a spurious 'i' after 'v'.
- **Exam Extra:** Watch out for pronunciation-driven errors: 'mischievous' has NO '-ious' ending (unlike 'envious'); it is spelt '-chiev-ous', never '-chiev-i-ous'.

### Q659 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. etiquete
B. ettiquette
C. etiquitte
D. etiquette

**Answer:** D. etiquette
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — etiquette (Correct):** 'Etiquette' is a French loanword spelt with a single 't' after 'e', followed by '-ique-' and a double 't' before the silent final 'e'.
- **A — etiquete:** The spelling 'etiquete' omits the second 't' required in the '-ette' ending.
- **B — ettiquette:** The variant 'ettiquette' mistakenly doubles the first 't' in addition to the final 'tt'.
- **C — etiquitte:** The form 'etiquitte' replaces the 'e' in '-ette' with 'i', corrupting the French suffix.
- **Exam Extra:** French diminutives ending in '-ette' (etiquette, silhouette, cigarette, omelette) always double the 't' at the end, never at the beginning.

### Q660 · Spellings - Correctly Spelt Word

Select the correctly spelt word.

A. camoflage
B. camouflage
C. camoflauge
D. camauflage

**Answer:** B. camouflage
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — camouflage (Correct):** 'Camouflage' retains the French vowel digraph 'ou' in 'camou-' followed by '-flage' (never '-flauge').
- **A — camoflage:** The spelling 'camoflage' drops the 'u' from the required 'ou' digraph in 'camou-'.
- **C — camoflauge:** The variant 'camoflauge' drops 'u' from the second syllable and wrongly moves it into the final syllable as '-flauge'.
- **D — camauflage:** The form 'camauflage' substitutes 'au' for 'ou' in the middle syllable.
- **Exam Extra:** Break 'camouflage' into 'camou + flage': the 'ou' belongs in the second syllable ('camou-'), while the final syllable has only 'a' ('-flage').

### Q661 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. calendar
B. seperate
C. grammar
D. receive

**Answer:** B. seperate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — seperate (Correct):** 'Seperate' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'separate' (with 'a' in the middle, from Latin 'separare'). All other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — calendar:** The word 'calendar' is correctly spelt with '-ar' at the end, unlike 'colander' or 'calender' (a pressing machine).
- **C — grammar:** The word 'grammar' is correctly spelt with double 'm' and '-ar' at the end, never '-er'.
- **D — receive:** The word 'receive' is correctly spelt because 'e' precedes 'i' immediately after 'c'.
- **Exam Extra:** Classic trick to spell 'separate': there is 'a rat' inside 'sep-a-rat-e', just like 'preparation' and 'comparative' keep 'a' in the middle.

### Q662 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. priviledge
B. committee
C. restaurant
D. parallel

**Answer:** A. priviledge
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — priviledge (Correct):** 'Priviledge' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'privilege' (no 'd' before 'g', and 'i-i-e-e' vowels). All other three options are correctly spelt.
- **B — committee:** The word 'committee' is correctly spelt with three doubled letters: double 'm', double 't', and double 'e'.
- **C — restaurant:** The word 'restaurant' is correctly spelt with '-au-' in the middle and '-ant' at the end.
- **D — parallel:** The word 'parallel' is correctly spelt with a single 'r', double 'l' in the middle, and single 'l' at the end.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice that 'privilege' and 'sacrilege' end in '-lege' without a 'd', whereas 'knowledge' and 'acknowledge' include 'd' before 'g'.

### Q663 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. possession
B. recommend
C. exagerate
D. schedule

**Answer:** C. exagerate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — exagerate (Correct):** 'Exagerate' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'exaggerate', which requires double 'g' (from Latin 'ex-' + 'aggerare', to heap up). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — possession:** The word 'possession' is correctly spelt with two sets of double 's' ('pos-ses-sion').
- **B — recommend:** The word 'recommend' is correctly spelt with a single 'c' and a double 'm' ('re-com-mend').
- **D — schedule:** The word 'schedule' is correctly spelt with 'sch-' followed by '-edule'.
- **Exam Extra:** Watch the 'g' count: 'exaggerate' takes double 'g' and single 'r', whereas 'exhilarate' has a silent 'h' and single 'l'.

### Q664 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. acquaintance
B. colloquial
C. rhythm
D. perseverence

**Answer:** D. perseverence
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — perseverence (Correct):** 'Perseverence' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'perseverance', which takes '-ance' (matching the verb 'persevere' + '-ance'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — acquaintance:** The word 'acquaintance' is correctly spelt with 'cq' before 'uaint' and the noun suffix '-ance'.
- **B — colloquial:** The word 'colloquial' is correctly spelt with double 'l' and '-quial' at the end.
- **C — rhythm:** The word 'rhythm' is correctly spelt with 'rh-' at the start, 'y' as its only vowel, and '-thm' at the end.
- **Exam Extra:** Words ending in '-ance' despite having 'e' earlier include 'perseverance', 'tolerance', and 'temperance'.

### Q665 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. pnuemonia
B. pharaoh
C. ecstasy
D. inoculate

**Answer:** A. pnuemonia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — pnuemonia (Correct):** 'Pnuemonia' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'pneumonia' ('pneu-' with 'e' before 'u', from Greek 'pneuma', breath). The other three options are correctly spelt.
- **B — pharaoh:** The word 'pharaoh' is correctly spelt with '-aoh' at the end, representing the ancient Egyptian royal title.
- **C — ecstasy:** The word 'ecstasy' is correctly spelt with 'c-s' in the middle and '-sy' (never '-cy') at the end.
- **D — inoculate:** The word 'inoculate' is correctly spelt with a single 'n' and a single 'c' ('in-oc-ulate').
- **Exam Extra:** Greek 'pneu-' (air/lung) always spells 'eu' in that order, as in 'pneumonia' and 'pneumatic'—just like 'eu' in 'eulogy' and 'euphoria'.

### Q666 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. sergeant
B. concensus
C. nuisance
D. cemetery

**Answer:** B. concensus
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — concensus (Correct):** 'Concensus' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'consensus' (from Latin 'con-' + 'sensus', feeling together—so it takes 's', not 'c', in the second syllable). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — sergeant:** The word 'sergeant' is correctly spelt with 'e' in the first syllable ('ser-') and 'ea' in the second ('-geant').
- **C — nuisance:** The word 'nuisance' is correctly spelt with '-ui-' after 'n' and the ending '-sance'.
- **D — cemetery:** The word 'cemetery' is correctly spelt using three 'e's ('c-e-m-e-t-e-r-y') and no 'a'.
- **Exam Extra:** Link 'consensus' to 'sense' (both have 's-e-n-s'), not 'census' (which starts with 'c' and means population count).

### Q667 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. noticeable
B. changeable
C. independant
D. tuition

**Answer:** C. independant
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — independant (Correct):** 'Independant' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'independent' (all vowels after 'ind-' are 'e': '-dep-end-ent'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — noticeable:** The word 'noticeable' is correctly spelt because words ending in '-ce' retain the silent 'e' before '-able' to keep the soft 'c' sound.
- **B — changeable:** The word 'changeable' is correctly spelt because words ending in '-ge' keep the silent 'e' before '-able' to preserve the soft 'g' sound.
- **D — tuition:** The word 'tuition' is correctly spelt with 'ui' in the first syllable ('tu-i-tion').
- **Exam Extra:** Words built on '-pend-' (depend, independent, pendant? Wait: 'dependent' and 'independent' use '-ent' for the adjective/noun 'independent'; 'superintendent' also ends in '-ent').

### Q668 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. sovereignty
B. chauffeur
C. miscellaneous
D. flourescent

**Answer:** D. flourescent
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — flourescent (Correct):** 'Flourescent' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'fluorescent' ('flu-o-r-' from 'fluorine', not 'flour'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — sovereignty:** The word 'sovereignty' is correctly spelt with '-eign-' (silent 'g') followed by '-ty'.
- **B — chauffeur:** The word 'chauffeur' is correctly spelt with 'ch-', '-auff-', and the French '-eur' ending.
- **C — miscellaneous:** The word 'miscellaneous' is correctly spelt with double 'l' and '-eous' at the end ('mis-cel-la-ne-ous').
- **Exam Extra:** Remember 'fluor-' (as in 'fluoride' and 'fluorescent') starts with 'fluo-', whereas 'flour' is what you bake bread with.

### Q669 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. assasin
B. hygiene
C. hierarchy
D. discipline

**Answer:** A. assasin
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — assasin (Correct):** 'Assasin' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'assassin', which contains two pairs of double 's' ('as-sas-sin'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **B — hygiene:** The word 'hygiene' is correctly spelt with 'ie' after 'g' ('hy-gi-ene').
- **C — hierarchy:** The word 'hierarchy' is correctly spelt with 'ie' at the start ('hier-') and '-archy' at the end.
- **D — discipline:** The word 'discipline' is correctly spelt with 'sc' in the first syllable and '-line' at the end.
- **Exam Extra:** Double-s symmetry: 'assassin' and 'possession' both feature two separate double-'s' pairs (ass-ass-in; pos-ses-sion).

### Q670 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. weird
B. decieve
C. achieve
D. foreign

**Answer:** B. decieve
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — decieve (Correct):** 'Decieve' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'deceive', following the rule 'i before e, except after c' when the sound is long 'ee'. The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — weird:** The word 'weird' is correctly spelt as a well-known exception to the 'i before e' rule, taking 'ei' after 'w'.
- **C — achieve:** The word 'achieve' is correctly spelt with 'ie' because the vowel pair follows 'h', not 'c'.
- **D — foreign:** The word 'foreign' is correctly spelt as an 'ei' exception because the vowel sound is not a long 'ee'.
- **Exam Extra:** Both 'receive' and 'deceive' (plus 'conceive' and 'perceive') come from Latin '-cipere' and strictly take '-cei-' after 'c'.

### Q671 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. parliament
B. government
C. temparament
D. environment

**Answer:** C. temparament
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — temparament (Correct):** 'Temparament' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'temperament' (preserving 'temper' + '-ament', never 'tempara-'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — parliament:** The word 'parliament' is correctly spelt with '-ia-' between 'parl' and 'ment'.
- **B — government:** The word 'government' is correctly spelt with the often-unpronounced 'n' retained between 'gover' and 'ment'.
- **D — environment:** The word 'environment' is correctly spelt with the 'n' kept between 'enviro' and 'ment'.
- **Exam Extra:** Keep the base word intact in 'temperament' (from 'temper') and 'parliament' (parlia + ment), even when a middle vowel is lightly pronounced in speech.

### Q672 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. immediately
B. occasionally
C. completely
D. definately

**Answer:** D. definately
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — definately (Correct):** 'Definately' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'definitely' (derived from 'finite', so it contains 'i-n-i-t-e', never '-ate-'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — immediately:** The word 'immediately' is correctly spelt by keeping the silent 'e' of 'immediate' before '-ly'.
- **B — occasionally:** The word 'occasionally' is correctly spelt with double 'c', single 's', and double 'l' ('oc-ca-sion-al-ly').
- **C — completely:** The word 'completely' is correctly spelt by retaining the final 'e' of 'complete' before adding '-ly'.
- **Exam Extra:** Root link: 'definite' and 'definitely' share the Latin root 'finis' (end/limit) with 'finite' and 'infinite'—none of them ever takes an 'a'.

### Q673 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. pronounciation
B. prohibition
C. precaution
D. persuasion

**Answer:** A. pronounciation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — pronounciation (Correct):** 'Pronounciation' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'pronunciation' (the 'o' of the verb 'pronounce' drops out in the noun, leaving '-nun-'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **B — prohibition:** The word 'prohibition' is correctly spelt with 'pro-' + 'hib-' + '-ition'.
- **C — precaution:** The word 'precaution' is correctly spelt with 'pre-' + 'caution' (matching 'cautious').
- **D — persuasion:** The word 'persuasion' is correctly spelt with '-sua-' and '-sion' (from the verb 'persuade').
- **Exam Extra:** Vowel-drop rule: 'pronounce' loses its 'o' in 'pronunciation', just as 'denounce' -> 'denunciation' and 'renounce' -> 'renunciation'.

### Q674 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. religious
B. sacreligious
C. contagious
D. prestigious

**Answer:** B. sacreligious
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — sacreligious (Correct):** 'Sacreligious' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'sacrilegious' (from 'sacrilege'—'sacri-' + 'leg' + '-ious', NOT from 'religious'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — religious:** The word 'religious' is correctly spelt with 'i' before 'g' ('re-lig-ious').
- **C — contagious:** The word 'contagious' is correctly spelt with 'g' followed by '-ious' (from 'contagion').
- **D — prestigious:** The word 'prestigious' is correctly spelt with '-tig-ious' (from 'prestige').
- **Exam Extra:** Etymology trap: 'sacrilegious' (irreverent toward sacred things) comes from 'sacrilege' ('legere', to steal), so 'e' comes before 'i' in '-legious', unlike 'religious'.

### Q675 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. suffered
B. offered
C. refered
D. preferred

**Answer:** C. refered
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — refered (Correct):** 'Refered' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'referred'. Because the stress in 'reFER' falls on the final syllable, the 'r' must double before '-ed'. The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — suffered:** The word 'suffered' is correctly spelt with a single 'r' because the stress falls on the first syllable ('SUF-fer').
- **B — offered:** The word 'offered' is correctly spelt with a single 'r' because the stress falls on the first syllable ('OF-fer').
- **D — preferred:** The word 'preferred' is correctly spelt with double 'r' because 'preFER' has final-syllable stress.
- **Exam Extra:** Stress-based doubling rule: 'offer' and 'suffer' are stressed on the first syllable, so 'offered' and 'suffered' keep a single 'r'; 'prefer' and 'refer' are stressed on the last syllable, so 'preferred' and 'referred' double the 'r'.

### Q676 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. existence
B. persistence
C. experience
D. appearence

**Answer:** D. appearence
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — appearence (Correct):** 'Appearence' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'appearance' (using '-ance', matching 'clear -> clearance' and 'assure -> assurance'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — existence:** The word 'existence' is correctly spelt with the '-ence' suffix attached to 'exist'.
- **B — persistence:** The word 'persistence' is correctly spelt with the '-ence' suffix attached to 'persist'.
- **C — experience:** The word 'experience' is correctly spelt with '-ri-ence' at the end.
- **Exam Extra:** Suffix distinction: 'exist', 'persist', and 'insist' form '-ence' nouns (existence, persistence, insistence), whereas 'appear', 'assist', and 'attend' form '-ance' nouns.

### Q677 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. treshold
B. withhold
C. household
D. manifold

**Answer:** A. treshold
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — treshold (Correct):** 'Treshold' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'threshold' (with 'th-' at the start and only ONE 'h' in the middle: 'thresh-old'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **B — withhold:** The word 'withhold' is correctly spelt with double 'h' where 'with' and 'hold' join together.
- **C — household:** The word 'household' is correctly spelt as the compound of 'house' and 'hold'.
- **D — manifold:** The word 'manifold' is correctly spelt with 'mani-' (from 'many') + '-fold'.
- **Exam Extra:** Carefully distinguish 'threshold' (single 'h' in the middle, 'thresh' + 'old') from 'withhold' (double 'h' because 'with' + 'hold' meet at the boundary).

### Q678 · Spellings - Wrongly Spelt Word

Select the wrongly spelt word.

A. obvious
B. grievious
C. previous
D. envious

**Answer:** B. grievious
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — grievious (Correct):** 'Grievious' is wrongly spelt; the correct spelling is 'grievous' (from 'grief' -> 'griev-' + '-ous', with NO extra 'i' after 'v'). The other three words are correctly spelt.
- **A — obvious:** The word 'obvious' is correctly spelt with '-vi-ous' (from Latin 'obvius').
- **C — previous:** The word 'previous' is correctly spelt with '-vi-ous' (from Latin 'praevius').
- **D — envious:** The word 'envious' is correctly spelt because 'envy' changes 'y' to 'i' before adding '-ous'.
- **Exam Extra:** Two-syllable trap: Like 'mischievous', 'grievous' adds '-ous' directly after 'v' ('griev-ous'), never '-ious'.

### Q679 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. It uses this trapped solar energy to convert carbon dioxide and water into glucose.
Q. Photosynthesis is the fundamental process by which green plants manufacture their own food.
R. Consequently, this biochemical reaction releases oxygen as a vital by-product into the atmosphere.
S. During the process, a green pigment called chlorophyll absorbs sunlight in the leaves.

A. PQRS
B. SQPR
C. QSPR
D. RQSP

**Answer:** C. QSPR
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — QSPR (Correct):** QSPR is correct: Q introduces photosynthesis; S refers to 'the process' and introduces 'sunlight' and 'chlorophyll'; P links to S via 'this trapped solar energy' (sunlight absorbed in S); R concludes with 'Consequently, this biochemical reaction' referring to P.
- **A — PQRS:** Starting with P fails because 'this trapped solar energy' has no prior mention of sunlight or trapping energy.
- **B — SQPR:** Opening with S is weak because 'During the process' presupposes an earlier sentence naming the process, which Q provides.
- **D — RQSP:** Beginning with R is invalid because 'Consequently, this biochemical reaction' signals a result that must follow the reaction described in P.
- **Exam Extra:** Look for noun-to-demonstrative chains ('absorbs sunlight' -> 'this trapped solar energy'; 'convert... into glucose' -> 'this biochemical reaction') to lock middle pairs in PQRS questions.

### Q680 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Before its invention, books had to be copied painstakingly by hand, making them rare and expensive.
Q. As a result, literacy rates began to rise rapidly across Europe within a few decades.
R. Johannes Gutenberg's invention of the movable-type printing press in the fifteenth century transformed human communication.
S. Once printed texts became affordable, ordinary citizens could access scientific and literary ideas for the first time.

A. PRSQ
B. RPSQ
C. QRPS
D. SPRQ

**Answer:** B. RPSQ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — RPSQ (Correct):** RPSQ is correct: R introduces Gutenberg's invention; P refers back to 'its invention' and describes books being 'rare and expensive'; S contrasts P with 'Once printed texts became affordable'; Q states the outcome with 'As a result, literacy rates began to rise'.
- **A — PRSQ:** Starting with P fails because the possessive pronoun 'its' in 'Before its invention' has no antecedent before R.
- **C — QRPS:** Opening with Q is illogical because 'As a result' is a concluding connector that requires a preceding cause.
- **D — SPRQ:** Beginning with S fails because 'Once printed texts became affordable' assumes printing has already been introduced and contrasted with costly manuscripts.
- **Exam Extra:** Chronological contrast markers ('Before its invention' -> 'Once printed texts became affordable' -> 'As a result') create an unmistakable timeline.

### Q681 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Therefore, urban planners are increasingly planting rooftop gardens and reflective pavements to cool down city centres.
Q. Dark asphalt roads and concrete buildings absorb far more solar radiation than natural vegetation does.
R. Many densely populated cities experience noticeably higher temperatures than the surrounding rural countryside.
S. This phenomenon, known as the urban heat island effect, drives up electricity demand for air conditioning during summer.

A. RQSP
B. QRSP
C. SRQP
D. PRQS

**Answer:** A. RQSP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — RQSP (Correct):** RQSP is correct: R introduces the general observation (cities warmer than rural areas); Q explains the physical cause (asphalt and concrete absorb more heat); S names 'This phenomenon' (urban heat island effect) and its impact on electricity demand; P concludes with 'Therefore' to give the planners' solution.
- **B — QRSP:** Opening with Q jumps straight into explaining why dark surfaces absorb heat without first stating the urban-rural temperature difference from R.
- **C — SRQP:** Starting with S fails because 'This phenomenon' requires a previously described phenomenon before it can be named.
- **D — PRQS:** Beginning with P is incorrect because 'Therefore' signals a concluding action rather than an introductory premise.
- **Exam Extra:** A classic Problem -> Cause -> Naming/Effect -> Solution structure ('higher temperatures' -> 'absorb radiation' -> 'This phenomenon' -> 'Therefore, urban planners').

### Q682 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. She performs a specialized figure-eight motion on the honeycomb known as the waggle dance.
Q. The other bees watch closely and soon fly straight to the exact location without wasting energy searching randomly.
R. By varying the angle and duration of this movement, the scout indicates both the direction and distance of the nectar source.
S. When a foraging honeybee discovers a rich patch of flowers, she returns to the hive to inform her colony.

A. PSRQ
B. QSPR
C. RPSQ
D. SPRQ

**Answer:** D. SPRQ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — SPRQ (Correct):** SPRQ is correct: S introduces 'a foraging honeybee' returning to the hive; P continues with 'She performs' (referring to the honeybee) and introduces 'the waggle dance'; R links to P via 'this movement'; Q concludes with 'The other bees' acting on the directions.
- **A — PSRQ:** Starting with P fails because the subject pronoun 'She' has no antecedent until S introduces the foraging honeybee.
- **B — QSPR:** Opening with Q is invalid because 'The other bees' and 'the exact location' presume a scout bee has already discovered nectar and danced.
- **C — RPSQ:** Beginning with R fails because 'this movement' and 'the scout' refer back to the waggle dance and bee introduced in S and P.
- **Exam Extra:** Track the noun-pronoun handoff: 'a foraging honeybee' (S) -> 'She' + 'waggle dance' (P) -> 'this movement' (R) -> 'The other bees' (Q).

### Q683 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. However, constant notifications from smartphones have fragmented our attention spans over the past decade.
Q. Deep reading requires sustained concentration, which allows the mind to process complex arguments thoroughly.
R. Setting aside just thirty distraction-free minutes each evening can gradually rebuild that lost focus.
S. Many people now find themselves skimming headlines instead of finishing a single chapter.

A. QPSR
B. PQSR
C. SQPR
D. RQPS

**Answer:** A. QPSR
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — QPSR (Correct):** QPSR is correct: Q defines the value of deep reading (sustained concentration); P introduces the contrast ('However... fragmented our attention spans'); S illustrates the consequence ('skimming headlines'); R concludes with how to rebuild 'that lost focus'.
- **B — PQSR:** Starting with P fails because 'However' requires a preceding baseline statement (Q) to contrast against.
- **C — SQPR:** Ordering S before P reverses cause and effect, placing the symptom ('skimming headlines') before the cause ('constant notifications').
- **D — RQPS:** Beginning with R is invalid because 'that lost focus' refers back to the fragmented attention spans described in P.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast words like 'However' rarely open a paragraph; they always pivot away from a positive or baseline claim such as Q.

### Q684 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. This remarkable feat made India the first Asian nation to reach Martian orbit and the first country worldwide to succeed on its maiden attempt.
Q. Launched in November 2013, the spacecraft travelled for over ten months across deep space before entering Mars's orbit.
R. Even more impressive was its modest budget, which cost less than the production of many Hollywood science-fiction films.
S. The Indian Space Research Organisation achieved a historic milestone with the Mars Orbiter Mission, popularly called Mangalyaan.

A. QSPR
B. SQPR
C. PQRS
D. RSQP

**Answer:** B. SQPR
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — SQPR (Correct):** SQPR is correct: S introduces ISRO and the Mars Orbiter Mission (Mangalyaan); Q continues with 'the spacecraft' reaching Mars's orbit; P refers back to entering Martian orbit on the first try as 'This remarkable feat'; R adds 'Even more impressive was its modest budget'.
- **A — QSPR:** Opening with Q fails because 'the spacecraft' has not yet been named before S introduces Mangalyaan.
- **C — PQRS:** Starting with P fails because 'This remarkable feat' points back to the mission's entry into Mars's orbit described in S and Q.
- **D — RSQP:** Beginning with R is illogical because 'Even more impressive' and 'its modest budget' require a previously mentioned spacecraft and achievement.
- **Exam Extra:** Degree modifiers like 'Even more impressive' (R) must always follow an initial impressive achievement ('This remarkable feat' in P).

### Q685 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Students who pull all-nighters before examinations often forget facts that they studied only hours earlier.
Q. During deep sleep, the brain replays neural patterns formed during the day and transfers key information to long-term storage.
R. Adequate rest is therefore not wasted time; rather, it is an essential partner to effective studying.
S. Neuroscientists attribute this memory loss to the deprivation of slow-wave sleep, which is critical for consolidation.

A. PQSR
B. SPQR
C. PSQR
D. QPSR

**Answer:** C. PSQR
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — PSQR (Correct):** PSQR is correct: P states the phenomenon (students pulling all-nighters forget facts); S explains 'this memory loss' by linking it to lack of slow-wave sleep; Q elaborates on what happens 'During deep sleep'; R concludes with 'therefore' that rest is essential.
- **A — PQSR:** Placing Q directly after P breaks the link between 'often forget facts' (P) and 'this memory loss' (S).
- **B — SPQR:** Starting with S fails because 'this memory loss' has no referent until P mentions students forgetting facts.
- **D — QPSR:** Opening with Q jumps straight into brain consolidation mechanics before introducing the problem of sleep deprivation and memory loss.
- **Exam Extra:** General phenomenon (P) -> Scientific diagnosis ('this memory loss', S) -> Mechanism (Q) -> Takeaway ('therefore', R) is a standard explanatory arc.

### Q686 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. If ocean temperatures remain elevated for too long, the stressed corals starve and eventually die.
Q. Without these colourful algae, the coral skeleton turns stark white and loses its primary source of nutrition.
R. Yet rising sea temperatures are causing corals to expel the symbiotic algae living within their tissues.
S. Coral reefs support nearly a quarter of all marine species despite covering less than one percent of the ocean floor.

A. RSQP
B. SRPQ
C. QRSP
D. SRQP

**Answer:** D. SRQP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — SRQP (Correct):** SRQP is correct: S introduces coral reefs' ecological richness; R contrasts with 'Yet' and introduces 'symbiotic algae'; Q refers to 'these colourful algae' and explains coral bleaching; P gives the fatal consequence if high temperatures persist.
- **A — RSQP:** Starting with R fails because 'Yet' is a contrast connector that must follow the positive statement about coral reefs in S.
- **B — SRPQ:** Placing P before Q reverses the sequence, making corals starve and die before explaining why they lose their algae and nutrition.
- **C — QRSP:** Ordering Q immediately after S fails because 'these colourful algae' in Q has not yet been introduced (R introduces 'symbiotic algae').
- **Exam Extra:** Follow the chain: 'Coral reefs' (S) -> 'Yet... symbiotic algae' (R) -> 'these colourful algae' (Q) -> 'starve and eventually die' (P).

### Q687 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. In ancient Rome, soldiers were either paid partly in salt or given an allowance specifically to purchase it.
Q. The Latin word for this allowance, salarium, eventually evolved into the modern English word 'salary'.
R. Salt was so valuable in the ancient world that it often functioned as a form of currency.
S. Today, although wages are paid in currency, the linguistic link to that prized mineral still survives.

A. RPQS
B. PRQS
C. QRPS
D. SRQP

**Answer:** A. RPQS
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — RPQS (Correct):** RPQS is correct: R introduces the value of salt in the ancient world; P narrows to ancient Rome ('given an allowance specifically to purchase it'); Q refers to 'this allowance, salarium' evolving into 'salary'; S concludes with 'Today' and 'that prized mineral'.
- **B — PRQS:** Starting with P fails because 'to purchase it' uses the pronoun 'it' before 'Salt' is introduced as a standalone noun in R.
- **C — QRPS:** Beginning with Q is invalid because 'this allowance' points back to the Roman salt allowance described in P.
- **D — SRQP:** Opening with S fails because 'that prized mineral' requires salt to have been mentioned earlier.
- **Exam Extra:** Broad-to-narrow funnel: 'the ancient world' (R) -> 'In ancient Rome' (P) -> 'this allowance, salarium' (Q) -> 'Today' (S).

### Q688 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. For these breakthroughs, she became the first woman to win a Nobel Prize and later earned a second in chemistry.
Q. Born in Warsaw in 1867, she moved to Paris as a young woman to study physics and mathematics at the Sorbonne.
R. Working in a makeshift laboratory, she and her husband Pierre isolated two previously unknown elements, polonium and radium.
S. Marie Curie's pioneering research into radioactivity laid the foundation for modern nuclear science.

A. SQRP
B. QSRP
C. SRQP
D. RSQP

**Answer:** A. SQRP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — SQRP (Correct):** SQRP is correct: S introduces Marie Curie; Q follows with her birth and move to Paris ('she'); R describes her laboratory work isolating polonium and radium; P concludes with 'For these breakthroughs' leading to her two Nobel Prizes.
- **B — QSRP:** Starting with Q fails because the pronoun 'she' has no antecedent before S names Marie Curie.
- **C — SRQP:** Placing R before Q disrupts chronology and places 'Working in a makeshift laboratory' before her move to Paris to study.
- **D — RSQP:** Putting P before R fails because 'For these breakthroughs' refers to isolating polonium and radium in R.
- **Exam Extra:** In biographical para jumbles, an introductory full-name topic sentence (S) precedes pronoun-led chronological milestones (Q -> R -> P).

### Q689 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Mangrove forests thrive along tropical coastlines where freshwater rivers meet salty ocean tides.
Q. Furthermore, their dense root systems trap sediments and absorb excess storm surge during coastal cyclones.
R. Unfortunately, rapid coastal development and shrimp farming have cleared vast stretches of these vital forests.
S. Protecting and restoring them is therefore one of the most cost-effective ways to defend vulnerable shorelines.

A. QPRS
B. RPSQ
C. PQRS
D. SQPR

**Answer:** C. PQRS
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — PQRS (Correct):** PQRS is correct: P introduces mangrove forests; Q adds a benefit ('Furthermore, their dense root systems...'); R introduces the threat ('Unfortunately... cleared vast stretches of these vital forests'); S concludes ('Protecting and restoring them is therefore...').
- **A — QPRS:** Starting with Q fails because 'Furthermore' and 'their dense root systems' require P to introduce mangrove forests first.
- **B — RPSQ:** Opening with R fails because 'these vital forests' refers back to the ecological benefits of mangroves in P and Q.
- **D — SQPR:** Beginning with S is invalid because 'them' and 'therefore' presuppose both the value of mangroves and the threat to them.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice how 'Furthermore' (Q) extends a positive description (P), while 'Unfortunately' (R) shifts to the negative threat before 'therefore' (S) gives the policy conclusion.

### Q690 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Along with silk and spices, merchants carried paper-making techniques, astronomical knowledge, and religious traditions across continents.
Q. Rather than a single paved highway, it was an intricate network of caravan trails linking East Asia with the Mediterranean.
R. For over a millennium, the Silk Road served as the primary artery of trade between China and the Western world.
S. This constant cultural exchange shaped the arts, cuisines, and technologies of dozens of ancient civilisations.

A. QRPS
B. PQRS
C. SRQP
D. RQPS

**Answer:** D. RQPS
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — RQPS (Correct):** RQPS is correct: R introduces 'the Silk Road'; Q clarifies what 'it' actually was (a network of caravan trails); P details what merchants carried alongside silk and spices; S summarizes 'This constant cultural exchange'.
- **A — QRPS:** Starting with Q fails because 'it' has no antecedent before R names the Silk Road.
- **B — PQRS:** Opening with P jumps into what merchants carried along the route before introducing the Silk Road itself.
- **C — SRQP:** Beginning with S fails because 'This constant cultural exchange' refers to the transfer of techniques, knowledge, and traditions in P.
- **Exam Extra:** Definition/clarification pairs ('the Silk Road' in R -> 'it was an intricate network' in Q) are reliable opening locks in historical PQRS sets.

### Q691 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Once in the bloodstream or digestive tracts of marine organisms, these tiny fragments gradually move up the food chain.
Q. Microplastics are plastic particles smaller than five millimetres that originate from degraded packaging, synthetic textiles, and vehicle tyres.
R. Because they are too small to be filtered out by standard wastewater plants, millions of tonnes wash into rivers and oceans every year.
S. Scientists have consequently detected traces of synthetic polymers in table salt, bottled water, and even human lung tissue.

A. RQPS
B. QRPS
C. PRQS
D. SQPR

**Answer:** B. QRPS
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — QRPS (Correct):** QRPS is correct: Q defines microplastics; R refers to them with 'they' and explains how they enter rivers and oceans; P follows with 'these tiny fragments' entering marine organisms and moving up the food chain; S concludes with 'consequently' showing human exposure.
- **A — RQPS:** Starting with R fails because the plural pronoun 'they' has no antecedent before Q defines microplastics.
- **C — PRQS:** Opening with P fails because 'these tiny fragments' points back to microplastics introduced in Q and R.
- **D — SQPR:** Beginning with S is illogical because 'consequently' marks the final consequence of food-chain contamination described in P.
- **Exam Extra:** Look at the escalating spatial sequence: origin (Q) -> wastewater into oceans (R) -> marine food chain (P) -> human food and lungs (S).

### Q692 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Beyond lending books, modern libraries offer free internet access, digital literacy workshops, and quiet study spaces for students.
Q. Critics sometimes argue that search engines and e-books have rendered physical libraries obsolete.
R. Far from disappearing, however, well-funded libraries remain essential anchors of equal opportunity in both towns and cities.
S. Such claims overlook the vital role these community spaces play for citizens who cannot afford expensive subscriptions or home broadband.

A. QSPR
B. PQSR
C. SQPR
D. RQSP

**Answer:** A. QSPR
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — QSPR (Correct):** QSPR is correct: Q introduces critics' claim that physical libraries are obsolete; S counters with 'Such claims overlook...'; P adds what modern libraries actually offer beyond books; R concludes with 'Far from disappearing, however...'.
- **B — PQSR:** Opening with P jumps straight into 'Beyond lending books' without setting up the debate over whether libraries are obsolete.
- **C — SQPR:** Starting with S fails because 'Such claims' refers directly to the critics' argument stated in Q.
- **D — RQSP:** Beginning with R is invalid because 'Far from disappearing, however' serves as a concluding counter-verdict rather than a starting premise.
- **Exam Extra:** In opinion/rebuttal paragraphs, the opposing claim ('Critics sometimes argue...', Q) precedes its direct refutation ('Such claims overlook...', S).

### Q693 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. In 1928, the Scottish bacteriologist Alexander Fleming returned from holiday to find a stray mould growing on a forgotten petri dish.
Q. That accidental observation ushered in the age of antibiotics and saved hundreds of millions of lives from bacterial infections.
R. Upon closer inspection, he noticed that the surrounding bacteria had completely dissolved wherever the mould had spread.
S. Curious about this clear zone, Fleming isolated the active substance from the fungus and named it penicillin.

A. PSRQ
B. RPSQ
C. PRSQ
D. SRQP

**Answer:** C. PRSQ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — PRSQ (Correct):** PRSQ is correct: P introduces Fleming finding a stray mould on a petri dish; R continues ('he noticed... surrounding bacteria had completely dissolved'); S refers to that bacteria-free area as 'this clear zone' and names penicillin; Q concludes with 'That accidental observation'.
- **A — PSRQ:** Placing S directly after P breaks the logic because 'this clear zone' in S is not described until R mentions dissolved bacteria.
- **B — RPSQ:** Starting with R fails because 'he' has no antecedent before P introduces Alexander Fleming.
- **D — SRQP:** Ordering Q before S places the global impact of 'That accidental observation' before Fleming even isolates and names penicillin.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice the exact descriptive link: 'bacteria had completely dissolved' (R) creates 'this clear zone' (S), which together form 'That accidental observation' (Q).

### Q694 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Within a few months, microorganisms break down this organic mixture into dark, crumbly humus.
Q. Gardeners prize this natural fertiliser because it improves soil moisture retention without introducing synthetic chemicals.
R. Instead of sending vegetable peels and dry leaves to overflowing landfills, households can convert them into nutrient-rich compost.
S. The process simply requires layering moist kitchen scraps with carbon-rich dry matter and turning the pile occasionally for aeration.

A. SRPQ
B. PRSQ
C. QRSP
D. RSPQ

**Answer:** D. RSPQ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — RSPQ (Correct):** RSPQ is correct: R introduces household composting; S refers to 'The process' and describes layering scraps and dry matter; P refers to 'this organic mixture' breaking down into humus; Q refers to humus as 'this natural fertiliser'.
- **A — SRPQ:** Starting with S fails because 'The process' lacks a referent until R introduces composting kitchen waste.
- **B — PRSQ:** Opening with P is invalid because 'this organic mixture' refers to the layered scraps and dry matter described in S.
- **C — QRSP:** Beginning with Q fails because 'this natural fertiliser' points back to the dark, crumbly humus produced in P.
- **Exam Extra:** Process para jumbles follow material transformation step by step: 'convert them into compost' (R) -> 'layering...' (S) -> 'this organic mixture -> humus' (P) -> 'this natural fertiliser' (Q).

### Q695 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Engineers tap into these reservoirs through deep wells to drive electricity turbines and supply district heating pipes.
Q. Unlike wind or solar power, this subterranean heat remains available twenty-four hours a day regardless of weather conditions.
R. As a result, nearly ninety percent of the nation's homes are heated cleanly and affordably using geothermal energy.
S. Located atop a volcanic rift zone, Iceland sits above vast underground reservoirs of superheated water and steam.

A. PSQR
B. PQRS
C. SPQR
D. RSQP

**Answer:** C. SPQR
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — SPQR (Correct):** SPQR is correct: S introduces Iceland's underground reservoirs of superheated water and steam; P refers to 'these reservoirs'; Q refers to 'this subterranean heat' and highlights its 24-hour reliability; R concludes with 'As a result, nearly ninety percent...'.
- **A — PSQR:** Starting with P fails because 'these reservoirs' has no antecedent before S introduces Iceland's underground reservoirs.
- **B — PQRS:** Ordering Q before P places 'this subterranean heat' ahead of the explanation of how engineers tap the reservoirs.
- **D — RSQP:** Beginning with R is invalid because 'As a result' introduces an outcome rather than an opening geographical fact.
- **Exam Extra:** Track the determiner chain: 'vast underground reservoirs' (S) -> 'these reservoirs' (P) -> 'this subterranean heat' (Q) -> 'As a result' (R).

### Q696 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Using that key, the French linguist Jean-François Champollion finally cracked the hieroglyphic code in 1822.
Q. That mystery began to unravel in 1799 when French soldiers unearthed the Rosetta Stone, inscribed with a single decree in three scripts.
R. For centuries after ancient Egypt fell, historians could no longer read the intricate hieroglyphs carved on temple walls.
S. Because one of those scripts was Ancient Greek, scholars realised they had a bilingual key to translate the Egyptian symbols.

A. RQSP
B. QRSP
C. SRQP
D. PQRS

**Answer:** A. RQSP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — RQSP (Correct):** RQSP is correct: R states the centuries-long mystery of unread hieroglyphs; Q introduces 'That mystery' unravelling via the three-script Rosetta Stone; S refers to 'one of those scripts' (Ancient Greek) as a bilingual key; P concludes with Champollion using 'that key' to crack the code in 1822.
- **B — QRSP:** Starting with Q fails because 'That mystery' refers back to historians being unable to read hieroglyphs in R.
- **C — SRQP:** Opening with S is invalid because 'one of those scripts' presupposes Q's mention of a stone inscribed in three scripts.
- **D — PQRS:** Beginning with P fails because 'Using that key' requires S to establish Ancient Greek as the bilingual key.
- **Exam Extra:** Notice the tight demonstrative chain: 'could no longer read' (R) -> 'That mystery' + 'three scripts' (Q) -> 'one of those scripts' + 'bilingual key' (S) -> 'Using that key' (P).

### Q697 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Many graduates enter the workforce with strong academic degrees yet little understanding of budgeting or personal finance.
Q. Without a clear grasp of compound interest, young earners often underestimate how rapidly credit card debt can snowball.
R. Introducing practical money management in secondary schools would therefore equip students for lifelong financial stability.
S. Conversely, starting even a modest monthly investment at age twenty-two gives savings decades to multiply.

A. QPSR
B. PSQR
C. PQSR
D. SPQR

**Answer:** C. PQSR
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — PQSR (Correct):** PQSR is correct: P introduces graduates lacking personal finance skills; Q explains the downside (credit card debt snowballing); S contrasts Q with 'Conversely' (early investing compounding positively); R concludes with 'therefore' proposing financial education in schools.
- **A — QPSR:** Starting with Q jumps straight into compound interest and debt without P's broader framing of graduates lacking financial literacy.
- **B — PSQR:** Placing S before Q makes 'Conversely' contrast with nothing, because the negative example of compounding debt (Q) hasn't been given yet.
- **D — SPQR:** Opening with S and P reverses logic, placing a contrasting investment tip before the core problem and before 'therefore' in R.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Conversely' (S) pairs two opposite sides of compound interest: rapid debt growth (Q) versus long-term investment growth (S).

### Q698 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. Because it chases summer across both hemispheres, this remarkable seabird experiences more daylight than any other creature on Earth.
Q. Six months later, it reverses course and covers another forty thousand kilometres back to the Arctic tundra.
R. Every autumn, the tiny Arctic tern leaves its northern breeding grounds and flies all the way to the edges of Antarctica.
S. Few animals undertake a seasonal journey as astonishing as the annual migration of the Arctic tern.

A. RSQP
B. SRPQ
C. QRSP
D. SRQP

**Answer:** D. SRQP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — SRQP (Correct):** SRQP is correct: S introduces the Arctic tern's extraordinary annual migration; R describes the southward leg every autumn; Q follows chronologically ('Six months later, it reverses course'); P concludes with 'Because it chases summer across both hemispheres'.
- **A — RSQP:** Ordering Q before R places the return flight ('Six months later, it reverses course') before the outbound autumn flight.
- **B — SRPQ:** Placing P before Q separates the two halves of the round trip and states the consequence of chasing summer before the return trip is described.
- **C — QRSP:** Starting with Q fails because 'it reverses course' requires R to specify the initial autumn journey to Antarctica.
- **Exam Extra:** Seasonal time markers ('Every autumn' -> 'Six months later') plus pronoun direction ('the tiny Arctic tern' in R -> 'it' in Q -> 'this remarkable seabird' in P) anchor the sequence.

### Q699 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. When a high proportion of a community is immunised against a contagious disease, the pathogen struggles to find susceptible hosts.
Q. Vaccination not only shields the individual recipient from severe illness but also strengthens collective public health.
R. This indirect barrier, known as herd immunity, helps protect newborns and immunocompromised patients who cannot be vaccinated themselves.
S. Falling immunisation rates can quickly erode that shield, allowing once-rare infections like measles to resurface.

A. PQRS
B. QPRS
C. RPQS
D. SPQR

**Answer:** B. QPRS
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — QPRS (Correct):** QPRS is correct: Q introduces vaccination's individual and collective benefits; P explains how community immunisation blocks the pathogen; R names 'This indirect barrier, known as herd immunity'; S warns what happens if falling rates 'erode that shield'.
- **A — PQRS:** Starting with P skips the overarching topic sentence (Q) that introduces vaccination's dual role.
- **C — RPQS:** Opening with R fails because 'This indirect barrier' refers to the community protection mechanism explained in P.
- **D — SPQR:** Beginning with S is invalid because 'that shield' points back to the herd-immunity barrier defined in R.
- **Exam Extra:** Terminology rule: An unnamed mechanism described in P ('pathogen struggles to find susceptible hosts') is subsequently labelled in R ('This indirect barrier, known as herd immunity').

### Q700 · Para Jumbles (PQRS)

Rearrange the sentences in the correct logical order.

P. In 1941, Swiss engineer George de Mestral noticed burdock burrs clinging stubbornly to his dog's fur after a woodland walk.
Q. Years of experimentation with nylon loops and hooks eventually produced Velcro, now ubiquitous on shoes, jackets, and spacecraft gear.
R. Examining the tiny hooks under a microscope, he realised that a synthetic version could fasten fabrics cleanly without buttons or zippers.
S. His curiosity about how nature solved the problem of attachment became a classic triumph of biomimicry.

A. PRQS
B. PQRS
C. RPQS
D. QRPS

**Answer:** A. PRQS
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — PRQS (Correct):** PRQS is correct: P introduces George de Mestral noticing burdock burrs in 1941; R describes him examining 'the tiny hooks' under a microscope; Q details how years of experimentation produced Velcro; S closes by calling 'His curiosity' a triumph of biomimicry.
- **B — PQRS:** Placing Q immediately after P skips the crucial microscopic insight in R that led to experimenting with nylon loops and hooks.
- **C — RPQS:** Starting with R fails because 'he' and 'the tiny hooks' have no antecedents until P introduces George de Mestral and the burdock burrs.
- **D — QRPS:** Ordering Q before R puts the finished invention of Velcro ahead of the microscopic observation that inspired it.
- **Exam Extra:** In invention narratives, sequence moves from observation (P) -> insight (R) -> product development (Q) -> broader significance (S).

---

## Chapter 10: Reading Comprehension & Cloze Test

### Q701 · Reading Comprehension - Main Idea

A municipal library extended its evening hours after a resident survey revealed that daytime schedules excluded working learners. Attendance rose sharply among college students and late-shift workers, prompting the library to carve out a quiet study zone within its existing floor plan. Because lending rules and annual book purchases stayed unchanged, staff concluded that adjusting service hours alone unlocked underused public value.

What primary conclusion can be drawn from the library's initiative?

A. Realigning existing resources with user schedules can widen public access without extra inventory.
B. Extending operating hours requires proportional increases in collection budgets and lending limits.
C. Evening attendance grows only when libraries replace lending services with quiet reading rooms.
D. Resident surveys are effective solely for redesigning physical floor plans in public institutions.

**Answer:** A. Realigning existing resources with user schedules can widen public access without extra inventory.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Realigning existing resources with user schedules can widen public access without extra inventory. (Correct):** The passage explains that extending evening hours following a resident survey widened attendance among students and shift workers without altering lending rules or buying extra books, showing that schedule alignment alone unlocked greater public value.
- **B — Extending operating hours requires proportional increases in collection budgets and lending limits.:** This contradicts the passage, which explicitly states that book purchases and lending rules remained unchanged.
- **C — Evening attendance grows only when libraries replace lending services with quiet reading rooms.:** Adding a quiet study zone complemented the new hours, but the passage does not claim lending services were replaced or made evening attendance depend solely on reading rooms.
- **D — Resident surveys are effective solely for redesigning physical floor plans in public institutions.:** The survey prompted the library to extend operating hours, not merely redesign floor plans across all public institutions.
- **Exam Extra:** Main-idea questions in SSC Reading Comprehension often reward synthesizing the cause (schedule change) and outcome (broader access without added inventory) rather than picking a minor detail.

### Q702 · Reading Comprehension - Inference

To curb groundwater depletion, an agronomist compared two irrigation schedules across adjacent plots of identical soil. The plot watered before sunrise consumed nearly a third less water while yielding a harvest comparable to the midday-irrigated plot. Rather than overhauling the entire farm at once, she replicated the trial across a second growing season to account for shifting weather patterns.

Why did the agronomist delay changing the irrigation schedule across the whole farm?

A. She doubted that early-morning watering could ever match midday crop yields.
B. She wanted to verify that the water savings held steady under varying seasonal conditions.
C. Soil differences between the adjacent plots made the initial comparison unreliable.
D. Equipment limitations prevented her from irrigating multiple plots before sunrise.

**Answer:** B. She wanted to verify that the water savings held steady under varying seasonal conditions.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — She wanted to verify that the water savings held steady under varying seasonal conditions. (Correct):** The agronomist repeated the irrigation comparison over a second growing season specifically to account for shifting weather patterns before applying the change farm-wide.
- **A — She doubted that early-morning watering could ever match midday crop yields.:** The first trial already showed that the pre-sunrise plot produced a comparable harvest while using nearly a third less water.
- **C — Soil differences between the adjacent plots made the initial comparison unreliable.:** The passage specifies that the trial was conducted across adjacent plots of identical soil, ruling out soil differences.
- **D — Equipment limitations prevented her from irrigating multiple plots before sunrise.:** No equipment constraint is mentioned; delaying the overhaul was a deliberate scientific precaution.
- **Exam Extra:** Look for purpose markers like "to account for" or "in order to" in short passages; they frequently signal the answer to "why" inference questions.

### Q703 · Reading Comprehension - Inference

A secondary school launched a weekly reading circle in which students chose titles across diverse genres rather than following a rigid syllabus. Participation climbed steadily over the term as reluctant readers discovered subjects that resonated with them. Even so, teachers resisted grading students on raw page counts, preferring brief written summaries that revealed genuine comprehension.

What does the teachers' assessment method suggest about their educational priority?

A. They valued reading volume over analytical engagement with the text.
B. They aimed to restrict genre diversity once participation stabilized.
C. They prioritized depth of understanding over superficial metrics of output.
D. They considered weekly discussions insufficient without standardized multiple-choice exams.

**Answer:** C. They prioritized depth of understanding over superficial metrics of output.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — They prioritized depth of understanding over superficial metrics of output. (Correct):** By rejecting raw page counts in favour of brief written summaries that revealed genuine comprehension, the teachers showed they valued depth of understanding over mere quantity read.
- **A — They valued reading volume over analytical engagement with the text.:** This reverses the teachers' stated stance, as they explicitly resisted grading based on raw page counts.
- **B — They aimed to restrict genre diversity once participation stabilized.:** Student choice across diverse genres drove participation up, and nothing suggests teachers planned to restrict genre variety.
- **D — They considered weekly discussions insufficient without standardized multiple-choice exams.:** Written summaries were used instead of page counts; standardized multiple-choice testing is never mentioned in the passage.
- **Exam Extra:** Contrast words such as "even so" or "rather than" highlight the author's or subject's true priority in SSC comprehension items.

### Q704 · Reading Comprehension - Cause and Effect

A mid-sized workshop replaced its ageing fluorescent fixtures with high-efficiency LED lamps. Within weeks, electricity bills dropped noticeably while task-area illumination remained well within safety standards. By diverting part of those energy savings into preventive machine servicing, management cut unexpected equipment stoppages during the following quarter.

How did the lighting upgrade indirectly improve production reliability?

A. Brighter illumination enabled workers to repair broken machines faster.
B. Reduced power draw prevented electrical surges from damaging factory motors.
C. Lower fixture heat eliminated the need for routine equipment servicing.
D. Cost savings from lower energy use funded preventive maintenance on machinery.

**Answer:** D. Cost savings from lower energy use funded preventive maintenance on machinery.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Cost savings from lower energy use funded preventive maintenance on machinery. (Correct):** Energy savings from efficient LED lamps were redirected into preventive machine servicing, which directly reduced unexpected equipment stoppages in the next quarter.
- **A — Brighter illumination enabled workers to repair broken machines faster.:** Task illumination remained within safety standards rather than becoming a tool for faster emergency repairs.
- **B — Reduced power draw prevented electrical surges from damaging factory motors.:** The passage links operational reliability to preventive servicing funded by cost savings, not to electrical surge protection.
- **C — Lower fixture heat eliminated the need for routine equipment servicing.:** Fixture heat and eliminating routine maintenance are unsupported; in fact, preventive servicing was increased using the savings.
- **Exam Extra:** Chain-of-events questions trace Step A (LED upgrade) -> Step B (lower bills) -> Step C (maintenance funding) -> Step D (fewer stoppages). Avoid options that skip the intermediate mechanism.

### Q705 · Reading Comprehension - Factual Synthesis

When a district clinic noticed high missed-appointment rates for routine screenings, its outreach team began sending SMS reminders to scheduled adults. Each message listed exact clinic hours alongside a direct helpline for rescheduling if conflicts arose. Attendance rebounded markedly, particularly among patients who had previously skipped visits simply because they lost track of dates.

Which barrier to healthcare access did the clinic's intervention primarily address?

A. Patient forgetfulness and friction in modifying appointment times
B. Public skepticism regarding the accuracy of routine medical screenings
C. Shortage of clinical staff during peak morning consultation hours
D. High transportation costs faced by residents in remote districts

**Answer:** A. Patient forgetfulness and friction in modifying appointment times
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Patient forgetfulness and friction in modifying appointment times (Correct):** The SMS reminders directly tackled patient forgetfulness ("lost track of dates") and reduced rescheduling friction by providing clinic hours and a direct helpline.
- **B — Public skepticism regarding the accuracy of routine medical screenings:** Patients missed routine visits because they forgot dates, not out of distrust toward medical screenings.
- **C — Shortage of clinical staff during peak morning consultation hours:** Nothing in the passage indicates a staffing shortage during morning clinic hours.
- **D — High transportation costs faced by residents in remote districts:** Transportation barriers are outside the scope of the passage, which focuses on date recall and rescheduling ease.
- **Exam Extra:** In problem-solution passages, match every component of the intervention (reminder + rescheduling helpline) to the underlying bottleneck it removes.

### Q706 · Reading Comprehension - Main Idea

Following persistent complaints about twenty-minute waits at a congested transit hub, city planners audited passenger boarding counts over six consecutive weeks. Using those patterns, they redistributed departures along the corridor to smooth peak intervals without adding a single extra bus to the daily fleet. Consequently, maximum wait times fell sharply while total operational trips remained unchanged.

What does the planners' solution best illustrate about urban transit management?

A. Eliminating commuter delays always demands expanding the vehicle fleet.
B. Data-driven scheduling can reduce bottlenecks within existing capacity limits.
C. Passenger complaints are less reliable than single-day visual inspections.
D. Increasing daily trip counts inevitably worsens congestion at major hubs.

**Answer:** B. Data-driven scheduling can reduce bottlenecks within existing capacity limits.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Data-driven scheduling can reduce bottlenecks within existing capacity limits. (Correct):** Auditing six weeks of boarding data allowed planners to cut maximum wait times significantly without adding extra buses or trips, proving data-driven scheduling can ease bottlenecks within fixed capacity.
- **A — Eliminating commuter delays always demands expanding the vehicle fleet.:** The passage disproves this claim by showing wait times fell sharply without adding a single extra bus.
- **C — Passenger complaints are less reliable than single-day visual inspections.:** Planners audited six consecutive weeks of boarding counts, but the passage makes no comparison with single-day visual inspections.
- **D — Increasing daily trip counts inevitably worsens congestion at major hubs.:** Total daily trips stayed constant rather than increasing, so no connection between more trips and worse congestion is stated.
- **Exam Extra:** Extreme qualifiers like "always" or "inevitably" in answer choices are classic SSC distractors when a passage demonstrates a flexible alternative.

### Q707 · Reading Comprehension - Inference

Organisers of a civic health fair evaluated printed flyers against automated text alerts to maximize turnout. Although digital messages proved far less expensive to distribute at scale, ward surveys showed that elderly and rural households frequently lacked reliable internet connectivity. Recognising this divide, the committee deployed both channels in tandem rather than abandoning print prematurely.

Which principle guided the organisers' final communication strategy?

A. Selecting the cheapest broadcast channel regardless of demographic reach
B. Eliminating print media whenever digital tools offer faster delivery
C. Matching outreach formats to the varying technological access of the audience
D. Prioritizing rural households over urban residents in civic campaigning

**Answer:** C. Matching outreach formats to the varying technological access of the audience
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Matching outreach formats to the varying technological access of the audience (Correct):** Even though text alerts were cheaper, organisers kept printed flyers alongside digital notices because elderly and rural households lacked reliable internet access—demonstrating audience-centered channel selection.
